PART ONE

G
CHAPTER I
REEN VALLEY was coming to life. In another half-
hour there would be day. Yellowish lamps glowed
dully from windows everywhere. They shone at ir-
regular intervals in the squat, cooplike log houses along the
main thoroughfare that ran flat in the mud and ice of the
lowlands. They made a long blur in the second story of
the Valley House up toward the railroad. And to the north-
ward on the hillside above the tracks, they stood out with
imperial regularity in the two dozen or more deal-board
houses of Company Row.
The side door of the Valley House creaked open and a
man with a flaming miner's lamp in his cap came out. As
he made his way down across the trampled, frozen sod of
the front yard he brought both his hands and his shining
dinner bucket-up to his face to cover his pipe and get it
going. He stumbled out into the road over the rough
ground and walked along the irregular row of log houses
until he stood before one that bulked a little larger in the
darkness than its neighbors.
He listened for a moment, then gave a shrill whistle which
ended with two softer, identifying notes. Hounds leaped
from under houses in a half-dozen dooryards and joined in a
deafening tumult. The man stood waiting, the flare of the
open lamp in his cap chasing lights and shadows across his
clean but grizzled face.
A man of the same age came out of the house. With
scarcely an audible word of greeting they started off to-
gether. They trudged eastward along the rough road for a
few rods and then turned into a path on the right which ran
3
4
THE FIREMAKERS
across a narrow valley tributary to the river farther east,
and up and along a high ridge toward a leaden glow on the
horizon where the gob piles of the mine were burning.
Other doors creaked and slammed; other men soon fol-
lowed. Flaming lamps in men's caps hurried with unsteady
waltzing movements from along the hillside and roadside, and
converged in this path. Soon the path marked a procession.
Some of the lights moved faster than others and fell into
groups; some went in pairs, some singly; but all moved in
one direction.
The lowlands were dull with smoke and tasted of sulphur;
the gob piles burned ceaselessly. But as the men climbed the
hill and came out upon the ridge a mile south of Green Val-
ley, they were above the smoky pall. They could look over
it as if they were on a mountain top looking across the backs
of the clouds. In the east a sun yet some distance out of
sight was inflaming a band of snow clouds that stretched
along the horizon. The men no longer needed their lamps
for easy walking, but many obliviously let them burn. As
they moved in procession along the ridge in the brightening
twilight, they loomed gaunt and oversize, like figures in a
spectral army.
With the coming of the light of day, the spectacle grew
earthly, definite, factual. The ridge had been overrun by
broomsedge and black locust, and stood bleak in the wintry
morning. The pall of smoke did not reach quite over to the
hills, and below it the valley had been left splotched and
desolate by inundations of red sulphur water from mine
openings. On the switch which crept from the railroad just
below Green Valley, a half-mile of lank flat-cars waited to
be pushed up to the tipple by the morning work engine
and filled with coal by the men who were on their way to
the mine.
In the light of day, the men themselves likewise seemed
less poetic. They wore discolored, dirty "diggers," with
THE FIREMAKERS
5
heavy patches of bed-ticking or leather on the knees and
seats of their trousers, and they carried huge, elongated din-
ner buckets containing compartments of food and coffee.
Occasionally a man carried a new clean-handled pick which
was making its first trip to the mine, or a broad blue shovel
with a handle equally untouched by the blackness of coal.
The men talked about poker games, revival meetings at the
Methodist Church, the price of bacon, the number of beers
a man could drink without getting drunk, the women in the
community definitely known to be purchasable, the cost of
school-books, the poor quality of calico handled by the Com-
pany Store-the Hand-out-and the outlook for the human
race, especially themselves.
Two of the young men who had started late, Luke Dab-
ney and Hi Crumblett, strode briskly in order to be at the
mine as early as the rest. As they passed the two old men
who had started first, Hi called out cheerily, "'Morning,
Mr. Dabney! 'Morning, Mr. Boyd." Apart, men spoke of
these two lean survivors as Old Theron and Old Nate. But
something in their grim serenity moved a man to hush up
all undue familiarities when he came into their presence.
They had served in the same company of Ohio Volunteers
for the duration of the Civil War, and since Appomattox
they had been buddies in the mines.
Hi said nothing until he and Luke were far enough ahead
to be somewhat out of hearing. The trouser-legs of their
diggers, stiff with the odorous dirt of the mine, whetted at
the knees in steady rhythm, and the coffee in their dinner
buckets sloshed accompaniment.
"Gad," he began when he was sure he would not be heard
by the two behind, "I never see your dad and Old Nate goin'
to work without I think what lucky dogs they've been.
Borin' around in these damned hills for twenty years and
better, and hardly so much as a busted thumb. I never ex-
pect to last like that."
6
THE FIREMAKERS
"I hope I never have to," Luke replied, with a bit of fire.
Hi glanced up dubiously at the side of Luke's face. He
liked Luke. But always Luke was startling him. Some-
thing of the elder Dabney's detachment, something of his
serene inscrutability, flashed across Luke's face whenever
anyone touched him closely. Neither the drab landscape
of which he was a part, nor the nondescript set of diggers
which covered all of him except his hands and face could
quite conceal the man that he was. He was alive and dis-
tinct in his movements. There was fineness in his good-
sized nose and ears. There was an inquiring, expectant ex-
pression in his blue eyes which said that he hoped to en-
counter something more interesting than anything he had
yet come upon. Hi was thirty-three and Luke was a good
ten years younger; yet when they were alone, Hi was always
on the point of being deferential.
Not that Hi was a dullard himself. In winter he went every
evening to Harry Byrd's cobbler shop to discuss the future
-if any of the wage earner. In summer he sat after sup-
per on the rickety front porch of his house in Company
Row, his stocky bare feet propped against the warped corner
upright, and struggled over pages of books on socialism until
it was too dark to see. He was much in doubt about the
meaning of most that he read. In his desperate efforts to
catch gleams from the pages, he fostered mental alertness.
Yet always when he was with Luke he felt that Luke's mind
was running far ahead of his own.
Just what did Luke mean now? Hi wondered for a time
as he walked on without speech, and then he pleased him-
self by assuming that he knew.
"When you get a mine of your own," he began as they
started down the long, gentle slope to the tipple, "you'll give
me the job of being boss, won't you?"
"When I get a mine of my own," Luke replied in a whirl
1
THE FIREMAKERS
7
of humor that was tinged with disgust at any such prospect,
"I'll give you the whole works."
Hi tried good-naturedly to conceal his disappointment at
having made the wrong guess. "Why, it's a damned nice
business-coal. The continent has to be kept warm, you
know the Governor told our state meeting last spring. Peo-
ple buy coal whether they want to or not. Mighty damned
nice business, I'd call that."
"Whose nice business, I'd like to know?"
Luke's voice was not without friendliness for Hi, yet it
was full of belligerency.
"Just where does the nice part come in? I don't see it.
Look at Bogart who owns this mine. Does he live here
where he can enjoy the niceness? Look at Dad and Old
Nate. You say they haven't had any cracked ribs or crip-
pled back; but look at them! Nice business for them, eh?
And what about Old Nate's flock of girls? Stylish business
for them, I suppose. And my mother-good God!"
Hi was sorry he had brought up the subject. This was
not the way to start the day off. Still he could think of
nothing to shift over to except something sure to make mat-
ters worse.
They jolted on down the frozen slope, their diggers and
buckets marking a more staccato measure.
Luke liked his buddy. He liked the other men. But they
seemed to be oblivious-most of them, at least of their
own plight. Luke was sure that he was not.
"What I want, Hi," he added at last by way of finishing
off in full amicableness, "is to get out!"
"No such luck for me," Hi replied, relieved at the tone
of Luke's voice. "You don't see me a-goin' far, do you? A
wife to buy calico for and four young mouths to feed! Boy,
those mouths reach from ear to ear, same as a young robin's.
Keeps me a-hoppin' like hell to find worms. So if you give
8
THE FIREMAKERS
me your damned mine, I'll take it. I'm countin' on bein'
around here for life."
He laughed. Luke accepted enough of the infectiousness
to throw his head back in a subdued but not unsympathetic
"Ha!"
"All right, I'll give it to you."
Luke smiled straight ahead as he thought of the absurdity
of ever owning a mine of ever wanting to own one.
Hi gave his imagination free rein. "That'll mean work
for all four of my boys. I'll give every one of 'em a good
job and see that they keep it. And I don't have any girls to
look out for at least yet. That's one thing I can let the
other fella worry about, thank God."
His imagination coursed along airily. "I hear 'em say
that Old Nate wants to educate the one of his that's left. I
don't know for a fact, but that's what I hear 'em say."
The mine just below drew their attention thither. Far
out in the tipple which stood high above the switch on
slender oak timbers, a dumper was already busy with a
string of cars held over from the afternoon before. The
enormous lumps banged and rattled over the screens and
tumbled with a thunderous roar into the empty flat-car
below. In front of the blacksmith shop one of the smiths
was engaged in the hazardous enterprise of shoeing a bank
mule. Inside the shop another was busily sharpening picks,
the dead "bimp, bimp" of the hammer on the red-hot steel
changing to a clear, bell-like "bing, bing" as the point of the
pick cooled on the anvil. Men hurried from the blacksmith
shop with sharpened picks and drills and augers. Others
came more deliberately from the powder house with small
black, corrugated iron kegs of powder on their shoulders. A
string of empty bank-cars with a mule at its head stood
waiting, and as the men completed their provisioning, they
put their powder and tools and dinner buckets on the bot-
THE FIREMAKERS
9
tom of the cars and straddled in themselves over the low,
flaring sides.
Jab Strohl, the driver, sat expectantly on the edge of the
front car. His nose was streaked with slitlike scars which
had resulted from the impact of a mule's heel, and his right
cheek bore a deep mark which the calk of the mule's sharp
shoe had left. He bore other marks of his profession. Yet
he enjoyed driving. His red face and heavy yellow mus-
tache were in a constant state of unstable equilibrium be-
tween a broad smile and a furious expression of wrath.
When the last man who belonged to the northwest run
was aboard, some one shouted from the rear cars, "Let her
go!"
Jab clucked to the mule. The mule tightened the traces,
but as he felt the load against his shoulders he shook his
head angrily up and down in protest.
"Git out of this, you lousy bastard!" Jab yelled in a
voice that echoed against the hills, and the mule lunged
forward. Slowly the cars began to roll solidly on the iron
rails, and Jab offered further inducement to speed by a
shrieking crack of his blacksnake whip at the tips of the
mule's ears. Then as the cars gathered headway, he gave
the mule a cut in the flank with the rawhide cracker of the
whip. The mule responded with a burst of speed, but
within two dozen yards slowed down to his customary pace
so that he could step in the deep, regular tracks which he
and his fellows had worn between the cross-ties.
They were entering the hill. Everyone who had been
standing ducked down quickly into a squatting position. As
the light of the morning began to fade, the men who for a
few brief minutes had possessed individual being on the win-
try slope above the mine, once more were only an army, a
legion of submerged entities before an adversary.
All the sounds of the world outside were lost in the roar
of the first few rods of the narrow entry; all the sights of
10
THE FIREMAKERS
the bleak landscape gave way to fleeting gleams that lamps
made on jagged ribs of coal, and to the one constant spot of
light back yonder where they entered the hill. Farther in,
the noise of the little train of cars was muted by the few mil-
lion tons of rock above and around them, and the constant
spot back yonder grew smaller and smaller until it was but
a pinhole of light in the blackness.
A third of a mile from the entrance they passed the fur-
nace at the foot of a deep shaft sunk from a notch in the
hill above. A giant fire burned incessantly and sent up
through this chimney of rock and earth a current of heated
air that let cooler air rush in at the entrance to the mine, at
the mouth of the air course on the other side of the hill, and
wherever else there chanced to be an opening.
Beyond the furnace the train swung off into a side entry
at the right. Here Jab began to drop cars and men.
Theron Dabney and Nathan Boyd were left at Room No. 1.
In the next three or four rooms nobody was working. As
the cars rolled by, the round, muted roar flattened out into
something hollow.
"Hear that?" Pewee Dunkle asked anxiously. "That
roof's loose." Pewee was an authority on disaster.
"Jesus Priest, Pewee," Hi Crumblett called from the next
car, "you hear roof a-fallin' all day. Do you hear it at
night, too? It's a good thing you don't live in a plastered
house."
But Pewee was persistent. "I'll bet there's ten ton of
rock hangin' there on a sliver no wider'n your eyebrow."
"Then Jab'll shake it down some day cussin' a mule," Hi
called back.
Jab commenced making other stops. When he came up
to No. 12 he left Pewee and his buddy, Sam Swisher. At
No. 15 he left Hi Crumblett and Luke Dabney. They
swung their car round into the neck of the room with a deft
push.
THE FIREMAKERS
11
Here was work that put the test to men's backs-a vein
of coal three feet nine inches high. Out in the entry there
was more space upward. Out there, if a man were not too
tall, he could almost stand straight. There was necessity.
The bone-coal above the true coal had to be taken down-
or the floor of the entry had to be taken up-to make height
sufficient for the small breed of mules that hauled the coal.
But the mules did not enter the rooms. The men had to
push their own cars up from the entry; and back again,
when they were loaded. The rooms were so expansive that
the great area of roof could not be taken down for the mere
comfort of human backs blessed with capacity to bend. If
the men did not wish to work throughout the day without
straightening up, they could go down by the furnace at noon
to eat their dinners.
Soon the heart of the hill was a-quiver with this army of
supple pigmies who battled against the stubborn line of coal.
Hi and Luke, after they had pushed their car up to the face
of their room, crept round it and began loading the coal
hurled down by the shot which they had lighted when they
left the room the day before. On their knees they lifted
huge lumps with their bare hands into the low end of the
car. In the same position, or on their feet with their backs
bowed tight against the roof, they shoveled in the smaller
lumps and the slack. Then they rolled more of the large
lumps along the flaring top of the car and piled more small
coal in between, so that the car carried every pound that
would stay on.
It took strength. Or it took some strength and much
skill. Old Theron Dabney and Nathan Boyd, for instance,
could no longer catapult a great lump into a car, but by
means of rhythmic, muscle-saving swings they could roll it
in at the end of the car with less effort and in the same time.
They could cut, too. If they had not quite so much sheer
flexibility of fiber, they had extra-sharp picks. With one
12
THE FIREMAKERS
shoulder on the floor of the mine, but with the forearm free,
they cut under and under a full yard, and then along the
face of the coal as far as a shot would have effect. They
could set an auger up deftly, too, so that it would eat its way
into the coal exactly where the powder would do most good.
They could tamp the most difficult shot so that the tamping
would not blow out and leave the coal standing. They could
twist the smooth four-foot needle neatly from the cartridge
when the tamping was all in, and leave a perfect path for the
lighted squib to sputter back through to the powder. Their
stiffened knees still enabled them to dart with a certain
reminiscence of agility out into the entry or into some other
haven of safety after they had set the squib alight. In the
battle against the glistening line of black that daily retreated
a little through the hill-though not without making many
sudden reprisals-they still advanced as rapidly as their
fellows.
They talked, too. They had accumulated much knowl-
edge and some wisdom. They recalled the days when the
region roundabout was covered with cattle and flocks of
sheep which their grandfathers had led over to the hills of
southeastern Ohio from the hills of Virginia. They had seen
peacocks parading in a hundred farmyards, deer in a dozen
orchard parks, and carriage loads of well-dressed men and
women exchanging visits at dignified white or stone or brick
houses. Then they had seen the rise of coal, and the conse-
quent steady encroachments of russet broomsedge on the
green of the hills as the farms were forgotten. They had
seen what coal had done to themselves and their children.
They could discuss such matters, these two grizzled ones.
There was time when they stopped to eat a chunk of bacon
in the middle of the forenoon. There was time when they
sat and waited for an empty car that Jab was slow in bring-
ing.
Talking-talking even about the devastation that coal
THE FIREMAKERS
13
had wrought-was pleasanter than listening too much.
Despite long and fortunate experience in mines, they did not
like the absolute negation of sound which prevailed in the
heart of the hill. Something in it suggested eternal vacuity.
In comparison, the silence of the highest hilltop seemed
tumultuous uproar. Sometimes, of course, the silence was
broken. They could hear the faintest chip-chip in the rock
above them, the "working" of the mine roof caused by men's
success in stealing the coal out from under some few million
tons of hill above. It was just as well to keep occu-
pied with other matters with the outlook for their kind, or
the future of one's daughter. There they were for life,
themselves, and they might as well make the best of it.
But Luke, as he worked, driving the point of his pick into
the face of the coal until the flying bits peppered his cheeks
with spots of stinging hotness, or swinging huge lumps of
coal into the car with the litheness of a young tiger, was
little concerned about the silence or all the suggestive little
noises above his head. He was looking. He looked beyond
the face of the coal, beyond the hill itself. What he saw was
a little vague, but it was very alluring. He meant to break
step with the army. He meant to get out.
T
CHAPTER II
HE women who were left behind in Green Valley
were occupied, too. As soon as the men were off
to the mine and out of the way, the women com-
menced getting the children up.
"Halcy! Oh, Halcy!" Mrs. Pewee Dunkle called up
through the twisted, whitewashed stairway to her first-
born. "Halcy! It's half after six. Get the other two
awake and come on down to your bread and coffee."
The first call was not fruitful. Mrs. Dunkle immediately
drew upon other moral resources. "Halcy! Do you hear
me? If you and the other kids ain't down here in five min-
utes, I'll be up there, and there won't be an inch of hide left
on your back."
While the three blear-eyed boys ate their porridge of
bread and coffee and picked lean strips from some thick
bacon that had remained warm on the back of the stove,
Mrs. Dunkle packed thick slices of bread and butter, chunks
of cold meat, and pieces of dried-apple pie into a big dinner
bucket for them to carry to school.
After breakfast Halcy joined the Crumblett boys in
front of their part of Company Row and laughed at a hound.
Whenever it barked, it pained its own ears, and then shook
its head in a violent attempt to free its ears of the stinging.
When half the boys on the hillside had assembled round the
dog and joined in the shouts of merriment over his distress,
a row of mothers appeared on the little front porches and
ordered all of their eligible offspring away to school. Soon
they went in reluctant procession down across the railroad
tracks, down through the main thoroughfare where others
14
THE FIREMAKERS
15
joined them, and on for three-quarters of a mile to a huge
maple tree by the river where their grandfathers or great-
grandfathers-had built a schoolhouse before a more recent
civilization had brought forth Green Valley on another spot.
They went shouting and jeering and fighting, banging one
another over the head with their dinner buckets, and eating
a part of what they found inside when some loose lid flew
off.
When the men were in the mine and the houses were
cleared of children old enough to go to school, the women
began their work proper. They always knew what to do on
a given day. On Monday-if the day chanced to be Mon-
day-they gathered up the dirty clothes from under the
stairway, and from two or three other corners, and piled
them on the kitchen floor. Then they turned a chair down
so that it rested on the floor the full length of its front legs,
and placed a washtub on the full length of its back legs.
Into this tub of foaming suds and of clothes blackened and
odorous with coal and powder smoke and oil, they plunged
a washboard of mammoth size and then rubbed vigorously
away, with sleeves pushed far above elbows, until the wash-
ing was done.
Mrs. Dunkle always had beans for supper on Monday.
They could cook while the stove was hot for wash day. She
could tell exactly when to take respite from washing and put
on the beans in order to have them done by the time she
was ready to hang out the last tub of clothes.
She baked bread on Tuesday while she ironed. Tuesday,
Thursday, and Saturday were bake days for her. When the
children were off to school she mixed the bread, shut the dog
out of the house, and put the loaves of dough under inverted
milk crocks before the warm fireplace in the front room.
Then she cleared the kitchen once more and ironed while
the dough was approximating the shape of the inside of the
16
THE FIREMAKERS
crocks. By the middle of the afternoon she was through
with both ironing and baking and could start supper.
On other days there was always something to fill in odd
times with. She had to make shirts and coats and breeches
for the boys. Once in a while she had to make a dress for
herself. And she always had some diggers to patch. Some
men patched their own-Mr. Boyd, for instance-but
Pewee was very awkward with a big needle and linen thread.
On Saturday while she was baking enough bread to last
through Sunday and wash day-a great ten-gallon jar of
it-she found time to bake some dried-apple pies or a layer
cake.
She always had her work out of the way and the washtub
of water ready for Pewee when he came home from the mine
at four-thirty or five o'clock. In winter she placed this tub
in the middle of the floor before the fireplace in the front
room; in summer, in the coal shed back of the house.
Green Valley was divided into two schools over the right
place for the tub in winter. Most of the women in Company
Row belonged to Mrs. Dunkle's school and placed it in the
front room. If it is in there, they argued, the man can come
home, strip to the waist, and clean up while you are getting
supper in the kitchen. You go in and clean up the floor
after him, and you are ready for supper. Mrs. Boyd headed
the other school. She believed in having him clean up in the
kitchen. She always started supper early. Then when
Nathan came home she could let him go into the warm
kitchen and clean up in a jiffy and throw the water out at
the back door. Then she could resume her preparations just
where she had left off, and in a few minutes have the supper
on the table. This method left the front room wholly un-
touched by diggers or black water.
Many of the women in Company Row thought Sudie
Boyd was prejudiced in the matter. Her case was not the
same as theirs. She lived in a nice hewed-log house and
THE FIREMAKERS
17
could have wall paper on the walls if she liked-in fact, did
have it in her front room. Of course that must not be
splashed. But chiefly, she had a nice rag carpet on the floor,
with stripes that matched, and she didn't want that black-
ened up. So it did not follow at all that her school of
cleaning up was a sounder one for people less genteelly situ-
ated.
Perhaps there were other reasons why they were skeptical
about the superiority of her method. She lived much of the
time in a world they never entered. She read books-hun-
dreds and hundreds of them, according to the best authority
in Company Row. Where they came from nobody knew.
Somebody said her grandmother brought them from Vir-
ginia. Somebody said she had high-toned relatives in Balti-
more who would not come to see her, but who made certain
amends by sending her books. In any case, she had them.
And from them she gleaned much wisdom incapable of ap-
plication in Green Valley. Shreds of lore, nevertheless, she
did find useful. She was accepted as the namer of children
for the entire region. If a child came to a family who had
given the matter little forethought and were at a loss for
names, somebody was sure to say, "Why don't you speak to
Mrs. Boyd? She knows such lovely names. Just look at
the names of her six girls." The five already gone were
Leonore, Camille, Gloriana, Arabelle, and Rosamond. The
one left was Valencia.
By the unfailing supply of names which she gleaned from
somewhere, Mrs. Boyd gained followers for her school of
cleaning up. Once when a boy had come a bit unexpectedly
to Hi Crumblett and his wife, and they had appealed to her,
she said with quiet casualness, "Oh, I know just the name
for him." And she named him Shelley.
"A funny name," Hi observed in semi-protest. "Sounds
like hickory-nuts or somethin'."
Mrs. Boyd assured him that some great man or other had
18
THE FIREMAKERS
borne the name of Shelley, and that any boy with such a
name to carry him onward might well be expected to arrive
on a high pinnacle.
Mrs. Dunkle thought any woman with such romantic
knowledge did not belong, properly speaking, in Green
Valley. Still she meant to investigate the virtues of Sudie's
well-known method.
It was, however, only in the details of social practice
that Sudie Boyd stood apart from those who had not
accepted her theory of cleaning up. In essentials they
knew she was of their kinship. Whatever their school, the
women constituted the support behind the lines which
enabled the men to battle the coal yonder in the hills.
They were one in their state of expectancy. After the men
had hurried away in the morning, who could know when or
how they would come back? They might walk two miles
to the mine only to learn that there would be no work that
day. They might come home at noon after a scant half-
day because the railroad company had not put enough flat-
cars on the switch to receive a full day's run of coal. They
might come in the middle of the afternoon because some
boy who tended a trap door had been negligent and the air
in a section of the mine had ceased to circulate, and rooms
were left full of dead air and powder smoke.
Or they might be brought. Early, at midday, or late, a
man's fellows might have to bring him. A shot might go
off hurriedly and finish a man with a lump of coal sent
like a cannon ball into the back of his head. Sometimes
neighbors on the other side of the entry forgot to shout
"Fire in the hole" when they lighted a squib, and a man
leaving his own room at the moment received stray fruits of
a charge of powder full in the face. Sometimes a loaded
car which had not been equipped with a brake rolled noise-
lessly down alongside a man and caught him where there
was little space between the track and the timbers of the
THE FIREMAKERS
19
room. And always there was something impending above
one's head-a hill, a dozen hills, rock, shale, earth, trees,
habitations of men, all pressing down with incalculable
weight in an effort to close up the little three-foot-nine-inch
space that audacious men had dared to create between the
center of the earth and its outer layer. A "pot" of slate
no larger than a bushel basket might suddenly be pinched
down with a splash that frightened one but did no harm
beyond a few bruises on shoulders and legs. Or a ton or
two might drop-a "horse back." Or a vast area, after
preliminary snappings and groanings, might come down
with the certitude of destiny, crushing heavy wooden props
and heavier pillars of coal into splinters and dust. Or gas
might develop where the workings were supposed to be
entirely free of it, and somebody's open lamp set it off and
bring a quick end to a dozen or a score.
There was reason, then, why those left behind in Green
Valley were always expectant. If old Ebenezer Canaday,
who lived in Company Row with a daughter who had mar-
ried a miner, sat in the sunshine dozing and chanced to look
up and see a group of men coming slowly down the hill
south of town, he pounded with his cane and called, "Fannie
May, they're fetchin' some one!" In two minutes all the
women in the Row would be out in front of their houses.
They brought some one the day Hi Crumblett and Luke
Dabney discussed coal on their way down to the tipple.
The snow clouds that had rimmed the sky in the early
morning spread thinly overhead before nine o'clock, and
by eleven had caused the sun to fade utterly. In another
hour, gusts of wind were driving fine pellets of snow through
the air-not steadily, but in swirls.
That afternoon Mrs. Dunkle finished her ironing and
baking, and took a bucketful of hot soapy water and a
broom to clean the muddy dog tracks from the rough floor
of the front porch. It was cold outside. She came in
20
THE FIREMAKERS
.
shivering, and after she had put the broom in its corner
in the kitchen and had hung the bucket upside down on
the post outside the coal house door, she stood sturdily in
front of the fireplace until she was warmed through. It was
about time to get the tub ready for Pewee. She must carry
in another bucket of coal and have the fire in the grate
roaring. The men's diggers would be frozen stiff on them,
and their legs as chilled as icicles, walking through this
wind and swirling snow for two miles after sweating all day
in the mine.
She wondered if Zink Williamson or any of the other
early quitters were coming yet, and looked across to the
hill to see. She had been intent upon her work. The savage
weather outside was not designed to hold in high relief the
dangers of being underground. For a moment she could
only stare in blank amazement at a group of men coming
down the hillside a half-mile away. The air was a bit white
with snow, but no one could mistake the regular outline of
the group. They were carrying somebody-one man at each
corner of a litter, and some reserves were following close
behind.
She threw the front door open, ran to the side of the
little porch, and called to Mrs. Crumblett: "They're fetchin'
somebody! They're comin' down the hill!" She ran to
the other side of the porch and called to Mrs. Swisher.
These women shouted the news to their neighbors in turn,
and in a moment or two the wives of the entire Row were
on their front porches.
Who was it, anyhow? Every woman who shivered in
the cold and strained her eyes to see more clearly through
the snow feared that she might be the one destined to suffer.
"My God, what if it should be Pewee!" Mrs. Dunkle
exclaimed. "Me left with all these boys, and none of them
big enough to work."
Only anticipation kept her from a flood of tears. Pewee
THE FIREMAKERS
21
himself was always afraid of mines. He was always read-
ing about men who were killed in them, and always talking
about the roof of the mine where he worked. It would be
just like him to go and have something happen to him.
"Oh, don't take on so, Mrs. Dunkle," Mrs. Sam Swisher
implored. "Maybe it's not Pewee at all. We don't know
who it is. It may be anybody. It may be"-she choked
at the thought-"it may be Sam."
She crossed herself and murmured from the Angelical
Salutation: "Holy Mary, Mother of God."
Some of the women hurried into their houses and came
out with crying babies half wrapped up in shawls or old
coats. They stood oblivious of the cold themselves, ges-
ticulating and talking and looking toward the hillside.
Halcy Dunkle suddenly appeared before them and de-
sired to know what they were looking at. He had contrived
an ingenious plea of illness which enabled him to get away
from school early. At the words from his mother, "They're
fetchin' somebody," he was off to meet the slow procession
and find out what it meant.
"They carry him like he's dead," Mrs. Dunkle said, her
voice breaking with fear.
"You can't always tell," Mrs. Swisher urged consolingly.
"When they fetched Jab Strohl we thought they carried
him like he was dead. But it was only a leg that the mule
kicked and broke. He was out again in a few weeks. You
can't always tell. This may be somebody that's just hurt."
Halcy sped across the railroad track, past the Hand-out,
down the frozen, rough road, and off into the path. He
meant to rush right up and ask just what had happened.
But when he came nearer, in the lowlands by Sugar Creek,
a strange terror seized him. Something in the steady regu-
larity of the procession's moving filled him with a sense of
his insignificance. The men were saying nothing. Some
of them looked toward him casually out of white eyeballs in
22-
THE FIREMAKERS
black faces as if he were but the most trivial atom. He
slowed down. He almost stopped. He swung far out of
the path into the whitening ragweeds to let the carriers
come by. They were all more or less unrecognizably black
with the customary work of the day.
What could be under those overcoats thrown across the
stretcher, with white troughs of snow accumulating wher-
ever there was a wrinkle? He swung on round as the
carriers moved forward, and fell in behind the reserves. He
came up close alongside these and walked in the snowy
weeds. He meant to ask, but his tongue was dry. He
dared not look up. He stared at one of the two dinner
buckets which the black figure beside him carried. Some-
how it seemed to belong in a world familiar to him. Then
he glanced up and found that he was walking beside his
father.
"It's Mr. Boyd," his father said, half under his breath.
Halcy waited a few steps. He was almost choked with
relief. "Is he dead?" at last he asked in an undertone.
"No, he's not dead. But he's banged up. Got some
cracked ribs and a broken leg."
Halcy bounded away past the carriers like a flash. He
was soon in the road. As he ran he shouted to two or
three boys half-breathlessly, "They're fetchin' Mr. Boyd."
He hurried past the Boyd house silently. He cleared
the railroad at two leaps. He rushed up the slope to Com-
pany Row.
"It's Mr. Boyd," he shouted.
"Oh, it's Mr. Boyd," the women echoed. "Poor old Mr.
Boyd. He's such a nice man, too."
"Did it kill him?" they called to Halcy.
But Halcy was not ready to give details. He wanted to
be the first to bear the news to the entire Row.
"See what I told you!" exclaimed Mrs. Swisher to Mrs.
THE FIREMAKERS
23
Dunkle triumphantly. "It ain't Pewee; it's Mr. Boyd.
And like as not he ain't dead, neither."
"Come back here and tell us whether he's dead!" Mrs.

from fear. But Halcy was far up the Row and did not heed
until he had carried the preliminary news to the women

"'Course he ain't dead," he explained, still out of breath,
when he came back. "Just four or five ribs busted and a
broken leg. 'Tain't very bad."
It made little difference now about schools of cleaning
up. The women pulled shawls or men's coats tight over
their heads and walked down toward the Boyds' house.
The carriers had already taken Nathan inside. One of
their number had hurried ahead to apprise Sudie so that
everything would be in readiness. She was tidied up for
the afternoon and reading a book.
"Oh, goodness!" she exclaimed and threw the book aside.
"I knew it would come sooner or later."
She moved about in nervous quiet, clearing the way from
the door to the high oak bed in the corner of the front room
and turning down the covers over the footboard.
"They had to fetch me at last, Sudie," Nathan said,
shivering with cold and pain, but trying to be casual.
"I'm glad it's no worse," was all she could say in reply.
But her eyes caught his as the men lifted him on to the
clean white bed, and her few undemonstrative words were
sufficient.
Dr. Ferguson, of Five Corners, chanced to be passing
through Green Valley and some person was thoughtful
enough to tell him that a patient awaited him at the house
of the Boyds. He was always looking for patients, and
drove briskly up before the hewed-log house, his dapple
team puffing healthily after many miles on frozen mud
roads.
24
THE FIREMAKERS
"Where's Val?" Nathan asked of Sudie while Dr. Fergu-
son was busy devising splints and bandages.
"She's not home from school yet, but she ought to be
here any minute."
Outside, the crowd that had gathered listened to Pewee
Dunkle's account of what had happened. Pewee had not
been present, but Theron Dabney had told him about it on
the way while they walked together, relieved of carrying.
Pewee was visual-minded and he reconstructed the complete
incident without loss of dramatic detail. Old Nate was
just finishing up a car from the noon shot while Old Theron
was starting to cut for the evening shot. The trouble was
that there was too much space where they worked that
had no props. Old Nate had been asking for them for a
day or two, but the mine boss didn't have any for him.
The roof seemed tight, so he worked ahead. He was edging
a big lump of coal to the rim of the car when the slate caught
him. "Ka-splash, she come down like a tubful of water on a
hard road."
Pewee stood silent for a moment in order to impress the
huddle of shivering women. His cap was well back on his
head, his eyes were glistening white in his blackened face,
his lips were clean and red. There was something theatrical
in his sinuous body, so warm in the blustery cold.
"Not much of it," he explained in conclusion, "just a
little 'pot.' Not more'n a few hundred pounds in all and
it wasn't solid. But it caught him just right. It got him.
Yes, sir, Old Nate got his at last."
He moved away with the part of the crowd that came
from Company Row. His heart was the least bit weighted
with the dread that he might be caught some day himself.
But for the moment his pride was nourished by his partici-
pation in this dramatic afternoon so long in coming to
Nathan.
CHAPTER III
T
HE next morning Luke Dabney entered a world
which he had little contemplated. Since he could
remember, he had lived in the Valley House next
door to his father. It was an accident that they slept in
separate rooms. They had possessed furniture which The-
ron Dabney refused to part with. In attempting to dis-
tribute it in the two rooms, he found that one bed plus other
furniture in each room fitted better than both beds in one
room. Luke was a tenderer boy than most in Green Valley,
and Theron early found other justifications for their sepa-
rate sleeping quarters: the boy's room could be kept cleaner
than his; and he himself could slip off to work without
waking "the poor little devil" at unseemly hours on winter
mornings.
Luke grew up, in consequence, without spending much
time with his father. In the morning he dressed when Mrs.
Canaday called him. He ate his breakfast. He went to
school. He amused himself after school till supper. He ate
his supper at a long table with his father and a dozen other
miners in red flannel shirts who fed themselves noisily.
After supper he watched the men play poker in the front
room of the hotel, or he listened to old soldiers who talked
about their heroic activities in the pulsing days of the
"Rebellion." Or he laughed at the maudlin observations
which Jude Canaday, son of the landlady, made after a
happy hour or two in the little saloon across the river in
Breadless.
He put school aside at fourteen and got a job as trap-
boy at the mine. It filled him with a sense of importance
25
26
THE FIREMAKERS
to know that if he neglected to close doors at air-crossings
after drivers came along with loaded cars or empties, the
miners back in the hill would soon be forced out by bad
air. He had much time to reflect. At sixteen he reached
the conclusion that he was a man and that there must be
more important things than "trapping" for him to do. His
father and Nathan Boyd took him in with them to show
him how to cut, and handle an auger, and place a shot, and
load a car. He learned quickly. After the briefest appren-
ticeship, he went to work with Hi Crumblett, who happened
to be in need of a buddy.
When he sat with all the men in their red flannel shirts
at Mrs. Canaday's supper table, he thought perhaps his
wiry old dad was as good as any of the rest of them.
Sometimes he admired him secretly with a great pity. But
of course he would never do anything so suggestive of
infancy as to spend time openly with him. And his father
was of a breed of Virginian little given to expressing pa-
ternal sentiment except in the most casual ways.
Old Theron had never worked with anyone except Old
Nate. He did not feel sure that working with anyone else
would be possible. He might try Luke, he thought, since
Luke had spent his brief period of apprenticeship with him
and knew his ways. And since Luke was willing, he faced
the interesting possibility of becoming acquainted with his
father. So much as that could come with a fall of slate
which happened to crack somebody else's ribs and leg.
What an awkward experience it was, this becoming
acquainted with one's origin! The trip to the mine in the
morning, of course, was not difficult. Luke always managed
to go along with some of the other men. They knew that
Theron missed Nate, and when they chanced by, they
slowed down to his pace and did their utmost to be inter-
esting. He, in turn, stretched his own legs so that younger
muscles not yet cramped by years in a mine could move
THE FIREMAKERS
27
with as little restraint as possible. The conversation was
safely general. But as soon as father and son turned off
from the entry into their own room they had to devise
ways of making themselves at ease in the seclusion of their
own company.
They discussed the fall of slate, and how long it would
probably be before Nathan was out again, and whether his
leg would be stiff. They talked a great deal more than
was necessary about every cut they made, about every shot
they placed and lighted, about every car they piled high
with block-like lumps of coal. By discussing these objective
matters, they developed a sense of belonging together.
Dinner time was easy-as easy as the morning trip to
the mine. They went, according to custom, out to the space
by the furnace along the entry and ate with a dozen others.
Here they could listen to Hi Crumblett's observations on
what would be done when socialism came; to Pewee Dun-
kle's picturesque accounts of mine disasters, or to his grave
doubts about the roof in four or five places over the entry
along which they worked; to Skinny Burch's red-hot
stories; to the restrained philosophical reflections of Sam
Swisher, expressed only after a broad smile had held his
face in a state of suspense for some seconds.
As the days slipped by, Luke was unregretful of what
the fall of slate had brought to him. He began to feel less
self-conscious in his father's presence. Then came a strange
audacity. He wanted to thresh out certain matters with
his father. Perhaps some day he would be able to bring
these up-quite casually, to be sure. Yet whenever there
was silence and an opportunity, a tenseness in the atmos-
phere restrained him.
Once they went back into their room after a shot and
fell to splitting and breaking the lumps too large to be
loaded, and otherwise dressing up what a blast of powder
had done so miscellaneously. But their car did not come.
28
THE FIREMAKERS
They talked about the needless delay, and Jab's practice of
delivering cars according to his own whims. They filled
their lamps. They filed the auger bit. They drank a little
coffee that remained in the bottom of their dinner buckets.
They lounged against the pyramid of coal and enjoyed their
pipes. Two open lamps in their caps made two spheres of
light in the utter blackness. These spheres enlarged and
contracted, as if a struggle were on between the soft fluid
blackness and the tiny lamps. Not the slightest sound
relieved the silence. The blackness seemed silence, too,
as well as blackness. No Egyptian kings buried in the
depths of the earth enjoyed more perfect seclusion. Then
in the roof scarcely a foot above their heads as they reclined
against the pyramid of coal came the faintest "chip-chip-
chip."
"This roof's beginning to work already," Luke remarked.
"Don't see how it's possible. Not more than three days
since the face of the coal was back here. There must be a
break in the rock up over us somewheres."
They listened. Again they heard the "chip-chip-chip-
chip" as if one were drawing one's moistened lips apart ever
so gently.
Theron reached for his pick, and with the handle perpen-
dicular tapped firmly with the pick-eye against the roof.
He was thinking of what happened to Nathan.
"Rings like a bell. Not ready to come down yet awhile."
Silence again.
Luke picked up a lump of coal as big as his fist and
examined it intently. "Lots of oil in this coal where we
are now. Look at these peacock tails."
They looked together at the lump. When they were
satisfied Luke hurled it into a remote, abandoned corner of
the room where it banged and cracked against the forest
of oak props.
They looked at other lumps.
THE FIREMAKERS
29
"What do you know about this!" Luke exclaimed. He
brought a lump up close to his face in the full light of the
lamp in his cap. "It's a fern or something. It is a fern.
Did you ever see a more perfect one? Just like it had
been pressed in a book."
Theron reached for the fossil and looked at it for a long
time.
"Your mother would have been interested in that. She
liked pretty things."
Luke was startled. He felt the cold sweat of some un-
expected great experience upon him. But he did not move
and he did not change his gaze.
The silence was absolute.
Finally Luke asked in unfaltering voice: "The story
about my mother is straight, is it?"
Theron did not reply immediately. He, too, was startled
by the turn the conversation had taken. At length he
answered, "Yes-that is, I s'pose it's what you've heard.
But there was nothing wrong between the two of us.
Everything was all right that way. And she thought the
world of you."
Theron looked straight away into the blackness. "We
got along at first-lived in the old brick house just back
of where Company Row is now. In those days it was as
nice and clean as any place you ever saw. We could see
clear to the river over the top of everything down along
the road below the tracks, and we could hear the water
pouring over the mill-dam last thing at night and first
thing in the morning. We had a front yard and flowers and
peaches and grapes and all sorts of truck. But they built
that damned Company Row right under our noses.
wasn't long till we had pig-pens and privies and God-knows-
what-all there on the hillside with us.
It
"The women in the Row said she was stuck up. The
dirty kids carried off her radishes and currants and phlox
30
THE FIREMAKERS
and dahlias and fall-roses and tramped mud all over her
clean steps and porch. What she did was about the only
thing left to do. I wasn't fit for anything except digging
coal-and she knew it."
He flipped a nut of coal far into the darkness and heard
it splash in a little pool of water.
"That's about all there was to it, I guess. She wasn't
there one day when I went home from the mine. Of course
everybody had to talk. They said she had run away to
town where she wouldn't have to look at people who worked.
I knew that was a lie. But I was puzzled, till I finally went
up to bed about midnight and found a note pinned to the
open end of the pillow slip. Her first plan was not to leave
you either, but I guess she didn't quite have the heart to
take you along."
Theron paused, but he did not cease to gaze at an imag-
inary spot along the roof beyond the edge of the circle of
light from Luke's cap. Luke knew he was not through-
that is, if the reports he himself had heard possessed foun-
dation. But he was afraid to move even so much as his
eyes, lest the thread of the narrative be severed.
"We found her the first thing next morning. It was by
the big leaning sycamore just where the switch to that new
mine crosses the river now. The water wasn't two feet deep.
Her hair was floating on top.
"There wasn't anything mysterious about it to me. I
could see plain as day why she did it. She didn't belong in
this black dump and she couldn't find any other way out.
We get stuck often enough just because we don't know
which way to go to find something different. But when
anybody commits suicide, people have always got to hunt
up some dirty reason for it. I didn't rave like a crazy man,
for the simple reason that I couldn't blame her very much.
So they hatched up the story that something had come be-
tween us."

Luke was glad to have the story first-hand. But that was

"Where did she get that big walnut bureau that's in my

"Her mother brought it from Virginia-Fairfax County."
"The tall vases, too?"
"No, her uncle presented them to us as a wedding gift.
He made them. Somebody presented us the two glazed

"Where did the blue dishes come from?"
"Somebody or other brought them. Made in England-
Staffordshire, or some such place. Ever see anything bluer
than that teapot? A hundred years old and better-least-
ways that's what I've been told."
"Do they make such things now?"
"Why-somewhere, I suppose."
"But I mean around here."
"Couldn't say as to that. Most everybody around here
digs coal, same as we do."
Far down the entry they heard the smallest little rumble
the ear is capable of recording-a complete complement of
noises, but distant like a mountain or a cloud seen through
the small end of a telescope. By imperceptible gradations
the rumble became roundly audible, yet muted always as if
there were no room for the sound to reverberate. Then they
could hear variations in the roar; then the bump and rattle
of the cars as the wheels struck uneven joints in the rails;
and then the eloquent voice of Jab: "Get out of this, God
damn you, or I'll lay bare every rib in your body."

THE FIREMAKERS
Luke went down to push the car into the room. "Heard

Luke and Theron worked to make up for lost time. They
got the coal on the car and turned to preparations for the
next shot. When they had settled down to the task of bor-

"Guess you're right. Unless you want to dig coal you'd
better not stay around here."
He worked on, waiting for his father to reply. When his
father made no reply, he added: "And I don't want to dig
coal all my life."
"What are you going to do about it?" Theron asked, as
if he himself had answered the question long ago.
Luke was deliberate. "Why shouldn't a fellow get out?"
"No reason in the world!"
There was irony in Theron's voice. Luke thought the
quality seemed strange in his father. Evidently Theron felt
the strangeness himself. He offered a little amplification:
"If he knows where he's going, and how he's going, and
what he's going to do when he gets there."
So Theron had thought about the matter himself, had he?
Luke worked away at the auger, glad to have opportunity to
reflect.
Theron did not wish to leave him too high and dry. "All
these farmers around here are going to Kansas-all of them
that ain't turned to digging coal. But if you go to Kansas

THE FIREMAKERS
33
you've got to know how to farm. If I was younger, I don't

"Everything exciting seems to have happened before I

"That's what I used to say when I was young. And my

"But you were in the Civil War."
"You don't call that a fairy tale, do you?"
"Sounds like it to me when I hear you all telling about it
around the fire, or on the Hand-out steps."
"Not much!" Theron was in an unmistakable state of
mind. "Not much! Forced marches-sleeping in the
snow-living for days on hardtack and water-holding on
to some hill with canister raining down all round you-men
with their heads blown off or their guts blown out stink-
ing dead horses, their heels sticking in the air-you don't
call that a fairy tale, do you?"
Luke had never before heard his father try to be elo-
quent. He was impressed; yet he was in that comfortable
state of mind where he could argue without being too seri-
ous. "But it don't sound like that when you and Old
Nathan and Randolph Lake and all the rest of them sit
around and tell your stories."
Theron was glad his own flesh and blood could see clearly.
"Well, anyhow," he replied, more inclined toward joviality
than Luke had expected, "what I've told you is as near the
truth as anything you ever hear us say when we're gassing
around the fire or on the Hand-out steps."
"Just the same, it was better than a coal mine, wasn't it?"
Luke urged. He felt that he was winning a point.
"Well, I don't know. I don't know. When you come to
think about it, there's not so much difference."
Theron knew that Luke was right in his main contention.
34
THE FIREMAKERS
But he did not want his only offspring to be running away
too soon. Selfish affection made it easy for him to cham-
pion what in this instance Luke questioned.
That evening after supper Theron went over and sat
with Nathan for an hour and gave him the latest news
from the mine. Luke saw him going and went upstairs to
make a complete inventory of what their two rooms con-
tained. He studied the vases, the spaniel dogs with gold
collars, the blue tea-set from England. Somebody, prob-
ably somebody no better than he, made these things. Think
of that! Somebody designed them! How would you get
those curves just right, so that the thing would not seem too
fat or too spindly? And those landscapes in blue on the
china: how did anybody ever get them there? That gor-
geous teapot with scallops taken out of its sides! A man
who could think that up, right out of his head, was almost
as great as God Almighty.
The idea did not leave Luke's head. If he put it aside
for a moment, something he saw or heard brought it back.
One day when Jab was slow again in bringing a car, Luke
asked without preliminaries: "Why couldn't a man make
something out of this clay that's sticking out of the hills
around here? There are pot-shops over north, aren't there?"
Theron had put his lamp down on the floor of the room
so that he could see clearly just where he was cutting. He
took several strokes with his sharp pick, the fine bits of
coal singing out into the air as if they were electric. "I'm
not sure but what you might," he answered, still intent upon
a spot under the ledge of black.
At last he sat up. After a breath or two he went on: "Of
course you'd have to know how. You'd have to know how
to mix clays, and make glazes that wouldn't burn off, and
fire your kilns just right. It would take time to learn all
that."
THE FIREMAKERS
35
"But there are places where a man can learn such things,
don't you think?"
"I suppose there must be."
A bit of slate, no larger than a dollar, dropped on
Theron's knee. There could be nothing wrong with the
roof, yet he gave it attention. He had been sensitive to
what was going on over his head ever since the slate had
caught Nathan.
"Then I guess I'll learn," remarked Luke.
Theron looked at him puzzled. Theron had been think-
ing about the roof.
A day or two later when they worked together at the
auger, Theron asked a question. "How much money have
you saved up?" He knew that Luke had been putting
something aside each pay since he began as trap-boy eight
or nine years before.
"About eleven hundred."
Theron worked on. There was plenty of time. He
reached over to the pile of chips and dust that the auger
had heaped up on a ledge below the hole and carefully
scraped it all away.
"I might be able to add a little to that."
He wanted to subordinate his proposal to make a contri-
bution. "Any notion of how long it would take you?" he
asked nonchalantly.
"Not the slightest. I don't even know where I'd have
to go. I'd have to find that out first."
That evening Luke walked from the mine in a strange
ecstasy. Was it a sense of assured escape? Or was it a
consciousness of his mother's blood in his veins? Or was
it only the exaltation of youth, still potent to lift one from
the dirt at twenty-two or twenty-three? Or was it but an
effect of the urging March sun, which grew warmer every
day? Perhaps it was some chance fusion of them all, some
happy cosmic arrangement that for the first time in the
36
THE FIREMAKERS
history of the world centered just here in just this fashion
in this man.
There was something pleasant in the rattle of the coal
over the screens as he climbed the hill to the ridge above
the tipple. There was something admirable in the men who
trudged along the same homeward path. They were cov-
ered with the dirt of a coal mine until their white eyes and
red lips were the only parts of their bodies not black. They
coughed out mouthfuls of black spittle and left an accumu-
lation in the bottom of their lungs which their most violent
coughing never disturbed. They laughed or were bitter or
told broad jokes as they hurried home to wash themselves
from their foreheads to their waists in a washtub of water
on the floor of the kitchen or front room. They would eat
a huge supper with their knives and fingers, and box their
children's ears, and make stolid retorts to wives grown
peevish with a dirty, cramped existence, and then get up
the next morning and live another day of the same kind.
These untutored men seemed utterly good to Luke as he
trudged along among them. He could see them better now
that he was already a little out of the blackness that en-
gulfed them. He wanted to fight for them as well as for
himself. He wanted to save them from some strangling
enemy whom he felt but could not see.
At the supper table that night he chatted with an ease
that startled even himself. The light of Mrs. Canaday's
red hanging-lamp over the long table brightened up every-
body's clean face into something he had not noticed the
night before, or the night before that.
After supper he slipped a necktie under the collar of his
red flannel shirt and walked up to the Hand-out and chatted
with the loafers awhile. He went down to the post office
and read the Centerville Gazette. He stopped to see how
"old man Boyd" was getting along, and thought as Val in-
vited him in that she was going to be the best-looking one
THE FIREMAKERS
37
of the half-dozen. He had not meant to go in at all, but
found himself dropping casually into a chair before the fire
where Nathan sat propped up with pillows in a rocker, his
splinted leg stretched stiff and useless-looking on a stool in
front of him. Nathan wanted to know if Luke and his
father were through the patch of sulphur they had run into
in the corner of the room, and whether Jab brought them
enough cars. Luke found it easy to discuss all these mat-
ters. Life in the mine was now off yonder a little where he
could see it and make generalizations about it.
He went to the Valley House feeling unready for bed.
Why should a man go to bed when there were so many in-
teresting things to think about? In his room he looked for
a long time at the vases, turning them in his hands as he
looked, and feeling the close checks in the glaze which saved
their graceful lines from hardness.
Could he design one? It seemed remote, doing that, and
therefore perfectly easy. He might begin by drawing the
outline of one of them on paper.
He took a sheet of ruled paper from a box in the top
drawer of the bureau and sat down with it at the wild-cherry
table with the curved legs. He drew a lead pencil from the
spring holder in his vest pocket. This pencil, now that he
tried to do anything with it, was more difficult to manage
than a pick handle. He had not thought of that when he
wrote a letter for somebody, for he wrote an easy hand.
But tracing curved lines with a steady stroke was another
matter.
Still, somehow, he felt at home in trying. He worked on
and on, intent, while Green Valley slumbered. Something
from somebody who had stood behind him, or something
from somebody who had stood behind his father or mother,
or his grandfather or grandmother, told him that he could
do it. He could not at least not that night. But he went
to bed undiscouraged.
38
THE FIREMAKERS
He slept restlessly. In his dreams he was making vases
and teapots. He could see himself moving about-half in a
coal mine, it seemed a master of clays and shapes and
glazes and kilns. There were scaffolds full of freshly made
tea-sets and vases and bowls and other graceful objects
half distinct in the blackness which the lamp in his cap
did not penetrate. And spaniel dogs with gold collars
moved noiselessly among great lumps of coal that lay wait-
ing to be loaded into a car which Jab did not bring.
CHAPTER IV
UKE followed Nathan's fortunes with the sharpness of
a benevolent hawk. Of course he wanted Nathan to
get well. But why be in a splutter? An old man
banged up more or less generally ought not to risk coming
back to work too soon.
In the quiet of midnight after they had carried Nathan
from the mine, he himself wondered if he should ever go
back to work. All his nerves were clamorously alive. He
knew that men sometimes came to the end of their string,
and he wondered if he had not come to the end of his. He
tried to be stoical. He did not wish to disturb Sudie, who
sat the night through in a rocking chair before the fireplace
and snatched bits of sleep while she listened for calls from
him. But he did not sleep. He only lay there and endured
pain and heard the noisy Waterbury clock as it whirred and
banged the slow hours away. By morning he was haggard
and feverishly weary. Out of the side window by his bed
he saw miners' lamps describing all sorts of irregular curves
in the blackness as the men who wore them in their caps
stumbled over the rough frozen ground on their way to
work. He heard their calls to each other, and the bellowing
of the hounds. And soon after all had become quiet again,
he saw the light of day creep into the room. Then he slept.
When he awoke he saw some one far beyond the foot of
his bed there it seemed a mile very, very quietly dust-
ing the chairs. She moved so noiselessly that he was not
altogether certain whether he was yet awake, or only dream-
ing. Yes, he was awake. It was Val, in a school dress with
deep red in it, and with her dark hair caught back loosely
39
40
THE FIREMAKERS
above her full brows. He was feverish again. This girl-
"the one left"-must not stay out of school. He must get
back to the mine.
All day he rolled as much as his splints and bandages
would permit, and sighed, and groaned. Sudie declared that
she had never in her life seen him take on so.
"But I never had to lay off before in my life," he argued
-unanswerably. "Think of all the money we are going to
be out."
But that afternoon relief came. "I am here to see your
father," Mr. Eli Raynor-Patent Leather Eli, locally-ex-
plained when Val responded to his genteel rap, rap, on the
door frame.
He glanced with alert eyes at Val, at the wall paper neatly
tacked to the hewed surface of the logs, at the square, white-
washed joists which supported the upstairs floor just above
his head. But it was only a glance. He proceeded to busi-
ness.
He spoke with casual ease about the natural hazards of
an occupation upon which our entire modern civilization
depends. "The Company-that is to say, Mr. Bogart-sent
me over to express regret that such a faithful miner had en-
countered a bit of hard luck, and to bring you this slight
token of esteem a little check for a hundred dollars. It
will pay a few of the bills."
Nathan forgot his hurts. He grasped the check eagerly
and held it away at arm's length until he could see glim-
meringly the amount written on it in large hand. Eli, dap-
per in well-cut clothes and high collar, stood beaming.
The enthusiasm of a child shone in Nathan's face. "And
you mean it's for me?"
"Absolutely."
"Sudie! Val!" Nathan called with enough energy to give
his ribs a terrific twinge.
"You know the preacher was in here this morning and
THE FIREMAKERS
41
prayed that something good might come to me out of this."
"Well, I went him one better," Eli replied with swelling
joviality; "I brought it."
They laughed, but Nathan's face was caught by a sharp
pain that ran out from his cracked ribs. "Oo-oo-oo!" he ex-
claimed under his breath.
"Must be pretty sore," Eli said, sobered from his own
laughter. "But while you've got your right hand on top
of the quilt, just wiggle your fingers enough to sign this re-
ceipt. Old Bogart will accuse me of spending the money
myself if I don't take him a receipt."
Nathan scrawled his name shakily.
"And if you will be good enough just to put yours here,
Mrs. Boyd, as a witness that he did the signing himself."
Think of that! A hundred dollars! Nathan felt en-
couraged to take his ease a bit. Although the pain that
night was worse than the night before, his somewhat peace-
ful mind helped him to sleep.
"Who knows," he asked the next evening when Luke
dropped in to see him and to congratulate him, "but what
the Company will send me a couple of more checks like this.
They ought to."
Had Nathan known of it, an episode in the office of the
Company-an episode strictly private-would have made
the arrival of other checks seem altogether unlikely. For
when Eli Raynor returned to Centerville from his call on
Nathan, he hurried jauntily through the outer offices into
the office of the president. Rufus Bogart, who trimmed his
beard after the manner of his idol, President Grant, asked
with only a casual glance from his walnut desk: "Did he
sign?"
"Tickled to death," Eli answered and chuckled at his
own shrewdness. "He was so damned excited at the sight
of a hundred dollars that he got his arm out on the cover
and I had him sign without so much as putting his glasses

THE FIREMAKERS
on. Couldn't have read a thing unless it was the big capi-
tal R. And she witnessed."
Eli pressed the paper flat on the desk so that Mr. Bogart
could read.
"February 12, 188-.
"Received of the Sparkling Diamond Coal Company to defray
expenses incident to injury sustained in mine of said Company
on February 11, 188-, the sum of One Hundred Dollars."
Then in very small type and tiny penmanship:

Sparkling Diamond Coal Company, hereby covenant with the said
Sparkling Diamond Coal Company that I will not commence or

"Witness: SUSANNAH BOYD."
But Nathan knew nothing of this episode in Centerville.
So he waited in hope, and took as much ease as his many
pains would permit.
Luke was pleased to see him reconciled to his absence
from the mine. Luke was getting counsel from an unex-
pected source, and he wanted the source to remain avail-
able. He had felt utterly alone. Hi Crumblett, Sam
Swisher, and the others seemed not to understand that it
was a fight to the death. True, they would be glad to have
less dirt. They wanted more hours in the sun, warmer
houses, better pay. Their wives wanted better calico than
the Hand-out troubled to keep on its shelves. But Luke
wanted something besides cleanness. He would be content
with the dirtiest job one human being ever devised for
another, if only he could do something that would enable
him to breathe more freely, to extend his own self into the
THE FIREMAKERS
43
world in which he lived. That satisfied a man's spirit. He
had felt stifled. The hills had pressed too closely round
him. "Do you think," they asked, "that you can dig our
foundations away and not have us come down upon you?"
And coal had said defiantly to him: "Well, now let's see
who is bigger. Go ahead and extend your spirit into what-
ever you like if you can." And then, when he was most
alone, a little quirk of destiny had revealed his father to
him, inculpable, heroic, a special ally.
Luke still walked to the mine with Pewee and Hi and
Sam. It seemed sacrilegious to discuss matters with his
father in the hustle of getting to the mine and getting home.
Unpropitious spirits might chance along at any time and
hear things that would seem nonsense, or worse, to their
untuned ears. It was better to move about casually. But in
the quiet of the mine, after they had worked an hour or two,
or after they had worked two-thirds of the day, they estab-
lished their rough but subtle kinship again, and talked inti-
mately between strokes of the sharp picks, or between
grunts as they twisted the four-foot steel needle from the
tight tamping. His father did not say much. He himself
said little more. But it was somehow evident that his father
understood much that he was unable to express or even to
conceive-perfectly.
One day Luke heard that Nathan's leg was not growing
together properly and would have to be reset. It turned out
that this was only a professional rumor set adrift by another
country doctor who felt that he should have had the case.
But the leg did not unite rapidly. The south winds of spring
came and blew some of the sulphur smoke away and brought
whiteness to the straggling cherry trees of Green Valley be-
fore he went about with much freedom. As the days grew
still warmer, he followed the shade of the house around with
the progress of the sun, and chatted away numerous hours
with old comrades who had abandoned work and were con-
44
THE FIREMAKERS
tentedly living, and letting their wives live, on twelve-dollar-
a-month pensions. The rough edges of the four years they
had spent in the Union armies had been covered over by
time; the more rhythmic outlines stood in high relief. Some
of Nathan's comrades had reviewed campaigns so regularly
for twenty years that they now spoke with assurance and
imagination.
Once Abner Burch, a Confederate carpenter who lived
back in the hills, came along, listened, and was asked to join
in the reminiscence. The men who had faced him on the
firing line heard him with respectful enthusiasm. He was a
"Rebel"; but he was a Virginian. On another day old
Lycurgus Ott happened along. The Civil War names which
the veterans were using registered some sort of impression
on his warped mind. He had gone into the South in '62 or
'63 to put the war down peacefully by moral suasion. After
he was forced to admit his failure, he set out in dazed apos-
tolic fashion to preach in general upon the necessity of "lift-
ing the veil and removing the pressure." He stood in the
road before Nathan's house and listened for a time-tall, his
white hair pushed back over his shapely head, his eyes dull
and indefinite from much looking for something which he
could not see-and then began his accustomed harangue.
But despite all that prolonged Nathan's absence, he at
length moved about freely with only a cane for support, and
Luke knew that his days in the mine with his father could
not be many. Possibly they might learn to talk things over
in their rooms upstairs at the Valley House. Why not? He
viewed the prospect with confidence. He began to view
every prospect with confidence. Something out of the past
came singing through him. He did not mean to work in the
blackness, the dirtiness, the pungent odorousness of coal
and oil and powder-smoke forever.
When men discussed any life other than mining, he lis-
tened as if they talked for his special benefit. When others
THE FIREMAKERS
45
got out, where did they go? Oh, it did not so much matter
just where they went. It was the fact that they were able
to get out at all which fascinated him.
He and his father came down one day to the furnace to
eat their dinner with some of the other men who worked
along their entry. Pewee Dunkle was still protesting that
the roof over the entry just up the way there was dangerous.
He wished Mr. Dabney would look at it.
"You ought to go to Kansas," Hi Crumblett said depre-
catingly. "You could work outdoors there-nothin' but the
sky to fall on you."
Luke sat alert while he ate at a huge chunk of cold meat
and drank coffee from the bottom of his bucket.
"Let's hear about Kansas," he wished, but said nothing.
"Go on with Kansas."
And everybody seemed ready. They talked about the
farmers who were leaving the hills and going to the open
states "out west," and of the adventures they were encoun-
tering.
"How about it, Mr. Dabney," Charley Ferris asked, "do
you want to go?"
Theron was examining a loose pick handle while he ate.
"No. Too flat. I want to be up on top of something once
in a while where I can see."
There was a heavy majority in concurrence.
"Who wants to live on land as smooth as the bottom of a
sorghum pan, anyhow?" asked Hi Crumblett.
"Yes, specially when it's red hot," Charley added.
They laughed approvingly. It was evident that they had
no intention of starting a fresh migration westward.
"Why just the danger of it is enough," Pewee Dunkle sug-
gested.
They did not laugh at him. The consensus of opinion was
against "out west," and they were ready to hear even doubt-
ful evidence which chanced to be on their side of the case.

THE FIREMAKERS
Charley Ferris was warmed up to the subject. "They
say it's hot as hell down in Texas eleven months out of the
twelve-eleven months in an oven and one month on top of
the stove." His wife was in favor of going out west-
"where everybody else is going"-and he felt a little readier
to meet her on the subject after he had marshaled his argu-
ments in the presence of friends.
"I've been told that about a third of the people who go
down there," Pewee said in great seriousness, "die of sun-
stroke."
Nobody would gainsay this.
"And what do they go for?" Hi Crumblett asked. "Just
to chase around on a ewe-necked pony tryin' to round up a
lot of tough, long-horned steers?"
"Long-horned? I should say they are," Pewee chimed in.
For once he felt that he and public sentiment moved in the
same direction. "Good God! Why, Marion Robinson,
when he moved back last December from out there, said you
couldn't ship more than six or eight of them steers in one
box car, their horns are so long.
"And sandstorms! Holy pokeroot! They say you can
build a sod house-if you can locate any sod-and one of
these sandstorms will come along and cover it up till you
can't see head nor tail of it."
He waited for approval. Everyone seemed to think he
had stated the case conservatively.
"And the rattlesnakes!" Charley Ferris added. "Did you
see them two cowboys over at Centerville last summer?
Talk about snakes! They had a Texas rattlesnake that was
seven feet one inch long-thirteen rattles on his damned
tail. One of them fellas told me they was so thick in Texas
you could step out of your house at night and hear them
buzzin' in every direction-just like hummin' birds, by
God, or bees a-swarmin'. They caught this one in their
house. They had been somewheres one night and when they
THE FIREMAKERS

walked into the house and commenced to kick around a little

And there he was on the bed, ready to go to bed with them.
Wow!"
"You can't grow anything out there, anyhow," Hi Crumb-

up, along comes a wind right off the top of a red-hot stove

"And if it ain't hot winds in summer out there," Pewee

Didn't you read in the Gazette how all them people froze to
death right in gunshot of their own houses out in Dakota

sleigh upside down over them, they both had their feet and
hands froze till they dropped off. That's what I call a hell
of a place to winter in."
Theron Dabney put his dinner bucket aside and began to
peck with a hammer at the handle of the pick he had been

"And the cyclones!" Sam Swisher said, the faintest smile
vanishing from his face.
"Now you're sayin' somethin'," exclaimed Lew Petit, who

The atmosphere had become beneficent for persons of

family in it and carried clean across his farm. His little girl

The smile stretched slowly over Sam's face. "I'd come

They all laughed. Their sense of the ridiculous began to
come more completely from its hiding. Hi Crumblett

two into the end of the pickhandle to tighten it in the pick-

"Why," Hi went on after Theron had admitted that he

cyclone just like you was beatin' a passenger train across the
track. It missed the man-just grazed his back-but it

the molasses."
They were ready to break up and go to work anyhow, and
jumped to their feet whooping and laughing.
When the uproar was over, Theron said dryly: "I guess
I'd better go and sound that roof for Pewee."
After he left, the rest of them revived the laugh over the

"Come to think of it," said Skinny Burch, looking about
to see if it were worth while to attempt to reconcentrate at-

"Watch out!" "Here she comes!" "I knew Skinny

They drew a little closer and inclined their heads to hear
what it was to be. In the moment of silence just when he
was ready to begin, they heard the faint click of Theron's
hammer against the hard rock over the entry, and then a
muffled roar as if half the hill had come down. For a mo-

THE FIREMAKERS
Two or three of the least benumbed of them rushed out
into the entry. They could almost see round the slight turn
where the northwest entry veered to the right.
"Hello-o-o!" one of them called in frightened voice.
There was no answer.
"My God, boys," said Lew Petit, "it must have got him."
Luke rushed out into the entry. "Oh, Dad-d-d!" he
called, his voice tremulous. "Are you all right?"
But there was no answer.
"It's got him, I tell you," Lew Petit insisted. And one in
their sudden great fear, all of them started back through the
entry, stumbling and falling and recovering themselves in
their efforts to get over the ties and mule tracks.
Their three or four lamps shone upon a huge pile of rock
and slate. But they could see no man that it had caught.
They clambered over the pile. It stretched fifteen feet or
more along the entry. At the other end, a hand still clutch-
ing a hammer extended from the bottom of it, close down
between the car tracks; and under the edge of the heaviest
pieces of rock was the side of Dabney's face, his tongue pro-
truding from his mouth, his eye bulging. He had never so
much as drawn a second breath.
