CHAPTER V
N the days that followed the burial of Theron Dabney
up in the corner of the little cemetery by the Methodist
Church, Luke was aware that his own state of mind was
without precedent in his experience. He had never been so
much alive. The smallest detail of sight or sound fixed it-
self in him as if it were a stupendous incident in the history
of the world. He recalled every step his fellows had taken
in digging his father from the slate. He saw as if in a flam-
ing photograph every attendant circumstance of their bring-
ing the body to Green Valley. He would hear as long as he
lived the tramp, tramp of the old veterans as they marched
up the aisle of the little church, their feet clumping hard on
the bare oak floor. Every time he closed his eyes he saw
the white blossoms on the running-briars by the rickety gate
of the cemetery. A hundred other things that were only
incidental to the matter of all importance recorded them-
selves ineffaceably upon his sensitized mind. Yet despite
this clearness with which everything impressed itself, life
seemed strangely insubstantial. He had lost contact with
his ordinary consciousness, and walked about in a world that
was sad and evanescent.
He wanted to get back to solid reality. Yet he shrank
from returning to work. While he was working with his
father in Nathan's place, Hi Crumblett had found a new
buddy. Luke could not go back to work with Hi. And he
did not relish working in his room alone, even for a few
days. He would stay out until Nathan was ready to go
back. Nathan thought he would be ready in about a week.
Luke sat on the front porch of the Valley House and
51
52
THE FIREMAKERS
rocked in the broken-armed wicker chair and reflected.
But that got him nowhere. He walked up along the railroad
track in the warm May sun. He was at the station a half
hour before each train was due, and noted who got on and
who got off. He wandered down the track to the switch
that pushed its way through the weeds and briars of the
river valley up to the mine two miles south. He walked
along the new switch that left the main one and crossed the
river to the new mine. He sat on the end of a cross-tie on
the low trestle in the shade of the overhanging sycamores,
now covered with vigorous youthful leaves, and watched
objects float slowly under him, whirl with a slow sweep, and
come to rest under the trees or, if too far out to be caught
securely, sweep on in the current again.
So this was the spot. His mother must have been a thor-
oughbred. He wished he might meet a woman of her
quality. Even her dramatic finish of the brief cycle filled
him with admiration. She was not afraid, anyhow.
For a moment he was diverted from his reflections by a
huge blacksnake that glided from among the pushing young
horseweeds with a strange attitude of body suggestive of the
smile on a kitten's face before it springs at its first mouse,
and, with a sudden rhythmic straightening out, fastened
upon a ground-mole which dared to let its back come above
the earth. A fine way to make a living!
In the late evening he walked to the railroad station once
more. After the two red tail-lights of the Centerville Ac-
commodation had disappeared in the night and left the track
clear he walked along the ends of the ties in the quiet. The
earth seemed to become less nervous, a little steadier in its
breath, about eight in the evening. He felt less tense, a
little surer of his footing. He looked up at the Milky Way
and wondered whether in fact his dad-anything of his dad
-could have been transported to one of those stars. It was
comforting to think so, but it did not seem likely. Yet
THE FIREMAKERS
53
maybe it was true. He was not prepared to say. His
knowledge was chiefly of things within the earth and not of
things in the skies.
On Sunday morning he went to church. He knew not
why. He did not go often and his visits were not wholly
worshipful. Ordinarily he sat in the rear seat on the men's
side-in Sinners' Row. Sometimes the seat overflowed into
the one in front of it, and it had been known to overflow
into two.
However distributed, the occupants of Sinners' Row main-
tained an essential solidarity of feeling. They made wise
cracks to each other about the preacher, about the singing,
about the brethren who testified concerning their tempta-
tions in life. When the congregation sang "Jesus Paid It
All," Lew Petit would say to Charley Ferris, "He didn't get
much for his money when he took over old Ebenezer How-
ard." When Azariah Bingham told, as he did every Sunday
when the invitation was given to testify, how the Lord had
sustained him when he was a soldier "in the army that put
down the Rebellion and struck the shackles from the slave,"
Charley would whisper, "He never got through West Vir-
ginia"-the most reproachful declaration that could be made
in the Green Valley area about any soldier in the war. “No,
sir! Nathan Boyd himself says Azariah never smelt gun-
powder-except once when he shot off his musket by acci-
dent. He never got through West Virginia!"
But this morning, church like everything else seemed to
Luke to be remote from its old attachments. He walked
past the little cemetery. He had thought that it would be
painful to see so soon the tiny, moundlike ridge of yellow
earth. He had had youth's fear of the trappings of death.
Yet he was comforted by the sight of the mound and the
running-briars. What a good fight his old dad had put up!
He was proud of the entire record-proud to see the tiny
copy of the Stars and Stripes fluttering over the clods where
54
THE FIREMAKERS
the G.A.R. had placed it as soon as the grave diggers had
completed their work; proud of the emblem of the Knights
of Labor; prouder still of something expressed in no
emblem, but which he could see in the hard, yellow clods
themselves.
He stopped outside the church under the locust trees
where the occupants of Sinners' Row usually held forth until
after the services had begun. Eli Raynor for some reason
was over from Centerville for the day. He was discoursing
to eight or ten on a state Republican convention which he
had recently attended and apparently had completely domi-
nated. But there was silence when Luke joined the group-
beyond a sincere "Hello, Luke," from all of them. He stood
awkwardly for a minute or two and then turned toward the
church door. He heard conversation among them again
just as he stepped up to the entrance.
He slid into the long, painted bench where he customarily
sat. A number of the regular occupants of Sinners' Row
were already there, and the others and Eli soon trailed in
and filled another seat. But there was no banter this morn-
ing. They cast one look in his direction and remained
silent. When the minister announced with more voice than
was required that the opening hymn would be "I Need Thee
Every Hour" they all stood up with the congregation, and
some of them, without being certain just why, joined in the
singing. Luke did not sing; he was not a singer. But he
felt overwrought and his eyes glistened when the squeaky
little organ with bass notes too powerful for the treble ones
struck up the hymn.
The preacher read a strange lesson from the Scriptures,
and preached on a text that required unrestricted faith.
He read: "Give not that which is holy unto the dogs, neither
cast ye your pearls before swine, lest they trample them
under their feet, and turn again and rend you.
THE FIREMAKERS
55
"Ask, and it shall be given you; seek, and ye shall find;
knock, and it shall be opened unto you:
"For everyone that asketh receiveth; and he that seeketh
findeth; and to him that knocketh it shall be opened."
Then he took as his text: "And he that seeketh findeth."
Luke did not care much for the sermon. The minister
very evidently had been doing many other things that week.
He tried to make up for thin thinking by shouting at the
top of his voice and pounding with his clenched fist upon
the huge Bible on his desk. He had a bigger head of steam
than his intellectual load required. But the text upon which
the sermon supposedly was built stayed sharply in Luke's
mind, like everything else that had chanced along that
week: "And he that seeketh findeth." Could that be true?
The next morning he tramped seven miles back through
the country to see an old man who operated a little log pot-
shop. He might as well look into this pottery while he was
doing nothing else. He might learn something about vases
and blue teapots.
He asked the old man many questions. Where did he get
his clay? How long did it take to learn the potter's trade?
Had he ever tried vases? How did it happen that chinaware
was so much whiter and so much bluer than anything he had
there in his ware-yard?
Out in the millshed he watched the old man distribute the
clodlike lumps of clay and the buckets of water in the cir-
cular track of the crusher. Then he stood and watched the
blind old mare go round and round, rolling the three high
wheels of the crusher over and through the clay until it
began to lose its lumpiness and become soft and plastic.
In the shop itself he watched the old man kick away at
his treadle, and transform the batted balls of clay with the
heel of his hand, his knuckles, the smooth wooden ribs, and
the chyme rib. Although he worked with great deliberate-
ness, it was not long-at least to Luke-until he had a board
56
THE FIREMAKERS
full of soft fruit jars. These he slid carefully back in the
drying-scaffold to await slipping and the kiln.
A kiln of ware stood ready to be drawn, and for Luke's
benefit the old man tore away the loosened bricks with which
the door had been walled up, and drew some of the jars and
jugs and fruit jars. He showed Luke how the edges where
sand had stuck could be smoothed with one motion of a
heavy file-like iron which he used in loosening up the pieces
of ware as he took a stand of it down.
Wouldn't Luke stay and eat a bite with him? It was a
long way back to Green Valley.
Luke stayed. Then he spent two more hours letting his
hungry and over-sensitive eyes drink in whatever was before
them.
To believe that anybody within seven miles of Green Val-
ley should be doing anything as interesting as this was well-
nigh impossible. He needed the hour and a half or more
which the homeward journey required, just to think it all
over. It seemed less real than his father's going. He had
come to know death. He could not engage in his occupation
without being reminded daily that death is a fact. But that
all the possibilities of that little pottery should be right there
within seven miles of him-that made his head swim in ec-
static unbelief.
What if he could get out and learn chemistry or whatever
it was that would be required in mixing glazes and colors,
and establish a pottery at Centerville or some other great
town and ship his ware with his name on the bottom of
every teapot and vase to-well, heaven alone could know
how far he might send it if he could make it beautiful
enough. At any rate, he would have the fun of making it.
As he descended the path past Company Row he saw the
usual assemblage of unoccupied people on the platform at
the red station waiting for the late afternoon train from
Centerville. It was one of the six or eight that the loafers
THE FIREMAKERS
57
had to see arrive and depart each day-unless the heavy
coal trains kept the single main track too completely to
themselves. He had just time to walk over before the train
arrived. There was great hubbub when the slender little
engine came to a steaming, clanging stop, and the conductor
and brakeman stepped down to assist passengers off. But
for all the noise and effort, only one passenger descended to
the platform.
Everybody stared at her with inquisitive eyes. Clearly
she did not belong in the Valley. A glance was enough to
reveal that. Her form at once set her apart from the
straight-sided, workaday women of Green Valley. And she
wore a most metropolitan hat that spread over her well-
rounded, clear face and turned up behind over her russet-
yellow hair.
"Why bless my heart!" Mrs. Canaday exclaimed as she
rushed up to her. "Winnie Pringle! How did you get off
of that train and me not see you?"
The spectators were a little more at ease. It was some-
body Mrs. Canaday knew. The crowd began to disintegrate
preparatory to supper and the coming of the eight o'clock
train, Centerville bound.
"Mr. Dabney!"
Luke jumped at hearing the name. It had been applied
only to his father heretofore.
"My niece has a heavy bag to carry down. I wonder-
would you mind?"
Luke did not mind in the least. But he was amazed at
Mrs. Canaday's gentle speech. Where did she get this style
that she suddenly brought to bear? Luke had lived at the
Valley House twenty years, and although he had little recol-
lection of the first years, since he was only two when he was
brought there, he was sure such social elegance had not in
that entire time slipped from Mrs. Canaday's lips.
58
THE FIREMAKERS
"Winnie-Miss Pringle-this is Mr. Dabney. He lives at
the Valley House."
"She knows how to introduce people, too-just as well as
anybody," he thought. Luke had participated in a few
formal introductions at dances in the Knights of Labor Hall.
They moved toward the dusty cinder path that led
through early summer weeds to the little hotel. The train
vanished with a dying whistle round the curve below town,
and the disintegrating crowd became only individual specks
hurrying to supper. In the late afternoon sun the air was
bright but lifeless. A Kentucky cardinal sang in fervid
solitariness in the top of a maple tree.
"My, how quiet it is out here!" Winnie observed as they
walked along. She spoke with easy casualness, too, about
other matters-how dusty the train was, how common the
people on it.
It was a simple matter for her to make talk. Luke
walked along with the luggage, wishing all the while that he
could speak with some of her facility. But he scarcely
spoke at all. It was always too late when he thought of
anything to say.
She looked at him with an interested, maternal expression.
Perhaps she was three years older than he was, but a life-
time older in shrewdness. "How did such a darling of a
handsome boy ever grow up in a coal mine?" That was what
she wondered. He was nearly six feet, he was graceful in
build rather than slender, his face was a trifle austere but
kindly, and there was in his eyes the persistent light of one
who struggles to see beyond the obvious. Occasionally he
gave a somewhat self-conscious twist to the points of his
youthful blond mustache with his free hand. "Yes,"
Winnie thought, "he is a darling boy."
After supper, in the refreshing sunset hour, Winnie ap-
peared on the front porch. She wore a cool-looking dress
that did no discredit to her shapeliness. The men in shirt
THE FIREMAKERS
59
sleeves and sleeve-holders looked at her penetratingly and
then one by one faded away in the direction of the Hand-out
or the station.
All but Luke. He stood not so much in awe of her city
airs. He had met her. He had carried her luggage down
from the station.
"Well!" she said in friendly fashion after the last of the
others had gone. She looked along the row of chairs to Luke
without adding anything to her preliminary exclamation.
Luke found it difficult to break into speech from such a
meager beginning, and appeared a little embarrassed. Why
hadn't he gone with the others? But he had not yet recov-
ered sufficiently from the week before to wish the trivial
chatter of the evening crowd.
"How peaceful it is in the evening out here in the coun-
try," Winnie said, in a better prepared beginning.
Luke wanted to say that they were not in the country;
that they were in the center of Green Valley. But instead
he replied, "Yes, it is."
A belated catbird sang and scolded in a gooseberry bush
in the garden lot by the side of the hotel.
There was a long but not a tense wait.
"Aunt Teena says you have just lost your father."
"Yes." It was all Luke could say. He felt uncomfort-
able that a stranger should speak thus. Yet the remark,
foreign and inauspicious, brought everything back. His
eyes glistened as he looked across the narrow creek val-
ley toward the path that led to the mine.
"So sorry," she said with feminine comfort in her voice.
"My father and mother are both gone. Mother died six
years ago and father last winter. So I have no one left
either."
The depth of his own feeling of loss kept him from the
slightest thought of craftiness in her speech. "I under-

THE FIREMAKERS
stand," he replied. He meant to say more, but nothing more
came.
"Aunt Teena says he was a very fine man. Boarded with
her for about twenty years, didn't he?"
"Yes."
"So many are killed in mines. It's terribly dangerous,
isn't it?"
"Yes."
Aunt Teena walked discreetly through the darkening
depths of the sitting room. She was glad Luke was still

hat. Soon he would be back at work. She did not want
Winnie to see him in diggers until his more gentle lineaments
had been well established in her mind.
Things must go casually; nothing should be set in motion
with obvious force. Fate is most convincing when he seems
to come despite all. So she happened to step out on the
front porch to push the chairs back. "Oh, you both here!"
she exclaimed in surprise. Then she dropped into a chair

Cousin Frank still doing so well in the meat business? And
how were her Uncle Joe's children getting along since they
had decided to keep the big farm in Kansas? And had the
company paid her all of her father's insurance money?
When Luke finally thought he might go up and see why

THE FIREMAKERS
61
Aunt Teena was well established in the discussion of some

Luke went his own way the next morning, and Winnie
stayed in bed till noon. She was going to have her sleep.
But when she did arise she put herself in complete order.
In the afternoon he chatted with her for a few minutes, and
found speech less difficult than the evening before. He
walked with a little lightness when he went up to the station
to see the Centerville train come in. There were, then, still
some people who possessed knowledge of the world outside
Green Valley. They did not happen along often, but their
occasional appearance was sufficient to assure one. How
much Winnie could tell him that he did not know!
That evening they talked for a brief five minutes without
sitting down. But all evening the other boarders kept clear
of the porch. No one who sat at Mrs. Canaday's long table
-no one except Luke-felt equal to conversing with a
woman of such accomplishments.
The next afternoon they chanced to talk again for a mo-
ment or two. But a moment or two was enough for Mrs.
Canaday. "Now if you two have got to talk again to-night,"
she began, mustering a reminiscence of flush that had long
been forgotten, "you'll have to walk up to the church grove
or somewhere. You're scaring all my boarders away." She
laughed an intimate little laugh which did not seem quite
appropriate to Luke, but which was accepted without protest
by Winnie.
"Where is the church?" Winnie asked.
"Up on the hillside around that way," Mrs. Canaday ex-
plained, and indicated northeast. "It's nice up there. They
built the church and planted the trees before this burg was
thought of."
Neither Luke nor Winnie spoke, but both knew they were
going.
They climbed the slope in the evening quiet and came to
62
THE FIREMAKERS
the broad stile over the rickety fence below the church en-
closure. The Methodists did not like to have stray mules
tramping carelessly about the front door.
"Let's stop here on these steps," Winnie proposed. Her
robust-looking body was not accustomed to hills. "I'm
panting-and my heart is all a-flutter."
They mounted to the top step and sat in silence while
Winnie, her cheeks aglow, her laced-up bosom rising in
mighty heaves, tried to establish normal again.
The face of the earth round about them looked as if some-
body had set out in premeditation to do it violence. Com-
pany Row, with its full complement of tumbledown coal
houses and privies, looked like an unkempt row of buildings
for animals of doubtful value. The road running diagon-
ally down the hillside in front of the houses had been
washed by heavy rains for so many springs that it appeared
to be only a great yellow ditch. The fields, ever ready to
restore themselves to vegetable cleanness if they have a
chance, had fared little better. In flood season the red
waters from the mines spread over the creek bottom land,
and sometimes over parts of the river valley as well, and
killed what they touched. Down the lower reaches of the
hillsides, below the outcropping vein of coal, broadening
paths of sulphur and slack from mine openings marked the
courses of small streamlets constantly supplementing what
the larger streams were doing. In the land out of reach
along the creek, horseweeds grew luxuriantly every summer,
and the growth of one season stood gray and cadaverous
until the winds of winter toppled it, and the growth of a
new season concealed it with reassuring green.
On the hillsides wherever any vegetation was possible,
broomsedge had crowded out the grasses fit for pasture, and
now gave the entire upper landscape the somberness of
bronze.
Evening was upon the earth. The blue, odorous smoke
THE FIREMAKERS
63
from the gob piles had begun to settle above the juncture of
creek and river.
At last Winnie took a deep breath of repossession. She
was pantingly trying to think of her immediate environment,
and not what the sulphur water and broomsedge had done
to the hills.
"It's like a story-book, isn't it?" she asked. "Sitting on
a stile on a hill."
Luke's mind had been confused with all sorts of memo-
ries and presences. He supposed it was, now that he
thought about it, not unlike something he had once read in
a school reader. He gazed into the distance, feeling for a
reply that would satisfy his sense of fitness.
"And what are the flowers I smell? Don't you smell
them?"
"Oh, that?" Luke answered. "That's the locust blossoms
on the trees there around the church."
"Why, it's wonderful. Could you get some of them?"
Luke thought he could; and they went to see. As he
reached up for some of the low blossoms, he glanced toward
the cemetery out of the corner of his eye and saw the cheap,
starched little flag on the yellow mound in the corner. He
gathered a great bunch of the blossoms and piled them in
her arms.
As they started away, Luke glanced again. She saw him.
"Oh, your father is buried here, isn't he? I'm so sorry!"
And she took his arm comfortingly.
They walked on, he comforted, she encouraged.
At the stile they sat on the top step for a time and she
sorted the blossoms in order to reduce the size of the bunch.
Then they descended the slope. From time to time she
lifted the blossoms and buried her face in their aromatic
depths. "How sweet they are!" she murmured. "We must
come up for some more before I go."
At the Valley House she went out into the kitchen to get

THE FIREMAKERS
something to put them in. She meant to have a bouquet of

Mrs. Canaday was still busy from supper. "Have a nice

"Perfect," Winnie replied.
Mrs. Canaday wanted full assurance. "Got along all

"Why, of course we did. We're going again."

Much flashed through Mrs. Canaday's mind. "Remem-

They went. They gathered more locust blossoms. They

something in common a journey to talk about. "When

been as long as a year before and she chatted away in in-

And they went again on Saturday evening.
"Got to be a regular thing," Elmer Slade remarked to

"Kind o' makes Luke forget the old man," somebody said

had not been buried so recently, there would have been fur-

Luke and Winnie rested on the stile. Then they gathered

They sat on the broad sandstone steps of the church.

"And I'll be back in the mine."

He started up as if his mind had been a thousand miles

"You won't be cross at me?"

That was encouraging.
Let's have it."
"Cross your heart you won't. Swear on a stack of

She liked the intimacy of such speech.

She waited for her words to make a full impression.

Luke was cheered. But he had not yet escaped from the

"Been lots of men better than me that have mined coal

66
THE FIREMAKERS
the truth is, I have been thinking myself about getting out."
"Of course you have. And you must. Why, you don't
belong around here. In Centerville there are lots of nice
clean jobs. Men who hold them aren't half as good as you
are. You ought to be in something-nice."
Should he tell her about the clay and the vases? Of
course, that was a little remote yet in his own mind. It cer-
tainly was not so definite as selling hardware all day in a
clean store, or counting out money in a bank. She might
be wiser, anyhow; she seemed so infinitely. He was con-
tent to bite absent-mindedly at a piece of dead timothy that
he had picked up. "I hope I'll be out sooner or later."
"But you mustn't say it so gloomily," she adjured him,
"as if you never expected it."
He smiled as he looked far across the desolate valley.
But he did not trouble her by explaining that it was doubt
about courses, rather than gloom over taking any course at
all, which restrained him.
"You need something to cheer you up, poor man," and
she pressed his arm ever so gently. They felt nearer to
each other than they had ever been.
It was a good time to go. They arose and walked toward
the stile. Winnie was accordant and sympathetic and warm
and blooming-at least she tried to be.
Luke steadied her up the steps and downward on the
other side. On the bottom step she stopped suddenly just
as he was stepping to the ground. The pull on his arm
brought him round with his face almost against hers.
"I'm as tall as you are now," she said, and laughed into
his alert blue eyes.
Her own eyes were not blue, or brown, but yellow, tan-
the color of broomsedge when the wind and sun are on it.
They were not beautiful eyes. But Luke, solitary and fam-
ishing, saw in their languorous depths beauty that was not
there. When he took her in his arms and kissed her again
THE FIREMAKERS
67
and again he was confusedly enjoying the belated tender-
ness of his father over there in his grave, and kissing the
persistent image of a more beautiful woman than he could
remember ever meeting. But Winnie experienced no in-
definiteness. She was being kissed by a good-looking man
whom she meant to possess.
Winnie came into the kitchen and found Mrs. Canaday
alone. Mrs. Jones, the cook, had just gone.
"Well?" Mrs. Canaday began, with a rising inflection
which she left suspended in mid-air.
"Just glorious," Winnie answered. She was much ex-
cited. She would have thrown her arms about her aunt
and kissed her had Mrs. Canaday not been dressed in her
kitchen clothes-Winnie felt as deeply about the matter
as that.
Mrs. Canaday swept all her real or pretended subtleties
aside with one frank bit of counsel. "It's time you settled
down and married some one. And sure as I am here on
this chair, you won't do better. He's got more push than a
whole pack of your city dudes, I tell you, with their smart
ways and palaverin' talk. You never saw anyone with
more get-up-and-go than he has. And he's full of notions
that the ordinary run of men never dreamt of. All you need
to do is to look at him to tell that."
CHAPTER VI
In
ATE Sunday afternoon Luke told Mrs. Canaday that
he expected to go to the mine the next morning. In
the evening he went to church with Winnie.
Green Valley the Sunday evening service was the one of
social importance. All the women who had clothes fit to
wear put them on and went to church on Sunday evening.
All the men who thought something interesting might hap-
pen went whether they could muster a complete suit of
clothes or not. Nobody said much to Luke because Luke
was with a stylish-looking woman. And when he and Win-
nie sat together for a time after church on the front porch
of the Valley House, no boarders disturbed them. Out-
wardly, the day was a very quiet one.
Yet Luke had a bad night. He was just dropping off to
sleep when he heard a fall of slate. In his effort to escape
it he jerked so nervously with hands and feet that he found
himself wide awake. He laughed. He thought of the
hounds that lay out in the sun on the edge of the dusty road
and slept through miniature yelps and strained workings of
their toes after they had eaten heavily of some farmer's
specially killed mutton. He thought he must have eaten
too big a piece of Mrs. Canaday's dried-apple pie for sup-
per.
How real that fall of slate seemed! It started with a
splash-like sound and ended in a thud. He could not go
back to sleep. He lay for a time wondering about his tools
in the mine. How were his picks when his father was
caught that day? It was difficult to remember. Would
most of them have to be sharpened? Well, his father had
68
THE FIREMAKERS
69
left his own there in the room-all but the one with the
loose handle and some of them would be sharp. And did
he need a keg of powder? And ought he to have patched
his diggers? He needed a new seat of leather in the trous-
ers, and the bed ticking on the left knee was getting ragged.
Still, he guessed they would do. They would not wear out
much more in one day.
He wondered how long he should have to wear diggers.
In a year, now, maybe he would have enough money to go
off and study clays and slips and glazes and buy the little
pottery over in the hills. If he should buy that little pot-
tery-but that was just where his mind became troubled.
Was that what he meant to do? He had thought so. His
mind had been perfectly clear until Winnie had come. She
wanted him to get out. She had said so. But she proposed
other possible ways. Should he go to Centerville and be-
come a business man and learn the ways of great places
and eventually hire somebody to make the pottery he
wanted made? That would be the more genteel way, cer-
tainly.
Luke had never read Pierre Curie's words about how
woman's love of life is always wrecking man's intellectual
explorations by pulling him away from them. Nor was he
aware that he had come to suffer in any such manner. Yet
something of the problem about which Curie wrote was
what pressed upon his mind and kept him awake.
Winnie had told him of "nice, clean" opportunities at
Centerville. They were there, undoubtedly. And she was
there-she who was so friendly and glowing and warm that
he scarcely dared think of her. Yet he did think of her.
He set her over against that dream of the pottery in the
hills, and the dream became doubtful and vague. If only
she lived over in the country
...
He heard the clock strike all the way to three. By four
it was light. As soon as he heard anyone moving in the
70
THE FIREMAKERS
kitchen, he slipped down to the abandoned smokehouse by
the kitchen door where the miners cleaned up, found his
diggers-they were not half so badly off as he had thought
-swallowed a bite of breakfast and two big cups of coffee,
and was out in the summer morning while the dew and the
foggy smoke from the gob piles still left the sun much to do.
Since Old Nate was to be his buddy from now on, he thought
he had better stop for him-this time, anyhow.
Nathan was up early, too. He was excited about getting
back to work. Sudie protested that he seemed to forget he
might get some more bones cracked. But he assured her
that it was like a kicking horse: "After he's done it once
you think he won't do it again for a time."
While he was telling Sudie that she might put a little
more coffee in the bottom compartment of his dinner bucket,
some one outside the house gave a shrill whistle followed by
two softer, identifying notes. Nathan caught himself up as
if a bullet had grazed his face.
"Sudie! Did you hear that? Why that's Theron's
whistle, or I'm not alive!"
The dead man's signal to go to work unsteadied him for
a few seconds. Then he walked to the front door. "Oh,
it's you, is it?" he called to Luke when he saw him standing
in the road. "I didn't know you could whistle like that.
I'll be ready in about three shakes-if you want to bother
loafing along with a cripple."
Nathan walked with a slight limp, but he made good time.
At the tipple he got a keg of powder at the powder house
and then walked over to the string of waiting cars. His
fellows gave him an ovation. "What do you know about
this?" "Look who's here!" "Why, it's the General!" "See
how he's fattened up!"
When everybody was ready, Hi Crumblett said with sly
pretense of gravity: "Now, Jab, we've got Mr. Boyd back
THE FIREMAKERS
71
with us this morning. So see if you can't be a little careful
with your language on this first trip."
There was a roar of challenging laughter.
"Git out of this, you long-eared, hell-born walkin' corn-
crib!" Jab yelled at the mule. Then he smiled at this proof
of his self-restraint until his heavy reddish mustache
stretched to the sides of his scarred, soap-washed, ruddy
face.
Beyond the furnace when they passed the curve in the
entry, Luke's heart pounded a little. He was glad their
room was on this side of the fatal spot. The Company had
promptly cleaned up everything and had had a bridge of
strong props and cross-caps built under the hole in the
roof. But at that, Luke did not care to see.
He and Nathan did not get to work immediately. Nathan
had to examine the cone-like hole in the roof which marked
the spot where the fall of slate had caught him, and to re-
mark how far Luke and his father had driven the room
ahead in the course of the spring. The black front was
steadily retreating through the hill. Then several harmless
little splashes of roof had dropped down on the track where
it approached the face of the coal, and these had to be
shoveled off into the niggerheads and other discard along
the ribs of the room. The tools had been left just where
they were when Luke and his father had gone out to eat
dinner on that last day, and these had to be gathered up
and Nathan's favorite tools substituted for some of them.
Luke picked up and put aside the new shovel which his
father had brought to the mine only a day or two before he
ceased using it.
For Luke the day was heavy. He suffered the crazing
pain of having his body in one place and his thoughts in
two or three others. Nathan was a cheerful old fighter. He
was more rugged in body than Theron Dabney ever had
been, and his humor was more robust. He felt quite at
72
THE FIREMAKERS
ease with his new buddy. He had known him from the
cradle, and he had always called him Boy. "What do you
say, Boy, had we better place that next shot about there,
or a little farther over to the right?" He knew the reason,
he thought, for Luke's long silences, and chatted away cas-
ually in order to make everything as nearly normal as pos-
sible. But Luke wanted to think. And thinking was hard
when he needed sleep. All day he was low-spirited and si-
lent.
In the late afternoon when he went home he felt no bet-
ter. The day was hot. After the long hours in the cool,
damp mine, the two-mile walk in the burning sun made him
faint and short-tempered.
He cleaned up and sat on the front porch. He missed
Winnie. He had known that she was going. Yet while the
catbird scolded and sang in the gooseberry bush by the
garden fence, he kept looking up expectantly whenever
anyone moved about in the house. He wanted to talk with
some one who knew something besides coal mining. She
was a person of wide experience.
After he had grunted through a hasty supper he sat on
the porch again. The world seemed suddenly empty. A
thousand interesting things that had only a few days before
seemed just beyond the horizon now seemed non-existent.
Even the men who sat on the porch and talked sounded far-
off and faint. And what drivel they talked. He had never
before noticed that. It was childish. It was nonsensical.
He would listen to no more of it.
Upstairs it was hot. The sun had poured its rays into
roof and weatherboarding and windowpanes until the room
was as smothering as an oven. Anyhow he would read the
Centerville Gazette. But newspapers, too, had gone over
to triviality. Murders and fights and political machina-
tions. Didn't newspapers print news any more? "How to
beautify your face." Not if you live in a mining town.
THE FIREMAKERS
73
"How to apply Paris green." Who wants Paris green?
"How to deal with the currant worm." But at last, here is
news: "Farmer claims distinction of possessing only five-
legged calf in the state." Fine! Elect him governor.
Why didn't they discuss pottery?
He pushed through the pages of the paper with ferocity.
He could hear his watch ticking in his pocket. He had
never heard that before. He wished he could talk with
somebody who knew something.
He threw aside the paper and rocked in the walnut rock-
ing chair as if he hoped to overtake somebody. Then
he found his box of stationery-used only on occasions of
great significance and his lead pencil. He wrote a brief
line:
DEAR WINNIE:
Are you coming back pretty soon?
Yours truly,
LUKE DABNEY.
Every day Luke walked with assumed nonchalance up to
the post office window, but he found no reply awaiting him.
He gave Mrs. Canaday full opportunity to discuss Winnie,
but Mrs. Canaday said never a word on the subject.
He began to feel less concerned. What difference did it
make? He knew what he wanted to do without asking any-
body. He meant to buy that pottery; and he meant to learn
how to make vases and teapots. One hot afternoon when he
walked home in the company of Nathan and Pewee Dun-
kle he startled himself by thinking that he might not mind
if Winnie never came back. Maybe she wouldn't approve
of his plans. He cleaned up and sauntered out to the front
porch to cool off a little before supper. There sat Winnie,
as luxuriant as ever, just as if she had never been away.
Well, since she was there, he would talk with her. She
liked "stylish" things. He would tell her how he meant to
74
THE FIREMAKERS
become a potter, and learn about clays and glazes, and
design vases and teapots.
They walked in the evening to the church on the hillside,
but they gathered no locust blossoms, for these were gone.
Instead they sat on the top of the stile and talked philander-
ingly, whimsically, intimately, like two lovers in a story
nobody believes to be true. The desolation of the valleys,
the somberness of the hills, did not claim so much of Luke's
attention. They sat long. When they descended, she
stopped on the bottom step and let Luke step to the ground.
They laughed into each other's eyes as if it were a great
secret, and she pressed herself into his arms and kissed
him until he forgot his dreams and came back to the ways
of life.
But when Winnie was gone, his dreams came back.
Slowly they came at first; timidly. Then they came
bravely. And at last they came again with such persistence
that he could think of nothing else.
One day in August Luke thought he ought to tell Nathan
something. It had been easy to talk to Nathan. He could
understand what a man had in mind almost before he said
it. For a week there had been no work, and the two were
catching up the threads of Green Valley news while they
made the cut for their noon shot. When the cut was made
and they were setting up the auger, Luke remarked as cas-
ually as possible: "I'm thinking of getting away from here."
Old Nathan looked up with immediate interest. Even at
this hour of the day his face was already too black to reveal
much expression in the light from his cap, but as he went
on with his work his attitude of body made clear his sym-
pathetic concern.
"Well, I don't know but it would be a good thing."
He suspended speech until he had twisted the set-screw
of the auger frame tight into the slate of the roof.
"Yes sir, I don't know but it would be a good thing.

There ain't much around here for a man to do without he

"Did you notice the girls on Decoration Day? All

Then some week when they count on taking some of the

76
THE FIREMAKERS
while that she was not a fair instance. "Isn't she about as

"Yes, Sudie is, for a fact. But we had a start. I never

Nathan turned away at the auger. His girls, yes. They
were beautiful when they were babies. He had often
enough gone on quarter rations when he was in the Union
Army in order that he might keep his pay intact for Sudie's
needs. And they were beautiful when they were young girls.
He worked gladly through long days in the mine and paid
for yards and yards of filmy goods and watched approv-
ingly while Sudie converted it into dresses fit for princesses,
and into all sorts of other finery little seen in Green Valley.
But in Green Valley the girls had nothing to do except to
dress up and meet the requirements of a coal miner's nice
girls. Left thus idly free, neither Sudie's skill in dress-
making nor her resourcefulness in names could keep the
fluffy garments of romance forever about them. Almost
before Nathan knew it, they were gone.
Leonore married a man who possessed a luxuriant imagi-
nation. He set all the tongues of Green Valley wagging by
declaring and executing his intention to build a plastered
house in Breadless, across the river, east of the school.
Leonore, as gentle and tidy as her mother, soon made the
house the envy of all her neighbors, who declared that evil
was abundantly in store for anyone who lived in such ele-
gance. Nor was the evil long in coming. Her husband's
prodigal imagination led him to look upon other young
women and find them interesting. He wearied of the mat-
ter-of-fact business of making payments on a house when
he already lived in it. The roof leaked where the wind
robbed it of shingles, and the plaster came down on Leo-
THE FIREMAKERS
77
nore's white bed. In five years they were living in a log
coop below a mine seven miles south in the higher hills.
Nor did the others give Nathan's memory anything more
quieting to feed upon. Camille married a good-natured
boy who trapped skunks. She began bearing babies as soon
as she was married. Gloriana did not so much as wait that
long. She felt sure there must be something more interest-
ing than being a coal miner's dressed-up daughter, and she
meant to find out what it was. Arabelle married a busi-
ness man-a bartender of pleasing address in the village of
Blue Run. Rosamond, lovely and believing, had married a
dashing mule driver from the Conway mines, romantically
seventeen miles distant, and in the mental confusion of his
customary Saturday nights he sometimes used the mule-
whip on her. When these daughters began to come back
with children, they brought such petulant little wretches
with unkempt noses and a capacity to help themselves to
anything they could find, that Mrs. Sam Swisher proclaimed
to her part of Company Row-hence to all of Green Val-
ley-that if the Boyd children hadn't all been girls, she
wouldn't believe these little brats they brought back with
them were the least kin to Grandpa and Grandma Boyd.
His girls, yes. They meant well enough-just as well as
anybody-but they did not have much to work on.
His thoughts had carried him so far that he had forgotten
where he was. He was turning ferociously at the auger.
"Here, let me turn awhile!" Luke commanded. "You're
working as if this was your last hour on earth."
Luke took the crank handle and Nathan sat in kneeling
position and watched the steady stream of black dust which
the auger carried out from the sharp bit that was eating its
way with much crackling into the solid face of coal.
"I'm hoping, though," he at last went on, "that my young-
est one will get out."
He straightened up in his kneeling position. His cap

"It's great you can send her. I wish somebody had told

Luke turned on and on in quickened desperation. Where
could he acquire knowledge of clays and glazes?

V
CHAPTER VII
AL turned early to her last year in the Green Valley
school. School always began early in Green Valley.
According to the three school directors, there were
certain local conditions that had always to be taken into
consideration. These local conditions had to do with the
children's feet, not their heads. When school began on the
first Monday in September, the children could go barefooted
to school for as much as six weeks. Some of them went
shoeless until the first of November. Sometimes after the
fields stood white with frost until late in the morning, and
the mud in the road was horny with cold, the hardiest of
the boys came to school as if summer still prevailed, and
warmed their red, tough-looking feet by standing on one leg
with their backs to the fire and sticking the bottom of the
free foot close to the stove. Occasionally somebody got his
foot too close; occasionally some foot touched the stove.
Halcy Dunkle said that the skin on the bottom of his foot
got so thick in the course of the long season without shoes
that it always sizzled awhile and gave warning before the
stove burned through to the quick.
When the cold became too violent for naked feet, the
residents of Green Valley bought shoes for all children who
went to school. Each boy and girl had to make one pair
last until the ground was endurable again in the spring.
Sometimes when the March winds carried snow squalls
across the naked hills and heaped white drifts along the
roads, there were many feet that had become shoeless.
They could not tramp through the snow to school. In or-
der to meet this annual emergency, the board of directors
80
THE FIREMAKERS
81
provided one term of six months which stretched, with a
generous vacation at Christmas, from September to March.
Then late in April when the sun had warmed the earth
again, there was another term of two months. Sometimes
a different teacher, with a different taste in textbooks and
methods, taught the summer term. Often enough, pedagog-
ical continuity was severely strained. But the arrangement
provided for sensitive feet.
Val began early, then, in her concluding effort to extract a
little from the multifarious happenings in Pomp McManus's
school. She was the only big girl enrolled, the lone survivor
of the scheme of things. When the younger children went
banging along the half-mile of road to the schoolhouse down
by the river, she trailed behind, a lithe, dark-eyed, beautiful
witch who appeared to be driving those who went restlessly
ahead.
The school was a long, barnlike room with a heavy can-
non-ball stove as large as a hogshead in the center. Next
to the stove in importance-both stood there winter and
summer-was the teacher's desk. Next to that, four rows
of pupils' desks running down the length of the room save
where space in the two middle rows had been taken out for
the stove. These desks ranged from very small, near the
teacher, to very large in the rear of the room by the door, the
theory of it all being that the larger pupils required less at-
tention than the smaller ones. There were few pupils to
claim the larger desks. Val sat alone in the rear cor-
ner seat where there was nothing behind her but four shelves
for dinner buckets. In the other rear corner was a low,
heavy rack for the wooden water bucket.
The teacher from whom she was to receive her inspira-
tion in this decisive year was a large, square-faced Irishman
who had been brought to the school because of his reputa-
tion for discipline. He often came late in the morning and
found the children-in winter, half frozen-clamoring in
82
THE FIREMAKERS
front of the schoolhouse, or trailing toward Green Valley
with many fearful, backward glances in the hope that they
might not see the teacher arriving. In case he came, he un-
locked the door and, in winter, built a roaring fire. For a
time the flames and most of the heat-would shoot from
the top of the small, square chimney which modestly sur-
mounted the ridge of the roof directly above the stove. But
eventually the heat began to make its way through the cast-
iron, and the pupils gathered in the nearest seats, regardless
of any who might claim them as customary occupants. Val
usually preferred to sit in her own seat with her checked
coat drawn over her shoulders until the middle of the fore-
noon, when the heat reached her corner of the room.
The region about the stove required Pomp's attention.
Those in the inner circle were roasting hot before those in
the outer circle had begun to feel the slightest glow. Those
inside wanted to get out and those outside wanted to get
in; and somebody in the interchange was almost certain to
make enough confusion to justify a thrashing. Pomp
started the day off with deadly alertness.
There was a program in printed letters at the unused end
of the blackboard. But Pomp rarely followed it far into
the day. In each of the four sessions, the youngest classes
came first, and he always began at the initial point in each
fourth of the program, regardless of where he had left off
at the session before. That meant that Val, whose classes
came late in each session, did not ordinarily have more than
two or three recitations out of the six scheduled for her in
the program. When the stove did not require all of Pomp's
attention, she sometimes had a brief recitation just before
the first recess. She marched up front and read aloud
about William Tell or Julius Cæsar, or explained in brief
words what Robert Burns meant in the last stanza of "Tam
O'Shanter" or what Thomas Moore had in mind when he
wrote:
THE FIREMAKERS
83
Let fate do her worst, there are relics of joy,
Bright dreams of the past, which she cannot destroy.
Pomp found many occasions for the assignment of Thomas
Moore. While Val read with quiet, imaginative intelligence,
Pomp sat erect and smiling. Like every other Irishman, he
was a poet. At least he once had been.
Sometimes, too, though much less frequently, he found
time just before the noon hour to send her to the blackboard
to diagram some long, difficult sentence. Pomp could main-
tain discipline while she worked quietly at her task.
But at noon the school assumed a less regimented ex-
terior. By the time the more hasty of the children had de-
voured the contents of their dinner buckets, Scat Zieler, a
lifelong friend of Pomp McManus's, had arrived on the
scene. Scat had lost an eye and a leg at Chickamauga. He
had been a brave soldier. But since the War, he told the
census taker gayly, his chief occupation had been that of
drawing a pension. That did not require all of his time. He
had much leisure to devote to his friends.
Val ate with her dinner bucket modestly down in the seat
beside her and watched the boys who stowed away thick
slices of bread, hard-boiled eggs, and wide pieces of dried-
apple pie in a race to be the first out before Scat should
appear.
"Hello, Scat!" they shouted to him as he came clumping
along the road.
"Hello, you ornery little devils," he called to them good
naturedly.
Then when he came nearer they begged: "Come on,
Scat, drill us like the war."
"Come on, nothing; I'll bat your thick skulls with my
loaded cane, that's what I'll do."
"Aw, come on; drill us like the war."
"Not to-day; some other time."
84
THE FIREMAKERS
"Oh, yes."
"No, I said. Can't you understand that?"
"Oh, yes, just this once. Please."
"But you won't obey my commands."
"Yes, we will. Just try us."
"All right, then. Fall in."
Both the drill and the accompaniments then follow this
formula:
The boys give a grand whoop and begin to shuffle each
other in their efforts to form a line. Scat stands watching.
He is short. He wears a blue blouse that buttons tight
around the waist. The hollow where his left eye once was
gives half of his face an immobile expression. But his atti-
tude of body, despite his wooden right leg, is easy and care-
free. When they have ranged themselves somewhat accord-
ing to size, he commands: "Right dress!"
He glances critically at them before he commands:
"Front."
One boy has purposely pushed his foot far out in front
of the line. Scat sees him. "Put your damned dirty foot
back there, or I'll have you in the guard house before you
can draw another breath."
In this manner the military exercises are carried on until
Pomp, a more deliberate eater, walks erect and smiling to
the level ground near the road.
"Company dismissed!" Scat calls, and he and Pomp are
away for refreshment at the little one-room public house
across the bridge in Breadless on the slight knoll above the
river.
The proprietor of this house interested himself in educa-
tion. He kept slate pencils, with paper stars and stripes
glued round to prevent breakage, in a little show-case at
the end of the bar, and he generously offered to give one
to any boy or girl who cared to stop and ask for it. But
Pomp had conscientious scruples about such matters. He
THE FIREMAKERS
85
believed it immoral for school children to enter a saloon.
In fact, he forbade them ever to go near the place. He vol-
unteered to bring a slate pencil to any boy or girl in his
school who had need.
He could not stay and drink with Scat all afternoon. His
obligations as an educator required him to be at the school-
at least sooner or later. Until he returned, the boys hunted
rabbits, or went skating on the river if there was ice, or
played fox and geese over the tops of the desks in the
schoolhouse.
It was always amusing to Val to guess the temper Pomp
would show when he returned-always alone-from his
noonday visit to the Last Chance. Sometimes he was rest-
less and carried on numerous brief recitations with nervous
dispatch and dismissed the school an hour early. Sometimes
he was dour. On such occasions, for some mysterious rea-
son, he always came early to the spelling classes which
closed the day's program, and made the entire school stand
up and spell down. Pomp towered aloft in the middle of
the room, a McGuffey's spelling book in one hand and a
long hickory or willow gad in the other. When a boy missed
a word and started to his seat, Pomp gave him a swishing
touch with the gad and made him hurry. When a girl
missed, he contented himself by moving toward her fero-
ciously. Then as she darted round some desk on her way to
her seat, he gave the desk a deafening rap with the butt of
the gad.
Most frequently, though, he came back in a state of pleas-
ant facial and mental flush. He made interesting com-
ments upon the lessons; he told illustrative stories; he
found a given recitation so interesting that he could scarcely
force himself to go on to the next one. Then by the end of
the afternoon recess at two o'clock, he grew silent and
drowsy. "Primary Class," he would call, and four or five
beginners would come forward and stand in a row at one
86
THE FIREMAKERS
side of his desk. "Arthur," he would announce, and Arthur
would step over to the desk. Pomp would open a book,
take a lead pencil from his pocket, and let its gracefully
sharpened point rest at the top of the page. "Begin," he
would command, dreamily.
"A-a-a-a!"
The pencil point would slide down the page.
"B-e-e-e!"
"C-e-e-e!"
Half way through the alphabet Pomp's chin would rest
peacefully on his huge cravat. Arthur would look at him
with puzzled childish eyes, the rest of the class standing
unsupported in the middle of the floor would press their
hands against their mouths to keep from laughing, and the
school in general would be in a state of abeyance until
somebody stood up and made grimaces, or else pressed the
head of a pin into the toe of his shoe and silently pushed
the pin up through the crack of the seat in front of him. In
some such manner Pomp was called back.
After he had thus been restored to wakefulness, he was
sensitive about his dignity. He bellowed at the pupils
until the little ones trembled in their shoes-if they had any.
"I'll show you who's running this school! If you try any
of your nonsense on me, I'll thrash you till you're black and
blue."
Val became adept in his moods. When he came back
pleasantly verbose she slipped her great, atlas-like geogra-
phy from her desk, opened one of her mother's volumes of
Dickens or Thackeray behind it and read, safely oblivious
of all that went on around her unless Pomp became too
theatrical in his threats upon the person of some devilish
boy. Or she sat with paper and pencil behind the geogra-
phy and made sketches of Washington Crossing the Dela-
ware, from the front of her textbook on history; or young
ladies with feet no larger than a purse, from the pages of the
THE FIREMAKERS
87
spreading geography itself. Sometimes she sketched from
life. Between the enveloping pages of the geography, she
kept a vast collection of profiles and backs and full faces.
She was destined this year to see Pomp return frequently
in pleasant mood. So she was untrammeled most of the
time. She read. She found it a profitable way to spend
school hours while the others were submitting to systematic
education. In October she saw the Virginia creeper in the
trees on the hills turn as red as blood and the oaks assume
the color of the broomsedge, while she read Martin Chuzzle-
wit and Dombey and Son. In the bright days of November
she walked through frosty air that tasted of mine sulphur,
and reviewed all that she was reading in The Virginians.
In December and January she saw the snows come and
cover the dirtiness of the earth with soft, rounded lines of
white while she read about Tiny Tim and David Copper-
field and Little Nell and Becky Sharp. She thought she
liked Becky a little more than any of the others. Throw-
ing that dictionary-it was wonderful! She found time,
too, to read Aurora Leigh, and Mrs. Havergal's poems, and
a volume called Idylls of the King, and such other poetry as
Sudie Boyd in some mysterious fashion had been able to
bring together in her hewed-log house in Green Valley.
Val would have been happy if she could have read books
in such fashion the entire winter through. But in mid-
winter all was brought to a stop by an irrelevant kind of
event. The Reverend Mr. Wingfield, of the Methodist
Church, organized a revival that in thoroughness was to
surpass anything ever attempted in Green Valley. Full op-
portunity to accept salvation was to be brought to every
man, woman, and child. And his efforts were not without
fruitage. The number of sinners who went forward to the
mourners' bench and prayed forgiveness for their many
transgressions became so great as to excite general public
attention. In the crowd that went every night to see what
88
THE FIREMAKERS
additional wonders might be wrought was Scat Zieler, who
stamped noisily into Sinners' Row, his wooden leg banging
against the base of the seat in front of him when he sat
down. One night when Scat had engaged heavily at the
Last Chance all afternoon and was in a state of deep re-
morse, one of the minister's personal workers pleaded with
him to give the Lord a chance to save him. The minister
saw that Scat was showing signs of conviction of sin, and
came to add his efforts to those of the personal worker.
There was great rejoicing-even among Scat's friends-
when he at last yielded, and stamped forward to kneel at
the mercy seat.
The minister pronounced it as clear a case of conversion
as he had ever seen in his extensive labors. Nevertheless,
he felt that Scat should be protected against unnecessary
temptation. "Keep away from evildoers, Scat," he coun-
seled. "Especially don't consort with revelers who hide
their sins under the guise of Romanism."
Scat understood. He would be safer if he left off visiting
Pomp's school.
But the case did not end so simply. Father McGuire,
pastor of St. Rose's Church on the hill a mile down the
valley from the Methodist Church, saw too many gathering
into the wrong fold. There was need of heroic action. He
accordingly held a mission. All who were thought to have
Catholic inclinations were urged to express their loyalty to
the true faith. The wayward who strayed habitually across
the river to the Last Chance were besought to sign the
pledge. The entire parish offered special prayers when
Pomp McManus marched forward and signed in large, nerv-
ous hand.
Father McGuire knew the temptation that would try
Pomp's resolution. He counseled him not to consort with
revelers who hide their sins under the guise of Wesleyanism.
Thus as soon as Pomp and Scat got religion they became
THE FIREMAKERS
89
suspicious of each other. In the post office or at the rail-
road station they spoke to each other only with the most
conventional formality. Scat remained strictly away from
the schoolhouse and both he and Pomp remained away from
the Last Chance.
Val's reading suffered accordingly. For with an unbroken
day ahead, Pomp began to carry through his daily program
with unheard-of regularity. He kept his large silver watch
on the desk before him and brought every recitation to a
close at the appointed minute.
Pupils in general were soon exploring parts of their books
which they had never seen. But Val especially felt the ef-
fect of the new order. She was kept so busy with problems
about race horses going at different speeds, with parsing and
diagramming, with the activities of the Federalist party,
with the boundary lines of Afghanistan, that there was no
time for such incidentals as reading and drawing.
A month of this new discipline went by. Then one day
when the boys trailed out at noon, Scat was there. But
Pomp did not so much as come out to see him. The next
day Scat was there again. This time Pomp came out. They
laughed in each other's face and jabbed each other in the
ribs, and went off across the river together.
So Val returned to her reading, and especially to her draw-
ing. She became more observant of the school. More of
her sketches became whimsical comments upon her environ-
ment rather than copies of lofty generals leading patriots to
immortality, or of small-footed young ladies in China. She
had, therefore, to be a little more careful not to let her
creations be seen. If anyone came near her corner, she
turned the leaf of the geography and became intent upon
the new page, with a blank sheet of paper held expectantly
under her pencil.
One day when the warm sun promised spring, Val sat and
sketched in the quiet of the schoolhouse, at noon. The pu-
90
THE FIREMAKERS
pils, nondescript in worn-out clothes like a flock of poorly
wintered sheep, played gleefully over the rapidly drying
mud. Pomp and Scat had gone to the Last Chance for slate

Suddenly Halcy Dunkle burst in from nowhere. He saw
her at work over a sheet of paper.
"What you up to, Val?" he asked, and walked toward her
desk.
"Nothing," she replied. "Go on about your own affairs."
She closed the geography with haste, and as she did so a
bunch of sketches slipped down into her seat beside her and

That afternoon at recess Nick Ferris took Halcy secretly
behind the coal house to show him something that would
make his eyes pop out of his head. The news got abroad,
and all went to look. Whenever anybody looked, the entire
assembly raised a boisterous, wanton shout. Pomp slept
through recess that afternoon, but was awakened by a roar
of laughter over something he could not fathom. When he
walked to the door to ring the bell, he heard another out-
burst-behind the coal house and decided to investigate.
This was not the time for foolishness. If they were laugh-
ing at him, he would make them laugh out of the other cor-
ner of their mouths.
As he stepped round the coal house they were all intent
upon a sheet of paper which Nick held in his hand.
"Pass that over here!" Pomp commanded, almost before
they saw that he was upon them.
Nick made a motion as if to put it in his pocket, but
Pomp seized him and grabbed the paper.
He opened it and there was a drawing of himself, inso-
lent in its fidelity. For a moment he was too inflamed for
words. Then he said, "You may all go inside."
THE FIREMAKERS
91
He rang the bell and the rest of the pupils came in.
When they were quiet, he said hotly to Nick, "You may
come to the desk." A few of the boys and most of the girls
did not know what had happened. Val was busy with Thad-

Pomp's attitude was that of a bulldog about to engage

"Then who did make it?"

He seized Nick by the arm and led him stiffly to the cen-

Pomp opened the door of the great, bulging stove and
looked in. The warmer weather had made firing unneces-
sary since noon, and only a bed of glowing coals filled the
firebox. He poured a bucket of small lumps in, stirred
them up with the glowing coals, and opened the draft. The
stove responded with a puff that sent smoke shooting from
round the door and through every other crack in its sur-
face, and then with a steady roar as the fuel began to burn.
"Now, I guess you'll tell," Pomp said.
Nick was as much mystified as ever.
92
THE FIREMAKERS
After a minute or two of waiting, during which the pupils
sat numb in anticipation, Pomp broke the silence by
throwing open the stove door. The inside of the stove was
a raging furnace. He had lost some of his anger and was
now interested more or less æsthetically in seeing how the
devices of authority would result in justice when justice was
essential.
"I burned a boy up once when I taught over in West Vir-
ginia, and I suppose I can do it again."
Nick now understood. He failed to see the other pupils
sitting about, some of them pretending to study, but all of
them intent upon him. He saw only Pomp, and he was no
match for Pomp. He burst into the wildest paroxysm of
fright: "But you wouldn't burn me up, would you, teacher?"
"Then tell!" Pomp roared.
"I don't know."
"Don't lie to me! Do you want me to ram you head first
into that stove?" He made a menacing move.
Some of the pupils detected the theatrical quality in
Pomp's speech. Yet there was Nick, frightened into hys-
teria.
"I hope to die, teacher, if I'm not telling the truth! I
cross my heart! Honest to God, I didn't make it and I
don't know who did. I picked it up on the floor! Oh,
teacher, would you burn me up for that?"
"No, you didn't pick it up off the floor," Pomp bellowed.
Val began to suspect what it all was about. She leafed
through her sketches in the geography. Aye, one was miss-
ing.
"I'll give you just thirty seconds to decide," Pomp said
as if the authority of the universe were behind him,
"whether you tell who made it or whether I ram you head
first into that stove."
Val slipped from her seat, walked over to Pomp just
THE FIREMAKERS
93
when he was beginning another threatening gesture, and
said simply: "If it's a picture of you, I drew it."
Pomp stood thunderstruck. The school rustled a little in
their seats. Nick looked toward Val as if she were an angel
that had saved him from hell's fire.
At last Pomp said, "You may go to your seat, Nick."
He cast a fearful glance toward Val. She was trying to
bury herself in her book. He closed the stove door with a
bang and walked to his desk. He did not like the silence of
the pupils. They were looking at him, too. He dropped
into his chair and mopped his brow.
He would have to thrash Val, then. The pupils were wait-
ing, for that would be something worth seeing. Val was
waiting, too.
Pomp mopped his brow repeatedly. He lifted up the lid
of his desk and fumbled about inside among the papers, the
lid between his face and the school. Here was a new prob-
lem: the young lady of the school, just turned sixteen years
of age. Now!
At last he stopped fumbling. He stood up at the end of
the desk where he always stood when he made far-reaching
announcements. "School is dismissed," he ordered.
Val took pains to get out before the last of the smaller
girls had squeezed into their worn, home-made coats, or
tucked their winter fascinators round and round their necks.
As soon as the boys had hurried out of hearing of the
schoolhouse, they discussed the incident. Nick felt humili-
ated, but greatly relieved.
"I don't believe he would have burned you," Shelley
Crumblett observed sagely. "But just the same I was glad
I wasn't in your shoes."
"What would you have done, Nick, if Val hadn't saved
you?" Beans Swisher asked sympathetically, but a little
bravely.

THE FIREMAKERS
Before Nick was moved to answer, Shelley broke in: "But

Shelley laughed derisively. "Not dare lay hands on a

Eli Raynor drove along toward Green Valley in his
red buckboard, his two slender horses asteam from two
hours on the muddy roads. "Come on, Val," he called to
her as she walked homeward with dignified, warm move-
ment. "I'll give you a lift. It's a pleasant day to ride."
She hesitated. Patent Leather Eli's smooth ways had
never impressed her. But for the moment she was not
wholly antipathetic; she needed human association after
her encounter with Pomp.
"Come on!" he called again, and despite herself-just a
little she climbed in.
"And I like good company," Eli added blandly, complet-
ing his remark about the pleasant day.
Val wanted to jump out, but he went suavely to other
matters, and she remained. She did not have to do any talk-
ing; he was always glad to do it. He rambled on about a
new mine, about the approach of spring, about school.
"Think you get much out of Pomp's school?" he asked.
"Oh, a little," she answered, flushing for some reason un-
known to Eli.

95
When they were almost before the Boyds' house, he asked

Val found it easy to express a defense of what she had
contributed to the afternoon. "Why, I want to be an ar-

"Fine! Fine!" Eli declared in full voice. "I knew an
artist once. She was a great old girl." And the mere mem-
ory led him to give Val a zealous squeeze.
She bounded from the buckboard at one full leap.
"Good-by!" Eli called as if she were merely getting out
because she had arrived at home. But to himself he said:
"Touchy little devil!"
Before he was through chuckling at her spirit in order to
gloze his ill success, she was explaining to her mother in
few words why the educational machinery had clogged a
trifle early that day, and how she meant to go back and face
Pomp and get whatever tiny crumbs there might be to pick
up in the days that remained of the winter term.
