CHAPTER VIII
NE day in September while Val was struggling to ex-
tract a little sustenance from Pomp's school, Luke
went to Centerville and ordered two suits of
clothes. He ordered them from a tailor who proclaimed in
gilt letters that he made suits for the best people. Winnie
had said that there must be nothing common about their
wedding.
They were married in Mrs. Canaday's parlor at the Val-
ley House under her solicitous eye. Luke wore a blue serge
suit and a white necktie with blue flowers buttonholed in
the ends of the bow. He was a gentleman beatified. He
now was to have a helpmate who would aid him in getting
out. According to her own promise made in low, alluring
voice, she was to "love, cherish, and obey" him. He did not
expect her to obey him; he contemplated no issuing of com-
mands. But it was pleasant to know that Winnie stood
ready to go to such extremities.
She was beautiful as she stood there beside him in pale
blue satin and with a bright rose in her auburn hair. Luke
could not help admiring her, even when he was most occu-
pied with the involved rhetoric of the questions which the
minister asked. Blessings as well as misfortunes seemed to
come doubly. He had always felt sure that getting out
would be a happy experience; but he had not contemplated
that experience as being of a parcel with marrying a beau-
tiful wife. He was quite lost in rapture when eventually
the minister placed his cool hand upon their joined ones
and offered an eloquent prayer "in behalf of this sacred
union." When the bride and groom had made their escape,
96
THE FIREMAKERS
97
Mr. Wingfield declared that it had been the most stylish
wedding he had ever solemnized in Green Valley.
Winnie had planned the honeymoon. She wanted to go
to Centerville and stay at the Jefferson Hotel overlooking
the park. When they chose to make themselves known to
her friends, they could do so. Luke was to find the business
employment best suited to his talents. Then if they did
not chose to live on at the Jefferson-they would find a
house.
They whiled away two or three late, lazy mornings look-
ing from their room over the browning tree-tops toward the
memorial statue where a diminutive soldier in Northern
uniform stood guard on top of an enormous column of
marble. Luke had never known such blissful days of re-
spectable leisure. His troubles were all in the past.
But toward noon one day he was moved by a persistent
eagerness to find that new job. It would be only another
godsend to add to the others which had come to him. Sub-
ordinately there was another reason. He had more money
than any other bridegroom who had come out of Green Val-
ley, but his mounting bills reminded him that he ought to
make some counterbalancing additions to his assets.
He found his hat and fumbled with it a little. "I believe
I'll go out and look around to-day," he observed casually
to Winnie.
"Well now, that's nice or else not!" she exclaimed petu-
lantly. "Are you tired of me already?"
"Of course I'm not tired of you," he replied with mock
gravity, and took her in his arms. She snuggled as close
as she could get.
"Wait till to-morrow," she half begged, half commanded,
when she finally looked up into his face. In her voice there
was a suggestion that he had purposely sought to hurt her.
"We'll never have another honeymoon."
98
THE FIREMAKERS
"All right," he assented, tenderly sincere. "I just meant
to begin looking for that job. To-morrow will do."
The next day she protested by pressing her cheek against
his shoulder a little longer. He waited again.
But on the day following he could not be restrained. “I
must go, Winnie," he argued with husbandly thought for
the future. "I must get the job and get started on the pot-
tery."
"Well go it, then," she answered, her face and tawny eyes
in a flush of hotness. "But-but-don't stay too long, you
sweet thing!" She saw the cloud overshadow his face at
her ungracious speech, and wanted to clear it away. "Get
a good one," and she kissed him.
Out in the street he was thrilled. Everybody seemed to
be moving about so pleasantly, everybody seemed to be
prospering so easily, that he could scarcely believe the peo-
ple he saw were at work at all. The entire scene was alien
to labor as he knew it. What if they had to heave a few
lumps of coal into a car? Or lift a car back on to the track
when it was half loaded? They were lucky if only they
knew it. It was almost like robbery to take money for
what these people called work. But it seemed that that was
the way the world was ordered. The harder you work, the
less you get for it. Funny! But you have to take things
as they come, he supposed, the easy things as well as the
hard ones.
Where would it be best to begin? All the places of busi-
ness looked alike. All looked inviting.
He might as well try one. He walked in and made in-
quiry for the proprietor. He made the inquiry so casually,
and his appearance was so genuine, that the young lady who
determined destinies in the little outer office sent him in.
The proprietor, a generous-looking man with a bit of beard
on his chin, looked at him with interest. Here was a young
fellow who had no bluff in his make-up. When Luke said
THE FIREMAKERS
99
that he had in mind an enterprise that would eventually be
important to Centerville in case his plans carried through,
the proprietor was more interested still.
"Well now," he said in kindly manner as he laid his spec-
tacles on the desk and leaned back in his chair, "I'll want
to talk that all over with you some day. But just now let's
talk business. I do need a man. What all have you done?
What particular thing can you do in the hardware trade?"
Luke explained that he had done nothing except work in
a coal mine. The proprietor was disappointed. In truth,
he was a little abrupt. He could not consider taking in a
man who was without experience. If Luke should go and
get experience, and then come back, there might be some-
thing for him. But there was nothing now. It cost a lot
of money to break in a man, and then just as likely as not
he'd go and get a better job somewhere else. He liked the
looks of Luke. He would put his name on file.
As Luke walked on he was more or less dazed. But he
must not be discouraged by one failure. He had a wife
now. She was back there at the hotel waiting.
His eye caught the front of a great establishment across
the street. It proclaimed itself a department store. In
one large window was queen's ware. Maybe he could get a
clean job that would at the very same time enable him to
learn about clays and glazes.
He entered one of the three or four doors. A brisk young
man greeted him. Luke explained that he was looking for
employment and should like to see the head of the concern.
"Well, now let me see," the young man answered in an im-
portant feminine voice, "maybe I'm just the man you're
looking for. I need a new man on this floor. Where have
you had experience as a salesman? Did you bring refer-
ences?"
Luke explained once more that he only had business in
prospect. "Oh, I see!" exclaimed the gentle young man.
100
THE FIREMAKERS
"That is too bad. But you just wait and I'll find out for
you."
He vanished in the rear of the store. Luke walked back
in that direction. In the depths somewhere he heard the
same voice: "A coal miner. He is a coal miner. He said
so himself. Gladys needs somebody up in the millinery de-
partment. Let's send him up to apply. Wouldn't she have
a cat-fit?"
Luke tiptoed back to the front of the store and out into
the busy, alien street. With all the hustle and strangeness,
he felt better out there.
He would think things out a little before he tried again.
He was not getting off on the right foot. He walked on and
on, thinking, puzzling. He passed through the section that
rattled with street-cars and heavy drays and brewers' wag-
ons drawn by stout horses with much shining brass on their
harness. Then he came out into blocks and blocks of resi-
dences with broad, high entrances and with beveled glass
round the doors. Would he ever live in a house like one of
these? Just now it did not seem probable. If he were to
take all the money he had saved thus far in his life, he could
scarcely buy one of those entrances. Certainly he could not
buy the furniture in the front rooms of the downstairs. But
then, who could tell, if once he got started?
He came out into the country. The air was better here.
He wanted to tell that ladylike young man what he thought
of him; or better still, pick him up and stick his nose in a
hole of sulphur water.
On the way back by another street he passed a glass fac-
tory where men with wooden reënforcements for their cheeks
were blowing bottles and all other sorts of containers. They
looked hot, those men. But their job was cleaner than his,
and there was no hill to plump down upon one at any min-
ute. Maybe he could get something to do there. Making
bottles, he could see, was not unlike making pottery. He
THE FIREMAKERS
101
had better look into the matter some day. But not now.
He wanted to get farther out than a glass factory promised
to take him.
It would be good to see Winnie again. He hadn't seen
her for two hours.
She heard him approaching their room and met him at
the door. "Why didn't you stay all day?" she asked peev-
ishly. Then she brightened up. "What kind of a job did
you get?"
He was downcast. "I didn't get any."
Warmth came to her cheeks. "Well, what have you been
doing all this time then, I'd like to know?"
Luke explained. She listened, a shadow over her face all
the while. He gave her only a summary of what had taken
place. He did not choose to tell her what the ladylike young
man in the department store had thought of doing.
It was all bad enough, to be sure. But of course, he had
only just begun. To-morrow morning he would try again.
He knew how to do it now.
Winnie sulked a little, but they went down to the dining-
room before much could be said. In the presence of
so many gentlefolk and so much food, she became very
hopeful for the morrow. From time to time she reached
round the corner of the table through the generous folds
of white tablecloth and squeezed Luke's hand.
He went out again the next morning-early. Confidence
alternated with fearful memories of the day before. One
man had no need of any help whatever; he wished he could
get rid of about half of what he already had. Another was
looking for a man, yes. He wanted a tailor who could also
arrange clothes with business-getting effectiveness in a show-
window. Another confessed brusquely that he had found
coal miners a harum-scarum, unreliable lot. While Luke
did not appear to be that sort, he found it better to carry
102
THE FIREMAKERS
on his business according to established principles. He
thought he had better make no deviation.
The glass factory came to Luke's wavering mind. He
took the long walk out there and hunted up the manager.
"Now you're in luck," the manager said to him as soon as
he explained that he was looking for work. "We need a
fireman." Forthwith he offered Luke the job-a job just
as dirty as the one he had in Green Valley and just about as
remunerative. Luke thought he would have to talk it over
with his wife first. But on the way back to the hotel he
admitted to himself that he had no thought of mentioning
the prospect to Winnie.
He tried again that afternoon. But before he tried, he
knew that he was attempting to pass where there was no
way. The world seemed to be made up of compartments.
If you found yourself in one and if other people found
you in one-why that was where you belonged. Men in
nice, clean compartments spoke as if they thought coal
miners ate hay.
He knew he was wasting time. He would go back to
Green Valley, put his affairs in shape, and learn the pottery
trade before his savings gave out. His money would buy
the little pot-shop back in the hills and keep Winnie and
himself for a year.
Sagely he broke the news to Winnie. When she caught
the full significance of what he was saying, she threw her-
self on the bed and moaned: "I won't live in that dirty hole!
I won't live in that dirty hole! Don't you hear what I say?"
Luke could hear. He did not want her to live in any hole.
But a man could not sit around in a hotel forever waiting
for something that wasn't going to come. He could buy the
pottery. He had enough money right now-and then some.
In it he could make all sorts of interesting and useful and
beautiful things, and he could sell them to people who
needed them. Then some day

Winnie's imagination remained untouched. Beautiful
things! What she wanted, she would have him understand,
was a nice house in the city and things she would not be
ashamed to have people see. She paced the floor theatri-
cally, blubbered, daubed her eyes with her handkerchief,
accused Luke of enormous deceit, and threw herself on the
bed and sobbed in feigned innocence.
But the pottery was at least a promise not to stay in
Green Valley. That was something. And it was remote
enough to present no immediate problem. She settled down
to crying quietly on the bed. She could hear Luke rocking
in robust meditation in the great upholstered rocking chair.
Finally she sat up. When she had attracted Luke's atten-
tion, she began a fresh whimper: "I didn't want any of
my friends here to see us until after you had a nice job."
So that was it! Luke had thought she understood his
great restlessness. He had thought she felt the pain he suf-
fered at being unable to do something satisfying to his own
spirit. Now it was beginning to grow clear that she was
concerned only with appearances. She was like most of the
women in Green Valley. Why had he never noticed that
before?
He wanted to get out of coal mining just as much as she
wanted him to, but it was for a different reason. He was
not ashamed of himself for having worked. He felt welling
up within him a great admiration for Old Nate, and Pewee,
and Hi, and Sam Swisher, and all the rest of his fellows who
risked their lives in the hills that the rest of the world might
keep warm.
They went down to the station the next afternoon. Two
of Winnie's friends came up to speak to her. She kissed
Mrs. Simpson and chaffed flirtatiously with Mr. Simpson.
Luke did not feel inferior to this man, whoever he was.
"And what is your husband's business?" Mrs. Simpson
104
THE FIREMAKERS
asked, her voice expressing a little of the inquisitiveness
which she meant to conceal.
"The pottery business," Winnie answered, as if she might
have engineered the entire venture herself. "We are run-
ning over to spend a few days with my aunt before we settle
down."
On the train Winnie talked about the commonness of
the people about her. Why didn't women with babies leave
them at home when they went to Centerville?
At the Valley House she threw herself into the arms of
Aunt Teena and assured her that she had had a glorious
trip.
The sight of so many people with jobs sent Luke off to
an uneasy night. The next morning he announced that he
was going over to see the owner of the pottery.
"Where is it?" Winnie asked. Something in her manner
suggested that she was as volatile as ether.
Luke explained that he thought she knew; it was six or
seven miles north.
"How big's the town?"
"It's in the country."
"And you want me to go there to live!"
She stood up in her pink embroidered nightgown, as lan-
guid and as fierce as a tigress.
"Not long," Luke answered. "Just until I develop some-
thing bigger. Not for more than two years probably, or
three at the most."
"Two or three years? Did I hear you say two or three
years? What did you marry me for, anyhow? To pack me

you are a lamb. I say not for three years, or two years, or
one year, or one day."
Luke shrank from her onslaught, but explained reason-
ably: "We have to go where the clay is."
"No, we don't have to go where the clay is. You prom-
THE FIREMAKERS
105
ised me that we would live in Centerville. Then you
brought me back to this dirty hole. And now you propose
taking me to the woods-to the woods!" she screamed at
the top of her voice. "And I'm not going. What in hell
do you think I am, anyhow?"
Luke could not answer. He was only just beginning to
find out. But he begged her to be reasonable. Maybe
things would come out better than she expected.
"Reasonable!" she shrieked. "Reasonable! did you say?
After me waiting for three days and you not able to get any
kind of a decent job. Any other man would have found one,

"There!
carry on like that. Why, we can work it out."
There!" Luke begged consolingly. "Don't
"Who's carrying on?" she screamed. "Who's carrying

face downward and combined wailing with violent kicking.
Luke walked to the window and gazed away. Slowly his
eyes became aware of the Green Valley landscape, and for
once he saw in it something friendly. Across the road Old
Nate came out of his hewed-log house and moved off toward
the station in his clean everyday clothes. Evidently there
was no work.
Luke picked up his hat and stepped toward the door.
"Winnie," he said, gently. But she only pressed her face a
little tighter in the pillow.
CHAPTER IX
N
ATHAN was happy to see Luke back. He was
sorry he had had no opportunity to offer con-
gratulations on the night of the wedding. He
wanted to offer them now-he was sure it was not too late.
And he grasped Luke's hand in a hearty, iron grip and ut-
tered in a cheerful gurgle what he found it impossible to say
in articulate speech. When at last speech began to come
again he said, "They all say you've married the most stylish
looker ever in this town."
"Who's your new buddy?" Luke asked as soon as he
could do so without seeming unappreciative of Nathan's
good wishes.
"Ain't got one yet. No work. Had a cave-in just inside
the mouth of the mine the same night you left, and we've all
been out. I've had a sneaking feeling all along that maybe
you'd be back."
Luke was deliberative. "I have plans for the future. But
I might work awhile, if you care to have me."
"Tickled to pieces!" Nathan replied. "That would be
great-just great," he said, his furrowed face cheerful. “I
don't like this business of changing buddies any more'n I
have to."
So it was arranged.
"Pewee says there'll be work to-morrow, but I don't be-
lieve anybody knows for sure yet," Nathan explained before
they parted.
Luke found his diggers hanging in the wash room undis-
turbed, and his dinner bucket, scoured and shining, on a
stout nail in Mrs. Canaday's kitchen.
106
THE FIREMAKERS
107
Winnie sulked all evening. Luke seemed to have his mind
made up about something, but she would not deign to ask
him what it was. She went to bed early and pouted herself
to sleep, her auburn head close in the corner at the head
of the bed.
The next morning while she was still in the middle of her
sleep Luke slipped out of bed and stole away to the mine.
He had to run the gauntlet of his fellows. They con-
gratulated him noisily. They enlarged upon the style which
his wife displayed. They made barefaced references to
what some of them regarded as the only essential part of
married life. But at length he reached his room, and he
and Nathan, after they had caught up the loose threads of
existence, had a fair day of work together.
When he went home late in the afternoon he slipped un-
obtrusively round to the wash room and cleaned up before
he let Winnie know that he had arrived. She sat on the
front porch and chatted casually with a drummer who was
staying over for the evening train.
The next day passed without storm; and the next; and
the next. Winnie was showing signs of adjustment. She
even grew cheerful. One evening when Luke came up from
the wash room clean and substantial-looking, she took him
by the lapels of the coat and planted a robust kiss square on
his lips. "I'm going to ask you for something," she said,
quite frankly, quite gayly. "I need a new dress and two or
three other things little things and my husband will
have to put up the money, won't he? I'd like to go to town
to-morrow and get them."
To Luke she seemed well equipped with finery. But if
such a small matter as a few dollars could keep not only
peace but gayety in the family, he would be only too glad to
pay the price.
"About how much?" he asked in intimate family voice.
"Oh, about fifty," she answered blithely.
108
THE FIREMAKERS
Luke had grown up with men whose wives did not spend
fifty dollars a year for personal adornment. But of course
they were not his wife. He did not want her to look like
them.
"All right, I'll get it for you," he replied, and his words
let her know that he was going to enjoy the experience.
She kissed him again. "You're a perfect dear, you old
sweet thing."
Luke would have given her another fifty if she had asked
for it.
He came home from work early the next afternoon and
went to the station to meet her. But she did not get off the
train.
She must be staying over, he thought.
As he walked alone back to the Valley House, he thought
inevitably of something else: What if she should not come

Valley, and with him.
Luke went to the mine the next morning thinking about
pottery. All day he was full of inquiries. Had Nathan ever
been interested in pottery? Had he ever seen anybody
make blue china? Did he know where one could find out
about such things?
He quit work early again that afternoon and went to the
station. Winnie did not come.
The next day he was more persistent than ever in his in-
quiries. His many questions at last stirred up Nathan's
memories of china he had seen in the houses of some of
Sudie's relatives when they once went back to visit them
many years ago. It took skill, he assured Luke-hoping
that he might not lose him to make such things. Yes, he
guessed it was nice work when you once learned how to do
it.
Luke went to the Valley House that afternoon with his
THE FIREMAKERS
109
old enthusiasm in full possession of him. Winnie sat on the

She occupied the evening with a detailed account of her

The next week she went to town to buy a coat. She re-

"Your wife needs something to keep her busy," Nathan

Luke thought so himself. "I believe she would get inter-

"Sure she would. It would give her mind something to

She flew into a tantrum. She shouted to high heaven to

Her campaigning was not wholly without recompense for

He could not deny that he liked her neatness, the appropri-

111
ends that were so much in evidence in the clothes of the

Mrs. Canaday persisted in her belief that her diagnosis

Winnie stayed in bed most of the day. But she was up

Not even her beratings could prevent him from rejoicing

THE FIREMAKERS
girl! Why didn't somebody find out how to know such

She found that most of the articles she required in her

Before she knew it, winter had come and gone, and

She was soon about again, and when she felt equal to a

Luke could not object to having nice things for his own

He walked up to the railroad station and on west along

was cool.
When he arrived at the deserted station he had not yet
had enough of a walk. A switch engine backing some cars
up toward the mine off in the valley put it in his head to
walk on down the track to the switch and up that way.
Where was he ever to come out if he traveled the road that
he and Winnie were now on? How could one know where
to get help when one needed it? And helping one toward
what? Two years ago he would have given all his money
and a mortgage on the rest of his life just to escape loneli-
ness. Now he had given all his money and a mortgage on
his life, and he would give them again, if he had them to give,
just to have his blessed solitariness back. He was aware
that a thousand things might be worse than being lonely.
He walked on and on, unable to think very clearly except
about this one point, unable to feel sharply about anything
else. He was only aware of an unmistakable heaviness on
his shoulders, an oppression in the atmosphere that he could
THE FIREMAKERS
115
not escape. Some doom that he had little contemplated now
seemed impending.
He came to the spur that led across the river to the new
mine and followed that. He walked to the trestle across the
river where he had sat after his father's death, and he
dropped down again on the ends of the cross-ties to contem-
plate.
Yes, his mother had found a way out. He did not feel
depressed at the thought. In truth, he felt very near to her.
He understood the confusion she must have experienced.
He understood the clearness of her mind after she had made
the decision. He sat there in the dim moonlight and imag-
ined that he saw her beautiful face-it must have been a
beautiful face, he thought-as she glanced furtively about to
see that no one watched her when she was ready to bury
herself in the quiet waters.
That was her way. He did not disapprove. Still he was
not ready to say that it would be his way. He did not see
anything ahead except dirty work, and a scolding wife, and
too little money to pay bills. These did not constitute an
inviting prospect. Yet-what? He seemed to be walking
into a gray stone wall. It might be an intangible wall. Per-
haps he could walk through it. What might be on the other
side-if there was any other side he did not know. But
at least he meant to wait a little and see if he could.
He walked back along the switch to the main track below
Green Valley, and then along the main track to town. All
the houses were dark and quiet. As he stumbled over a big
beer bottle that somebody had thrown into the path, hounds
on the other side of the road bellowed forth, and then as
suddenly subsided.
Winnie was sound asleep, and the twins hugged their pil-
lows in the expansive double crib.

CHAPTER X
UKE did not talk about Winnie with Nathan. He often
wished he might. It would have afforded him relief
to unburden himself to some one-the right one.
But from somewhere out of a hazy past he had come into
possession of a feeling that it was not white to say much
about one's domestic encounters.
He wondered just how much Nathan knew. But Nathan
likewise was prejudiced against carrying his own or other
people's troubles to the distorting light of discussion. Luke
had been coming to the mine whenever there was work, and
that was as far as Nathan cared to be informed. One morn-
ing, when he had been wondering if Luke planned to leave
soon, and was fearful of having to hunt up a new buddy, he
did venture to say quite casually, "Well, I see you are still
here." And Luke quite as casually replied, "Yes, I suppose
I may be around for some time yet."
Anyhow, Nathan had domestic affairs of his own to pon-
der over. Val finished off her last year in Pomp's school,
and then took up a dazzling year at Oakdale. Every morn-
ing she started early-almost as early as the miners-on her
five-mile walk to this community that enjoyed the extrava-
gance of a two-room school. Every evening she reached
home after the miners had returned from work. In mid-
winter she saw the procession of lamps ahead of her on the
ridge path as she began the journey, and in the evening the
last of the miners coming into town as she herself followed
the long ridge homeward.
She had much to tell. She was studying civil government
and two or three other subjects which Nathan had supposed
116
THE FIREMAKERS
117
were reserved for heavenly guided boys on the road to be-
coming president of the United States-or, at least, of a
large coal company. The upper teacher was as much of a
marvel as had been reported. He had attended college. He
could read two foreign languages-at least there was no one
to gainsay the report. He could play two or three musical
instruments. He could sing, and sometimes did. And he
could draw with crayon and paint with water colors.
Through Val scraps of information about him came to the
residents of Green Valley. Eventually it was rumored that
he was to honor the region with a visit. But this rumor was
very insubstantial.
Val had something to say, too, about the pedagogical
methods employed at Oakdale, as contrasted with those em-
ployed by Pomp. Before the end of the year Pewee Dunkle
and Hi Crumblett and Charley Ferris, inspired by their
wives, began to ask why they could not get rid of Pomp
McManus and give the school to Old Nate's girl. Val was
not certain that that was the most direct road to becoming
an artist. Still she did not know of any that was less devi-
ous.
The school committee declared that they were not in the
least concerned about the matter, one way or the other.
But they were besought by patrons who contributed gener-
ously to the school's population, and they were inclined to
yield to the pressure. The only pressure on the other side
was that exerted by Scat Zieler. So it was voted that Miss
Valencia Boyd, provided she passed the county examination,
should take charge of the school the following September.
As the time drew near there was much speculation as to
whether she would be able to handle the discipline. Would
the boys look upon a girl as deserving obedience? Would
a boy submit to a thrashing administered by a young lady
of scarcely eighteen who only a little more than a year ago
was a pupil in the school herself? Before the first day of
118
THE FIREMAKERS
school arrived, many who had championed her election
began to entertain grave doubts about her ability to keep
order. Nathan had no doubts-he never had any about
Val-but he was her father, a prejudiced witness.
The pupils heard much of this discussion while they ate
their potatoes and gravy at the supper table, and they, too,
began to wonder. Some of them went so far as to devise
ways and means. So everyone was expectant when Miss
Valencia Boyd in her best clothes went off to school early
on a pleasant September morning to await her pupils.
The pupils were early, too. They anticipated activity of
some sort and they did not wish to miss any of it.
Promptly at eight-thirty o'clock she called the school to
order. When the pupils had slid into their seats she stepped
over to the end of the teacher's desk as if she had been ac-
customed to doing so for a lifetime.
"Now, of course," she said, "we can do nothing on the
first day except get ready. We are going to begin by clean-
ing the schoolhouse. How many of you boys, instead of
studying between now and noon, should like to go home and
get buckets to carry water in?"
All the male hands in the school went up with gusto.
"And how many of you girls, instead of studying, should
like to go home and get some coarse rags, and some lighter
ones to wipe things dry with?"
Every female hand in the room went up.
"Very well, then. But remember, you are all to be back
as soon as you can get here."
The women of Green Valley saw the entire school coming
joyfully up the road. "Now, didn't I tell you?" Mrs. Cana-
day asked Mrs. Dunkle as they stood together on the steps
of the Hand-out. "They're back before they begin. It just
ain't in a woman to hold 'em. It takes somebody strong."
The children soon disabused every skeptical mind. They
wanted buckets. They wanted rags. It was easy enough
THE FIREMAKERS
119
to find rags. It was not so easy to find extra buckets. But
when they were found, the entire school went shouting down
the road again.
Long before lunch time everybody had reported. "Now,"
said Val, "it is not quite time to eat"-in fact, it was an hour
till noon-"and we might work a little first. We had better
not trouble Mrs. Riley by going to her well for all the water.
We'll get it from the river. While you were gone I found
a little place where it is clear and nice, and you can get the
water without falling in. Two of the biggest boys-Halcy,
you and Nick, I guess-are to dip it up and see that it
doesn't get roiled and muddy. The rest of the boys will
carry. The girls will stay here with me and we'll clean."
Val put on a loose-looking outer garment which she ex-
plained to the girls was a smock-something artists wore
when they painted-and she was ready to do her part.
That was the kind of school to have. The boys kept more
water on hand than could be used. They eased up in their
carrying, and then threw a few bucketfuls on one another
amid universal shouts and many gleeful warblings.
On one trip Shelley Crumblett received a commission.
Val decreed that Pomp's brass spittoon, discolored beyond
metallic identification, and glued to the floor with the ac-
cumulations of a late season, was to be removed. "We
shan't need it," Val said, a little disgust in her voice.
Shelley looked at her with eyes that revealed a puzzled
mind. "Where are we going to spit?"
"You are not to spit-unless you want to spit out of doors
at recess or noon."
Shelley kicked the spittoon loose and carried it triumph-
antly toward the river.
"What you mean to do with it?" the boys shouted to him
as they saw him coming.
"Do with it?" he echoed as he came down to the water's

THE FIREMAKERS
edge. "That's what," and he gave it a desperate heave into
the river.
The boys were mystified. "Who told you to?"
"She did."
"But where'll we spit?"
"Ain't a-goin' to be no spittin' 'cept at ree-cess and noon

"No spittin'?"
"'S what she said."
"Criminee!" exclaimed Halcy Dunkle. "What do you

Other memorials of Pomp's régime disappeared. "Just
look at my hands!" Kitty Coombs exclaimed after she
had worked long with the soapy water. "I never saw them
as white as that in my life."
Some of the boys, too, had a similar report to make.
They worked less directly with the water, but they noticed
a pinkness in their hands when they came up to the school-
house for lunch.
"Wouldn't it be nice to sit under that big elm tree for
lunch?" Val suggested to them. And everybody rushed to
the great elm in the edge of the pasture back of the school
grounds.
The pupils did not hesitate to spread their lunches out in
full view. All the dinner buckets contained very much the
same articles of food.
In the afternoon the work went on a little less enthusi-
astically, but a little more carefully. Quite before the regu-
lar closing time the last of the scouring and scrubbing was
done and pupils and teacher went joyfully home.
The next day everybody went early again. It would be
interesting to see what a clean schoolhouse, dried out and
all, would be like. The new teacher, moreover, might have
other startling methods to reveal. Somebody who was down
THE FIREMAKERS
121
by the Boyds' house very early saw her going off to school
with a big flat something under her arm.
When they arrived, they found out what it was a pic-
ture with a gilt frame.
It was a matter for the entire school to marvel at and
ponder upon. Where could it have come from? Did some-
body really draw such things, or were they just manufac-
tured? Wouldn't she tell them?
Well, yes, maybe. Some time when lessons were over,
perhaps she could tell them about it. She might possibly
tell them at the end of that afternoon. And the day sped.
She told them about a great wonderful world that they
had never seen. She had never seen it, herself, in fact,
though she meant to do so some day. But there was no
doubt about its existence. People lived in it. There were
pictures of some parts of it in her geography. And there
were other records. Her teacher the year before had told
her where she could send and get pictures of it. She had
been doing that. And Mr. Burch had framed three or four
of them for her.
At the mention of his name there were movements of
consternation. He was "the Rebel." Union soldiers met
him on somewhat equal terms, but small boys walked past
his house back in the hills with open mouths and bated
breath. He stood just slightly higher in the scale than the
devil himself. And here was the new teacher not only hir-
ing him to frame pictures, but mentioning his name in public
without shrinking.
Their minds went back to the pictures. She meant to put
up some more. She could carry only one from the house
each day. But sooner or later there would be several on
the walls.
When she was ready to start to school the next morning, a
half-dozen boys stood waiting in the road in front of the
122
THE FIREMAKERS
house. "Where are they?" they wanted to know. "Why
not take them all at once and be done with it?"
"You won't break the glass?" she asked.
No, they would not break the glass. They would not let
anything else enter their minds until the pictures were safely
there-two boys to each picture.
The excitement over having pictures on the walls had not
yet died down when the pupils learned that there were to be
other innovations. One morning Val announced that they
were to sing. Introducing such levity into the program
seemed to be pleading for disaster. Singing did not belong
in school; it was not disagreeable enough. When the song
books arrived and Val passed them out, the boys would not
sing. It did not seem proper. Halcy would look at Shelley
and Shelley would stop vocal utterance as if it had suddenly
been shut off by a spigot. Then he would turn toward Nick,
and Nick would stop in like fashion. But the girls sang-
or tried to; and eventually the boys found that it was more
fun to sing than to remain prominently silent. Slowly Val
induced them to develop volume. And when they found
that they could have fun at it, they filled the schoolhouse as
completely as they did with their roar after a neighboring
Plymouth Rock rooster had broken the silence by crowing
just outside the window.
One day their singing attracted a visitor. Old Lycurgus
Ott chanced to be passing, and so many children's voices in
pleasant unison drew him to the door. Without knocking
he walked inside. The pupils were frightened at his fa-
miliar figure when it was towering above them, but Val re-
quested them to sing on, and they did so. Lycurgus stood
fascinated by the singing and by Val. "You have aura," he
said, speaking directly to her in full voice. "You have
sometimes almost penetrated the veil."
The boys wanted to laugh, but there was some prodigal
THE FIREMAKERS
123
quality in his disordered mind that made them listen, despite
the fact that they did not understand.
He launched into his accustomed harangue, little seeing
what was immediately before him, but looking with uncon-
centrated gaze into the distance. When he had finished he
made a sweeping adieu to Val and strode from the room.
Some of the patrons who had thought it immoral to have
pictures on the wall but had refrained from action because
they had originally favored Val for teacher, were sure that
something should be done about the singing. "Anything
that will interest a crazy man," they said, "must be wrong."
Anybody ought to be able to see that. Singing ought to be
restricted to church and funerals. Somebody ought to look
into the matter.
There was early opportunity. Val asked the pupils to
invite their parents to school on Friday afternoon at half-
past two for an hour's visit. They came. They filled the
few vacant seats. They filled all the other seats large
enough to admit their stout bodies, and the pupils stood in
the aisles along the walls. A wave of curiosity had swept
most of the women and a few of the men schoolward.
Val took fifteen or twenty minutes to tell them what the
pictures were about. She had learned from her teacher and
from some slips that accompanied the pictures when they
came. The parents were interested. There seemed to be
nothing immoral in the pictures when one understood them.
Then the pupils sang. They stood in rows, according to
size, in the front of the room, like a choir. The songs were
no more harmful than the pictures, so far as one could see.
Mrs. Charley Ferris's mother, who had hobbled along with
the younger women, said she used to sing "Flow Gently,
Sweet Afton," when she was a girl. It made her feel young
again just to hear it.
Some of the women wanted to ask questions. "If you
can have a purty house like this to teach school in," Mrs.
124
THE FIREMAKERS
Dunkle asked, "why can't we have purty houses to live in
at home? That's what I'd like to know?"
Val's year in Oakdale had surrounded her with a golden
halo. She would be asked all sorts of questions now which
she could not possibly answer, and she would be asked
many others which she could have answered quite as well
before she went away. She explained to Mrs. Dunkle that
they could have modest adornments for their houses if they
wished, she felt sure. She would help any of them send
away and get pictures, and Mr. Burch would be glad to
frame them. He did his work well, and his charges were
reasonable.
There was a flutter at the mention of Mr. Burch's name,
but she went straight on with her explanation: "We are
going to plant some trees and flowers out in the side yard of
the schoolhouse next spring, and if anybody else wants to
plant any, I shall be glad to help."
What prophetess had risen among them?
Mrs. Ferris introduced an important question. Where
could they get seeds for flowers that would be pretty?
Every year she received garden seeds and a few flower seeds
from Washington, but the garden seeds never grew and the
flowers never bloomed.
Val was able to answer her question. They could send
and get seed catalogues-she knew where to get the ad-
dresses-and then they could do their own ordering, and
not be dependent upon any Congressman who was out for
reëlection. They could get the catalogues this winter and
have the seeds early.
And when the time came she not only provided them with
addresses but wrote postcard requests for catalogues.
These came in February and the women in Company Row
spent many an hour looking at the colored plates-"just as
real" of double petunias, mammoth chrysanthemums,
THE FIREMAKERS
125
dahlias of unbelievable colors, and gladioli as smooth-
looking as wax.
The trouble was not with Val or the seed catalogues; but
the flowers did not thrive in Company Row. The women
planted hopefully. They covered the flower beds with small
maple branches to keep off the children and the dogs. But
the ground was yellow and fine, and the hounds scratched
the branches away in order to have fresh earth to lie upon.
By July the stray petunias and phlox were thin and starved
looking. The dahlias scarcely got started at all. The
women had to find such comfort as the world afforded by
going down past Mrs. Boyd's house and gazing upon the
robust flowers that grew in every bed of her fertile front
yard.
Many of Val's other startling ideas made their way into
the current of Green Valley life. She had studied physiol-
ogy and hygiene. Some of the women declared that the
pictures in the book were indecent-the men were not so
sure about it-but they were glad to have the fruits of Val's
perilous inquiries. The subject first became a matter of
public discussion when the women visited the school on
another Friday afternoon. Mrs. Crumblett complained that
they couldn't have houses like other people's because the
men's cleaning up every day sooner or later splashed and
spoiled everything in the house. That was why it was no
use to buy a carpet, even if one had the money.
Val went into the subject very thoughtfully. A man could
not clean up in a freezing coal house in winter time, that was
certain. They had to clean up where it was warm. But
according to physiology, even the cleaning up they did in
the house was both incomplete and dangerous. What they
really ought to have was one bath house for all the men who
worked in the mine. It might be in town, but it would be
better if it were at the mine. The men could then wear their
clean everyday clothes to the mine in the morning, slip into
126
THE FIREMAKERS
their diggers, work all day, leave the diggers in the bath
house, clean up, and then come home again in their nice
everyday clothes. The women would then have no dirt in
their houses at all from the mine.
Was it possible to believe that such a miracle might be
brought to pass? No dirt in the house except the clean
yellow mud which the children carried in on their feet? No
dirty diggers, saturated with damp and coal-dust, to smudge
everything they touched, or to leave their odor of coal and
burned oil and powder smoke wherever they stirred the air?
It seemed as romantic as the old times in Virginia that they
had heard their grandmothers talk about.
The women carried the discussion home and reopened it
at the supper table. Why couldn't they have clean houses
like other women? That was what they wanted their hus-
bands to tell them. The men admitted the practicability of
the plan when viewed in the large, but they thought it more
than might reasonably be expected in their specific instance.
Still, something might be done.
The discussion spread. It reached the ears of Eli Ray-
nor, and he hurried away to Centerville to bring it to the
attention of Mr. Bogart. "Just like some damned woman's
idea," he remarked jauntily after he had summarized the
situation. But Bogart felt that it had so much common
sense in it and revealed so many possibilities of development,
that immediate steps should be taken to head it off. "Why,
it would take all the profits for a year to build the place,"
he declared.
He and Eli went over the situation very carefully. Could
Eli start a backfire of some sort that would leave this femi-
nine conflagration without fuel?
Soon Eli was back in Green Valley. He revealed a warm
interest in everything that pertained to the town's welfare.
He dropped into the current of local conversation. He
talked at the Hand-out with the women who came to shop
THE FIREMAKERS
127
in the morning, and with the men who came to loaf after the
day's work was done.
He heard references to baths and clean houses as if the
slightest inkling had never before come to his ears. But
from his great fund of experience he was able to render im-
mediate judgment upon the plan-which, he begged to say,
was an old one long ago considered and rejected. It would
keep the houses clean, yes; but it would kill the men.
He didn't care who had read something in some physiol-
ogy or other. It was not that; he was as enthusiastic about
Val, he wanted it understood, as anyone could be. "She is
a fine teacher, everything considered; of course she is. And
she's a damned pretty girl, which doesn't hurt her any. But
she ought to keep in her place. Anybody knows that if you
clean off that protective coating of coal-dust and sweat, and
then walk out into the winter wind, you'll be dead in a week
from pneumonia."
He was prolific in arguments against the innovation.
"This is what would happen. The Company would install
the baths and before the end of the first season one third of
the men in the mine would be dead of pneumonia or con-
sumption, and then everybody would be suing the Company
for damages. Do you think we'd be fool enough to build
such a death-trap? Not much! Not yet awhile!"
There was something apostolic in his fervor. "Would you
accept the say-so of an eighteen-year-old girl before the
highest medical authority?" he asked of the men who did
not turn an utterly deaf ear to him. He did not mention any
specific authority. It was more convincing to leave the
reference vague and universal.
The majority of the citizens remained with Val. But the
Company owned the mine and its environs. "How are you
going to make them build baths if they don't want to?"
Nathan asked Val one evening after she had brought the
128
THE FIREMAKERS
latest reports to the supper table. "I'd like to see them
come. We need them. But I don't see much prospect."
Val was willing to let the idea make its own way natu-
rally. She was sure it was a good idea. It must find accept-
ance, then, must it not? In the meantime there were plenty
of other things to claim her attention. She wanted a smaller
stove in the schoolhouse. There was a pleasant mean be-
tween frying and freezing which she wished to see estab-
lished in the room. She had, too, to pick out some likely
trees for planting in the side yard, and she had to order
flower seeds. Above all, she had to devise means of squeez-
ing her pupils into the small space available. What she
really needed was a new schoolhouse. The community
ought to have two rooms. The people over at Oakdale had.
Some one to teach hygiene and music and drawing-the
glimpse of all that flashed through her mind was so romantic
that her face colored with warmth.
A two-room school for Green Valley! Now that was an
idea. Even Patent Leather Eli declared immediately in
favor of it. It would take the people's minds away from
baths. A free people must not be denied opportunities to
educate themselves! He would use his influence to have the
township authorize the necessary funds for the Green Valley
school district.

CHAPTER XI
AL'S ideas took hold of Luke with a hurting grasp.
He was not the kind of person who slumped into
mere sulkiness, but the events of the past two or
three years had done much to make him conscious of his
situation. Winnie had gradually extended her visits in
Centerville until she was now no longer much at home. She
tied Luke without tying herself. The one freedom she al-
lowed him was freedom of thought. And thinking devel-
oped alternating moods.
He worked. He said grimly: "Here I am, in for some-
thing I had not contemplated. I do not know when I shall
get out-possibly not at all. But I do know I've got to have
money at the end of next week to pay Mrs. Canaday, to say
nothing about cash for Winnie when she unexpectedly ap-
pears on the scene. Anybody can see what I am to do be-
tween now and the end of next week." Without pushing the
analysis further he would roll enormous lumps of coal into
the car with a furious bang, or lie with one shoulder tight
against the damp floor and cut with such terrific power that
the tiny particles of coal shot singing from the point of his
pick like bullets. If the pick chanced to strike a nigger-
head, the sparks flew as if the pick had touched an electric
wire. He lost himself by driving his energy into the black
wall of coal that confronted him.
Then he became supersensitive. He wanted to know why
they couldn't have cars on time. Why didn't they have a
couple of drivers to collect the loaded cars, and just one man
to haul them the rest of the way out to the tipple, so there
wouldn't be so much of getting mixed up and losing turns?
129
130
THE FIREMAKERS
Why did they have to depend on mules in a mine, anyhow?
If men could put up poles and have electric street-cars, it
ought to be easy to have motors in mines. In fact, he had
heard that they already had them somewhere. When the
air was bad he wanted to know why they could not have
fans to pull plenty of air through the mines, instead of de-
pending on the feeble draft created by a furnace. And why
not have a regular night man who knows his job to see that
there is no loose roof hanging over an entry when the men
go to work in the morning? And why not have double-
entry mines as they had in plenty of places, instead of stak-
ing everything on one entry?
He was in one of his moods of sensitiveness when he first
heard of what Val was doing in her school. She was putting
up pictures, was she? Well, let her. She was going to make
something beautiful, was she? Well, there was nothing new
in that idea, was there? He had thought of that long ago,
himself. He had talked about it, too. But did anybody
listen to him?
This jealousy which his delirious hunger for something
seemly brought upon him was but transitory. Of course
what she had said was true. What she was doing was just
what ought to be done. Did anybody know that better than
he did? He must not be annoyed at seeing somebody else
doing what he had wanted to do and could not.
A desperate new longing for all that he had once dreamed
about took hold of him. Could something yet be done?
Less than three years ago-yes, less than three years ago

and he drove the pick into the coal with such ferocity

THE FIREMAKERS
131
Something saved him from despairing utterly. If only he
could make a little more than was required to meet the bills!
If only he could slip a few dollars away every two weeks
when he went to the Hand-out to get his pay envelope!
Would Winnie shriek until she got it? Well, she would have
to shriek a good long time. In fact, if he ever had any extra
dollars again, she would have little chance to know much
about them.
How he might do anything even if he had the money was
too remote a question to possess reality. The one fact that
would not go from before his mind was that he could do
nothing whatsoever unless the money was first saved up. If
he could sneak aside five dollars every two weeks, how long
would it take him to have two thousand again? Aye, yes,
how long? He would be getting gray-headed, he had to
admit. He would be too old to make the shift. He would
feel as Nathan felt, as his dad had felt. He would be there
for good. Maybe! But if once he could get a start by sav-
ing fives, he might stumble on to some good luck.
He was working up to his limit-to any man's limit. He
drew a fatter pay envelope than any other man who loaded
coal in the mine. If he meant to save money, he must have
a job that would pay more. One day when he had been
thinking it over for the thousandth time while he worked, he
paused for a moment and looked toward Nathan as if he
contemplated speech. "Do you suppose there's any job
around here anywhere that would pay me more than I make
working by the ton?"
Nathan did not know of any. He doubted whether any-
body in Green Valley except the mine boss made more. But
Nathan would be on the lookout for anything that might
happen along.
He did not have long to wait. Lou Sellers, the mine boss,
was looking at turn-offs for new rooms one day, and while he
squatted under the expanse of unsupported roof where a
132
THE FIREMAKERS
switch was to be made in the track, a long "horse back" of

The men cleaned it up in an hour or so after they got him

Patent Leather Eli, whose function in the mining industry

was ready for work when the others were. He would prob-

only what the Company wanted him to see. Other things
could pass discreetly by.
Charley was amazed when he learned the news. He had
supposed Luke would be the new boss. In truth, he had
favored Luke. But since the honor had come, he would do
his utmost to serve the best interests of everybody con-
THE FIREMAKERS
133
cerned. And he undertook to make his language more dig-

He could not help feeling Luke's disappointment. He

But there was a trace of remoteness in their attitude toward

One day after they had eaten their dinners by the fur-

When the other men had gone, Charley began quite

change things myself, and well, you know how it is: a fel-

pretty wet in spots, and they'll pay pretty well." He men-

told me yesterday. And if you like, you can work every
Sunday."
Luke promised to think it over, and Charley promised to
speak to no one else until Luke gave him an answer.
Nathan listened gravely while Luke unfolded the pro-
posal when they were together back in their own room.
"Think we might as well do it, Boy," he said at last, and
resumed his work. He grunted dryly as he socked the pick
into the face of the deep cut. "Don't know as I care to
work seven days in the week-at least every week. It's too
much like war, and I'm too old for that. But that
won't prevent the rest of you from working as much as
you like."
134
THE FIREMAKERS
Hi and Pewee also looked upon the proposal favorably.
Pewee was sure the roof in the hill north of town was safer
than the one where they now worked. Sam Swisher, who
enjoyed driving air courses because they were so narrow no
one could possibly work with him, smiled broadly and made
his complete reply: "Suits me."
They made the change at once. For a time they worked
in two shifts, day and night. Every two or three weeks they
alternated, because some of the women objected to packing
up dinners in the evening and then having to keep quiet the
next day while the men slept. Mrs. Boyd had no special
preference. She "managed" well. There was never much
noise in her house, anyhow. And Nathan had no feeling in
the matter. He did not sleep so much as he used to, and he
seemed to be able to get enough to last whether he slept day
or night. The other men preferred doing one thing or the
other all the time. But the women insisted that the incon-
venience be passed around. "Just as soon as I get so I can
sleep in the daytime," Hi Crumblett protested, "damned if
the old woman don't make me begin all over again and learn
how to sleep at night."
Luke preferred night work. He never required a full day
of sleep. He was free, then, every afternoon to romp with
the twins. Every three weeks or so their hazy young heads
were mystified at seeing him every day after they had been
seeing him only for a brief space in the evening before Mrs.
Canaday put them to bed. He enjoyed the midnight, too.
Sometimes they all came out and ate their meal in the black,
smith shop, before a glowing fire which they found boyish
fun in developing with the roaring bellows. They pushed
their own cars out and dumped them. It was not difficult,
since the coal in that region rose slowly westward, and pro-
vided a gradual descent in the entry, with occasional dips,
from the face of the coal eastward to the mouth of the mine.
All that a man had to do in order to get a loaded car out
THE FIREMAKERS
135
was to squat low on the rear of the car and use his foot as a
brake or propeller as the case required.
Luke enjoyed the electric repose of midnight in autumn.
There was quietness, but quietness which suggested that
something might begin almost any time. There were soft
but persistent winds in the lingering leaves on the trees in
the woodland to the north of the mine, and there was a
ceaseless purring in the river far below the tipple where the
water raced over a miniature rapids. As winter came on,
he paused shivering to see the wind swirling the snow over
the tracks and through the gaunt timbers of the tipple, and
on and on and on into the pale night. Those hours after a
snowstorm was over but before the wind was through with
its work fascinated him with their starkness and their
cruelty. Occasionally he saw a light in Father McGuire's
house high on the ridge by St. Rose's Church. Some poor
mortal was struggling for his breath, and the Father was hur-
rying away through the wind and snow to assure him that
some promises do not fail.
Nathan changed his mind about working seven days in
the week. He was still thinking about Val. By boarding
at home she could save most of the pay she received. She
received thirty dollars a month-one hundred and eighty
dollars for the winter term, and sixty dollars for the sum-
mer term. She meant to save enough to go somewhere-
Nathan thought of it only as a very vague, very remote
region-and study art. She would need a thousand dollars.
But of course she could not accumulate that much very soon
at the rate she was going. So Nathan worked seven days in
the week, and every two weeks on pay day gave her the
earnings of two Sundays. It was a dramatic fortnightly
incident. "Here, Val," he would call in matter-of-fact voice,
"is a little envelope for you." And she would answer in a
voice more expressive of her feeling: "Are you sure you
ought to do it, Pa?" Then she would take the little en-
136
THE FIREMAKERS
velope and hurry away to her bedroom and slip the
money out and count it and then put it away with her other
savings.
"You must be getting a good deal," he said one Saturday
night to her, a sly smile on his rugged face. "Somebody will
be wanting to rob the house pretty soon."
Luke, too, was saving some money. Sometimes he put
much aside, sometimes little. But always there was some-
thing left when the bills were paid. And what seemed to be
good fortune in strange guise lingered with him more and
more. Winnie chose to spent most of her time in Center-
ville, and she apparently had discovered other ways of get-
ting money.
When he worked on the day shift, he was out and off
early every Sunday morning just as if he had not already
worked the other six days of the seven. When members of
Father McGuire's parish who wanted to have most of Sun-
day free for visiting, or for boxing over on Gobbler's Knob,
or for taking an excursion train to Centerville, and therefore
went early to mass, were climbing the steep hill to St. Rose's,
they always saw Luke and Nathan pushing across the hills
toward the new mine. And when the Rev. Mr. Wingfield's
Methodist congregation had begun to come to life late in the
day after a heavy Sunday dinner of fried ham and thick
gravy and mashed potatoes and dried-apple pie, they saw
Luke and Nathan returning, very black except for their
white eyeballs and red lips, very weary, yet very content.
If salvation had to be bought with blood, very well, blood
would be given.
In April they were ready to start two side entries off the
main one. That meant that all four men were now to work
in the daytime in these two entries, and Sam Swisher was to
go on with his narrow little air-course that sooner or later
would let a current of air through from the side of the first
hill to the west. An old, familiar world seemed to be return-
THE FIREMAKERS
137
ing when they all went to the mine together for the first
time.
That spring even Luke-even Pewee-offered thanks to
the stars that a mine roof was overhead. After a cold
winter, the air suddenly became warm and springlike in late
March. The heavens opened and the rain came down. It
rained day and night. Sometimes- the clouds came accom-
panied by a terrifying bombardment of lightning and thun-
der. Sometimes they came unheralded out of the west and
suddenly obscured the sun. But always they were business-
like with their drenching wetness. The few farmers who
had not gone to Kansas or Iowa could get nothing done.
The gutters of Company Row ran yellow with muddy water
until some of the locust posts on which the houses stood
were in danger of being washed out of the ground. Every
day the creek was a raging yellow torrent, and the river rose
with heartless dignity until it flooded all the flat land of the
valley. Fodder shocks that had stood in the field all winter
were lifted so gently by the rising waters that the bundles
of fodder, held together in the shock by one small hickory
withe, remained intact. Then the shock slowly moved off
down the valley, often with a muskrat clinging to its side,
amazed at his easy means of transport.
And still it rained. Soft, wet-looking clouds kept coming
out of the west and spilling thousands of tons of water on
the earth, already so thoroughly saturated that the new fall
ran from the hillsides not in streams but in sheets.
One Sunday morning as the five went to work through a
downpour, Pewee was voluble on the subject of floods. He
was not so well informed on floods as on some other kinds
of disasters, but he had read about the one at Johnstown,
Pennsylvania, and some few others, with considerable thor-
oughness. The external conditions, as they walked across
the hills, were conducive to reflection. In the valley below
them only the high levels of land appeared at all. The hills
138
THE FIREMAKERS
were yellow with water that could not find ready entrance
into the earth's surface. A little run that was dusty all
summer roared down its steep way above the mine like a
mountain flume.
"You know," Pewee said pessimistically as they slipped
and caught themselves and slid and caught themselves again
on the steep bank, "they hadn't ought to have run that
main entry under there. It's not more than forty foot or so
down to the roof from that whirlpool there just above where
she comes over the bank. If there'd ever be a crack when
the roof begins to settle, good-by."
The others laughed. "I don't know whatever God Al-
mighty intended you to be, Pewee," Hi Crumblett declared
with grim joviality, "but one thing is certain: He never in-
tended you for a coal digger."
"Well, such things have happened, haven't they?" Pewee
insisted. "You haven't forgot that 'tastrophe out in Illinois,
have you? Didn't the water stand for days all over the
prairie in a big lake three or four foot deep? And wasn't it
sixty or seventy foot above the coal, instead of forty? And
then one day when all the men was at work, didn't the lake
find a way down through and rip out a hole for itself as big
as the shaft of the mine? And didn't it drown every one of
the eighty men or so?"
"But this is no shaft mine, Pewee. Couldn't the water
run out of the mouth of the mine as fast as it could ever get
in?"
Pewee was defeated but not convinced. What he didn't
like, he said, was the idea of having "any damned Niagry
Falls" running over the top of his head, no matter what the
arguments in the case might be.
Anyhow it was good to get underground away from the
rain. The wet season had made its way down through the
thinner parts of the hill. All along the entry for the first
hundred yards or so there was plenty of dripping, and the
THE FIREMAKERS
139
drain beside the track in the entry carried a goodly stream
of water. But farther in, the dripping diminished, and at
the head of the entries where they were to work, the dry-
ness, according to liberal standards, was absolute.
When noon came they did not go out to the blacksmith
shop to eat. It was cosier up where they worked. They
were in cheerful mood over the "Sunday special" that every-
one had in his dinner bucket-extra ham or beefsteak, an
extra quarter of pie, or a fat piece of layer cake.
Pewee's great piece of layer cake got away from him and
rolled into the black dust on the floor. "Anyhow," he said,
as he picked it up and blew a few of the larger particles off
before he resumed his eating, "there ain't no cleaner dirt
anywhere than new coal. I wouldn't eat anything as dirty
as that outside a mine. But coal dust is good for you. Fur-
nishes grit for your gizzard."
Luke and Hi took loaded cars to the tipple about two
o'clock. They reported that it was raining harder than ever.
A mile and a half through such a downpour after a hard day
of work was not alluring.
A half-hour or more before quitting time Sam came in
from where he worked, and loafed till the others were ready
to go. They coupled the two cars together and Hi squatted
on the rear end of the second one to see that it coasted along
over the ridges and dips without jumping the rough iron
track. The others walked crouchingly along behind, stum-
bling a little in the uneven darkness which their lights left.
They were coming into the heaviest dripping toward the
mouth of the mine when the cars ground brusquely on the
narrow rails and came to an unexpected stop. The impact
jammed Hi's forehead and lamp into a huge lump of coal
piled high on the car. "What the hell!" he exclaimed, as his
lamp went tumbling into the drain and sputtered out in the
stream of water.
140
THE FIREMAKERS
"Hey," he called back to the others, who had not quite

They came up, their steps heavy in the moisture and

"There's something ahead here," he explained.
"Cave-in?" Nathan asked as he came up.
"Guess there must be something down," Hi answered.
"Jammed my head against that big lump of coal and
knocked my lamp to hell knows where."
Nathan squeezed past the cars close to the sopping ribs

"Looks as if the whole hill might be down," he said, a
trifle concerned.
"We'll have to leave the cars here and crawl over it,"

Nathan peered into the darkness close to the roof. "I
don't see any place where a chipmunk could crawl over it."
"What's that, Mr. Boyd?" Pewee asked nervously. "Are

"We may have to dig a hole through," Nathan replied,

we can get a better look."
They tugged at the cars and he pushed. At last the low
wheels ground off the slate on to the clean rails.
The other four came forward.
"I don't see much chance of getting home very soon,"

"Oh, my God!" Pewee exclaimed, his fear suddenly tak-

THE FIREMAKERS
141
of the entry. "I remember just when we put in these black-

"In the name of Christ!" Pewee implored. "What will

Nathan went on deliberately: "It would take every bit of

They all clambered as close as they could get to the pile
that filled the entry. They strained their eyes for the slight-

looked inquisitively at the props along the entry; then back
to the rock and slate ahead of them, and then away again,
like bears in a very strong cage.
"Do you really think they can get to us in three days?"
Pewee urged.
Nathan's own nerves were a little tight, but he was judi-
cial. "That would be my guess. If they get to work as
soon as they miss us this evening, we ought to hear them
banging on the track by Wednesday. That's if she's down
all the way to that sheeting of solid rock. If she's not, they
may get to us sooner."
"How about working from this side, too?" Hi asked. He
was as much concerned as Pewee, but he tried not to appear
SO.
Yes, they would have to answer that question. It was a
nice question which they could answer just as they chose.
Whichever way they answered it might mean death and it
might mean life.
They pushed the cars on back to where there was not so
much drip. Then they squatted on the track to answer Hi's
142
THE FIREMAKERS
question. If their entombment was to be short, then they
could suffer no ill from making it shorter. If it was to be
long, they had better save their reserve to feed on. A few
scraps from their Sunday dinner would not sustain them

Sam Swisher was moved to utterance. "I'm glad I didn't
throw away that extra piece of pie Cindy put in."
They laughed, and they felt a little more courageous.
"What do you say, Mr. Boyd?" Pewee asked with great
respect. His fear kept him from being diverted from the
original question, and led him to see in Old Nate a depth of
wisdom that he had never before sounded.
"Yes, we mean to leave it to you," the others said in con-
currence. "You know more about mines than all of the rest
of us put together."
He accepted the responsibility, and went at once to make
a final tour of inspection before giving out his decision. The
others sat quietly while he peered about again, like some
angular, subterranean ghost, his face somewhat distinct in
the light from his cap close against the roof, his body and
legs fading down to undecipherable blackness.
At last he turned and walked back. He did not speak
until after he had sat down.
"Well, sir, boys," he began with great deliberateness, "I
suppose it will be pretty hard just to sit still, but I believe
we ought to let them take it all out from the other end.
That stretch just this side of the cave-in is right under that
little run up on the surface. The roof's awful wet. If we
begin from this end we might start something we couldn't
stop."
They accepted his decision without a word of dissent.
But how could they put in the seventy-two hours? They
left the cars standing where they were and tramped all the
way back to the head of the deepest side entry. They felt
THE FIREMAKERS
143
more at home where it was dry, and where a face of clean
black coal confronted them.
At first they were self-conscious. They had never before
stopped to ask what the inside of a mine was like when there
was nothing else to look at. "Let's see," Hi finally asked in
an effort to be casual, "did I leave anything in my dinner

They all explored their buckets. Together they could
pool about two dog-rations of bread scraps, gristly meat,
and pie and pie crust.
"Over at Blue Rock, before the Civil War," Pewee said
authoritatively, "those fellas had to get along for thirteen
days and eighteen hours on about that much and a little
lard oil and a rat or two. Guess we ought to manage for
three days."
He felt braver after he had made that comparison.
"No rats in this mine yet," Sam Swisher said, smiling
broadly but faintly.
"That's right," Hi averred. "I ain't seen a rat yet in the
whole works."
"What about oil?" Luke asked. He had been singularly
silent all the while.
"Yes, what about oil?" Nathan echoed. "Our jug is most
empty. I meant to bring some in to-morrow."
"Ours is on our car down the entry there," Hi Crumblett
explained, referring to the jug. "There's not a tablespoon-
ful left. I filled my lamp just before we started out."
They had an abundance of one thing-time-keeping.
Nathan carried a big hunting-case, key-winding silver watch
that had not failed him in all the days of the Civil War and
since. Hi Crumblett had another almost as big. They
would know how time passed.
At eight o'clock they prepared for bed. Each had worn
an old overcoat that morning to keep out some of the rain,
but they had left these in the blacksmith shop outside.
144
THE FIREMAKERS
They scraped together good-sized piles of fine slack and
made themselves pillows. Then they stretched out on the
hard floor of the room and Nathan puffed out the last lamp.
Thank God, they were where it was dry. What if they had
to sleep in a place as dripping wet as that stretch down by
the cave-in?
Going to sleep was not easy. It is one thing to work in a
mine; it is another to sleep in one. Silence, a silence like
that of being on a grain of star-dust a few tens of millions
of miles from any similar, neighboring grain, is not what one
requires to go to sleep by. There should be some familiar,
transitory, beneficent sounds a hound barking over on the
hillside, some friendly neighbors laughing past one's door-
step on the way home from a K. of L. dance, or a child
breathing peacefully but audibly in a trundle bed in the
same room. When a man works, if he stops shoveling coal
back some day and rests against the pile until the silence
begins to close in about his head with a maddening whirr, it
is only necessary to go to work again, or to bang the back
of his shovel against a lump of coal. But at night when it is
a man's business to sleep, he cannot bang his shovel very
conveniently to break the shrieking roar in his ears. On a
grain of star-dust somewhere in the heavens a man would
be isolated, to be sure. But at least he would be on top of
something. There would be expansiveness. He would not
feel crowded. But in the mine there is only the silence
of something that waits to close in upon a man and catch
him unawares.
The men rolled restlessly, nervously in the darkness.
"Must be this bed is too damned hard," Hi said to Sam
about eleven o'clock.
"Makes me think of sleeping on the march somewheres,"
Nathan said.
Then they all talked for a time, like boys who have been
sent to bed, but who are not sleepy. Eventually there was
THE FIREMAKERS
145
silence again. Then Old Nate began to snore. It was a re-
assuring sound, and soon the others were asleep with him.
Nobody expected to hear any evidence of rescue the next
morning. Still they lighted a lamp or two and stumbled
along the entry to the cave-in, eager to listen.
They could hear nothing but the drip, drip of water every-
where. They made a little pool in the drain, and drank
from it. The water was sulphurish. "It needs sweetenin'
or somethin'," Hi observed.
Back at the head of the entry, they tried to think of some-
thing to do. They had never done anything but work, and
they found themselves with scant resources when they
wanted to play. "If we just had a deck of cards," Pewee
lamented, "we could play poker."
They smoothed off a place in the floor where the hardpan
was not stonelike and played mumble-peg. They guessed
distances to points along the entry which they could just
make out, and then measured them. They speculated on
who composed the shift at that moment digging through to-
wards them. "I'll bet Jab has a hand in it," Hi declared.
"It will give him a good chance to cuss."
At noon they had a lively time flipping a brass car check
to determine whether they should eat everything then, or
divide the snack between that day and the next.
That night they slept better. They were up early the next
morning and off down the entry to listen. They heard the
steady drip, drip of water.
On the way back Pewee said to Nathan: "Mr. Boyd, is
there any danger of breathing up all the air in here before
they get to us?"
Nathan was sure there was not. "I don't know as I could
figure it up on paper, but there ought to be enough in here
to last five men for ten days or two weeks. It was pretty
good air to begin with, you know."
Just the same he proposed early that evening that they
146
THE FIREMAKERS
put out the light. "No need burning up any more oil than

They were awake early Wednesday morning. "Are we
going to hear anything?" Hi asked Pewee, and poked him
resistlessly in the ribs.
They made their way down to the cave-in, got on their
knees close to the rails and listened. They heard the

Drip, drip.
"This damned water is gettin' on my nerves," Pewee said
as he bent down to have a drink. "I wish it could speed
up or slow down once in a while."
They listened again. "I hadn't expected to hear any-
thing before afternoon," Nathan said when the faces of the
others expressed disappointment. He was honest; he had
not.
The day seemed a century long. Every fifteen minutes
somebody asked what time it was. Nathan remarked once,
as a trace of a smile crept over his face, that he didn't know
but what it might have been easier if they had had no watch
at all.
"Why not put in some time eatin'?" Hi asked merrily.
"Here, have a nice slice of beefsteak, right out of the thick-
est hind-quarter." And he passed his empty dinner bucket.
"Now don't be backward about taking the last piece."
They had great fun for a half-hour helping themselves to all
sorts of delicacies, including nice crushed lumps of peacock
coal for dessert.
They finished in due time and then moved expectantly
down the entry. "We've got to hear something this time,"
Hi announced as he kneeled over the rails.
They held their breath. Drip, drip, drip, drip.
They breathed. Then they listened again. Drip, drip-
"Hold on!" Luke shouted excitedly. "I heard something.

They listened. "Hear that?" But nobody else could

He whacked the rail three times, and just as faintly the

He thought the roof must be pretty soft. They'd prob-

The next morning they were down early and signaled.
The clicks from the other side were very much nearer.
In the afternoon they went down again. They could hear

the rocks. They would have to spend another night on their
pillows of slack.
They had been back in their entry-head an hour, perhaps,
when there was a shuddering far down the main entry and
a slight concussion of air. With scarcely a word, they stum-
bled to their feet and hurried to see what had happened.
When they clambered round the two cars in the edge of the
dripping area, they saw. The roof over the stretch of entry
that was wettest had come down.
