CHAPTER XII
VENTS outside the mine had followed an easy logic.
On Sunday evening the women waited for their hus-
bands until supper began to get cold. Then Mrs.
Hi Crumblett spoke her mind to the interior of her house in
general about men who were so "on-Christian" as to want
to work from daylight to dark every Sunday just the same
as week-days. She threw a coat over her head and darted
into Pewee Dunkle's house, where Mrs. Dunkle, who was
trying to keep the warmed-over beefsteak from drying into
a crisp, concurred in all she chose to say. But when Mrs.
Sam Swisher came in and wondered if anything might have
happened, the other women were seized with the same fear
that had taken hold of her. Of course something might
have happened. Something might happen any time. Mrs.
Dunkle then appealed to Charley Ferris, since he was mine
boss now, and he and two or three men who boarded at the
Valley House started off through the drenching rain to learn
why the men had not come home.
At the mouth of the mine everything seemed normal.
When they were just about to accept the theory that the
men had sought to avoid some of the mud by walking home
on the circuitous railroad track, and had therefore not met
them anywhere on the path, Ferris said, "Boys, do you
know the cars they use are not down on the tipple? They
always just let theirs stand on the main track."
They started into the mine, silent and fearful. When
they came up to the cave-in they were not startled. It was
what the absence of the cars on the tipple had led them to
expect. "I told Eli they'd have a cave-in under that little
149
150
THE FIREMAKERS
stream some day," Charley Ferris observed, "if they made
the opening as far out toward the river valley as they did.
But it saved them building a lot of tipple."
They saw at a glance that the cave-in was serious, and
hurried back to Green Valley to spread the news and organ-
ize shifts of rescuers. Within two hours men were at work,
and others were hurrying with additional tools from the old
mine south of Green Valley. Men and women and children
ran panting and bedraggled through the rain and mud to the
new tipple and talked excitedly and helplessly in little
groups.
The rescuers were quick to see that the outer end of the
fall was to the east of the little stream. If the roof had
come down all the way back under the stream, as it prob-
ably had, there was little hope that they could ever dig
through it while the entombed men lived.
They worked desperately. One shift, as many men as
were of any possible use in an entry eight feet wide, flung
themselves into their task until they were exhausted. Then
they gave way to another shift, who tore into the mud and
shale and rock like wild beasts. Mrs. Dunkle, Mrs. Crumb-
lett, Mrs. Swisher, and Mrs. Boyd stood together in the rain
with shawls over their heads and shoulders, and wept in si-
lence over the workers' ill-concealed attitude of futility.
Toward morning most of the women went home; the men
said they might as well. But by breakfast time they were
back with dinner buckets of bread-and-butter, thick slices
of meat, and coffee.
"They'll need lots of coffee," Mrs. Boyd suggested. "Why
not take a wash-boiler and some other things to the tipple
and make the coffee there in the blacksmith shop. We
could warm up their meat for them, too."
So they worked, the tired men feverishly swallowing huge
quantities of food and strong coffee before they hurried into
battle with the mud and rock again, the women carrying
THE FIREMAKERS
151
fresh supplies from Spring Valley and making the coffee and
warming the food over the roaring fire in the blacksmith
shop. The boys helped to carry provisions, and they kept
the bellows roaring even more than was required.
Some of the men fitted up little bunks under the protect-
ing roof of the tipple and tried to get snatches of sleep. But
they slept little. The air was too tense. Whenever they
looked toward the hill it shouted back at them: "I can come
down as fast as you can dig me away-maybe faster."
They must remain awake and vigilant.
By Wednesday they were a gaunt and weary-looking
group. They were all that Green Valley could muster for
the battle, but they seemed insufficient. They tried to keep
up courage, but their slow movements belied their preten-
tious hearts. There seemed to be nothing ahead but mud
and slate and rock and still more mud and slate and rock.
Then they were electrified by hearing Luke's taps on the
rails inside. Boys ran excitedly to Green Valley to tell the
few who remained there that the men in the mine were alive
and that the rescuers would be through to them very soon.
The men on the outside forgot their weariness and worked
with the abandon of madmen. When a shift was relieved
the men came out, gulped down their coffee, swallowed their
bread and meat without chewing, and then rolled nervously
in their bunks, or tramped among the little knots of women
in the rain until their shift was called back to work.
That night they encountered a great solid sandstone. It
filled the entry from side to side, and was too firm in tex-
ture to be pounded to pieces. They would have to blast it.
But blasting it would be slow work. Then they found that
the first blast was not sufficient.
Thus it came to pass that they set off the second blast
late Thursday afternoon while the five men sat in cheerful
anticipation in their dry entry-head. The rescuers heard
the trembling that followed the blast and feared what had
152
THE FIREMAKERS
happened. They feared that more roof had come down,
yes. But they had a greater fear. When they resumed
their tappings on the rails, they received not the faintest
response. Probably the men inside, eager to be set free,
were just at the other end of the cave-in waiting when the
blast brought the new fall. If they were, they were all
buried.
The rescuers drooped as if all were over. Still they
worked on. The air was poisonous and dead, because too
many of them were breathing it at a time, and there was no
circulation except the feeble one created by their own exits
and entrances with the car. Still with the large rock out of
the way they could make rapid progress. But how far
would they have to go?
If only it would stop raining and filling the earth's sur-
face with more and more water! Nobody except the oldest
inhabitant with a well reënforced memory could recall such
a spring. The men who worked were wet and caked with
the yellow mud that relentlessly oozed down upon them out
of the hill. Whenever they came out, the obstacle in the
entry was magnified by the external gloom-the lowering
sky, the river valley full of muddy water, the bedraggled,
fearful women begging for good news.
By Sunday morning the gloom had overwhelmed nearly
everyone. Added to all the other difficulties as the rescuers
got farther in under the wet area, was that of keeping the
rest of the hill from coming down upon them while they
worked. They were constantly setting up the strongest tim-
bers they could handle, but a wet hill treats strong timbers
with little respect. Every hour or two the men tapped on
the rails and listened, but the men inside gave no response.
They must be dead. If the hill should once get the better
of them for five seconds, they themselves would be dead.
A dozen other women would be widows and three or four
dozen other children would be orphans.
THE FIREMAKERS
153
The entire region came to the mine Sunday morning.
Father McGuire, high upon the ridge above the mine, had
contented himself with one mass early that morning and
then had come down to the tipple. The Rev. Mr. Wingfield
had dismissed the few who came to Sunday School, because
he said it seemed like sacrilege to do anything except help
the endangered. From miles about, the curious of all faiths,
and none, had deserted their usual Sunday occupations and
had come by twos and threes and half-dozens to see a cave-in
that had lasted a week. Hundreds of men and women who
were accustomed to wet weather, but who felt something
profane in being wet all day on Sunday, stood about on
every eminence under spacious umbrellas.
It was in such surroundings that the persons concerned
clustered together and sought to decide whether the dan-
gers of rescue work should be risked longer. If there was
to be a cessation of effort, it ought to come before other
lives were lost.
Somebody stood on an upturned mine car and stated the
purpose of the meeting. He thought he had better call
upon some prominent citizens before the vote was taken.
He would begin by calling upon the Rev. Mr. Wingfield.
Mr. Wingfield thought there must be others in a better
position to discuss the matter. He wished to say, though,
that no one suffered more in behalf of the entombed men
than he did. His heart was heavy, too, for the wives and
their children. It was appalling to think of the men buried
in the hill. It was even more apalling to contemplate bury-
ing others. The responsibility of deciding what should be
done was very great, and he prayed that those who had to
make the decision would have heavenly guidance. If it
should be decided to risk other lives in working on, he stood
ready to go in and take his turn with the other men at the
hazardous enterprise.
Even in such a crisis, the chairman evidently reasoned,
154
THE FIREMAKERS
things must be nicely balanced. He called upon Father
McGuire to say a word, also.
Father McGuire felt very much as Mr. Wingfield felt.
He, too, wanted to offer his services if it should be decided
to continue the work. "I shall be happy to take turns with
Brother Wingfield," he said with a sincerity that no one
questioned, "or I will go in and work with him, side by
side." He turned and stepped over towards Mr. Wingfield
a few feet away, and they clasped hands with a high-pitched
cordiality that sent a buzzing of approval through the
dreary crowd. Then he went on to say that if the hazard of
going on in and recovering the bodies should be regarded as
too great, he would have the ground blessed so that the
family of Sam Swisher would have the comfort of knowing
that he rested in holy peace.
The assembly should hear from others, of course. But
which others? That was a puzzling question for the chair-
man. Should he call upon the wives of the entombed men?
If they spoke honestly they would find it embarrassing. Or
should he call upon the wives of the rescuers? They like-
wise would find it embarrassing. It seemed that the only
safe course was to call upon no one directly concerned.
In the moment in which the chairman tried to decide defi-
nitely, some one in the edge of the crowd began: "Ladies
and Gentlemen." They all turned and saw Lycurgus Ott
in full view on top of the gob pile which the new mine had
already accumulated. So many people in assembly had
moved him to customary speech. He stood majestic in his
uncomprehension. "If we could only lift the veil and..."
"Cut out your crazy nonsense," some one yelled, "or I'll
brain you with a niggerhead.”
The chairman addressed some one conveniently near:
"Get him away from here. Take him down to the railroad
track and tell him they want to see him in the next town."
The interruption was a happy one for the chairman. He
]
THE FIREMAKERS
155
saw Eli Raynor. Yes, to be sure, he would call upon a rep-
resentative of the Company. Then he would have them
vote.
"Will you say a word, Mr. Raynor?" he asked.
Yes, Eli would. He climbed to the top of a cone of slate
and shale which the rescuers had wheeled out, and glanced
over the crowd. He was pink and dapper despite the rain.
He spoke with prepossessing facility. He shuddered
when he thought of the men in the hill. But unquestionably
they were now beyond poor human help. Of course, if it
was decided to work longer at rescuing their bodies, he was
authorized by the Company to say that every man who
worked would receive compensation for every hour he put
in. "And I am sure a generous community will do all that
can be done for the widows and orphans. But let us pause
before we make other widows and other orphans." He then
painted a devastating picture of fatherless homes in Com-
pany Row. He was filled with pain when he saw all that.
When he finished there was a stirring in the crowd, and

"Shall we express our feeling on this momentous

come.
The hollow-eyed, exhausted miners and their weary
wives wished they could run away before the vote was
taken. In the tense moment that followed the chairman's
question, they were numb with fright and helplessness.
"If you are ready to vote-"
But the chairman was once again interrupted, this time
by an electric voice that might have been heard half-way
across the valley: "Wait a minute!"
Everybody turned toward the mouth of the mine where
the voice seemed to come from. Jab Strohl, his face, his
clothing, his hands covered with mud and black, stood on a
slight ridge by the mine entrance. He looked like some
156
THE FIREMAKERS
violated statue of a man as he stood there in complete isola-
tion.
"I got somethin' to say," he shouted when all eyes turned
toward him. "I don't know as I can say it. A mule is about
the only thing I can make understand. But I aim to tell
you if I can git through with it."
At least no one was present to chide him about his lan-
guage. He could speak with unrestraint.
"I just now come out, and I got up here to see what was
a-goin' on. And I heard you a-tryin' to decide whether to
give up or not.
"Do you think that's a hard question to decide? Why
that's a hell of an easy question. What if you was in there
yourself? Would it be a hard question then?
"You act like they was all dead in there. How does any-
body know? My guess is they're not. My guess is they
all kept back at the head of the entry most of the time,
where it would be dry. And that's about where you'd find
'em now if you was in there.
"You think they're dead anyways. Well, what if they
are? Ain't this a good time to find 'em? If they're dead
when we find 'em, all right; let's find 'em dead. But in
Christ's name, let's find 'em.
"You think we're a-goin' to git squeezed ourselves while
we're at it? Well, maybe so. Only God Almighty knows.
Water's pourin' through the roof same's if it was a sieve.
If any of you want to git close enough to mud to find out,
you can see for yourselves how much water's runnin' out
the mouth of the mine here. But hell, are we a-goin' to
throw up the sponge for somethin' that ain't happened to
us yet?
"I don't believe we'll be squeezed, and I don't believe the
men in there have been either. They're back at the head
of the entry, I tell you. It stands to reason. Can't you see
Old Nate settin' in there on a pile of slack a-waitin' for us?
THE FIREMAKERS
157
Can't you see him? Can't you hear him a-poundin' on that
rail in there and then a-listenin'?
"And you think maybe you'll quit the job. Hell's fire,
did you ever hear of Old Nate a-quittin'? Did anybody
fetch any report about him a-runnin' away at Gettysburg,
or wherever it was, just because he saw a few more thou-
sand a-wavin' their bayonets? He's in there in the middle
of this hill! And all the rest of 'em too. And by God,
they're a-goin' to be dug out. If they're still alive, we'll
give 'em the fresh air. If they're dead, they're not a-goin'
to be left in any holy peace in a damned hill; they're a-goin'
to be buried in a cimitry, same as the rest of us.
"There ain't a-goin' to be no vote on this question. It's
settled. Do you git that? Any lousy quitter that don't
want to dig don't have to. But he'd better go home out
of the rain and save his soft hide. Whenever he's ready to
go I'll find him an umberell. But the rest of us mean to
stay on the job. And forty men from the Bluefield mines
will be here to-morrow at daylight to take turns with us so
we can sleep a little and git ready to dig harder.
"'Course the air is bad. But we can lay some pipe, and
fan some air in. Any kind of a fan with a little muscle
behind it will start a current. Why, if Eli Raynor would go
in there and blow his breath, he could start enough of a cur-
rent. But he won't go in, so we've got to find a windmill-
and a damned big one, at that."
Jab took such delight in his wise crack about Patent
Leather Eli that he was ready to stop, and he did so
abruptly.
Everybody was cheered by the sight of one man who
seemed to know what he was about. All sorts of volunteers
tendered their services. Only experienced miners could be
used; but Jab was comforted to know that so many men
were not quitters.
The rescuers worked desperately. Some of them rigged
158
THE FIREMAKERS
out a large-sized windmill that had stood in a farmer's barn
since the days when he had cleaned his own wheat and
buckwheat, and began to pump air in to the shift of men at
work. The big improvised pipe leaked, but at least they
succeeded in getting an occasional fresh breath all the way
in. The railroad company sent an engine to haul stout oak
timbers from a sawmill down the river at Culbertson, so
that a tight ceiling could be built against the wet hill that
persisted in oozing down upon the workers.
Could they get through to the men inside in time? It
seemed doubtful. But Jab had phrased their feeling. Now
was the time to dig them out, whether they were dead or
alive. If they should require a grave, let it be under the
comforting sod, and not in the black bowels of any hill.
CHAPTER XIII
N the days that followed the second cave-in, com-
plications about which the rescuers could know noth-
ing were developing inside the mine. As soon as the
five men hurried down the entry and saw how long the new
fall of roof was, they knew that their chances of being taken
out alive were slight. They stood for a time in consterna-
tion and gazed at what their eyes could not fail to see. Then
they turned away trembling.
Luke had maintained a singular silence throughout most
of the four days. Pewee, always a little overwrought, had
wondered whether Luke was as anxious to get out as the
rest of them were. But Nathan had entertained no such
thought. He felt a close understanding between himself
and Luke. Speech on Luke's part was unnecessary.
Now, however, as they stumbled fearfully along the en-
try away from the many new yards of rock and mud which
blocked their only exit, Luke was moved to make a pro-
posal. "Looks to me," he said, with an abruptness that
startled the others, "as if we'd better do some mighty close
planning. We're in here for a long time."
"Yes, a long time," was all that Nathan said in reply;
and the others said nothing.
When they reached the head of the entry Pewee was
nervous almost to tears. "What do you propose we do,
Luke?" he asked, his voice revealing his agitation.
"I don't know as I propose anything. I'm just wonder-
ing if we hadn't better make plans for a pretty long stay.
I'm hungry, for one thing. Never went without food for
longer than a half day before in my life. But people live
159
160
THE FIREMAKERS
a good many days without food if they do it right. They
make up for it by drinking water, don't they, Mr. Boyd?"
Nathan replied that he had lived more or less on water
in the Civil War-when he could get it.
"Well," Luke said, and he brightened up a trifle as he
saw the humor of the situation, "there's no doubt about be-
ing able to get plenty of good sulphur water."
"And what about air?" Pewee asked. "We've got to
breathe?"
Yes, air.
"If your girl was here," Hi said, addressing Nathan, "she
could figure up, couldn't she, just how long the air in these
entries and room necks would last five men?"
Their depression was not quite so abysmal as it had been
when they first saw the new fall. "Yes, if she was here,"
Nathan answered, "yes. But she's not here. Maybe it's
just as well that we have to get along without knowing, any-
how. She couldn't make any more than there is."
"How much air does a lamp burn up?" Luke asked.
"Just what I was thinkin'," Hi said in response. "If
we've got plenty of matches, why..."
Pewee squirmed. He thought sitting in the dark would
be as bad as having no air to breathe. "Just as bad to see
nothing as to breathe nothing-that's how I feel about it."
"And eat the extra lard oil," Hi proposed, going back to
his own suggestion. "There's grease in lard oil. What do
you say, Mr. Boyd?"
Nathan uttered a grim rejoinder. "There's not enough
oil to eat or to burn either. Everybody was low on Sunday;
and we've burned a good deal since then."
The inventory was complete: Plenty of sulphur water,
no food, a bit of tobacco, little oil, restricted air. Dura-
tion of their stay, indefinite. They had matches. And one
of their watches had a hunting-case with a broken crystal
so that they could feel the time in the dark.
THE FIREMAKERS
161
But time did not pass.
They took up any diversion that anybody proposed, but
when they were through with it and somebody asked Na-
than to "feel" his watch, the minute hand seemed to be just
about where it was when he felt the last time.
Once when time seemed not to move at all, Pewee asked:
"Mr. Boyd, do you s'pose we could work a little at this
end?"
But he was promptly voted down. "We've no timbers,
and the rest of the hill would push right down in the place
of what we'd take out," Luke answered in outlining the
other side of the case. "Anyhow, we wouldn't last long."
"I should say not," Hi declared in concurrence. "My
belly is as hollow as a bass drum."
"I guess they'll have to come to us from the outside,"
was Nathan's final comment.
The next morning when they awakened they had light
for a while, as a luxury. "Breakfast time!" called Pewee,
restored somewhat by sleep; and he yanked his belt up two
holes tighter. "Look at that, will you, while the light is
good?"
Nathan started off down the entry in the dark. "I'm
going to see what it's like without a lamp."
"Watch out you don't run into the cars," Pewee called.
"I'd thought of that," they all heard Nathan say as he
stumbled on. He stooped low enough to keep his right hand
running along the rail of the track. He became aware of the
cars just before he touched them. The air pressure, or some-
thing more subtle still, warned him in time.
He felt his way round the cars. As he stepped out into
the entry again, his feet splashed in water. "I didn't know
it was dripping that much," he said to himself, quite aloud.
As he went farther, he splashed into deeper water. Water
was soon seeping over the lacings of his heavy brogans.
"Well," he said, and stood for a moment.
162
THE FIREMAKERS
"The drippings can't get through the drain," he reasoned.
Yet he was not quite content with his explanation.
He went back to the rest of them and spent the day talk-
ing and lounging and trying to treat their situation casually.
They tried to sleep during the day, too. "It's just as dark
one time as another," Hi observed. "So why not sleep any
time?" But his philosophy did not work well.
Hi did, however, succeed in shortening the day. His
mind turned to his newest author, to the paper bound copy
of Karl Marx's Capital which he was just now struggling
with in the long evenings. He did not say much about
the volume to his fellow miners, for he was not altogether
certain that he understood it. But in his heart he had
somehow come to feel that this book was more important
to the human race than any other-more important than
the Bible, and almost as mysterious. As for the author of
it, he was a greater benefactor to the human race, espe-
cially coal miners, than Jesus of Nazareth had ever been.
Of that Hi felt sure.
"If you fellows had ever read Karl Marx, we would have
something to talk about."
"Why not talk about him anyhow?" Pewee asked. His
mind was abnormally alert. "You tell us about him."
Hi began. Almost before he was through with the first
sentence he found himself engaged in an argument. The
others did not accept premises that he took for granted. Or
if they accepted them, they held different degrees of accep-
tance.
"What's the use to begin," Luke asked, "by saying that
everybody can have the same opportunity?"
"Well, can't they?" Hi asked.
"It wouldn't last long." Luke replied. "Let's say that
the government or somebody gives us a pair of pigs apiece.
When they grow up, my sow has a litter of fourteen and
THE FIREMAKERS
163
yours has six. Do you mean to say that we go on having
the same opportunity for fried ham?"
He laughed in the darkness.
Hi protested that it was not just like that. Maybe the
government could act as a just judge and keep things
straight.
"Yes," Luke answered in joviality that had a touch of
warmth in it, "somebody would be kept mighty busy divid-
ing up the new pigs every morning, and the new veins of
coal and all the rest of it. They'd have to keep their eye
peeled for all the Bogarts. Thirty years ago these hills
belonged to farmers from Virginia and one place or another.
But who owns them now?"
Hi did not profess to understand it all. They would have
to read the book. Then they would find out that Marx was
a greater friend of the common people than Jesus ever had
been.
Nathan had said nothing. But at this remark by Hi he
observed casually: "He must be a big man, then."
Nathan had been thinking about the water. He ought to
go down again before bedtime. But perhaps it would be
better to save his legs.
They did not sleep well. The hunger was beginning to
tell. Pewee started up in his sleep with a frightened cry
that awakened the others.
"What's the matter, Pewee?" Hi asked, irritated yet sym-
pathetic. His own stomach was sending out sharp, desper-
ate reminders that it existed.
It was Nathan's first bad night. He had been hungry be-
fore. He could forget his hunger-somewhat. But he
could not forget that water. Was it still raining outside?
Was that little run still as large as a good creek? Several
times in the night he opened his watch and felt gently for
the hands. When they were at twenty minutes past five, he
got up.
164
THE FIREMAKERS
"Where are you going, Mr. Boyd?" Pewee asked. Every

the "Ah-um-m" when some one sat up that it was Nathan.
"Guess I'll go down and listen a little," Nathan answered.
Pewee protested. "Now look here, Mr. Boyd, there's
no sense in you bein' the only one wearin' yourself out

Pewee was the last man in the mine that Nathan would

"It's my job. Don't you remember? You elected me.
Anyhow, I've learned how to get down there in the dark."
Some distance back in the entry before he reached the
cars, he splashed into water. "Fast as this?" he asked in
his habit of talking aloud to himself.
He walked back out of the water, sat down on the track,
took off his shoes and socks and rolled up his trousers and
red woolen drawers. Then he waded in. He could feel the
water steadily becoming deeper as he splashed along down
the entry. It was well up on his legs when he reached the
two cars and waded past them. A strange feeling to be
there in the dark and yet in water so deep that he could
touch it with his hands without lowering himself from his
half-stooping position!
The water had a fresh, earthy smell. He steadied him-
self by reaching over to the ribs of the entry with his right
hand, and then stooped farther down and drank like a horse
at a trough. It tasted a little of mud, but at that it was
better than sulphur water.
As he held his breath while he swallowed, so that the
water would not run in at his nose, he heard a distinct sound
close over the smooth surface of the water. It was a gurgle,
a pouring, the noise of a miniature waterfall.
Now he knew. Above the new fall, the wet earth and
rock had given way on and on up to the stream above. The
stream was pouring no small part of itself into the mine,
THE FIREMAKERS
165
and if the rains kept up, no one outside would ever discover
that a part of the water was being diverted.
The situation was so critical that he became absolutely
calm, like a man in battle after he has been nervous in an-
ticipation.
When he had listened until there could be no doubt in
his mind, he splashed back through the water to his shoes
and socks. He drew them on deliberately, and sat for a
moment reflecting. Then he began to feel along the drain
for a stray lump of coal or a niggerhead. He found one,
and as best he could, stepped off twenty yards back toward
the head of the entry. He felt very carefully for a secure
angle where the rail crossed the tie and placed the nigger-
head there. He could find it easily when he next came down
with his hand sliding along the rail to guide him.
Then he went back to his fellows. The distance had
never seemed so great to his legs. He knew that it was no
greater than it had been the day before. In fact, he knew
that it was not quite so great.
Since he was not a talker, his silence aroused no suspi-
cion. And the inky blackness that filled the mine saved
them from seeing him. He managed to put in two or three
hours and then announced that he felt as if he wanted to
stretch his legs a little. No one could deny him the privi-
lege.
How many yards of the twenty would be covered by the
water? When he thought he was approaching the nigger-
head he kept his hand continuously against the rail. He did
not find the niggerhead, but he splashed into water.
What was this? Had he missed the niggerhead? Or was
the water coming in that fast? If it was, it must be almost
up to the roof of the entry down there where it gurgled in.
He listened. He could hear a more subdued but a more
voluminous gurgle as the water poured down out of the hill.
He would be wiser. He stepped off twenty paces and
166
THE FIREMAKERS
pressed a new lump of coal against the rail. Then he

He started back to rejoin the others. On the way, he

"Thought I'd mosey down and see what was up," he said

"I think I have."
He waited.
"We're going to be drowned out."

"Yes."

Yes, it would be better to wait a little. They could take

turns for a time, or wander out into the entry in casual style

But the next morning Hi Crumblett heard one of them

When Nathan came back, Hi at once began on him.
"You and Luke found a bakery or a meatshop down the

It hurt to laugh, anyhow. It stirred up one's angry stomach.
Nathan deliberated. He made a sound with his breath

"I can tell you if you want to know. But 'twon't do you

the fall. Didn't start right away, but it's got started since,
and it's made a pretty big hole for itself to come through.
It's way back in the entry now, and it's marching right
along."
They heard Pewee whimpering. Then he burst out:
"Oh, my God, Mr. Boyd, are we all to be drownded up

Nathan wanted to be as hopeful as the facts would per-

There was nothing to say in reply to that. Silence, and
utter darkness, and the water coming-it was not cheerful,
even to rugged hearts.
"How much oil is there?" Pewee asked. His voice

"Not much," Nathan answered. "Just a little in one

The ordinary marks of days and hours were swept out

him and followed. Nathan's legs had never felt like that

Luke was weary and Nathan completely exhausted when

"Back?" Sam Swisher asked.
"Yes."
Sam had been more completely speechless than was usual

"Just about."

Nathan knew not what Sam's special need was, but he felt

up and stared blinkingly about. Hi Crumblett's neck was

Nathan and Luke and Hi were respectfully silent. But

his Act of Contrition, Pewee struck up in over-pitched voice
snatches of "Jesus Paid It All," "I Am Coming, Lord," "I
Need Thee, Oh, I Need Thee."
When Sam had finished Nathan snuffed out the tiny smok-
ing flame and they were in darkness again. He sat with
his legs extended and with his back against a smooth post
which they had put up close against the side of the entry
the last day they worked. He got tired of lying on the un-
yielding floor. It felt good to let the round post fit into the
hollow of his back.
No one spoke. They were growing too feeble even to
have a hurting desire for food. And the heavy air was be-
ginning to drug them into stupor.
A long, long time after-everything was so hazy that it
was hard to say how long-Luke thought there must be
something wrong with himself. The air felt thin, as if his
lungs were not full. He struggled to his feet and straight-
ened up as far as the roof of the entry would let him. His
knees wabbled. "Good thing there're no bats in here," he
thought. "One might fly against me and knock me over."
The water! Yes, the water. He remembered now. Of
THE FIREMAKERS
171
course, he had heard of men's bodies being hauled from
mines after an explosion or a flood. It was to be expected-
some other fellow's body. But his own; he had never
thought of having them haul it out. Everything seemed so
hazy. Ideas were so slippery a man couldn't hold on to
them. Maybe he was getting as crazy as Pewee. Steady,
now. Could he remember what was what? Yes, the entry
was full of water.
He crept along, feeling for the last lump of coal or the
niggerhead that Nathan had leaned against the rail. Yes,
there it was. Of course he was not crazy. He felt along
farther for the water. Yes, he must be crazy, after all.
There was no water. He came to a second lump. Of course
he was crazy. Perhaps he was standing in water and didn't
know it. He felt nervously on and on. He found another
lump.
"What is this?" He kicked about with his right foot to see
if he were not already in water, and tumbled over in the
process. No, he was not in water. It didn't splash when
he went down.
His weary heart began to thump. He went a few feet
farther. No water.
Suddenly his mind was aflame. He saw what had hap-
pened. The water had not only stopped coming in; it had
gone. The rescuers had come so close on the outside that
the long reservoir of water had squeezed a passage through
the side of the cave-in for itself. That meant not only no
certain death, but certain life.
He called. His voice was broken and nervous and fright-
ening. But he called again. The sound was smothered in
the entry. He ran and stumbled and crawled a part of the
way back.
"Hey! Hey, in there!" he shrieked like an emaciated
demon. He heard some far-off responses.
"For God's sake, come on! The water's gone!"
172
THE FIREMAKERS
Nathan's head was still clear enough to see what had hap-
pened. "We're saved, boys;" he shouted, "if the water's
gone! Do you hear? We're saved!"
Hi and Sam started crawling and trying to run. But
Pewee was dazed.
"We're saved, Pewee!" Nathan shouted close to his ear
in an effort to break through to his comprehension. "Come
on! Hurry! They're waiting for us!"
Pewee burst into sobs and laughter. Nathan was not
sure whether he was insane or only hysterical.
"We're saved, I tell you!" he shouted again. "Hi and
Sam and Luke have gone. We must hurry and catch up."

heard.

Luke was at the cave-in by the time the others were well

"They're out there," he called, as the others came up-
first Sam and Hi, and then, much later, Nathan and Pewee.
They could hear the men yelling and working outside,
and they could see stray shafts of light along one side of the
entry. Then a good-sized rock tumbled down right in front
of them, and a round hole of yellow lamplight dazzled their
eyes.
THE FIREMAKERS
173
"Are you all there?" somebody called through the hole.
"All here, and all well," Luke answered.
"Better cover your eyes against the lights," the same
voice called from the other side.
Soon more slate and rock rolled down and somebody on
the other side said, "I believe one of us could squeeze
through there."
A moment later a man with a lamp in his cap squeezed
through. His red face was ablaze with a great smile and
his big mustache stretched almost to his muddy ears.
"Well, God damn your souls!" he said in the gentlest
voice he had ever commanded. "I'm mighty glad to see
you all. Did you think we was never a-comin'?"

PART TWO

I
CHAPTER I
T was the aftermath of a victorious war. First came
unrestrained jubilation. Had not everyone in Green
Valley helped to turn imminent defeat into glorious
triumph?
Quickly followed days of competition. There was not
enough glory in having participated along with others.
Some had to lay claim to especially significant participation.
And who had actually done most? The ramifications of
that question were countless. If some woman had not made
exceptional coffee so that some man was moved to go in
and relieve Jab Strohl just when Jab was to be needed to
make a speech outside, the men might be in there yet. And
who now was able to make the tastiest delicacies for bring-
ing the men's stomachs and nerves back to normal so that
the battle with coal could go on? Women who conjured
recipes from unused corners of their memories only with
the utmost difficulty, devised precious dainties which they
themselves had long ago forgotten the taste of, and hurried
with them across back yards to the wives of the men whose
stomachs were not yet equal to Green Valley fare. And
still other events contributed to the competitive spirit. Mrs.
Charley Ferris, who, despite the fact that her husband was
mine boss, had felt subordinated because her husband had
not been entombed in the hill, enlarged for the twentieth
time on how her own father had once been in a cave-in for
three and a half more days than Nathan and Luke and Hi
and Sam and Pewee. Of course it was enough to have to
be shut in at all. But Nathan and all the others had been
177
178
THE FIREMAKERS
able to walk-or at least crawl-and her father had to be
carried out on a stretcher.
Then came the full discussion of incidentals. Mrs. Pe-
wee Dunkle told, for instance, what she overheard Winnie
Dabney say when the rescuers made the break-through. A
day or two before, Winnie had arrived at the mine from
Centerville, attired in subdued garments and otherwise pre-
pared to mourn appropriately over the fate of her husband
and the future of her children. Had the father of these
children put aside enough money to make them and his
widow comfortable? But when the news was quickly re-
layed out that the men were safe, Mrs. Dunkle averred that
Winnie turned aside with her hand to her brow and ex-
claimed, "Oh, my God, they're all alive!" So Mrs. Dunkle
averred.
On the heels of this period of repetitious discussion came
emotional weariness. Nobody knew anything except that
he did not want to discuss the cave-in. Yet what else was
there worth discussing? For the period of the cave-in the
outlines of their lives had been sharp, their objectives defi-
nite. Now everything was glimmery and confusing. All
they could discern clearly was that from the last day of the
cave-in, existence had been only a steep declivity.
A more definite revulsion of feeling set in. Men began to
ask each other whether they might not reasonably expect
similar disasters from time to time unless precautionary
measures were taken. This feeling was accentuated when
the Company began to repair the entry where the cave-in
had taken place, instead of making a new opening farther to
the westward beyond the little stream. That was where
the mouth of the mine should have been in the first place.
Another wet season might come some day and the whole
hillside slip off its well-soaked, sloping clay base and twist
the main entry into nothing at all. But the Company pro-
THE FIREMAKERS
179
ceeded with the installation of timbers designed to resist
any pressure that might be concentrated upon them.
This mood was accentuated further, too, when the res-
cuers received a response to the bill they had presented to
the Company for clearing the entry. Eli Raynor had made
public declaration that every man would receive his full
due. But now Eli declared that the bill for services had
been padded. Too many men had worked too many hours.
It seemed that they had taken out no time for sleep! The
Company would have to strike an average. The Company
wished to be fair.
So what were the men going to do about it?
They sat together, a dozen of them, on the oak-plank
steps of the Hand-out and discussed the matter.
"I favor a strike," Pewee Dunkle declared, and rose to
his feet and stepped about in order to be impressive.
"But how can you prove," somebody at the other end of
the steps asked, "that the bill was not padded?"
"But it was not!" Pewee replied with hotness. "You've
said yourselves that you all had to work without sleep."
"Course I have. I'm not saying it was padded. I just
say it would be mighty hard to prove anything."
"I'm in favor of a strike anyhow," Pewee answered con-
tentiously. "I'm in favor of a strike for better workin' con-
ditions."
He spoke fortunately. Every man was in that belligerent
state where a lucky phrase is all that is required to bring
union.
"Now you've said it!" Al Painter declared with feeling.
"And I guess you ought to know if anybody does."
Pewee experienced an exaltation he had yearned for all
his life. He had been accepted by his fellows. They had
always thought of him as nervous, over-suspicious of roofs,
and rabbit-headed in general. But now they were to see
him in his true light. Had he not told everybody about
180
THE FIREMAKERS
the bad roof over the entry which in time caught Theron
Dabney? And had he himself not suffered from a roof
which he suspected? True, he had not suffered altogether
stoically. Hi Crumblett had told one or two intimates that
if they had been left in the mine another half-day, Pewee
would have been as crazy as a bedbug. As it was, he had
"a few bats in his belfry" before Luke discovered that the
water had left the mine. Old Nate had had a hard enough
time making him understand that they all were saved. But
the nervous extremity to which he had been forced by hun-
ger and darkness now only enhanced the wisdom of his ut-
terance. He had been "through it." He knew what he was
talking about.
There was a long silence of approval. The men smoked
or chewed away in the peace of complete understanding.
Then somebody's mind ran ahead a few steps: "Do you
think we'll get better conditions if we do strike?"
"We're sure not to get 'em if we don't," Al retorted with
pleasant warmth. "Did you ever know Bogart to give any-
thing to people who didn't ask for it?"
"Did you ever know him to give anything to anybody
who did?" the voice at the other end of the steps shot back
at him. Everybody laughed.
The discussion did not become very definite. But the
next evening after supper the men strolled toward the steps
with easy promptitude. And there were more of them.
How about Old Nate? Could anybody induce him to
take an active part in the new war? He ought to be ready.
He had been squeezed a little himself just because the Com-
pany had not provided him with props.
And how about Luke? He, too, had suffered, had he not,
from the wrong kind of working conditions?
Later Luke came, and they put the question directly to
him. He felt as the rest felt. But whether he could afford
to be out of work long was a question. "I've got a pretty
THE FIREMAKERS
181
expensive family," he explained, in order that his position
might be unmistakably clear. The men who sat on the
steps below with their backs to him smiled without turning
their heads.
They needed Luke. He read more than the rest of them,
or thought more, or held some such advantage. They were
not quite certain what it was, but it showed even in his
posture as he sat on the steps. He had his eyes open. He
would not be afraid to ask Bogart questions. They must
convince him that his family could get along with less money
-for the duration of the strike.
When Luke went to the Valley House long after the
stars of early summer were bright in the sky, he walked
alone in meditation. Did he wish to strike? Yes, that was
just what he wished to do. He was ready to tear into some-
thing with all his might. But all his savings would then
go to support Winnie-if she chanced along and then
where would the pottery be, and the blue china?
The next day when he stopped at the post office, the post-
master pushed a long envelope through to him. It was im-
posing. Whatever it was, it did not invite public scrutiny.
He doubled it once, slipped it into his shirt pocket, and
walked to the Valley House and up to his room. He cut
the envelope open. The letter was official-looking.
He read it through a half-dozen times. Then he picked
up the envelope to make certain that there had been no er-
ror. Then he read the letter through again. Yes, it evi-
dently was for him. But could it be true? Did things hap-
pen along so casually, and at the right time?
That evening he walked briskly, even jauntily, up to the
Hand-out. He went through the store, which smelled of
roasting coffee, and came out on the platform and sat down
on the top step. Many were there, but thus far nobody had
been moved to say anything in particular. Luke waited a
182
THE FIREMAKERS
moment, and then without preliminaries declared: "Well,
I'm ready for the strike."
Pipes came out of mouths and then all the faces turned
toward the man whose voice they recognized without look-
ing.
"Ain't your family just as expensive as it was yesterday?"
Hi Crumblett asked in good-natured banter. Somebody had
to say something, and Hi, comrade of Luke in their great
extremity, felt equal to taking liberties with him.
"Won't be, very long," Luke replied.
He had never discussed his domestic concerns with his
fellows. But his relief on this occasion was so great that he
could not resist his inclination to be communicative. "My
wife is suing me for divorce," he said. "I received notice
from the court to-day."
"And you're goin' to let her have it?" Hi asked in mock
skepticism.
There was a roar of laughter which seemed to question
Hi's sanity.
They all smoked a puff or two. "What's her grounds?"
Al Painter asked, a little timorously, but with a sly intona-
tion which suggested that Winnie might have named a co-
respondent.
"Gross cruelty and non-support," Luke answered, a little
humor in his own voice.
Jab Strohl leaped from the lower steps, threw both arms
wildly in the air and gave a yell that rent the valley.
"Hell's fightin' cats! What a joke!"
The strike was obscured. They spent the evening talking
about divorces and reasons for divorce. They brought
forth many specific instances of wives in embarrassing posi-
tions, and of hard-working husbands who found them out.
Marriage, the general opinion seemed to be, was almost as
risky as going to Kansas.
When Luke walked back to the Valley House, his exuber-
THE FIREMAKERS
183
ance was gone. He wished he had said nothing in such pub-
lic fashion about Winnie. Still he was glad too glad to
sleep.
The following afternoon he received a letter from an en-
terprising young attorney in Centerville who wished simply
to call Luke's attention to the fact that he was especially
fitted to handle his case for him. This attorney had found,
on looking into the matter quite unofficially, that "said wife"
was prepared to bring forward a witness in the person of a
traveling salesman of good repute who had, when a guest at
the Valley House at divers times, heard her cry out in the
agony of distress and pain. This was long before "said
husband" had driven her from her own house and children
to live elsewhere.
Luke said nothing about this letter. But the next morn-
ing, when he learned that there was to be no work, he hur-
ried seven miles across the hills to spend the day with the
old potter. The potter told him that if he was quick to
learn, as he looked to be, he could master the art of turning
plain ware in a few months. As for himself, he was get-
ting old, his daughter out in Kansas wanted him and his
wife to come out and live the rest of their days on the
prairie, and if he could sell his pot-shop even for a song, he
supposed they would go. Luke rolled up his sleeves, put
on an extra apron which the old man found, and had his
first lesson in grinding a mill of clay, carrying in the heavy,
impressionable blocks, weighing them out into somewhat
modeled "bats," batting these into nicely shaped balls free
of air pockets, and putting them in a pile on the corner of
the wheel table within reach of the potter. It was going to
be easy, he could tell that already. He liked the feel of the
smooth, cool clay. It yielded to his touch.
Late in the evening when he came back to Green Valley,
he saw lights in the Knights of Labor Hall. He went up.
The hall was full of men. They had spent the idle day
184
THE FIREMAKERS
talking each other into excitement over the proposed strike.
Luke waited at the door while Hi Crumblett finished his
speech. Hi talked about "the rights of man" and "danger-
ous, intolerable conditions" with a fervor that stirred every-
one, including Luke.
After the cheering was over, Luke tiptoed halfway down
the aisle to an unoccupied seat. There was more cheering.
The chairman cheered with the others and then remarked
that it ought to be clear to everyone that the meeting was
ready to hear from their fellow worker, Luke Dabney.
Luke told the assemblage in brief sentences how he felt
about the strike. He was thinking a little of moving away-
there was, he thought, at least one chance in ten-but he
favored the strike if the Company refused to grant the bet-
ter working conditions. "There are too many men getting
killed in these hills!" he declared in closing.
It was so voted. If the Company did not take necessary
measures, there would be a strike. Two days later they
would meet and fix the time for going on strike, and discuss
ways and means of bringing the contest to early, successful
issue.
In the two intervening days, Pewee had opportunity to
reflect upon what he had done. He had proposed a meas-
ure-in fact, he had originated it-and here it was, already
in process of being carried through. Nothing in his becom-
ing an authority on disaster, nothing in his helping to carry
Nathan Boyd home or in his helping to dig Theron Dabney
from a fall of slate which he had repeatedly predicted, had
given him such a feeling of importance. If he could pro-
pose one thing and forthwith get it, why could he not pro-
pose other things and get them? Hadn't he been through
something himself? Wouldn't they have to hear him if
he stood up? He was filled with a sense of destiny.
So when the men assembled to fix the date of the strike,
Pewee had something to say. "The Company sells its coal
THE FIREMAKERS
185
for big money, and we get damned little of it. Why not get
more? Why not ask for more pay while we're about it?"
What did Luke think about that, they wanted to know.
Luke was ready to say. He thought it was not the right
time to ask for the extra pay. "We've just been dug out
of the hill-some of us and if we demand better working
conditions, everybody will think of the cave-in and suppose
that we must be right. But if we tack on a demand for
better pay, the Company will make out to everybody that
we're just out to grab as much as we can get."
"Well, ain't we?" Pewee demanded. "I won't refuse a
little more."
Others reasoned as casually as Pewee. "If we aim to ask
for more money sooner or later," Al Painter argued, "why
not get it now and have the good of it?"
Fixing the date of the strike was forgotten. For an hour
there were speeches and half-serious jibes and disorderly
repartee. Then Pewee made his final appeal. He had
something up his sleeve that he was ready to reveal. Wasn't
the Company getting big money for its coal? He had not
been asleep. He was on the way to have evidence. They
would see. He had written on waxed paper, and put the
crumpled bits in with his coal as he loaded it into the car.
He had asked that the users of the coal, when they found
his crumpled letter, write back and tell him how much a
ton of coal cost them. A load of his coal would be dumped
into pretty nearly every flat-car. It would go everywhere.
Maybe he would get replies from six or eight different
states. He would show how the Company gulled them.
And he could use some of the excess himself. "After what
I've been through," he concluded in a tone of martyrdom,
"I need every red cent I can dig up. If Luke is going to
quit us anyhow, maybe it won't matter to him, but it means
a hell of a lot to me. That's the way I feel about it."
"That's the way we feel, too," the others said or at
186
THE FIREMAKERS
least a majority of them. Before they escaped from their
moment of over-pitched sentimentality, they voted to ask
for more pay as well as for better working conditions.
"What do you say to that, Luke?" somebody called; and
then half the assembly and more caught up the question:
"Yes, what do you say to that, eh?"
"I say you made the wrong jump, that's all," Luke an-
swered with a little hotness.
They were sobered for a second or two. Luke by right
of originality had led the minority, and now that there was
to be a strike, the others suddenly wondered if he would
bolt. "Oh, I'll not turn yellow dog," he assured Pewee and
his followers, at once divining the question in their minds.
"Go ahead; I'll support you. But I'm just afraid, boys, we
can't win both at the same time."
Luke had a strange feeling that he was no longer one of
the group. He wanted to go his individual way. These
men were only a unit in the scheme of things. True, they
were arrayed against the Company; yet they were only an
element in an order of which the Company was the other
part. They and the Company could not get along without
each other, yet it was the business of each to fight the other
into submission. It was war. You might be on one side,
you might be on the other, but you were a part of the
struggle. That you could not escape. The war provided
you with your outlook, and your outlook was always on
more war, immediate or remote. If you tried to think for
yourself, no matter which side you were on, you were out-
lawed. A man had no right to make subtle distinctions.
They might lead him to believe that there was a degree of
merit-or a degree of error-on both sides.
Luke sat and looked into the rung of the chair in front
of him and pressed his foot self-consciously against it. He
knew that in the hubbub of remarks to neighbors, and
laughing rejoinders, and exuberant whistlings that followed
THE FIREMAKERS
187
the seconds of soberness, he was being eyed by all his fel-
lows. They, too, knew that he was not one of them. Even
if he did work in a coal mine-or had coal had not sub-
dued him. His way of looking at the world was not theirs.
He felt hot and tingling. He was alive with a vitality
which in itself was pleasant. That enabled him to look
about without shrinking. That filled him with hope. He
would kick free. Yet he seemed to be on the inside of a
rubber ball. Whenever he extended himself in a given
direction, something always pressed him back into a state
of non-extension.
Here that very thing was happening in this meeting. He
was not afraid. He could stand up and let himself out-
tell them to go to hell. He might have to work in the mine
himself till next spring. Then he would be through. But
he wished them well-he always would. So why wrangle
with them over anything?
The meeting resumed the discussion of the date for the
strike. The majority favored late summer. Maybe they
could save up a little money, if there were any orders for
coal in the summer; and certainly they could bring together
supplies of food. They and their wives could pick rasp-
berries and blackberries, and they could can extra bushels
of peaches. There was still time to plant cabbage and
tomatoes and turnips. In these vegetables they would have
much substantial provender right at the time when it would
be required.
Some favored September 1. "If he don't give in at
once," the proponents of this date argued, "he will lose all
his fall contracts." But the proponents of August I said
that that was just the trouble. Bogart never gave in to
anybody at once. He would lose the contracts, and then
they would all be without work for the winter. Bogart
could winter if the mine should not run one ton of coal,
but they could not.
188
THE FIREMAKERS
In the end August 15 was fixed upon as a compromise
date. It would be late enough to have all of the advantages
of September 1, but early enough to give Bogart time to
think things over before his customers decided to buy coal
from some other operator.
Pewee was jubilant-jubilant over the thought of a strike,
over the demands to be made upon the Company, over
everything. "You'll see," he declared, feeling himself more
or less lifted from the ground as he walked homeward;
"when I commence to get answers to my glazed paper.
You'll see. Why, I'd not be surprised if some people pay
ten dollars a ton for this coal we mine for seventy cents.
You'll see when I get my first letter."
Pewee waited long for his first letter. Everybody begged
to know what news he had received, and everybody laughed.
"All right," he said, "you'll see."
After Pewee himself had almost despaired, the postmas-
ter told him one day that he had a letter for him. Pewee
knew it was from a consumer of his coal. There was no
occasion for anyone else to write to him.
He was filled with a sense of triumph. "Well, here she is,
boys," he announced to the half-dozen or more in the post
office. "We'll all see together."
He tore open the envelope. The letter inside consisted of
a badly crumpled piece of waxed paper pinned to a sheet of
foolscap. Pewee recognized his own stiff handwriting on the
waxed paper: "How much do you pay for this ton of coal?
I get seventy cents for mining it. J. W. Dunkle, Green Val-
ley, Ohio." On the other sheet was written: "You get too
damned much. Your coal is full of niggerheads. Edward
Burrell, Lawrence, Kansas." Below was a postscript longer
than the reply itself. "Why don't you quit rooting around
in the black and dirt and come out here to God's country
and farm?"
The others burst into loud guffaws. They slapped Pewee
THE FIREMAKERS
189
on the back. They jabbed him in the ribs with their
knuckles. They looked into one another's faces and then
laughed some more.
Pewee persisted in looking straight ahead at the letter.
He had to think his way out of his predicament. When no
brilliant inspiration came, he declared with weak bravado
that, as a matter of fact, the reply was just what he had
expected.
When the next one came he was more discreet. He
slipped the unopened envelope into his pocket and walked
homeward along the path to Company Row. On the sedge-
covered bank above the railroad track he sat down and drew
the envelope from his pocket. When he saw that he was
quite certainly not being observed by anybody near, he
opened it and read:
"What are you kicking about? You get as much pay as
I do, and you work in a nice, cool mine. I have to shovel
your dirty coal into a red-hot furnace."
Pewee tore the letter into fine bits which no one could pos-
sibly piece together and dropped them into a hole against a
fence post that leaned limply over the edge of the railroad
company's right-of-way. Nobody would hear about that
letter!
He received one other. It came from up in Michigan. “I
paid twelve dollars for one ton of your fireproof coal to run
my engine with, and I had a hard enough time to get it at
that. It's not much good, but why in God's name can't you
work faster?"
Ah! This was the one to show to Luke. But when Luke
saw it he was not in the least excited. "You miss the point,
Pewee," was his serious word of comment. "Nobody in
Green Valley that I know of thinks we get enough money.
I just don't believe we ought to ask for everything at once."
Through the hot weeks of early midsummer there were
more idle days in the mine than in most summers. Luke
190
THE FIREMAKERS
spent every one of them at the pottery, where the old man
declared that he had never seen anyone who had a greater
knack with clay. Luke walked out of an old world when-
ever he climbed the hill above Green Valley and made his
way northward to the little pot-shop. He walked regretfully
out of a new clean world, whenever he washed the fresh-
smelling clay from his hands and turned homeward. He
dreaded to see the desolate hillsides, the weeds along the
edges of every road and path, the deep yellow gutter that
was the street in front of Company Row.
But as August 15 drew nearer, he came back from his new
world with less regret. He felt a new spirit abroad in Green
Valley. The strike was coming. Concerted action was
bringing evidence of life. Here was something to fight for,
to put oneself into. Here was grim romance. It was a
showdown. Maybe they might win.
They must be prepared. Wives cautioned their children
not to waste things. They must learn to eat the crusts of
their bread cleaner. A little burned spot on a nice piece of
bread wasn't going to hurt anybody.
They might be glad enough to get as much as a crust
before it was over. Wives slipped dimes away and said
nothing to their husbands. Husbands folded an occasional
dollar bill into a neat rectangle and pushed it into the watch-
pocket of their trousers without mentioning the fact to their
wives. Some of them dropped from a ten-cent package of
tobacco to a five-cent one. They faced a great event.
Being at the heart of something momentous, that was
what made a man somebody. Here they were, about to do
something that would affect men living a hundred or five
hundred miles away. Won't somebody find out how much
fun it is to want coal-aye, maybe to need coal-and not be
able to get it? A man in Detroit in a big office with rugs
on the floor will be saying: "Why can't they settle their dif-
ferences down there in Green Valley so we won't have to
THE FIREMAKERS
191
close down?" A man in Chicago will send a telegram to
Bogart and say, "What has become of those three carloads
you promised me for last week?" And they will say to
Bogart-Bogart who boasted that nobody told him what to
do-"Make the mines safer, and pay us more money, and
we'll work our heads off."
A week before the date set for the strike, a strange rumor
made its way about. There was to be no strike, after all.
Bogart was going to hire a mine expert who would safeguard
miners' lives; and he was going to increase pay ten cents a
ton. The rumor could not be traced to a definite source, but
it persisted. "They say" became the fixed preliminary to
all sorts of versions of the report.
Pewee was disappointed. He had prepared himself for a
strike, and now he wanted it to come. In fact, he thought
he would strike, anyhow. When they were working in a
nice, safe mine and getting extra pay, he meant to be in a
position to say that he was the first man-perchance the
only man-who went on strike in demand of the improved
situation. August 15 came near midweek that year and on
the Friday preceding, Pewee did not go to the mine, although
there happened to be work on that day for everybody.
"What's the use of going in just for three or four days?" he
asked unanswerably. "I'm going to weed my turnips, and
tie my tomatoes to their stakes, and kill the worms on the
little heads of cabbage."
Luke did not view the matter simply as warfare. He
thought he saw clearly. For him, as well as for the other
residents of Green Valley, life was in focus again. But he
saw in a different direction. If the old man back in the hills
meant all he said when he expressed willingness to sell his
pot-shop for a song, Luke knew where he could use three or
four days' pay, whether there was to be a strike or not. He
had an adventure of his own in prospect, hadn't he? Per-
haps not one other human being on the face of the earth was
192
THE FIREMAKERS
in the least concerned whether he kept to his feet until he
got somewhere, or went down for good along the way. But
late at night while Green Valley slept he could stand out in
the quiet and see nothing in the entire sweep of the universe
that compared in importance with his own destiny.

CHAPTER II
HE evening of the fourteenth of August came with-
out news from Bogart. Evidently he meant to try
some new tactics. He was going to keep them
waiting. Instead of exhibiting strength, he meant to be cun-
ning.
Men came from their houses early that evening. They
were in their shirt-sleeves collarless, but with bright-
colored sleeve-holders decorating their muscular arms.
They smoked their pipes with quickened rhythm as they
strolled to the post office and the Hand-out.
What was this? A large placard on each of the Hand-
out's two heavy doors. They read those who were quali-
fied to do so and looked at one another in bewilderment.
The Company Store, the placard said, would not open on
the morning of August 15, and it would remain closed ac-
cording to circumstances.
The news spread quickly. The boys hurried to the Hand-
out from wherever they were and tested Pomp's and Val's
pedagogical methods by trying to read what the placard
said. The women, too, as soon as they could put the supper
dishes aside and run a "reddin' comb" through their hair a
little, came down and joined the assembly. They read the
placard-or listened while some one else read-expressed
their unrestrained contempt for the Company Store's goods,
talked in comradely fashion among themselves, and carried
on mock philanderings with one another's husbands. What
did they care if the Hand-out did close? They couldn't get
much there, anyhow, and they had to pay more for what
they got than anywhere else. They would go to Oakdale-
193
194
THE FIREMAKERS
or send their husbands and buy groceries there. Or why
could not Joe Goodland, the Green Valley postmaster, put
in a stock of goods and supply their needs? He kept miners'
caps, as it was, and plug tobacco, brogan shoes, and peanuts.
Why shouldn't he stock up? They moved about and talked
cheerily. It was going to be a lively strike.
They were out again early the next morning. They were
eager to experience a new war against something or some-
body; or to learn that they had already won. Many of the
men still hoped for a message of capitulation from Bogart.
They walked from the Hand-out to the post office; from the
post office to the shoe shop; from the shoe shop to the sta-
tion-hoping vaguely that they might learn that such a mes-
sage had arrived. But before noon they wilted in the hot
August sun, and their hopes languished with their bodies.
They were fools, they admitted, ever to have expected
Bogart to yield without a fight.
They settled down to the solid business of playing poker
and predicting how long they would have to keep Bogart
"on the hummer." They played in the sedgy field above
the railroad, under three or four battered apple trees that
marked the spot where a thriving orchard had once covered
the hillside. As the sun moved westward, they moved
northward and eastward with the shade. Sometimes they
did not eat at noon. But at four-thirty they ate supper and
then drifted toward their favorite loafing spots down town.
As the days dragged heavily by, their evening discussions
turned more and more toward food. Always the Civil War
veterans had the final hearing in these discussions, for they
could tell, without fear of contradiction, how much hungrier
they were in the war than anybody could ever have a chance
to be in a strike. Were they not at the moment surrounded
by farmers only a few miles away who produced fair crops?
In case of dire need, these necessities of life could be had
for the asking-or for the taking-whereas in the war some-
THE FIREMAKERS
195
body else had usually been along first and taken everything
that was worth troubling with.
One evening they fell to discussing the prize meals of their
lives. Jonas Kerwin, who had never mastered the intrica-
cies of gaining information from a printed page, said that
his prize meal was one that he once enjoyed at the Jefferson
Hotel in Centerville. He and Sam Swisher wandered in
there one day just to see what it was like. The negro waiter
handed him a large bill of fare. Not to be humiliated, he
said to the waiter quite casually, "We want the whole
damned geester."
Here was illuminating information, for no one else in the
group had ever felt financially competent to look inside the
dining-room at the Jefferson.
"It cost us nine dollars apiece, but it was a great old meal.
We had six kinds of drinks, every one of them in a different
kind of tumbler. And the waiter had plenty more in a
bucket of ice on the floor by the table leg."
Their exclamations were cut short by the appearance of a
figure who trudged along the road eastward. They watched
in silence. It was Lycurgus Ott. His white head was bare
and dusty, and in his left hand he grasped his worn old hat
and his staff-like cane. "Gentlemen!" he said loftily, and
with a sweeping gesture, but without pausing on his way.
They watched him until he was out of hearing down the
road. Just when it seemed that conversation was to be re-
sumed without reference to him, Jonas observed sagely,
"Old Lycurgus was never known to stop where grub was
scarce."
Jonas had prevented speech by Joe Goodland. Joe was
on the point of asking Nathan Boyd a question. "I just
wanted to know what your prize meal was, Mr. Boyd."
Meals were coming to be inviting subjects to linger upon.
"Oh, mine?" Nathan asked modestly, as though he were
no part of the group of heroic eaters. "Why, that wouldn't
196
THE FIREMAKERS
be hard to answer. It was in the Virginia campaigns. I was
on picket duty one night and a dozen Johnnies swooped
down upon me and carried me off. Put me in a log jail. I
got away later, but I had a hard time on my way back to the
lines. I had to keep under cover, and I got hungry. When
it was half light one morning and I was still traveling, I hap-
pened on some middlings that had spilled out of a hole in
some farmer's mill-sack. He had stopped on the way home
from the mill to water his horses, and the cowfeed had run
out of the sack while the horses stood at the trough. I
mixed up some middlings and water and had breakfast.
That was pretty good, I can tell you."
Before the end of August all stories of extravagant meals
had been repeated so many times that there was no nourish-
ment left in them. The atmosphere of the Valley became
drowsier and drowsier. It was a question of time. There
was too much time. Nobody knew how to put in the day.
The proprietor of the Last Chance, just across the river, wel-
comed all who wished to make the hours pass swiftly, but
the Last Chance was conducted strictly on a cash and carry
basis, and cash was already becoming hard to find.
Hi Crumblett wondered if this were not the time to intro-
duce the gospel of Karl Marx. One morning he determined
to find out. He ate a thin breakfast, and walked along the
yellow, dusty path which served as a sidewalk for Company
Row, intent upon whittling a chew of tobacco from his
dwindling plug. He almost ran into a stranger.
"Good morning," said the stranger. There was some-
thing authoritative in his manner. "Are you a tenant in one
of these Company houses?"
Hi explained that he was; that he had been ever since the
houses were built.
"Well, I'm the sheriff. I've been sent over here to vacate
all these houses. You are hereby notified that you are to be
out by next month-that is, day after to-morrow."
1
THE FIREMAKERS
197
For a moment Hi was speechless. When he recovered his

"Well, I've got nothing to do with that," the sheriff an-

Hi was no small man, but the sheriff towered above him,
tawny, muscular, businesslike. "I'm to notify everybody
who lives in a Company house."
In the presence of such a show of authority, Hi felt
abashed and inadequate. "All right," he said at last, not
knowing what else to say. "I live in the third house up the
way. You won't need to stop there."
Going to be kicked out! Day after to-morrow! Hi
walked on as if he were an alien in some strange land. But
at the post office he encountered some of his kind. Immedi-
ately he felt more at home, although Karl Marx had passed
out of his mind utterly. "Say," he announced, "the sheriff's
here. He's goin' to put us all out on the street. We've got
to clear out before next month. He's already served notice
on me."
In an instant the drowsiness disappeared. Old Bogart,
the invisible enemy, had revealed his presence, had he?
But could he put them out? "Of course not," said some.
"Of course he can if he wants to," said others. "They're
his houses, ain't they?"
"But he has to serve notice or something, don't he?"
"Of course he does," Hi answered. "Didn't I say I'd had
mine-from the sheriff? He's up there. Go and talk to
him."
"But what will become of us?" Jonas Kerwin wanted to
know. "Leaves a man in a hell of a fix to be kicked out of
house and home with no roof over his head."
Joe Goodland, postmaster: what did he think about it?
Joe thought he would not be surprised if it was all bluff.
"Of course I can't say what the county sheriff will do. I

only represent the United States of America. But it all

Other residents of the Row came in. With the mounting

Jonas Kerwin, who from the first had shown belligerency,

When they reached Company Row the sheriff was already

"How about this," Jonas asked, "are we to be kicked

The question provided the sheriff with the advantage he

In the immediate presence of the law they were over-
awed. The sheriff looked them straight in the eyes. "Bet-
ter move out to-morrow, boys, and save trouble."
He walked on importantly toward the little railway sta-

sophisticated grown-ups.
After the train had come and borne him out of sight they

menacing bluff on the part of the Company.
"I'm in favor of findin' out," Hi Crumblett declared, his

"Now you're beginning to talk sense," Jonas Kerwin de-

The women, some with their morning brooms in hand,
THE FIREMAKERS
199
some with their checked aprons held alongside their heads

They gave one another strength. And when the men

follow Hi's suggestion, and just wait and see?
There were no houses to move to anyhow. Where could
thirty families go when they had only a day or two to do it

After a night of sleep the men felt all the more secure in

their places, that was what they wanted to know?
"I'd rather not say what I'd do," Luke replied cryptically.

"Yes, if we lived in our own," broke in Jonas Kerwin, "we

Evidently that was about what Luke had in mind. In

They demonstrated what ten men can do in fifteen min-

Hounds shied at so many strangers, and snarled and

Some of the women wept. Some of them raged and called

THE FIREMAKERS
203
kitchen stove, she "accidentally" tumbled a chair over in his

By evening the deputies, smudged and sweaty, had done
their work. There was a padlock on the front door of every
house; and along the yellow ditch that served as a street
were as many piles as there were families. Kitchen tables-
the only kind of table known in Company Row-kitchen
stoves, yellow oak bedsteads, pans, washtubs, boilers, cans
of blackberries, paper sacks of flour, stove pipes, coal
buckets, and crayon enlargements of stern grandfathers and
patient grandmothers, were piled in heaps in the edge of the
dusty thoroughfare.
As soon as the miners who lived in the Row saw the army
of deputies, they abandoned the hope that the threat of evic-
tion was a bluff, and began to scout for places to sleep that
night. It was just as well that they were away. Their bel-
ligerency had increased to the point where a word would
have started a fight. A look might have done so.
As the sheriff walked along the Row toward the railroad

get your garden truck off, too."
"But we can't get the garden stuff off yet," Mrs. Dunkle

nothin' of ripe, and the turnips and late cabbages are just
good and started."
"They'll have to be off to-day," was the sheriff's reply.
"The Company will want to be using its property and then

The other residents of the Row squeezed in wherever they

They talked long about the misfortune which had befallen
them. Then the men made their way to the Knights of

Up there next the roof where the sun had done its worst
all day, it was as hot as an oven. The first arrivals threw
open the windows and leaned out to inhale breaths a little
cooler than the air inside the hall. Nothing in the atmos-
phere was designed to soothe men's troubled spirits.
The chairman tried to begin with language a bit more
formal than the easy speech prevalent among the citizens of
the Valley region. "What shall we do, fellow miners," he
asked when he had finally induced the sweating men to come
to order, "about our pigs and garden truck? You all know
what the sheriff told the women."
They were of one mind. They meant to let their truck
grow. More than that, they meant to go and get whatever
they chose. "The rights of man are being trampled on,"
declared Hi Crumblett. "I mean to leave my truck where
it is, and whenever I want anything, I mean to stick a six-
shooter in my hip pocket and go and get it!"
There were shouts of approval and prolonged stampings
of feet.
"Better not say anything you'll have to take back," Na-
than Boyd counseled.

205
Maybe it would be just as well. They couldn't dig their

"Yes, we let things run their course to see what would

"And now we know. It's up there in the road."
Their furniture, yes. It ought to be moved. But just
