Nobody knew. Wise heads looked into the west where
the sun still made a dull outline of horizon discernible, and
concurred in the belief that it would be quite safe to leave
the furniture where it was. It was an easy conclusion to
reach, since there was nothing else to do.
That night every log house in Green Valley had two or
three times its usual number of occupants. The women
slept in the beds, three or four women crosswise in each bed.
The men and the children slept in rows on the floor. Mos-
quitoes came in throughout the night at the open doors and
windows and kept everyone fighting blindly in his half-
sleep. In the early morning when light began to come,
armies of flies that had spent the night on whitewashed
ceilings descended upon lips and ears and made even half-
sleep impossible. Some of the men, stiff and languid in their
sweaty clothes, thought they might as well get out of the
way so that the women would have a fair chance. They sat
up on the floor, their hands clasped loosely over their knees,
or they rolled over and rubbed the backs of their heads,
which seemed to be in a state of paralysis.
What was that? They were startled by a crash and a
long explosive roar. They hurried to the doors. A slender,
white summer cloud stood up brightly over their heads in
the oncoming light of the sun. Its neck was dark and
looked as if it might have a few drops of rain in it.
A few drops came-great drops that pounded like hail;
then more of them; and then more until there was a down-
206
THE FIREMAKERS
pour. The cloud seemed not to move. It hung in the bright
morning heavens and spilled its cleansing waters upon Green
Valley.
Their
The women were hysterical. Their furniture!
straw bedding! Their sacks of flour! They rushed about.
They asked one another frantically what was to be done.
They called Bogart and the army of sheriffs all the names
they had accumulated in Green Valley in a lifetime.
The downpour increased. Then it eased off just a trifle.
Then it began again-a terrific thumping of great drops, and
then a roar. Suddenly it stopped.
Under the little cloud in the sky they could see the wall
of rain moving eastward.
What had happened to their possessions? They hurried
to see.
The dust of the road had become loblolly. The trees over
their heads beyond the Valley House dripped and spattered
as if it still rained. At the railroad right-of-way every gully
was a-gurgle with thick, muddy water.
Their household effects were all there, yes. But the
water, rushing down the side of the road, had been ob-
structed by the piles. It backed up at each pile until its
weight forced a passage and left remnants of itself wherever
it touched.
That afternoon the men carried and carted the water-
soaked furniture to the Knights of Labor Hall-the best of
the things left. The sheriff happened along to see how well
his orders had been obeyed. "You'd better take your gar-
den truck, too," he said casually to Hi Crumblett, and
walked on toward the railroad station.
CHAPTER III
UKE thought he was getting himself untangled. He
was rid of Winnie, and that was much. And he was
becoming a craftsman in clay. Mr. Cale Sturgis,
back in the hills, marveled whenever he watched Luke at
the wheel.
That was the life! To take a ball of clay, slap it on the
center of the slowly revolving wheel, kick the treadle until
the wheel spun faster and faster, shape the clay roughly
with palm and knuckles, and then bring it into final form
with two or three different kinds of turner's ribs split from
the body of some apple tree-that made a man's day com-
plete. There was no occasion for him to think of Green
Valley while he was thus engaged.
But when he stopped late in the evening-two hours or
more after early supper-he climbed the long ridge to the
house, and stood with his elbows on the smooth top of the
paling fence and pondered. As it grew dark and whippoor-
wills ventured forth to take up their night's railleries, Luke
sometimes felt detached from pottery and wandered in
thought back to Green Valley. The twins, yes, of course.
And the sheriff-had he come back? If he had, had the
Crumbletts moved into the two rooms at the Valley House,
as Luke had told Hi to do? Did Mrs. Canaday find the
twins such a burden as she professed? Were the men going
to win the strike?
Three or four evenings of such meditation brought Green
Valley into higher relief. Five or six such evenings, and
Luke was ready to take a day off and walk over to see what
was happening.
207
208
THE FIREMAKERS
He found the Valley in a clamor of expectancy. Nathan
Boyd had been in Centerville the day before, and an old
comrade who held county office and expected to be a can-
didate for reëlection the next year told him very confiden-
tially that the Company was planning to send a carload of
strike-breakers to the Valley the next afternoon-"Hunkies
or Swedes, or somebody from over there"-and that they
might have a bad effect on the strike. Immediately the
miners had decided upon a course of action. Somebody was
to go to Centerville, find a way of getting into the car with
the foreigners, and convince them that they were acting un-
wisely.
But how? What argument could be used other than
threats of niggerheads and six-shooters? Some were for
practical methods-warfare. Nathan was not. He thought
violence did not often get a man anywhere, and usually did
not leave him even where he was. After many expedients
had been proposed, some one with a sense of humor moved
that Pewee Dunkle be sent over to ride back with the for-
eigners and tell them of the unparalleled danger of working
in these particular hills. The proposal met with immediate
favor. Pewee himself was not altogether convinced that
the motion was an unqualified compliment to him, but he
agreed to undertake the mission.
Pewee was over there now, the men told Luke, and at any
moment he and the Hunkies or Swedes-the distinction was
very vague and inconsequential-might be arriving. Or if
Pewee had been able to get an early start with them, they
might not come at all.
The afternoon passenger train from Centerville came in.
Eli Raynor stepped off, suave and alert. He had come over
to unlock the houses in Company Row.
"Hear you're bringing a lot of Hunkies or something to
start the mine with," the men said to him.
THE FIREMAKERS
209
Eli held his position because of his skill in diplomacy.
"We'd rather have you work for us than anybody we could
think of," he said cautiously. "But of course if you won't,
we've got to find somebody who will. That's fair enough,
I guess."
"I don't know whether it is or not," Hi Crumblett an-
swered. Hi's mind could not seize upon any specific fal-
lacy in Eli's reasoning, yet somehow Eli's conclusion did
not seem to follow.
"Well, I guess it is," Eli replied with an ingratiating smile,
yet with a little of the upper dog in his tone.
He went at once to Company Row and began to unlock
houses. Before he had reached the middle of the Row, a
freight engine whistled in the distance and a dumpy little
train came into view.
As it rolled leisurely down to the station the anxious pop-
ulation of Green Valley could see Pewee in the flat-car
among the men speaking earnestly yet good-naturedly. He
was gesticulating with dramatic movements intended to sug-
gest impending doom. While the engine clanged and
threatened and finally stopped, the leader of the foreigners
conferred with Pewee. Then at a signal, the men began to
clamber over the sides of the car.
"Hello," they said in friendly fashion.
The engine puffed discreetly back in the direction of Cen-
terville. The strike-breakers walked about and smiled, and
soon were exchanging chews of tobacco with the miners.
"We go back," they said. "Too much danger. Pewee tell
us."
That evening they all boarded the Centerville Accommo-
dation and vanished in the twilight.
Pewee was exultant. "I told 'em," he said, "that there
was a peculiar slippery oil in these hills that made them
slide whenever anybody disturbed their insides. I had the
210
THE FIREMAKERS
whole damned township slipping and swaying around on
about a dozen rotten oak props. Told 'em how we was in
the hill like moles in the bottom of a frog pond for ten days,
and that if they escaped a cave-in for a week they'd be
luckier than they could count on."
The Valley was grateful. The children were never quite
ready to forget food, but the grown-ups overlooked slim
fare and cramped lodgings in this unquestioned triumph
that had come to them. They would win every point in just
this way. They would win the strike. They would have
normal life again, and on their own terms. The battle must
go on.
Val opened school. There was no diminution in enroll-
ment. "The kids might as well be in school as anywheres
else,” the women said. They spoke thus in understated
recognition of Val's abilities, and in justification of their
own wish to have what they called a little peace.
Val stood before her pupils as well-dressed, as fasci-
nating, as eager as ever. But a change had come over her
pupils. The boys looked like scarecrows, and the girls like
last season's rag dolls. Whatever they chanced to have on
when the sheriff emptied their houses into the street is what
they wore to school. Books were in much the same state.
Some of the more enterprising had delved into what the
rain had left and brought forth readers with pages water-
soaked and crinkled, or spelling books discolored with
calico or blackberry juice. But most of them had found no
books at all, and seemed not to be troubled about their loss.
Some, too, came without dinner buckets. In the general
chaos, such incidentals were not to be easily located. They
came with a few slices of unbuttered bread and a half head
of cabbage or a boiled potato wrapped in newspaper, and
ate silently, but by taking great assaulting bites as if to
prove their superiority to circumstance.
THE FIREMAKERS
211
The two-room school-no, it had not been forgotten.
And now that Pewee had successfully penetrated the
Hunkies, the Valley found relief in turning away from the
strike and dreaming of something as pleasant and as remote
as a schoolhouse with two rooms and two teachers. Pewee
went so far as to say that if the township trustees did not
want to build it, they could build it themselves. They had
done other things, hadn't they?
On another afternoon when Luke came over from the
hills to see how things were going, he encountered all this
discussion. "When we get Val her two-room schoolhouse,"
everybody was saying as a preliminary to comment on other
great beatitudes which the future undoubtedly held in store.
For a moment envy clutched him. But he was soon free-
somewhat free-again. "I've got my pot-shop in sight," he
said consolingly to himself. "In a year or two I'll be mak-
ing vases and teapots and all sorts of things."
He decided to eat an early supper and return before night.
He must be at his work in the morning. But while he and
the rest ate at Mrs. Canaday's long table, somebody who
had come from Centerville on the belated afternoon train
brought the vague report that another carload of strike-
breakers might be expected that evening. Luke thought he
had better postpone his journey to the hills and see the fun.
The entire Valley-men, women, and children-quickly
assembled at the station. It was the night for the Green
Valley Cornet Band to practice in the K. of L. Hall, and as
members came from the edges of the country and learned
the news, they stuck their instruments under their arms, or
looped their arms through them, and joined the crowd.
Nobody knew just what was going to take place. No-
body knew what was to be attempted this time, either by
the Company or by the miners. They were in the hands
of unprejudiced fate. But it was as well to be present and
watch the mills of the gods turn.
212
THE FIREMAKERS
What if it were all a false alarm, like that story of Bo-
gart's surrender? Yes, that would be a joke, wouldn't it?
They laughed over the possibility and nudged and jostled
each other about on the warped oak platform.
"We'll have a concert if they don't come," some happy
spirit suggested.
He had scarcely made the suggestion when somebody pro-
fessed to hear a whistle. There was silence for a moment,
then tumult again.
"Here she comes!" somebody yelled above the laughter,
the spasmodic toots of the baritone and the tuba, the gen-
eral skylarking.
Sure enough! Far up the track and over a low ridge they
could see a wisp of smoke. They watched impatiently as it
grew larger. Then the train itself came round the curve.
"It's the slow freight!" Bella Kerwin declared. She had
recently become sixteen, and despite the fact that she sported
little raiment of note, she felt blooming, and equal to the

"It's not!" Shelley Crumblett replied.
"Tis too!"

"Tis!"
She gave him a push and a significant pinch meant to

While they waited, they saw Eli Raynor drive up to
Company Row, his horses panting from a speedy trip. At
once he began to unlock houses. He had not risked com-
ing too early this time. Yet on the station platform and
roundabout he saw more people than he had ever seen there
before.
Well, he could do nothing about it. He himself had no
desire to clash with the miners. Probably these men would
THE FIREMAKERS
213
be working for the Company again some day, and he would
have to deal with them. Anyhow, who wanted to get mixed
up with brickbats and niggerheads? He must be only a
disinterested emissary of the Company, engaged solely in
the legitimate business of unlocking Company houses.
As the train rattled slowly down to the station, somebody
in the crowd called, "Turn on the music. Let's give them
a reception."
The scattered parts of the band struck up. But they
struck up different tunes, whereupon the crowd shouted in
merriment, and waved their arms wildly in reception to the
men in the two flat cars.
Clearly the Italians in the cars had not expected such a
noisy welcome. Their faces expressed doubt as they looked
alertly at the crowd. One of their number said something
to another in a spirited, strange voice which the crowd heard
above the pumped-up merriment.
"Dagoes! Br-r-r-r-r-r-ano!" somebody in the rear of the
crowd yelled, and everybody, especially the boys, began to
produce utterances and to make gestures which seemed to
them an imitation of Italian.
The man in the car who had spoken spoke again, more
excitedly. There was a great shout from the platform, and
then more imitations of him. He spoke yet again. The
crowd outvoiced him. Then several other Italians spoke
in the same excited manner. That was just what the crowd
wanted. They outnumbered the men in the cars several
to one. Why be afraid?
So they yelled and shouted, and spoke Italian jeeringly,
and blew the tuba and pounded the bass drum which some-
body had thoughtfully run to bring from the hall, and
pointed the trombone up toward the men in the car and
fired away with ripping, unmelodious sounds.
More of the Italians tried to speak to the leader. The
214
THE FIREMAKERS
more they tried, the more the crowd shouted and laughed
and jeered. Somebody in the fringe of the crowd be-
thought himself of an additional means of noise, and emp-
tied his six-shooter into the ground close to his feet.
In consternation at the shots, the Italians waved their
arms frantically and shouted to their leader.
If six shots could do that much, what might not twelve
do? So two other men in the fringe of the crowd emptied
their six-shooters.
The Italians shouted, not only to their leader, but toward
the engineer who looked from his cab with interest that
revealed no worry. The men closest to the leader were
striking downward with choppy, emphatic gestures. At last
he turned from his position and signaled the engineer to
start back.
The bell clanged, the little engine wasted enough steam
to move the two cars, and then moved slowly away with its
freight. The crowd shouted louder than ever, blew their
horns, beat the drum, and the men with six-shooters fired
them.
So that was done! What else could they not do if only
they used their resources?
Luke thought he had better hurry away. He wanted to
cover the seven miles before the night was too old. As it
was, it would be dark before he had gone very far. He did
not care to kick into a skunk in the quiet lanes that led
through fields and thickets and woods-pastures.
The other residents of Green Valley spent the evening
felicitating one another on their ability to scare off any of
these inferior peoples hailing from foreign lands. They
walked about proudly, hoping to meet still some one else
who wanted to talk it all over again. Some of the men
stuck their lamps in their caps and walked up to the gar-
dens back of Company Row and picked tomatoes and pulled
turnips for the next day. This done, activities gradually
THE FIREMAKERS
215
subsided until there were no lights at all anywhere except
the flare of lightning in the west, and no sounds save the
occasional explosive bellow of some hound that in the course
of a bad dream had seen some enemy drawing near.
The next morning Green Valley was not awake early.
There was nothing to get up for. But when the first drowsy
men did stretch themselves and walk forth from whatever
temporary lodgings they chanced to occupy, they thought
they were in dreamland. Soldiers in neat blue uniforms,
and carrying businesslike rifles were moving about quietly,
taking up positions by the Hand-out, moving off down the
railroad track in the direction of the new mine, and filing
along the path toward the old mine. Then men in collar-
less shirts blinked in amazement. They had never seen so
much blue uniform. It was Decoration Day multiplied by
five or ten.
Joe Goodland was just coming to the post office to send
out and receive the seven o'clock mail. A sturdy officer
with brusque, positive manners, was giving some of the
soldiers final instructions.
"What's up?" Joe asked.
"What's up?" the captain echoed. "Did you ask what's
up?"
"Yes," Joe answered innocently.
"Poor little lamb!" the captain exclaimed, half in amuse-
ment, half in disgust. "Out of town last night, I suppose."
Joe was more mystified than ever.
"You know nothing about it, eh?" the captain asked, a
little derisively.
"No, sir, I don't know what you're talking about."
"Then you'll have to read the paper when it comes in on
the train from Centerville."

The article said that ever since the beginning of the strike
the Company had feared for the safety of its valuable Green
Valley properties. Lest damage be done to the dwelling
houses, the sheriff had been asked to evict all tenants.
This was not the first trouble, the Gazette went on to ex-
plain. Once before the Company had sent a force of men
over to operate the mine and the strikers had driven them
from town, though not so violently as this time. Following
this ugly demonstration against the Italians, and the burn-
ing of a new mine tipple and several flat-cars, the local com-
pany of the militia had been rushed to the scene by special
train.
Not even the spectacle of so many uniforms and rifles
could keep the residents of the Valley from laughing. Cap-
tain McNab saw them laugh and suspected that they were
laughing at him and his soldiers. "You'd better cut out that
monkey business," he warned them. "You're not dealing
with a bunch of Dagoes this time. You've got somebody
here now that it'll take more than six-shooters to scare away.
"And by the bye," he added, "can any of you gentlemen
tell me where to find a young fellow named Luke Dabney?
Doesn't happen to be in town, does he? No, I thought not.
That's the reason we want to see him."
Nobody could think of a word in the face of this voluble
officer.

CHAPTER IV
HOUGHT of Luke Dabney cheered Captain Mc-
Nab. The prospect of a man-hunt enlivened the
drab business of doing guard duty in a dirty little
mining town. He would be ready as soon as certain gen-
eral preliminaries were disposed of. Many of these pre-
liminaries he looked after himself. He told the citizens, for
example, about the restrictions which martial law had
brought to their lives. Nobody was to go beyond certain
marks along the road and railroad and on the hillsides-
at least without his special permission. "And your reason
for going will have to be a mighty damned good one." No-
body was to go into Breadless beyond the river; nobody was
to go near a mine; nobody was to trespass on any of the
Company's town property.
The miners thought the first prohibition unimportant.
They had no money left to buy drinks with; so why should
anybody wish to cross the river? The second was a huge
joke, they thought. Even for money they were refusing to
go near a mine, and now the military comes along and for-
bids them to do what they cannot be hired to do. The third
was not so easily accepted. The former occupants of Com-
pany Row still had turnips and cabbage and a few tomatoes
in the gardens they had left. They believed they ought to
be permitted to go back from time to time for food.
"Why didn't you take them with you?" the captain asked
in martial voice, when Mrs. Pewee Dunkle went to head-
quarters in the first of the houses above the railroad and
applied for permission to get some turnips for dinner.
"How do I know which turnips belong to you?"
218
THE FIREMAKERS
219
"I can show you."
"But how do I know you are showing me the right gar-
den? What if some other woman comes up here in about
fifteen minutes and tells me that I have given you permis-
sion to pull her turnips? See? Then what?"
Captain McNab was busy with such matters all morn-
ing. In the afternoon he had to go down the track a mile
and a half and up the switch to the new mine where the
tipple had been burned and see how the men had established
themselves. The men had been on duty a full half-day
and had already despaired of seeing any firebug lurking in
the tawny sedge or the green and scarlet sumac. They
could not see just why he would come back anyhow, since
the fire had burned not only the tipple but the half-dozen
or more flat-cars that stood under it waiting for coal yet in
the hill. They had, therefore, carried a bench out of the
little blacksmith shop that stood far enough back from the
tipple to be out of reach of the flames, and joined in a noisy
game of seven-up.
The men knew their captain intimately in Centerville.
The company which he commanded was, in consequence, a
democratic organization. When he came unexpectedly upon
them round a coloring maple tree, they did not so much as
rise. But they did begin to ask him questions.
"For God's sake, Cap, how long do we have to stay in
this hole?" they asked as if they wanted to know.
"Christ A'mighty, don't ask me," he answered. "Not
long, though, I'm sure. They'll be ready to go back to work
pretty soon. But how about the man-hunt-are you all
ready? I hear the fellow we suspect of being the firebug
may be back here in the hills some six or eight miles. We'll
have to round him up, I guess."
"Wow!" they growled with mock savagery. "Turn the
dogs of war loose on his trail, huh?
Anything would be better than this

Very well. Captain McNab would hunt him up if he

When Eli had gone Captain McNab became nervously
active. He smoked desperately as he walked to headquar-
ters. Then he sat for an hour and dreamed and smiled and
rapped his knuckles in a noisy tattoo on the table which had
been improvised for him. At one-thirty o'clock he brushed
his uniform, put himself otherwise in order, and took the
road eastward.
When he passed the schoolhouse, recess was in full swing.
Threadbare children covered the playground beside the
building. He slowed up, intending to chat with some of
them, but when they saw him they self-consciously talked
to one another in low tones and then went awesomely into
the schoolhouse.
He walked on, all the while trying to catch a glimpse of
the interior of the school through door or window. He
came to the bridge and found two men in uniform sitting on
the extended flat abutment busy with a deck of cards. He
had almost forgotten that he had told Sergeant Cary to
leave a man or two at the bridge. He told them that he was
just going to the trouble to see that all orders were strictly
enforced; cautioned them against letting anybody who
looked like a coal miner pass eastward without a permit
from headquarters, and commanded them to pounce upon
any young fellow about twenty-six or twenty-seven maybe,
who was tall and light-haired and good-looking and pretty
independent.
He walked through the covered bridge into Breadless.
"The Last Chance," he read. "They have one here, too, do
they?" he chuckled, and moved toward the square, weather-
stripped little saloon that stood above the road.
The proprietor sat behind the bar asleep. Captain

night. I'm in charge here. I can exercise discretion if I
want to."
He was at the post office early the next morning. He
read the Gazette and otherwise lived casually until he caught
a glimpse of Val starting off to school. Yes, he approved of
her. This afternoon he would be on time.
But should he wait until afternoon? There would be
more leisure then; her day's work would be done; it would
be appropriate to go for a little promenade. Still, was not
a bird in the hand-and he moved off down the road in full
pursuit.
As he came up, she turned to see who it was, and he
touched his cap. When he was alongside he said, not too
colorfully, "Well, I noticed yesterday that you have quite a
school," and adapted his cadence to hers.
"Yes, sixty-two," she replied, not without a slight flush,
but with little of the country girl in her manner. He
stole a glance at her. There he was, right beside her, yet
something in her face seemed as far away as she had seemed
226
THE FIREMAKERS
the day before when he came over the river tardily from the
Last Chance.
"All in one room?"
She laughed. "Why, yes! Do you think I could look
after them if they were in two rooms?"
He laughed, too. "Say, that was a pretty dumb one, I'll
have to admit."
They walked again in silence for a moment. Out of the
corner of his eye he looked upon her. She was remote, yes;
she seemed to be living a long distance away. But she was
alive, and her face was beautiful, her hair and brows dark
and heavy, and her neck and shoulders shapely. "God,
here's where we camp for a month," he said to himself; but
to Val he said, "I've got two men posted down there at the
bridge beyond your school, and I'm doing a little personal
inspection. 'Don't leave it to anybody else, if you want it
done,' is my motto."
"What's the use of having all these soldiers here every-
where?" Val asked.
Captain McNab saw that she was not far away when she
asked that question. She meant business.
"Well, now," he began with a great display of sagacity,
"the truth is, it's a kind of dirty affair all round." He did
not wish to disagree with her. Still, it would not do to say
too much. If he said there was no reason for martial law-
if he so reported-Company B would be withdrawn; and
just now he was not ready for that step. "But you see," he
went on at last in an effort to unshoulder the responsibility,
"we go where we're sent."
"Who sent you here?" Val asked honestly, but with irony
in her voice.
Captain McNab was disturbed. Was she trying to cross-
examine him? He could see that Eli Raynor was right; she
had too many ideas.
"We were just ordered out," he explained, trying to clothe
THE FIREMAKERS
227
his presence in as much mystery as possible. But before he
had uttered the last words of the sentence, he knew that his
explanation was not being accepted.
He tried again to be agreeable. "I hear they're talking
of building a two-room school here."
Immediately she seemed less hostile. "I hope they do.
As things are, I simply can't teach some of the subjects I'd
like. No one teacher can."
"What subjects, for instance?" The captain wished to
keep her going where the way seemed pleasant.
"Well, there's literature. All these boys and girls of fif-
teen and sixteen might as well be in school reading some-
thing enjoyable."
Captain McNab blinked. He was afraid he was slipping
beyond his depth. "Never read much myself; always too
busy."
With the last word he thought, "Now there goes another
one. She'll ask me what I've been doing." But she did not.
"I'd like to teach drawing, too," Val continued, "but
there's not much time for it."
Captain McNab thought he had better remain silent.
"Do you know how many classes I teach in a day?" Val
asked, wishing to leave no doubt about the need of another
room and another teacher.
"Haven't the slightest idea."
"Twenty-eight."
"Say, you do grind them through, don't you?"
Thus they were talking when they came up to the school-
house. It was in need of paint, and two or three strips of
weatherboarding had slipped down at the joints so that the
bones of the plastered interior were exposed.
"Wish to see where I teach?" Val asked.
"Tickled to death," Captain McNab answered. She was
warming up a little, eh?
She unlocked the door, swung it open, and waited.
228
THE FIREMAKERS
"You first," the captain said, lifting his cap and bowing.
He wanted to make full use of his store of gallantries.
She stepped inside, and he followed, taking a quick glance
as he did so to see if any of the children were yet near at
hand.
"Quite foxy, I should say," he commented as he sur-

the desk, and there were smaller ones on the side walls be-
tween the windows. The big stove, not yet needed, was
virtually concealed in maple boughs. On her desk was a
bouquet of fall-roses.
"Makes me want to come to school myself," he ventured
to say. She looked at him so honestly that he felt himself
coloring a little at the proposal. "What the hell's the mat-
ter with me?" he thought as he felt the blood in his face.
"Am I getting young and innocent again?"
"We don't have big boys," she said, and laughed. "There
isn't room in a one-room school."
He laughed, too, though he did not know just why. It
was easier than not. "I'll start, then, when you get the
new schoolhouse."
He thought that was not bad. "And I hope you get it."
Some children came slyly up to the door-the advance
guard of the sixty-two. In awe they listened to this soldier
talking with their teacher. Captain McNab saw them, and
believed he had better go on his way and learn what rioters
the guards at the bridge had pounced upon.
It did not take him long to learn. He was soon back in
Green Valley. He would have to round up the firebug, he
supposed. He walked briskly down the railroad track and
up the switch to the black, scorched spot which marked the
site of the tipple.
The men saw him coming. "For the love of Christ, Cap,"
they implored him, "when you going to get us out of this
THE FIREMAKERS
229
dump? Firebug? Judas Priest! We haven't seen a tum-
blebug."
Captain McNab was consoling. "Day after to-morrow
we shall probably start the man hunt. Then you'll have
action enough-if you don't get plugged by somebody's six-
shooter or double-barreled shotgun. Things are pretty
ugly up in the Valley and I've got to stick around for a day
or two yet before I can leave things."
On the way back he could see the little schoolhouse there
by the bridge. "She's a damned funny one," he said, and
wiped his brow and the front of the sweat-band of his cap.
"But wait till I get hold of her!"
He blustered about in the Valley till mess-time, bolted
his food and gulped his coffee, smoked two or three cigars,
tattooed his table with his knuckles, smoothed up his uni-
form, and started eastward. "Soon be a regular thing," he
said to himself as he smiled in anticipation.
He chatted with the two men doing bridge duty, went
over to the Last Chance and drank a beer or two, continued
his discussion with the proprietor for a time, came back to
the bridge and chatted with the men again, and stood ready
to start townward whenever he saw Val come from the
schoolhouse.
This time he knew exactly how he was going to proceed.
He had thought it all out. "They're all alike," he assured
himself, "once you get close to them. Getting a start is the
great thing. Now in this case I've got to go easy at first,
and then when the time is right, close in-and there you
are."
When he came up with her he was all ready with his first
well-thought-out remark. She turned, but did not seem
much surprised. "Those two guards must require a lot of
attention," she said teasingly before he could begin with
his carefully chosen observations.
His program vanished. He was obliged to depend upon
230
THE FIREMAKERS
chance sources of supply. "Don't rub it in," he entreated
her. "If I can't go to school, I at least want to see the
teacher sometimes."
He laughed invitingly, but Val did not fall into his mood.
Still they talked along, none too seriously. The children
engaged in the pastime of stopping to look back and then
running to catch up.
Before the two arrived in Green Valley proper, a num-
ber of men watched down the road from the post office.
They could see Captain McNab in his most gallant atti-
tudes.
"There goes Old Nate's last one," Joe Goodland declared.
"She's gone already when she lets a fella like that get that
close to her."
They watched without shrinking till Val had turned in at
her hewed-log house, and Captain McNab had gone by on
his way to headquarters.
"Sunday night!" Eli Raynor counseled Captain McNab
the next morning. "Bring her from church Sunday night.
That's the way it's done in this part of the world. Better
go to church Sunday morning, too, if you can stand it. I
come over and go once in a while, just as a matter of policy."
According to Captain McNab's confession, he had not
been in a church for a coon's age. But if going would get
him anywhere, he was ready to submit. He had thought of
closing the church as a matter of military discipline, but
perhaps it would be a wise stroke to announce that he had
no thought of interfering with divine worship.
"She's a saucy little devil," Eli said warningly.
"That's the kind I like," the captain assured him.
Captain McNab spent Saturday of that week thinking up
supplementary aids to his campaign for Sunday. Why not
hunt up this old man Boyd and engage him in conversation
about the relative abilities of Meade and Lee? But he
abandoned that because he decided the move would be too
THE FIREMAKERS
231
obvious. Anyhow, he was not certain that he knew as
much as he ought to know about Gettysburg. And maybe
Boyd might not care to talk with him. Perhaps, after all,
he had better restrict himself to Val. She was willing to
talk, at any rate.
Should he go to church twice on Sunday? Would any-
body stay away in the evening if the officer enforcing mar-
tial law went in the morning? That was a nice question.
He decided to stay away Sunday morning. Perhaps it
was just as well. But by doing so he missed a bit of drama
not often encountered in Green Valley. The minister when
he came into the pulpit had lost something-he had lost his
sanctimoniousness. In the course of the week he had come
upon something real. In thinking over the life of the peo-
ple in Green Valley, he had unwittingly stripped his own
soul of a vast accumulation of mannerisms and dead in-
crustations.
He did not so much as take a text. He stood in rapt sim-
plicity beside his desk and talked to his people. "This is
the last Sunday I shall spend with you before I go to the
annual Conference. I should be shirking my duty, violating
my sacred obligations, if I did not say to you before I go,
what I am about to say."
Even the two full benches of Sinners' Row listened to
him with respect. He discussed the merits of the strike.
Would Jesus approve of imposing military authority upon
a people who wanted reasonable safety and fair wages while
keeping the wheels of industry going and the people of a
continent warm? Would He approve bullying men into un-
necessary dangers so that the profits of a few could be a
little larger? Who burned the tipple, anyhow? Was there
anybody that one member of his congregation could fix upon
as a likely doer of the deed? It was tyranny to shoulder an
army on to a free people just because somebody had done
something that nobody knew anything about. It was
232
THE FIREMAKERS
against such tyranny as this that our Revolutionary fore-
fathers had protested and fought. He himself had taken
one homeless family into the parsonage, and he only wished
it was large enough for another. "I have no money," he
concluded, "because whatever money I receive comes from
you, and you have none. I have kept back just enough to
take me to Conference and bring me back home. But any-
body is welcome to anything he can find in our house and
garden as long as there is a bite left." Then he prayed that
his people might have power to hold out to the end.
That was what Captain McNab missed. So he went in-
nocently to church in the evening. The minister saw him
there and inferred that he had come for the purpose of
altering by his presence the pulpit utterances of the evening
service.
But the minister was in a fighting mood. "I see the offi-
cer in charge of Green Valley in the congregation to-night.
Lest there be any misunderstanding of what I said this
morning, I want to repeat it this evening."
He bore in so heavily once or twice that Captain McNab
almost forgot what he was there for. But he kept his head,
and while the minister pronounced the benediction he looked
out of the corner of his eye at Val. "Time to close in," he
said to himself.
The parishioners eyed him silently as they pushed past
him toward the door. Val was talking with some one up
front. Why didn't she come along. Did he want to stand
there by himself, like a fool? "The preacher will think I am
waiting for him."
Presently she came.
"I wish to speak to you about something," he said in gal-
lant tone, "and perhaps this is a good time."
"Why, yes," she replied cheerfully. But she colored.
She knew the minister and one or two others up front were
watching her.
THE FIREMAKERS
233
The people already outside were watching, too-looking
back out of the darkness. "Hardly thought Nate's girl
would do it," they said. "But you know all the rest of them
took up with somebody or other."
The two stepped out into the darkness. "You sure know
I wanted to see you," Captain McNab began, "or I never
would have lasted through the old buzzard's bombardment.
The bullets were whizzing round my head as thick as flies.
But you notice I stuck. I wanted to have this little walk
with you."
They climbed the steps of the stile. He touched her el-
bow gently in assisting her.
"What was on your mind?" she asked, a little too casually,
a little too unromantically to please him, as she took the
first step down on the other side.
"This," he answered, stepping quickly before her.
It was as if he had laid hold of a panther. She clawed
his face, gave him a resounding whack on the jaw, and with
one tremendous squirm freed herself-all before he could
utter a word. Then she ran down the path and caught up
with the others while she was yet the subject of their con-
versation.

CHAPTER V
APTAIN MCNAB jerked open the sagging door at
headquarters and blustered into the room. A kero-
sene lamp with an uneven wick burned with much
smoke. In a rocking chair an orderly slept with one knee
over the arm of the chair and with his head drooping down-
ward until it almost touched his knee.
"Wallace!" the captain roared.
Wallace straightened open like a pocket knife.
Captain McNab pressed his cheek with a handkerchief
splotched with blood. "Ran into a God-damned wire
fence," he explained when he saw Wallace scrutinizing the
handkerchief and his face. Then warmly still, but a little
more officially: "I want you to take a message down to the
tipple for me."
He dropped into the chair that Wallace had vacated and
began to scrawl something on a sheet of paper. "I'll tell
you what's in it," he said as he wrote, "but-that-squad-
down there-won't believe it-unless it's in writing. We're
going to have a man hunt to-morrow, and they must be
ready. Better say nine o'clock. Here, go down the track
and up the switch so you won't run into anything like I
did."
Wallace vanished in the darkness. Then Captain McNab
talked intimately with himself. "Who the hell does she
think she is, anyway? Leading a fellow into a trap! But
just you wait, little girl; I'll get you, by God, see if I don't."
He lighted a cigar and paced the floor. He smoked a
few puffs and threw the long remainder into the corner of
the room. As he covered his beat, his sentences became
234
THE FIREMAKERS
235
briefer and briefer until nothing remained of them but ex-
clamation points.
The next morning he was up early. He scrutinized his
face in the small mirror on the whitewashed wall. The
scratch showed more than it did the night before. It would
be as well not to go down to the region of the post office.
Wallace could go. He instructed Wallace to keep a sharp
lookout for Mr. Eli Raynor and, if he found him, to request
his presence at headquarters.
Wallace did not return at once. But some time after the
morning train from Centerville came in, he reported at
headquarters with Eli.
Eli entered, bland and expectant. At once he noticed the
fresh narrow scab on Captain McNab's face. "Did she
scratch you?" he asked as he sat down. He wanted to be
facetious.
McNab coughed a little laugh. He was not certain
whether Eli had heard anything or not. Since he was in
doubt, he turned at once to business.
"Hear what this crazy preacher said yesterday? Got
clear away from religion and talked about this strike. Of-
fered to give the strikers anything he had so long as there
was a bite left. And he prayed to God Almighty to give
them strength to hold out to the end of time. What do you
say to that, eh? Is that what they preach in churches now-
adays?"
"He knows which side his bread is buttered on," Eli an-
swered quite lightly-"if there's any butter on it."
"Or if there's any bread," the captain concluded.
"We'd better padlock it, hadn't we?" Captain McNab
asked, thinking of the church as a source of supply.
"No-o-o-o!" Eli answered with a great flourish of mock
generosity. "He's going to the Conference this week where
he gets his marching orders for next year. Old Bogart has
church connections. So maybe about next Sunday night
236
THE FIREMAKERS
when the bishop reads off the new appointments, the Lord
will have got in some of his mysterious work and Brother
Wingfield will find himself on the way to another charge.
It'll be easy enough. About to-morrow the news will begin
to get round among the high and mighty at Conference that
he's far too big a man for this congregation. See?"
He laughed and poked McNab in the ribs. "Is that all
that's troubling you?"
McNab smiled as if he were much heartened. He wished
to come without any show of abruptness to what was upper-
most in his mind. "Well, what about the priest over there
on the hill above the tipple? Where does he stand? Had
we better have him derricked, too?"
"Wouldn't bother. He's sick just now, anyhow. Under-
stand there was no mass yesterday."
McNab seemed to be straining for any other slight ob-
stacle. "And the school-ought we to do anything about
it?"
"Now you're talking!" Eli exclaimed, springing to his
feet and facing McNab. "In my opinion," he declared with
feeling, "that's where all the damned nonsense started in
the first place. Why don't you close it up?"
"Well, that's just what I've been turning over in my mind
for a week," McNab replied, with much show of the judi-
cial attitude. "I've got a theory. This young Val-or
whatever they call her-must be using public money to
support the strike. It stands to reason. The old man is
not working. His pension is not more than twelve dollars
a month, probably. And yet he and his family and the
whole pack of Dunkles are living in his house and they
all look as if they fed well."
Eli beamed as if some one had whispered to him from
the battlements of a high-class heaven. "Wonderful!
Wonderful! If we can make out a case of that kind against
her, we'll be rid of her for good and all."
THE FIREMAKERS
237
Why not? They sat on the edge of Captain McNab's
table with their heads close together.
At last they stood up again. "Didn't I tell you that the
time for action was at hand?" Captain McNab asked. "And
to-day we're going to round up the firebug. I'm waiting for
the men from the tipple now. I'm going to direct the hunt
personally. To-morrow morning the school directors will
be notified that school will open again whenever they find a
teacher who is not guilty of unprofessional conduct."
"They'd better get a man," Eli suggested.
"Yes."
"And you'd better put some guards down at the school-
house."
"Yes, there will be two men there to-morrow morning."
"Better make it more than two," Eli suggested, urgently
tactful.
Again Captain McNab was not certain whether Eli had
heard about the incident at the stile. But he assented to
the proposal to have the schoolhouse well guarded.
The men from the tipple came. Another squad from the
old mine came. And at ten o'clock Captain McNab led
them into the hills to round up the firebug. He wanted to
meet some resistance. He hoped the man who had burned
the tipple would be entrenched in a cave or a pot-shop with
plenty of arms and ammunition.
No miner would say just where Luke was at work. This
fact-proof in itself of his guilt-made it necessary for
them to sweep along northward asking from farmhouse to
farmhouse. It was a strange country they soon got into-
no coal mines, no broomsedge, no red waters in the streams.
The air was clear, the hillsides were green with autumn pas-
ture splotched with sumac and with fiery maples somewhat
ahead of season. The farmhouses were clean white, or they
were the soft red of hand-made brick, or they were the tan
238
THE FIREMAKERS
gray of sandstone. Occasionally there was a log house
which had not yet been displaced.
By one o'clock they had covered the seven miles.
"Wonder what it would be like," Lee Pittenger asked,
"to look along a rifle barrel at a man's head?" Lee was
still in high school. He was the "nice boy" of his squad.
"Well, you are just about due to look along one that the
other fellow is leveling at your head," Captain McNab re-
plied, a bit paternally. "See that log building ahead yonder
in the hollow with the board shed at this end? If these
farmers haven't been bamboozling us, that's the place."
They formed into squads and quickly extended into a
thin line long enough to make a complete circle round the
pottery while yet a distance from it. Then they closed in.
Captain McNab designated four men to accompany him.
They walked cautiously up to the low door and listened.
There was a defiant stillness inside. "Hey!" Captain
McNab called. No answer; no sound of any sort inside.
"We'll have to go in, I guess," the Captain suggested.
"Now, for God's sake don't give him a chance to shoot
first."
They pushed warily through the door into the shadowy
depths of the little pottery. Scaffolding filled with long,
smooth boards of ware in various stages of drying occupied
more than half of the space. In the lighted area by the
window was a potter's wheel, and at the side of the wheel
table, a board half filled with fresh ware. Somebody had
been there in very recent minutes.
They stood and listened, taking easy, silent breaths of air
heavy with a perfume which for thousands of years had
been imprisoned in clay. There were not many places in
the room where a man could conceal himself, but there were
a few.
What was that? A scuffling sound in the millshed. One
man who had forgotten to cock his rifle cocked it.
THE FIREMAKERS
239
"Who's there?" Captain McNab demanded.
Complete silence again.
Captain McNab made a silent gesture with arm and head
and lips.
They moved toward the millshed door and with great
caution peered through.
A blind, flea-bitten old mare, the claygrinder, stood in her
stall in the corner and stared with her sightless eyes in the
direction of the voice she had heard.
"Guess she's not dangerous," somebody whispered, and
they all smiled in relief.
Had he escaped, after all? It began to look as if he had.
Still, they had better make sure.
There was a large opening near the door up into the loft.
"Take a peek up there," Captain McNab commanded in a
stern whisper.
But who wanted to be first to stick his head up within
easy range of a six-shooter? So they thrust their heads up
simultaneously. They saw nothing except great piles of
jugs and fruit jars and milk crocks and churns waiting to
be carried to the kiln and burned.
The kiln, yes. Better look there. They went outside
and filed up the crude steps to the platform between the
kiln and the shop loft, and looked through the door. The
sunlight from the flue-holes and cat-holes in the dome made
dazzling lines on the glazed interior.
"What do you know about that?" Lee Pittenger whis-
pered. And all of them lost some of their fear in their
wonder.
When they came out Captain McNab called from the
platform to the dozen men who surrounded the pottery:
"See anything?"
"Not a thing," they answered.
"Better go up to that house yonder on the ridge," he com-
manded; and he led the way.

THE FIREMAKERS
The tension was slackened. The men talked freely about
what had probably become of him. He must have got word
from some one in the Valley that very morning.
"Funny ridge," somebody thought. "Stretches clear up
from the pottery to the level of all these hills."
They made inquiry at the house.
"Why, yes," an old man explained to them, "he works
down there. And he eats and sleeps right in this house.
Don't see how you missed him."
"Can you tell me," Captain McNab then asked with due
solemnity, "whether he was here a week ago to-day?"
"I can for a fact," the old man answered. "He was not.
He was in Green Valley."
"When did he get back?'
"That night."
"What time?"
"Could hardly say off hand. But me and my wife had
gone to bed. We heard him come in just about the time
the thunderstorm broke."
The commanding officer clucked in confirmation to the

"He came in," Captain McNab said, "after he had set

"Didn't hear of that," the old man answered.
"Well, you hardly expected him to come in and tell you,

"Look here," he said stoutly, "don't you see that you are

"I don't know where he is," the old man answered. He

"Said when he was up to eat at eleven-thirty that he'd have
THE FIREMAKERS

to go to town before long. But whether he went this after-

"Don't know as I'm anxious to do ary one," the old man

"Did you see that funny bunch of whiskers on his chin?"

"You're getting poetic, Cap," one of his old pals in Cen-

"You are damned right I am," Captain McNab replied,

out to burn another tipple right now. You notice, don't you,
that he doesn't tell the old man anything about his move-

On the way down the ridge they discussed the ways of
firebugs. It was interesting, Captain McNab thought, that
they set things afire at regular intervals-every week, or
every two weeks, just like getting drunk.
When they passed the pot-shop they looked in, but did
not expect to find anyone. And they did not.
Along the wagon track which constituted the road below
the pottery, they stopped at a clean, grassy spot to have a
bite to eat. They lounged and stretched for a time, wished
they were somewhere else, and wondered what a real war
would be like.
Soon after they resumed their way they saw a man with
a pick examining something in the edge of a thicket.
When he saw them he stopped his explorations and, pick in
hand, walked over to the roadside.
242
THE FIREMAKERS
"What's up?" he asked, appearing to be surprised at the
sight of so many men of any kind in such a secluded spot.
"Chasing a firebug," Captain McNab replied.
"What did he burn?"
"Haven't you heard?"
Captain McNab looked the man over critically as he
asked the question. There was something vaguely remi-
niscent about his features and bearing.
"No, don't hear very much back here."
Captain McNab took another scrutinizing look. "Why,
he burned the new tipple over at Green Valley."
The man started up as if he were interested in tipples.
"When did it happen?" he asked.
"A week ago to-night."
"Oh," the man replied.
Something in his open manner aroused McNab's suspi-
cion. People who were frank usually had something to con-
ceal, he thought. He studied the man's face a moment and
then inquired, "Your name doesn't happen to be Dabney,
does it?"
"That's just what it is."
"Luke Dabney?"
"That's it."
"Well, we want to see you."
"All right, look at me," Luke answered, unperturbed and
smiling.
"I guess you'd better go along with us to Green Valley
and tell what you know when we get there. You may have
some information about the tipple."
"I'm afraid I don't," Luke protested. "You can see I
don't know any more about it than the man in the moon."
"We'll talk about all that when we get to headquarters,"
Captain McNab replied. "Do you want to march along, or
do you want us to take you?"
Captain McNab looked at the sharp chisel-like points of
THE FIREMAKERS
243
the clay-pick, and hoped that Luke would choose to make
no trouble.
"Why, I'll go along, if it will do anybody any good."
He pitched the pick with an easy swing into a little thorn
bush where he could find it when he came back.
When it had been left safely behind, Captain McNab re-
newed the conversation. "A damned innocent world, this is.
You never find anybody who is guilty of anything."
Thus they talked the seven miles away. Luke was puz-
zled, but good natured. He said just enough to keep the
captain going. The men said nothing except when they fell
behind in twos or threes in order to make observations to
each other.
When they came down the hill to Company Row Captain
McNab said to Luke: "There are plenty of houses along
here without tenants, and since we shall want to ask you
some questions bright and early to-morrow morning, we
are going to have you occupy one of these to-night." He
thereupon commanded the men to take the prisoner to the
house next to headquarters.
"Just what is the point to this big joke?" Luke asked.
"We'll talk about that to-morrow," McNab answered.
After Luke had gone protestingly but unresistingly into
the house designated, Captain McNab hurried to the little
railroad station and sent a telegram to the sheriff. "Am
holding firebug awaiting word from you. Can you come to-
morrow?"
The sheriff was down in the south end of the county.
Some ex-Kentuckians were making moonshine in an aban-
doned coal mine, and he hoped to get a reward. But he
came.
He handcuffed Luke to his own strong wrist and led him
to the station. "Hello, Luke," some of the strikers called
to him as they loafed past on the platform. They made
him understand that they were sympathetic.
244
THE FIREMAKERS
Others who saw him began to admit that the evidence was
pretty strong against him. They recalled that there was
something "hidden" in his mother's ways, and it was not
at all unlikely that he had a streak of the same thing in
him. He had a natural grievance against the mine in his
father's death and in his own ten days or so of entombment.
And now that he was planning to get out of mining, prob-
ably he thought he would just even things up a bit.
But whatever their attitude, the sheriff had him. And
the sheriff carried him away to Centerville.
Captain McNab declared that this was only the begin-
ning. His quartet of men saw to it that very morning that
Miss Valencia Boyd did not open her school. Captain
McNab himself went to the school directors and informed
them that they might open school again whenever they
found a proper person as teacher. The present one had
been a disturbing element. In various ways she had been
guilty of unprofessional conduct.
The directors had held the vague opinion that she was
doing pretty well. But of course they wanted to do the
right thing.
Could they find a good teacher to take her place? It was
late now. Teachers were settled for the year.
They rode horseback to other school districts and ex-
plained their need. But there was a shortage of teachers,
it seemed. One district had not yet opened for the year be-
cause no teacher had applied. After two or three days of
futile searching, they abandoned hope, climbed the long hill
above Breadless, and invited Mr. Pompey McManus to take
charge of their school once more. He accepted with pleas-
ure.
McNab's martial edict, however, did not bring an end
suddenly to Val's activities. "Do you suppose I'm going to
let him keep me from teaching school if I want to?" she
asked Nathan. "Some teacher somewhere is sick or has
THE FIREMAKERS
245
died or has got married or run away or something. I'll find
another school."
She set out. There was a vacancy, she had heard in the
summer, over north of St. Rose's Church. She tramped
over there, but found a young Irishman named Sweeney
teaching while he studied law. On the way back she stopped
at the parish house by the church to ask for a glass of
water. She was not a Catholic, but she entertained no
mysterious fears. She said "Father McGuire" quite as if
she were within the pale.
He himself, just out of bed from his lumbago, a trifle
rigidly pumped a glass of water for her under the grape
arbor and chatted with her while she drank.
"And how is school going? Tell me, won't you?"
In brief words she explained what had happened.
"Oh, how sorry I am!" he said with great gentleness.
"But don't you let that trouble you. The good Lord does
not let things like that pass unnoticed."
She went her way.
Father McGuire went his, too; but he could not help
thinking about her.
He tried to take a nap when he was weary that afternoon,
but he saw himself standing under the grape arbor with this
comely, honest young woman, and he heard her clear voice
as she told him what had happened.
The next morning he believed he was strong enough to
drive to Green Valley. He meant to make a few inquiries
on his own responsibility. But when he arrived he heard
something that caused him to forget Val's case utterly. He
heard that the sheriff had carried away Luke Dabney for
burning the tipple.
He hastened to Captain McNab's headquarters. Captain
McNab sat at his desk.
"Have you imprisoned a man for burning the tipple?"
Father McGuire asked with a little heat.
246
THE FIREMAKERS
"We have," Captain McNab answered no less positively.

"Well, you'll not find it easy," Father McGuire replied, a

He was just able to catch the train for Centerville. What

Captain McNab saw unusual commotion, and walked

Captain McNab was not to be humiliated easily. "Who

"I said it."

CHAPTER VI
ATHER McGUIRE was not so successful with Val's
The school directors told him that they had
met the requirements by signing a contract with
Pomp for one year. Val would have to find a school else-
where.
She turned impetuously to the task. She went to all the

the schools consequently began late. But they had em-

Yes, they needed a teacher. But they wanted a man.
"We don't take much stock over here in these women teach-
ers. That's not to say you're not a good one. But they
don't quite fill our bill."
Hurriedly she tramped through the early autumn sun to
every other district where the need of a teacher was so
much as rumored. Never before had she imagined how
many things could be wrong with a young woman in need

That evening she rested from her labors for a time after

could fit into the situation.
But they had heard that she had been put out at Green
Valley for unprofessional conduct. They were gentlemen;
they said nothing to embarrass her. As a matter of fact,
they did not understand just what was meant by unprofes-
sional conduct. But it sounded immoral. After she had
gone, they agreed among themselves that the charges were
probably well founded. She was pretty enough.
She walked along the ridge road in desperation. Not
even the beauty of the hills could call her away from the
spiritual maze in which she looked frantically for an exit.
She was getting nowhere. Rumor spread heavily before
her. Wherever she turned, the sulphurous smoke of Green
Valley gob piles was always a little in advance so that she
was never quite able to get through it.
And here she was spending money, too, for carfare. She
had better stop that at once. She could walk back to Green
Valley this very day. Straight through the hills southward
it could not be more than seven miles. She was not eager
to face that many, but of course she could. And she would.
250
THE FIREMAKERS
Twenty cents meant twenty cents in strike time when one

She stopped to ask directions at a log house with a lean-to

Yes, she could get to Green Valley if she followed the

A pot-shop! Could this be Luke Dabney's shop? Since

What if this should be the place? What if he should be
there? Should she look in? Although Luke had lived

in Val's mind that fact somehow added many years to his
age. But now that there was the possibility of meeting him

spiritual solitude, he did not seem so remote in years or in
acquaintance. Her heart grew fluttery. She forgot for a
moment that she had no school.
As she descended the long ridge she could see the little
pot-shop nestled down there in the hollow. Butternut trees
spread their low limbs over the corner of the millshed and a
giant elm stood beyond the kiln and dwarfed it.
She came out into a triangular bit of greensward in front

ware looked soft, yet it looked permanent and age-old also.
She very quietly approached the open door. The smooth
squeak of a treadle told her that somebody was at work.
THE FIREMAKERS
251
Through the slush-spattered window she could just make

He went inside. But he was too much unsettled to go to

of the bench developed a crust in the drafty air during his
half-hour's absence with Val? He felt the edges of the clay
and satisfied himself on that point. Then he slipped into
the millshed and gave the blind claygrinder a cut with a
willow gad for loafing on the job while she heard him away
from his wheel. When he came through the millshed door
he closed it for a moment and looked at the three pieces on
the end of the expansive board. In his nervousness he came
near dropping one of them.
He swung the door open again, walked through the shop
and out into the ware-yard. Val was still in sight along the
wagon track of a road that led through the woods and on
out to the narrow, yellow highway. While he looked Val
turned-just as she was about to enter the woodland-and
saw him looking after her. He lifted his arm high in final
salutation, and she waved in reply.
THE FIREMAKERS
259
She entered the woodland. Her own state of mind and
the state of the forest about her made intoxication easy.
The great trees-the oaks, the hickories, the beeches-were
still green, or at most only touched with bronze. But the
papaws were bright yellow and the ground under them, the
dogwoods were deep claret, and the Virginia creeper, reach-
ing up along the trunks of the trees and far out toward the
tips of the branches, was blood red. She journeyed on and
on, as if she were walking in a trance. She knew not what
to make of herself. Here she was without a school, and
with the quite certain prospect of getting none; yet she en-
joyed an unspeakable exhilaration. She would gladly have
marched on in that state for countless years. If only there
were no end to the woodland!
But at last she came out upon the yellow, dusty road. She
followed it, content to walk in the dust and look upon the
goldenrod, the bending elderberry bushes, the purpling
blackhaws that filled the fence corners along the way.
When she left the road and turned across the fields, there
was still enrapturing color. The edges of the fields and the
uncultivated sides of deep ravines were scarlet with sumac.
It was only when she came out on the ridge above Green
Valley and beheld the broomsedge everywhere about her
and on through the haze to the southward as far as she could
see, and breathed the air from the gob piles, that she was
reminded of her physical fatigue, her hunger for food, her
isolation.
She walked straight on down the hill. At least she could
get something to eat. One of her mother's custards would
make her a little more courageous, perhaps.

CHAPTER VII
AL found Green Valley in full possession of some-
thing fresh to talk about. While she was absent the
new Methodist minister had arrived he and his wife
and five children. He had begun his ministrations almost
before he was off the train. He shook hands with every-
body. He referred in solemn voice to the "temporary un-
pleasantness"-which, he was sure, would be amicably ad-
justed as soon as everyone approached the problem in the
spirit of prayerfulness. He called at military headquarters
and left an invitation to church for Captain McNab. At
once he gave all sorts of exhibitions of the tact for which he
was noted.
Val was not interested in him. He did not ring true. He
went through all his tactful formularies as if he had re-
peated them daily for a lifetime. They meant nothing to
him and they meant nothing to Val. She was thinking of
Green Valley as she had seen it from the hilltop. She was
seeing-even when her eyes were closed-the pinched faces
that were becoming more numerous among the children, the
defeated faces that were becoming more numerous among
the men and women. And they had tied her hands-they in
authority, whoever they were-and they meant to have the
bonds good and firm. Some of the wise ones were already
saying that Old Nate's youngest one would have to settle
down the same as the rest.
She thought of the sumac, the woodbine; of the white
oaks and hickories towering to the skies on unviolated hill-
sides; of the pottery and Luke and his vase and Keats's Ode.
What a muddle she was in!
260
THE FIREMAKERS
261
When everybody went to church on Sunday morning to
size up the new preacher, she went, too-somewhat lan-
guidly, somewhat belligerently. She had already sized him
up.
He told those assembled in the church how happy he was
that God had called him to minister to such an interesting
people. He wondered if they fully appreciated what an
essential part in life Providence had given them to perform.
Coal was the very lifeblood of the nation. "If you go into
any matter far enough, you will always find coal."
"Sure," whispered Pewee Dunkle to Hi Crumblett in Sin-
ners' Row, and coughed a hacking little cough in mock fu-
tility. "I've got a pretty good vein of it in the bottom of
both my lungs. Do you s'pose he knows that?"
"They can't make a machine run," the minister went on,
"they can't move the trains on our railroads, they can't keep
warm in our cities, they can't cook the food which keeps
people alive, without coal. The Lord has abundantly sup-
plied the earth with it. All we have to do is to go into the
hills and dig it out. And he has probably favored our glo-
rious nation with a greater abundance of it than any other
people. If all the coal mined in the United States in one
year should be loaded on one train, that train would reach
from New York City clear across the Appalachian Moun-
tains, clear across the great prairies of the West, clear across
the Rocky Mountains, and goodness only knows how far
out into the Pacific Ocean.
"What a great privilege it is to work in an industry of
such magnitude! Yes, and what a great responsibility. If
the miners throughout the country were to lay down their
picks for a month-just one single month-in the dead of
winter, industry would be paralyzed, commerce would be at
a standstill, and tens of thousands in our cities would be
hungry and freezing. How important, then, that we exer-
262
THE FIREMAKERS
cise our God-given privileges and responsibilities with
thoughtfulness for the comfort of others!"
"Where does he get that sorghum, anyhow?" Pewee whis-
pered to Hi.
Hi did not know. Maybe it was from the Bible, but he
doubted it. He was sure it was not from Karl Marx.
With this preface on current affairs, the minister preached
a sermon on The Spirit of Coöperation. He closed with a
dramatic recital of some poetry which he said sustained him
almost as if it were from Holy Writ:
Look at the roses saluting each other,
Look at the herds all at peace on the plain-
Man and man only makes war on his brother,
And dotes in his heart on his peril and pain.
Val did not see the appropriateness of the lines. But
their soothing cadence was not without effect. Mrs. Pewee
Dunkle, who decked herself out in one of Sudie Boyd's old
dresses and went to hear the new preacher, said she thought
the poetry wasn't bad to round off with, no matter what it
meant.
That afternoon, for the first time in many weeks, there
was pronounced diversity of opinion in Green Valley. Men
lounged before the post office, on the quiet station platform,
on the no less quiet steps of the Hand-out, and discussed
what the preacher had said. Some, who were hungrier than
others, thought it was the unadulterated gospel. Some de-
clared that the man did not know what he was talking about
-he could not keep on the track. Others declared that he
knew only too well what he was about; that was the trouble;
he probably was paid by the Company to come and put on
the tremolo. Hi Crumblett was certain that it was genuine
capitalistic fodder.
Val was sure of only one thing: the entire performance in
the pulpit was pumped-up and unreal. She could tell reality
THE FIREMAKERS
263
when she encountered it. Two or three times in her life she
had had the experience when she made her first crude
painting, when she was confronted in her school by her pu-
pils' honest questions, and when Luke had shown her the
tea-things and the vase.
Luke himself had seemed real.
The school was gone. With it had gone the opportunity
to learn more about painting. Could it be possible that the
realities of one's life were swept away so casually-by some
man's desire to have a larger bank account, or by some
other man's lust for flesh? Was the world ordered after
that manner?
In the latter part of the afternoon she climbed the hill
north of Green Valley. She must reflect upon this matter.
For an hour she sat under a great maple tree which by its
solid shade throughout the summer kept a circle far out
from its base clean of undergrowth. Red and yellow leaves
drifted down-forerunners of the tens of thousands that
would be coming down in a week or two.
She did not dream. She thought. And she thought about
something more specific than the causes of adversity. She
thought about the future. If all went as all was now going,
she would end up just where her sisters were. There had
been nothing inferior about them. They had aspired quite
as honestly as she had. She like them could choose. She
could marry a mule driver or a bartender or a skunk hunter.
Or she could marry a man who battled against the black line
of coal and become a widow in five or ten years with a fam-
ily of hungry children to look out for; then, if she should be
lucky, she could marry a second coal digger who had been
left with a houseful of children in need of a mother. Or
she could do neither. She could stay at home and become
an old maid-a quick process in Green Valley-and then
some day when her father and mother died, be left alone,
too old to marry, too old for any good purpose whatsoever.
The prospect frightened her till she sat tense and desper-
264
THE FIREMAKERS
ate under the maple tree, one elbow upon her knee, her chin
in the palm of her hand, her frightened eyes looking past
every object immediately before her. She must not be any
of these things.
If only she could get beyond the blighting whispers that
she was a coal miner's daughter who had had trouble of
some kind with the army officer in charge of Green Valley.
If only she could go somewhere and work and earn her way
out. If only she knew where Out was, she would go there,
whether she had money or not. She would tramp and tramp
straight toward it until she wore her shoes out on the road,
and then she would throw them away and tramp joyfully in
her bare feet as the hardy boys did when they came to school
in late autumn. She would cry, "I am coming, Out, and you
must not deny me freedom to work and make something
beautiful according to what I see before my eyes wherever
I go."
But she did not know where Out was. There was no road
to be seen. Luke Dabney was making pottery. Some day
he would probably make something too beautiful for words.
A flash of envy swept through her. She almost disliked him
because of his good fortune.
Still, he was not altogether out. He had yet to learn
about glazes, and all kinds of clays, and colors, and kilns.
In some heavenly place there were men who knew all that
had ever been discovered about pottery. They had been
saving up this knowledge until they had it all. Other men
could go there and avail themselves of the accumulation.
That was what Luke meant to do if he could learn
where it was, and could save enough money, and could find
somebody to look out for the twins.
A thought flashed through her mind with burning clear-
Her pulse pounded in her ears. Little twitches of
ness.
hurrying blood warmed her face.
Aye, why not? Why not help him herself. She was done
THE FIREMAKERS
265
for. She was lost in the suffocating fumes of the gob piles.
Why not help somebody else who still had a chance to get
away?
She saw herself carrying through every detail of the enter-
prise-living in seclusion, mothering Winnie's twins, endur-
ing whatever pain fate might bring. It would be easy, even
glorious, if one kept looking beyond the difficulties to what
was to come after them.
At last she stood up. Once again she became aware of all
that was immediately about her. Never had Green Valley
seemed so desolate. Yet never had it seemed so remote.
By process of an hour's thinking, it had become objective,
something to be looked at from afar.
She gathered some branches of maple leaves and walked
homeward. She felt "trembly," but she also felt certain.
Making a decision brought health, even when it had to be
made in desperation.
Pewee Dunkle and his family were just starting up to
Company Row to see if any belated turnip or tomato lin-
gered in their garden.
"I'm going back into the country to-morrow morning,"
she announced as soon as the Dunkles had gone. "Those
three school directors have got to give me that school. I'm
going to tell them everything that has happened, and then
they can't refuse me."
"Go ahead," said Nathan, a little skeptically, but also a
little proudly. "It'll do no harm to try."

CHAPTER VIII
AL took the morning train again to Culbertson.
When she arrived she was in a confused state of
thinking and feeling. Should she go directly to the
pottery, or should she go, in fact, and present herself once
more to the chairman of that board of school directors? She
did not have to decide the matter at once, for the road was
the same in either case for two miles. If she should insist,
would the directors hold another meeting and reconsider her
application? Before she came to the end of the two miles,
she had decided and redecided the matter a dozen times.
When the forks of the road were immediately ahead, she
chose the road to the school. It would hurt nothing to find
out what the chairman thought.
When she came up to the white house with fan windows
in the gables, the chairman of the board of school directors
was engaged in putting white-oak soles on his farm sled.
He was down on his knees over an auger, with the side of
his face to her, his long, reddish-gray beard resting on the
auger handles while he looked to see if the bit were coming
through at the right place. He caught a glimpse of her out
of the corner of his eye and looked up just when she was
ready to speak. He was disturbed to see her again in his
wood-yard.
"I thought I'd stop," Val said in her pleasantest manner,
"and see if you did not wish to talk about the school again.
I can explain everything that happened over at Green Val-
ley."
He seemed irritated, and fussed with the auger needlessly
before he straightened up. "Wouldn't do you the least bit
266
THE FIREMAKERS
267
of good," he answered then. "Don't know as I want to
hear you, anyhow. We got our information from your direc-
tors. What they said was official."
"But it might not be true even if it was official," Val an-
swered, doubtful herself of the use of answering at all.
He looked at her as if Salome had questioned the words
of John the Baptist. "No-o-o," he said, speaking out of
eternity; "it's settled."
Val stood wavering for a moment. It was not so much
that she was not to get the school; she wanted to go to Luke
anyhow. But she was in agony because the old man saw in
her something that was not there. She wanted to cry out,
"Look here, you benighted old sinner, I'm going to tell you
the truth whether you want to hear it or not."
Instead she said in a meek, fading voice as she turned to
go, "Good-by."
"Good-by," he said with a rising inflection, as he tugged
at the sled sole.
She was sick unto tears. Then she was angry. Then she
was glad. What did she care about teaching a cramped-up
little school for some bewhiskered old man. She was going
to a more expansive region, to a place of greater light.
She tramped along the ridge road enveloped in a dream.
It was all very indefinite, but perhaps for that reason all
the more alluring. A great freshness of spirit came over her.
She walked once more in warmth and hope.
But her heart was jumpy. Never had she felt upset in
just this way. Her recent encounters, especially with the
bewhiskered old chairman of the board of school directors,
had planted doubt in her mind. When she saw the little log
house on the long ridge, and the path that led from it down
to the pottery in the deep hollow, she welcomed the sight.
This was what she was interested in, after all. Yet when
she came to the end of the path at the bottom of the hill,
fear swept through her. What if Luke should not care for
268
THE FIREMAKERS
her help? He had had experience with one woman who had
declared her intention of helping him to get out. Perhaps
that was enough. That solid possibility had never before
entered Val's head.
Still here she was, approaching the low doorway again.
She could at least ask about the vase and tea-things. It was
well that she had made the trip to see the old man. Her call
at the pottery thereby became casual-casual enough to
satisfy her.
She stepped nervously into the doorway. Great quietness
prevailed. Luke was not at his wheel. He was not any-
where else in sight. Had he gone to Green Valley? Was
she glad if he had? She was not certain whether she wanted
to see him or to think it all through again first.
While she stood in the cool silence she heard a great crash
of chucks and broken ware on the smash-pile out by the
kiln. Of course! He said he might be ready to draw kiln
by Monday.
She climbed the ladder to the loft and walked noiselessly
among the pyramidic heaps of unburned ware to the kiln

With a great clatter he was rasping off the rough edges of a
stand of jars which he had taken down. She ducked her

of warm air swept her face.
"How did they come out?" she asked.
He whirled round. His face was ruddy from the warmth
of the kiln, and his spirits were not low. "Why, Val, did
you come clear over here just to see how they came out?"
In his manner there was the deepest gratitude.
"I wanted to see," she answered simply.
This reply satisfied Luke, but it did not quite satisfy Val.
So she went on: "I was over to talk to them about that
THE FIREMAKERS
269
school again, although I hardly expected it would do any

Luke felt her essential purpose in making the trip. "Why,

Val's impetuous self was in charge. She saw no better

"Do you want me to tell you something that will make

"You couldn't do it," he answered, smiling. "But go

She was not so ready as she had felt herself to be. "I've

She picked at the edge of a fruit-jar chyme in a stand of

They've finished me. I can't earn any more money here,

It was too sublime a rapture for kisses. They stood

THE FIREMAKERS
"No. They will understand."
