PART THREE

N
CHAPTER I
OT the least of Luke's and Val's difficulties when
they were married was finding transport for their
possessions. There were no teams of horses resi-
dent in Green Valley. A little removed, the farmers on the
hills and in the river valley had plenty of teams; but the
farmers did not wish to stop seeding and corn-husking for
such inconsequential matters as moving somebody's effects.
Mr. Cale Sturgis, the old man over at the pottery, had, to be
sure, the blind claygrinder and a spring wagon. When, how-
ever, they were brought together in harness-as they ocса-
sionally were both looked as if they might fall to pieces
with a little shaking.
There were other complications. Some of the women who
had had to consult Captain McNab about getting turnips
out of their gardens felt sure that it would be a breach of
martial law to haul things out of the Valley. Still, Luke be-
lieved that if he could find horses and wagon, he would take
a chance with the martial law.
When the problem became a matter of public discussion,
Jab Strohl proposed a solution. Since the beginning of the
strike the Company's mules had had nothing to do. He had
been keeping an eye on them a little for Eli Raynor-to see
that they did not run away or get tangled up in a barbed-
wire fence. If he could borrow a "big wagon" from some-
body, he would try giving a couple of the mules a liftle exer-
cise.
The dilapidated wagon which he found looked very large
behind the two mules. Without doubt it would carry all
that Luke and Val possessed.

THE FIREMAKERS
They loaded in the walnut beds that had stood in the
Valley House for twenty years and more, the bureau, the
wild-cherry stand with the curved legs, the twin baby car-
riage that Winnie had bought, the blue tea-set, the china
dogs, and the vases. Val contributed some kitchen utensils
which her mother had "raked up," some dishes, a new rag
carpet which Sudie had had woven, but had not put down,
and a shelf of books. "No people are just what they ought
to be," Sudie averred, "unless they have carpet on the floor."
She thought, too, that Val would find the books useful be-
fore the end of the winter. "They keep people out of mis-
chief-and out of the dumps."
All the Valley, since there was little else to do, came out
in the middle of the morning to attend the departure. Jab
put the twins in their outgrown carriage where it was
crowded in at the front of the wagon and told them with
much good-natured profanity that he was about to take
them for a long ride. They would have to behave them-
selves, he declared, smiling to his ears, because he carried
a blacksnake whip which made a hell of a crack. In order
to convince them, he cracked the whip with such a sting of
sound that all the boys and girls who were clustered about
the wagon jumped in fright, and one of the mules lifted its
left ear ever so slightly, as if it had heard a familiar sound.
The twins looked at him in silent awe, yet with a trace of a
smile, for they discerned something in his battered face that
was kindly.
Luke and Val meant to walk. They were sure they could
keep up with the wagon. But Jab made places for them
high on the pile of beds and bedding, and insisted that they
ride. "It is more like," he declared, "for the bride and
groom to be hauled."
"Well, there goes your last one, Mr. Boyd," Pewee Dun-
kle observed when Val climbed to the top of the pile and
settled herself in a soft feather tick.
THE FIREMAKERS
281
"Yes," Nathan answered, looking away a little sadly, "my
last one." He did not wish to end in that key, so he added,
"But she's got a mighty fine man for a husband."
"Now you've said it," Hi Crumblett declared, by way of
joining in. "There never was a finer fellow in Green Valley,
unless it was his father-or his father-in-law."
Nathan's bleak face colored a little at the compliment,
but he laughed with the others.
While they talked, Jab tested the clothesline with which
things were tied on. Then he took his seat on the front edge
of the wagon-bed, level with the backs of the mules. Just
when they were ready to start, Mrs. Canaday came running
out from the kitchen with a sordid-looking armful of cloth-
ing. "You forgot your diggers, Luke," she called.
"Don't want them," Luke answered. "Anybody that
needs them is welcome to take them along." He felt gener-
ous in spirit and easy in speech.
Amid good-bys from the hungry-looking but cheerful
neighbors who filled the road, they moved off.
As they approached the railroad crossing by the station,
Captain McNab, who had been sitting out in front of head-
quarters, arose and walked down the path toward the track.
Jab saw him coming. "Get out of this, God-damn you!"
he yelled as if he were unaware of the captain's approach,
and gave the mules such provocative cuts with his black-
snake whip that they immediately broke into a trot.
Captain McNab stood on the bank and watched them go
by. While the mules trotted on the full length of the
downward stretch beyond the track-Jab smiled, and
hummed a scarecrow of a little tune, and amused himself by
cutting the tops off faded goldenrod that chanced to lean
out over the edge of the road within reach of the cracker of
his whip.
They went slowly, very slowly up the hills, but swiftly
down. Jab said if they were good mules they could keep
282
THE FIREMAKERS
out of the way of the wagon without his bothering with the
lock. Anyway, the lever was under the edge of something
or other and hard to get at.
Far over in the hills, where the fields were fringed with
sumac, and the wooded ridges and hollows were brilliant in
reds and yellows and browns, Val could not sit still on her
feather bed. She must walk. She could enjoy more of the
landscape when she was active. And Luke walked with her.
On the way up a hill they always passed the wagon; but it
always passed them on the way down.
As the mules tugged at their heavy load on the ridge that
led up to the little log house, Jab gave so many warnings
of his approach that both the Sturgises were in the front
yard awaiting the wagon when it arrived.
"I've the smokehouse scrubbed and clean," Mrs. Sturgis
informed them. "You can put your things in there till we're
out of the way. They'll keep nice and dry in there."
Her chief attention, however, went at once to the twins.
"You little dears!" she exclaimed in grandmotherly good-
ness as Jab stoutly handed them to her. "Wasn't it a long
ride? And ain't you as hungry as squirrels?" But they
only looked in speechless bewilderment.
It was dinner time and Jab accepted Mrs. Sturgis's invita-
tion to stay and take pot luck with them. It was the best
luck Jab had come upon since the middle of August or soon
thereafter.
He said little while he ate. He was accustomed to doing
one thing at a time-not more. Mrs. Sturgis was busy, any-
how, talking to the twins, and mashing potato and mincing
some chicken for them.
When the meal was over she slipped some potatoes and
great turnips and solid red tomatoes into an old paper flour
sack and put the big bundle in the corner of the wagon-bed.
"We'll be off soon," she explained in magnanimous effort to
THE FIREMAKERS
283
avoid the appearance of charity, "and you might as well
have them as not."
Luke walked to the wagon with Jab when he was ready to
return.
"I mustn't forget to pay the driver," Luke remarked, by
way of introducing the subject.
Jab looked at him as if he were on the point of a violent
explosion. Then he playfully but vigorously poked him in
the ribs and said in gentle voice: "To hell with your pay!
Can't I give you a little weddin' present if I want to?"
And there the matter rested when they said good-by and
good-luck to each other a half-dozen times and Jab drove
off down the road and Luke went back into the house.
Mrs. Sturgis had been showing Val about. The house
consisted of a large, square, log part a story-and-a-half high,
fronting the east, with a lean-to kitchen of deal boards built
on behind. Since this kitchen was larger than Mrs. Sturgis
required, she had a board partition built across one end of it
in order to have a combination extra bedroom and store-
room. It was a depository for sacks of flour, jars of dried
apples, a ten-gallon breadjar, popcorn on the ear, dried
peppers, bunches of dried catnip and mullein, and clothes
that were out of season.
The chinked walls and the oak-joisted ceiling of the front
room, as well as the walls and ceiling of the kitchen and
"little room," were whitewashed with clay. Mrs. Sturgis
knew just where to find lumps of clay in the pen at the mill-
shed that would make a white mixture with no bluish streaks
in it. When storms beat too persistently against the sides of
the house and softened the mortar in the chinks, the water
sometimes came through and spread in a great discoloring
splotch on the whitened wall, which then required special
treatment to restore the whiteness.
A steep, ladder-like stairway in one of the corners made
by the projection of the huge fireplace into the room, led to
284
THE FIREMAKERS
a cavernous chamber above with one tiny window high in
the south gable. In the light under the window stood a low
chest of some kind, and in the corner where the naked roof
sloped down to within a few feet of the dark floor of enor-
mously wide oak boards, there was a bed large enough for
a giant. In the opposite end, where the stone chimney
pushed upward to the roof, the corner that matched the one
where the stairway came up was full of sweet-potatoes
nicely spread out on heavy papers. Since the snow often
enough sifted through the curled lap-shingles of the roof,
Mrs. Sturgis thought it useless to try to keep the walls of
this room white. This was the room where Luke and Val
were to stay until the Sturgises got off to Kansas.
Downstairs again, Val scrutinized the walls and the floor
of oak boards, each sixteen or twenty inches wide. She
thought she preferred wall paper to whitewash. And would
there be enough carpet?
When the tour of inspection was over, and Mrs. Sturgis
had shown Val how to manipulate the door of the dirt cave
built for winter warmth against the outer side of the chimney
at the north end of the house, she said cheerfully and with
a very wise, reminiscent expression of face: "Now see here;
if you are interested in pretty things, why don't you have
Luke take you over to Wiggam's Glory this afternoon? I
can take care of the twins. We'll be packin' off to Kansas
soon enough, and I'm not meanin' to try to do much before
I go. I'd love to look after the little dears. And goodness
knows, I know how. So run along. I told somebody only
yesterday that we're never young but once. It will rain
some of these days, too, and down will come all the pretty
leaves, and there'll be little left to look at."
Val was interested. "I've heard Luke mention Wiggam's
Glory."
"Yes, he knows all about it. He can tell you. It's right
over there-across the big hollow-by the forks of the road.
THE FIREMAKERS
285
There-see that glimpse of something white through the
trees? That's the place. It's not a half-mile straight across
as the crow flies, but you have to walk round by the ridge
road to get there.
"Maybe there'll be other things you'll want to see, too.
So run along. Take your time. Just so you're back in time
for your suppers."
She commanded them both as if they were children, and
they liked her for it.
The high ridge road led round to Wiggam's Glory in a
great unsteady semi-circle. They were on top of the world.
As far as their eyes could see were brilliant woodlands,
green pastures, white houses. No dirty red rivulets spread
over the lower hillsides from mine openings. No smoke
obscured their view. The air was fresh. It was experi-
encing a natural life.
There were many questions to be answered. Why did
one maple become perfectly yellow, and the one beside it
perfectly red? Would somebody tell them that? And why
did a hickory turn brown and the dogwood under it turn the
deepest of clarets? And why did the willows and black-
oaks, despite all the early onslaughts of frost, stay as green
as if it were midsummer?
"Oh, I know what I'm going to do," Val exclaimed in a
somewhat subdued voice. "I'm going to paint these trees..
There! Just look at that!"
"Sure! Of course you can!" Luke answered in a tone
that expressed unlimited confidence in Val's ability.
They stood where they could look along three or four
ranges of hillsides that were dotted with ancient maples.
"I bought a picture for my school"-and her voice stuck
a little at the word "that looks like that. It was a copy of
a painting by a man named Inness."
It was difficult to do anything but look. Finally Val said:
286
THE FIREMAKERS
"I've no colors to speak of. I wonder if Mr. Cator over at
Oakdale couldn't send me some? I'll write and ask."
They walked enraptured, sensitive to the subtlest color,
the slightest sound, the gentlest breath of air.
Before they knew it, they were at the forks of the road.
In a park-like enclosure that sloped abruptly eastward from
the ridge road, a gas well sent a roaring flame high in the
air. In the full light of day, the flame was bluish and un-
earthly.
"Wiggam thought he'd get an oil well," Luke explained to
Val, "but when they were down nine hundred feet they
struck gas, and he called them off. He said he'd just throw
a little light on the world by letting her burn."
They walked along an unfinished esplanade among the
locust trees. In an open space between two trees, a gro-
tesque sandstone figure of Abraham Lincoln towered aloft.
"What do you think of that?" Luke asked, not without a
touch of humor.
"But his legs are too long."
"Well, then, how about this one?"
They turned to a similar sandstone representation of
General Grant.
"But his legs are too short. See where his waistline is!"
"Take your choice," Luke said, laughing one of his quiet
little laughs. "You'll not be tramping on any toes of mine.
I didn't make them."
Under some of the statues the ground had settled un-
evenly, and the personages leaned in undignified fashion.
Through some of the soft sandstone faces, too, the rain had
begun to cut disfiguring lines.
"How about this one?" Luke asked once more. This time
they came up to a representation of Jesus. He was extend-
ing his hand toward the multitude, and his hand was not
empty. It contained a laboriously modeled cake of soap.
THE FIREMAKERS
287
Below the figure was an inscription: What This World
Needs Worst.
"Was he thinking of coal mines or of people's souls?"
Val asked, and they both smiled at the strange quirk in Wig-
gam's mind.
They walked on. At the end of the park, with a path
through the grass to it, was a low, white marble figure of a
girl half kneeling, half sitting, her hands clasped between
her knees, her eyes gazing into the distance in rapt contem-
plation.
"Why, it's beautiful!" Val exclaimed. "Too beautiful for
words!"
She walked round the figure, scarcely able to believe that
she was not dreaming. "Why-why could you believe there
was anything as beautiful as this anywhere?"
She was almost as much entranced as the figure before
her. "But did he make this, too?"
"No," Luke replied. "This is the one he didn't make.
But it started the trouble for him."
"Then who did make it?"
"Some Frenchman who carved the thing-a-ma-jigs on the
new courthouse over at Centerville. Got the marble from a
tombstone man. Made this at odd times when it rained or
when it was too cold to work. He thought he could sell it
to Centerville for the park in front of the courthouse. But
the town wouldn't buy it. Wiggam heard about it, and went
over to see it. He said he'd buy it. It cost him all the
money he had, they say, and it took three teams of horses to
get it up the hill."
The season's grass had grown high, but Val read through
the top edges of it on the base of the figure: "Jeanne
d'Arc."
"Why we studied about her over at Oakdale," she said, as
she perused the strange words.
"Pretty nearly had a row," Luke went on, far removed

"People got after him and tried to make him take it down."
"Take it down? What for?"
"They said it was indecent. Showed the human shape

"Why, for goodness' sakes! Why-it's the loveliest thing
I ever saw."
"Of course it is."

"I guess he didn't do anything. He told them-that's
what people say that if they made him take it away be-

Val regarded the figure once more as if it had come
straight from heaven. "You could make a little one like it
out of clay if you knew how."
"Maybe I'll know how some day."
Luke continued the story of Wiggam. "They said they
were coming over here some night and knock her head off
with sledge hammers. But they never did. Never touched
a hair of her head. See, she's just as perfect as the day he
put her here. Ever see anything whiter?
"He spent most of his time up here, they say. Then he
decided he'd be a sculptor himself. He had no marble, but
there was plenty of sandstone down in the edge of the hill
and he worked for weeks getting back into the hill far
enough to find blocks that had no seams in them. Then he
spent his time in seeing what he could make. Worked in his
barn. At last he started putting up his own things. They
don't look very well beside what the Frenchman made out
of the marble, do they? But I guess he had some fun while
he was at it."
"Where is he now?" Val asked.
"Mighty hard to say. He's dead. Died last year."
"Whose is it now?"

"A man named Holcomb-Larry Holcomb's. He's great
on picnics. He invites everybody to come and use the
grounds. Always manages to be around, himself, and join
in about meal time. See the seats down there."
Their eyes looked inevitably beyond the rows of planks
under the trees, down to the deep wooded hollow and to the
wooded hillside beyond. Across on the ridge just above the
line of treetops stood the log house with the whitewashed
kitchen.
"You could walk on them," Val said, referring to the tree-
tops, "they are so softlike and smooth." She stood for a
moment and contemplated the silent undulations of autumn
coloring. "Let's go back that way-down through."
"Better go down the path to the pot-shop and then up the
ridge," Luke suggested. "The shop is just about there"-
and he indicated a spot not to be seen because of the edge
of the woods. "Pretty thick undergrowth if you try to go
straight across."
"No, let's go straight across," Val insisted.
"All right. But I'm telling you in advance that you'll get
scratched."
"All right, then I'll be scratched."
And straight across they went down through a neglected
meadow where briars had grown head high, and goldenrod
in its efforts to keep in the sun had stretched up almost as
high as the briars; on down through clumps of papaw
bushes; on through other low tree-growth which presented a
perfectly even leaf surface when viewed from the park, but
which was irregular enough when one tried to go through it;
and on, at last, into the high timber of the hollow where
there was open space among the trees, and where a tiny
stream, fed the year round by unfailing springs, made the
slenderest little gurgles imaginable, or stood in quiet, clear
pools where fallen leaves rode motionlessly.
They stopped. They stood panting for a moment, en-
290
THE FIREMAKERS
tranced by the calm, by the repose of the great gray beeches
that covered the narrow strip of land along the stream and
reached far up the hillside, by the cleanness of the ground
everywhere under the trees. Birds on their way southward
darted leisurely among the lower branches close to the
stream. A gray squirrel, hopping along with intermittent
rustlings in the dying leaves, terrified to find himself con-
fronted by such gigantic beings in the heart of what he had
supposed was his exclusive domain, rushed up a tree and
away, crashing from branch to branch as if his greatest
enemy were within reach of his tail.
They laughed at the squirrel's fright and then stood silent
again.
"It's unbelievable," Val said at last. "It's like a picture.
So near Green Valley, and yet I never knew it was here."
They climbed the hillside slowly, for their feet slipped on
the smooth beech leaves. From time to time they stopped
to look about and to catch their breath.
"That anything could be as clean as this!" Val said.
"One thing we've got plenty of over here," Luke re-
marked. "Plenty of clean space."
"Yes."
"Sure there's not too much space? Sure you won't get
tired of it?"
"Such a question!" she exclaimed, with contempt for it
and affection for Luke. "Whatever do you have in your
head, anyway?"
Then they walked along, diagonally up the hillside, Val
steadying herself against his arm.
Luke came back to the subject of importance. "You
know we're gambling for life."
"Of course I know it. I shouldn't be surprised if I
thought of that before you did."
She gave him a swaying push to emphasize her words.
THE FIREMAKERS
291
"I believe we'll win," he said. "That's the way I've
always looked at it. But we're in for some hard going."
"It will be easy."
"Easy when you've only one dollar and it's about half
gone?"
"Yes, easy."
Abruptly they came out into the pasture on the ridge not
two hundred yards from the house. The west side of every-
thing was bright in the late afternoon sun. Mrs. Sturgis's
whitewashed kitchen stood hopefully among the fruit trees.
"Just in time for supper," she said when they came up.
"We don't wait," addressing herself to Val, "till it's dark
to eat our suppers. It's nice to stir around a little after-
wards and look at things. Did you have a nice time? I
knew you would. You'd better go some place again to-
morrow. It won't stay like this forever. Me and the twins
just had a lovely time. You'd better see all you can while
you're foot-loose."
CHAPTER II
UKE and Val took over the responsibilities of the log
sooner than they had expected. The
Sturgises, upon the urgent request of their daughter
in Kansas, decided to leave early. When they came face
to face with packing, they decided also to take few things
with them. Why pack so much along when their daughter
and her husband lived in affluence? As a result of this deci-
sion, Luke and Val saw their responsibilities not only hur-
rying but mounting. They bought the big wood-stove in
the kitchen, the cow, the Plymouth Rock chickens, and the
three or four scolding guineas. When this strictly financial
transaction had been completed, Mrs. Sturgis threw in the
sweet-potatoes upstairs, the dried fruit in the little room
off the kitchen, some seed beans, and various other odd-
ments which she thought it would be a trouble to move.
Mr. Sturgis gave Luke many final words of counsel about
the craft of the potter. Every day while he was active in
preparation for his long journey to his daughter's, he thought
of something which he ought to tell Luke, and forthwith
dropped whatever he chanced to be doing and hurried down
the long ridge to the pot-shop before he should forget. He
showed Luke how to shape the mouth of every kind of piece
of ware that he thought Luke might ever wish to make. Не
explained how much longer the kiln would have to be fired
if he put large-sized jars "tens" and "twenties"-in it. He
made out a table of weights for the clay that should go into
the balls for all the different sizes of all kinds of pieces,
so that Luke would have to waste no time experimenting.
He marked notches, too, in the slender fingers of the gauges
292
THE FIREMAKERS
293
to indicate how they should be set for the various shapes
and sizes of ware.
Then one forenoon a man with a stout-looking team of
roan horses drove over from Culbertson and loaded in the
trunks and boxes, and eventually the Sturgises themselves.
Mrs. Sturgis was the first to take her place in the springy
seat of the big farm wagon. While she waited, her best
coat on, her bonnet snug on her substantial gray head, she
gave Val some last directions about feeding children in win-
ter. She hated to leave the house, yes. But it was better
to go now before the rains set in and made everything sad-
looking. And it might rain almost any time, Cale thought.
So it was better to go now. With people in charge who
cared for things, it was not quite so hard.
Mr. Sturgis hustled about till the last minute, thinking
of all sorts of things that he had not thought of before for
years. "First time we've moved for over thirty years," he
explained as he rushed nervously about, "and I guess I've
kind o' set. I start thinkin' again when I try to tear loose."
He gave Mrs. Sturgis plenty of time for her directions to
Val.
After half an hour he climbed up beside her and the
driver. The seat was crowded; but his high, cow-licked silk
hat, which he wore once or twice a year, and his green-black
frock coat, which he wore just as often, gave dignity-or
at least picturesqueness-to the otherwise squeezed-up ef-
fect.
It would be glorious, Luke and Val had thought, to have
the place all to themselves. But when the wagon disap-
peared over the hill toward Culbertson, and they turned to
go into the house their house-they were both seized with
a sudden great loneliness. They feared to tell each other.
But their faces could not hide their feeling.
"Aha!" somebody seemed to be saying in the hollow
house. "You are close up against your dream now, are
294
THE FIREMAKERS
you? And what are you going to do about it? You wanted
to get out; you wanted to untangle yourself. Well, here
you are. Aren't you untangled? Can't you go ahead just
to suit yourself? Make your pottery, if you like. Paint a
house full of pictures if you can. Nobody is clutching your
arm. Nobody is so much as watching you-no one except
myself, and they say I look on with a fine indifference."
They would work off the mood by plunging into their own
concerns. They would carry out the things they did not
want, and they would carry in their own. They would esti-
mate the number of rolls of wall paper the large front room
would require, and they would cut the carpet and have it
ready to put down.
At noon they stopped long enough to have a bite to eat-
something which Mrs. Sturgis had left-and then they re-
turned to their rearranging, upsetting, carrying out. They
would have, it seemed, a smokehouse full of the Sturgises'
cast-off things to take the place of their own which they car-
ried in.
In the late afternoon the sun disappeared slowly in a soft
haze. The haze became a dull cloud. Before night, rain
began to fall. It rained for a time very gently, and the
drops came straight down. Then it rained generously, and
the fresh southeast wind sent the drops and the leaves scur-
rying through the air.
Night seemed to come on swiftly. It brought heavier
gusts of wind, heavier downpourings of rain. After they
had eaten another somewhat improvised meal, they sat by
the warm kitchen stove and listened. In a lull of the wind
they heard a steady drip, drip, drip of water somewhere
upstairs. Then the drips came so close together that they
were undistinguishable. Luke took the lamp that stood on
the kitchen table, and he and Val went in the direction of
the sound. The water was coming through the roof in a
tiny stream. Since they could not find just where it came
THE FIREMAKERS
295
through, they could only deal with it after it arrived. A
large jar would catch the drip for several hours. "Hard on
your carpet," Luke observed a little grimly, "if I can't get
that stopped."
They wondered

what was going
They covered the sleeping twins in their trundle bed and
sat once more by the pleasant kitchen fire.
when the rain and the wind would cease.
where the Sturgises were. They wondered
on in Green Valley. The man who had come for the Stur-
gises had brought the report, current in Culbertson the night
before, that the strike was settled, and that the soldiery had
been withdrawn. Bogart had won.
"Your soldier boy has departed," Luke said chaffingly.
"You can now return to your home town."
"And you can stay out of jail."
They smiled a little. It had been a weary day.
The world which greeted them the next morning was
not the one they had seen the day before. All night it had
rained. The heavy drops battered off the leaves or loosened
them for the wind. And now a chill, gusty blast from the
northwest was doing all that the rain had left undone. The
world was wet, bedraggled, disconsolate. The brilliance of
a few days ago was now only a vast smudge.
They stood together at the kitchen door and looked across
the deep, wooded hollow toward Wiggam's Glory. "It has
washed all the color out, Val," Luke observed, not deject-
edly, but tenderly.
"I'm afraid I'll have to wait till next year to paint."
Luke wanted to be encouraging. "We can get your
paints and have everything ready by that time. Spring
ought to be a good time-beeches, and redbud, and dog-
wood blossoms."
By the middle of the afternoon the wind had carried the
last of the clouds away. The hole in the roof was easily
repaired, and there seemed to be no others. Luke felt bet-

THE FIREMAKERS
out of the way of the wagon without his bothering with the
lock. Anyway, the lever was under the edge of something
or other and hard to get at.
Far over in the hills, where the fields were fringed with
sumac, and the wooded ridges and hollows were brilliant in
reds and yellows and browns, Val could not sit still on her
feather bed. She must walk. She could enjoy more of the
landscape when she was active. And Luke walked with her.
On the way up a hill they always passed the wagon; but it
always passed them on the way down.
As the mules tugged at their heavy load on the ridge that
led up to the little log house, Jab gave so many warnings
of his approach that both the Sturgises were in the front
yard awaiting the wagon when it arrived.
"I've the smokehouse scrubbed and clean," Mrs. Sturgis
informed them. "You can put your things in there till we're
out of the way. They'll keep nice and dry in there."
Her chief attention, however, went at once to the twins.
"You little dears!" she exclaimed in grandmotherly good-
ness as Jab stoutly handed them to her. "Wasn't it a long
ride? And ain't you as hungry as squirrels?" But they
only looked in speechless bewilderment.
It was dinner time and Jab accepted Mrs. Sturgis's invita-
tion to stay and take pot luck with them. It was the best
luck Jab had come upon since the middle of August or soon
thereafter.
He said little while he ate. He was accustomed to doing
one thing at a time-not more. Mrs. Sturgis was busy, any-
how, talking to the twins, and mashing potato and mincing
some chicken for them.
When the meal was over she slipped some potatoes and
great turnips and solid red tomatoes into an old paper flour
sack and put the big bundle in the corner of the wagon-bed.
"We'll be off soon," she explained in magnanimous effort to
THE FIREMAKERS
283
avoid the appearance of charity, "and you might as well
have them as not."
Luke walked to the wagon with Jab when he was ready to
return.
"I mustn't forget to pay the driver," Luke remarked, by
way of introducing the subject.
Jab looked at him as if he were on the point of a violent
explosion. Then he playfully but vigorously poked him in
the ribs and said in gentle voice: "To hell with your pay!
Can't I give you a little weddin' present if I want to?"
And there the matter rested when they said good-by and
good-luck to each other a half-dozen times and Jab drove
off down the road and Luke went back into the house.
Mrs. Sturgis had been showing Val about. The house
consisted of a large, square, log part a story-and-a-half high,
fronting the east, with a lean-to kitchen of deal boards built
on behind. Since this kitchen was larger than Mrs. Sturgis
required, she had a board partition built across one end of it
in order to have a combination extra bedroom and store-
room. It was a depository for sacks of flour, jars of dried
apples, a ten-gallon breadjar, popcorn on the ear, dried
peppers, bunches of dried catnip and mullein, and clothes
that were out of season.
The chinked walls and the oak-joisted ceiling of the front
room, as well as the walls and ceiling of the kitchen and
"little room," were whitewashed with clay. Mrs. Sturgis
knew just where to find lumps of clay in the pen at the mill-
shed that would make a white mixture with no bluish streaks
in it. When storms beat too persistently against the sides of
the house and softened the mortar in the chinks, the water
sometimes came through and spread in a great discoloring
splotch on the whitened wall, which then required special
treatment to restore the whiteness.
A steep, ladder-like stairway in one of the corners made
by the projection of the huge fireplace into the room, led to
284
THE FIREMAKERS
a cavernous chamber above with one tiny window high in
the south gable. In the light under the window stood a low
chest of some kind, and in the corner where the naked roof
sloped down to within a few feet of the dark floor of enor-
mously wide oak boards, there was a bed large enough for
a giant. In the opposite end, where the stone chimney
pushed upward to the roof, the corner that matched the one
where the stairway came up was full of sweet-potatoes
nicely spread out on heavy papers. Since the snow often
enough sifted through the curled lap-shingles of the roof,
Mrs. Sturgis thought it useless to try to keep the walls of
this room white. This was the room where Luke and Val
were to stay until the Sturgises got off to Kansas.
Downstairs again, Val scrutinized the walls and the floor
of oak boards, each sixteen or twenty inches wide. She
thought she preferred wall paper to whitewash. And would
there be enough carpet?
When the tour of inspection was over, and Mrs. Sturgis
had shown Val how to manipulate the door of the dirt cave
built for winter warmth against the outer side of the chimney
at the north end of the house, she said cheerfully and with
a very wise, reminiscent expression of face: "Now see here;
if you are interested in pretty things, why don't you have
Luke take you over to Wiggam's Glory this afternoon? I
can take care of the twins. We'll be packin' off to Kansas
soon enough, and I'm not meanin' to try to do much before
I go. I'd love to look after the little dears. And goodness
knows, I know how. So run along. I told somebody only
yesterday that we're never young but once. It will rain
some of these days, too, and down will come all the pretty
leaves, and there'll be little left to look at."
Val was interested. "I've heard Luke mention Wiggam's
Glory."
"Yes, he knows all about it. He can tell you. It's right
over there across the big hollow-by the forks of the road.
THE FIREMAKERS
285
There-see that glimpse of something white through the
trees? That's the place. It's not a half-mile straight across
as the crow flies, but you have to walk round by the ridge
road to get there.
"Maybe there'll be other things you'll want to see, too.
So run along. Take your time. Just so you're back in time
for your suppers."
She commanded them both as if they were children, and
they liked her for it.
The high ridge road led round to Wiggam's Glory in a
great unsteady semi-circle. They were on top of the world.
As far as their eyes could see were brilliant woodlands,
green pastures, white houses. No dirty red rivulets spread
over the lower hillsides from mine openings. No smoke
obscured their view. The air was fresh. It was experi-
encing a natural life.
There were many questions to be answered. Why did
one maple become perfectly yellow, and the one beside it
perfectly red? Would somebody tell them that? And why
did a hickory turn brown and the dogwood under it turn the
deepest of clarets? And why did the willows and black-
oaks, despite all the early onslaughts of frost, stay as green
as if it were midsummer?
"Oh, I know what I'm going to do," Val exclaimed in a
somewhat subdued voice. "I'm going to paint these trees...
There! Just look at that!"
"Sure! Of course you can!" Luke answered in a tone
that expressed unlimited confidence in Val's ability.
They stood where they could look along three or four
ranges of hillsides that were dotted with ancient maples.
"I bought a picture for my school"-and her voice stuck
a little at the word "that looks like that. It was a copy of
a painting by a man named Inness."
It was difficult to do anything but look. Finally Val said:
286
THE FIREMAKERS
"I've no colors to speak of. I wonder if Mr. Cator over at
Oakdale couldn't send me some? I'll write and ask."
They walked enraptured, sensitive to the subtlest color,
the slightest sound, the gentlest breath of air.
Before they knew it, they were at the forks of the road.
In a park-like enclosure that sloped abruptly eastward from
the ridge road, a gas well sent a roaring flame high in the
air. In the full light of day, the flame was bluish and un-
earthly.
"Wiggam thought he'd get an oil well," Luke explained to
Val, "but when they were down nine hundred feet they
struck gas, and he called them off. He said he'd just throw
a little light on the world by letting her burn."
They walked along an unfinished esplanade among the
locust trees. In an open space between two trees, a gro-
tesque sandstone figure of Abraham Lincoln towered aloft.
"What do you think of that?" Luke asked, not without a
touch of humor.
"But his legs are too long."
"Well, then, how about this one?"
They turned to a similar sandstone representation of
General Grant.
"But his legs are too short. See where his waistline is!"
"Take your choice," Luke said, laughing one of his quiet
little laughs. "You'll not be tramping on any toes of mine.
I didn't make them."
Under some of the statues the ground had settled un-
evenly, and the personages leaned in undignified fashion.
Through some of the soft sandstone faces, too, the rain had
begun to cut disfiguring lines.
"How about this one?" Luke asked once more. This time
they came up to a representation of Jesus. He was extend-
ing his hand toward the multitude, and his hand was not
empty. It contained a laboriously modeled cake of soap.
THE FIREMAKERS
287
Below the figure was an inscription: What This World
Needs Worst.
"Was he thinking of coal mines or of people's souls?"

They walked on. At the end of the park, with a path

"Why, it's beautiful!" Val exclaimed. "Too beautiful for

She walked round the figure, scarcely able to believe that

She was almost as much entranced as the figure before
her. "But did he make this, too?"
"No," Luke replied. "This is the one he didn't make.
But it started the trouble for him."
"Then who did make it?"
"Some Frenchman who carved the thing-a-ma-jigs on the
new courthouse over at Centerville. Got the marble from a
tombstone man. Made this at odd times when it rained or
when it was too cold to work. He thought he could sell it
to Centerville for the park in front of the courthouse. But
the town wouldn't buy it. Wiggam heard about it, and went
over to see it. He said he'd buy it. It cost him all the
money he had, they say, and it took three teams of horses to
get it up the hill."
The season's grass had grown high, but Val read through
the top edges of it on the base of the figure: "Jeanne
d'Arc."
"Why we studied about her over at Oakdale," she said, as
she perused the strange words.
"Pretty nearly had a row," Luke went on, far removed

THE FIREMAKERS
from Val's interest in the name's historical significance.
"People got after him and tried to make him take it down."

"Why, for goodness' sakes! Why-it's the loveliest thing

"He spent most of his time up here, they say. Then he
decided he'd be a sculptor himself. He had no marble, but

don't look very well beside what the Frenchman made out

"Where is he now?" Val asked.
"Mighty hard to say. He's dead. Died last year."
"Whose is it now?"

"A man named Holcomb-Larry Holcomb's. He's great

And straight across they went down through a neglected

pools where fallen leaves rode motionlessly.
They stopped. They stood panting for a moment, en-

THE FIREMAKERS
tranced by the calm, by the repose of the great gray beeches
that covered the narrow strip of land along the stream and

They laughed at the squirrel's fright and then stood silent

"It's unbelievable," Val said at last. "It's like a picture.
So near Green Valley, and yet I never knew it was here."
They climbed the hillside slowly, for their feet slipped on

"Yes."
"Sure there's not too much space? Sure you won't get

"Of course I know it. I shouldn't be surprised if I

She gave him a swaying push to emphasize her words.

"I believe we'll win," he said. "That's the way I've

Abruptly they came out into the pasture on the ridge not

"Just in time for supper," she said when they came up.
"We don't wait," addressing herself to Val, "till it's dark
to eat our suppers. It's nice to stir around a little after-

knew you would. You'd better go some place again to-
morrow. It won't stay like this forever. Me and the twins
just had a lovely time. You'd better see all you can while
you're foot-loose."
CHAPTER II
UKE and Val took over the responsibilities of the log
sooner than they had expected. The
Sturgises, upon the urgent request of their daughter
in Kansas, decided to leave early. When they came face
to face with packing, they decided also to take few things
with them. Why pack so much along when their daughter
and her husband lived in affluence? As a result of this deci-
sion, Luke and Val saw their responsibilities not only hur-
rying but mounting. They bought the big wood-stove in
the kitchen, the cow, the Plymouth Rock chickens, and the
three or four scolding guineas. When this strictly financial
transaction had been completed, Mrs. Sturgis threw in the
sweet-potatoes upstairs, the dried fruit in the little room
off the kitchen, some seed beans, and various other odd-
ments which she thought it would be a trouble to move.
Mr. Sturgis gave Luke many final words of counsel about
the craft of the potter. Every day while he was active in
preparation for his long journey to his daughter's, he thought
of something which he ought to tell Luke, and forthwith
dropped whatever he chanced to be doing and hurried down
the long ridge to the pot-shop before he should forget. He
showed Luke how to shape the mouth of every kind of piece
of ware that he thought Luke might ever wish to make. He
explained how much longer the kiln would have to be fired
if he put large-sized jars "tens" and "twenties"-in it. He
made out a table of weights for the clay that should go into
the balls for all the different sizes of all kinds of pieces,
so that Luke would have to waste no time experimenting.
He marked notches, too, in the slender fingers of the gauges
292
THE FIREMAKERS
293
to indicate how they should be set for the various shapes
and sizes of ware.
Then one forenoon a man with a stout-looking team of
roan horses drove over from Culbertson and loaded in the
trunks and boxes, and eventually the Sturgises themselves.
Mrs. Sturgis was the first to take her place in the springy
seat of the big farm wagon. While she waited, her best
coat on, her bonnet snug on her substantial gray head, she
gave Val some last directions about feeding children in win-
ter. She hated to leave the house, yes. But it was better
to go now before the rains set in and made everything sad-
looking. And it might rain almost any time, Cale thought.
So it was better to go now. With people in charge who
cared for things, it was not quite so hard.
Mr. Sturgis hustled about till the last minute, thinking
of all sorts of things that he had not thought of before for
years. "First time we've moved for over thirty years," he
explained as he rushed nervously about, "and I guess I've
kind o' set. I start thinkin' again when I try to tear loose."
He gave Mrs. Sturgis plenty of time for her directions to
Val.
After half an hour he climbed up beside her and the
driver. The seat was crowded; but his high, cow-licked silk
hat, which he wore once or twice a year, and his green-black
frock coat, which he wore just as often, gave dignity-or
at least picturesqueness-to the otherwise squeezed-up ef-
fect.
It would be glorious, Luke and Val had thought, to have
the place all to themselves. But when the wagon disap-
peared over the hill toward Culbertson, and they turned to
go into the house-their house-they were both seized with
a sudden great loneliness. They feared to tell each other.
But their faces could not hide their feeling.
"Aha!" somebody seemed to be saying in the hollow
house. "You are close up against your dream now, are
294
THE FIREMAKERS
you? And what are you going to do about it? You wanted
to get out; you wanted to untangle yourself. Well, here
you are. Aren't you untangled? Can't you go ahead just
to suit yourself? Make your pottery, if you like. Paint a
house full of pictures if you can. Nobody is clutching your
arm. Nobody is so much as watching you-no one except
myself, and they say I look on with a fine indifference."
They would work off the mood by plunging into their own
concerns. They would carry out the things they did not
want, and they would carry in their own. They would esti-
mate the number of rolls of wall paper the large front room
would require, and they would cut the carpet and have it
ready to put down.
At noon they stopped long enough to have a bite to eat-
something which Mrs. Sturgis had left-and then they re-
turned to their rearranging, upsetting, carrying out. They
would have, it seemed, a smokehouse full of the Sturgises'
cast-off things to take the place of their own which they car-
ried in.
In the late afternoon the sun disappeared slowly in a soft
haze. The haze became a dull cloud. Before night, rain
began to fall. It rained for a time very gently, and the
drops came straight down. Then it rained generously, and
the fresh southeast wind sent the drops and the leaves scur-
rying through the air.
Night seemed to come on swiftly. It brought heavier
gusts of wind, heavier downpourings of rain. After they
had eaten another somewhat improvised meal, they sat by
the warm kitchen stove and listened. In a lull of the wind
they heard a steady drip, drip, drip of water somewhere
upstairs. Then the drips came so close together that they
were undistinguishable. Luke took the lamp that stood on
the kitchen table, and he and Val went in the direction of
the sound. The water was coming through the roof in a
tiny stream. Since they could not find just where it came
THE FIREMAKERS
295
through, they could only deal with it after it arrived. A
large jar would catch the drip for several hours. "Hard on
your carpet," Luke observed a little grimly, "if I can't get
that stopped."
They covered the sleeping twins in their trundle bed and
sat once more by the pleasant kitchen fire. They wondered
when the rain and the wind would cease. They wondered
where the Sturgises were. They wondered what was going
on in Green Valley. The man who had come for the Stur-
gises had brought the report, current in Culbertson the night
before, that the strike was settled, and that the soldiery had
been withdrawn. Bogart had won.
"Your soldier boy has departed," Luke said chaffingly.
"You can now return to your home town."
"And you can stay out of jail."
They smiled a little. It had been a weary day.
The world which greeted them the next morning was
not the one they had seen the day before. All night it had
rained. The heavy drops battered off the leaves or loosened
them for the wind. And now a chill, gusty blast from the
northwest was doing all that the rain had left undone. The
world was wet, bedraggled, disconsolate. The brilliance of
a few days ago was now only a vast smudge.
They stood together at the kitchen door and looked across
the deep, wooded hollow toward Wiggam's Glory. "It has
washed all the color out, Val," Luke observed, not deject-
edly, but tenderly.
"I'm afraid I'll have to wait till next year to paint."
Luke wanted to be encouraging. "We can get your
paints and have everything ready by that time. Spring
ought to be a good time-beeches, and redbud, and dog-
wood blossoms."
By the middle of the afternoon the wind had carried the
last of the clouds away. The hole in the roof was easily
repaired, and there seemed to be no others. Luke felt bet-
296
THE FIREMAKERS
ter. And Val could not tell how much better she felt.
Among the maples that stood naked or half-naked, an oc-
casional tree remained clothed in the palest of fairy yel-
lows. The oaks were still defiant.
Next summer! Next summer Val could paint, and Luke
could sell ware, and they could make further plans for the
future. From one summer to the next-that was the way
to count the ensuing two or three years. The odds and ends
in between summers might have some coherence or they
might not. They would be lumped together to bridge the
time from one ware season to another.
Next summer! When Luke went each morning to the
little pot-shop, the stretch of months till summer seemed al-
most longer than he could imagine. Each day in itself
seemed long. He had plenty of time for everything. So
he went up home each day for dinner. He stopped his work
early in the afternoon, too, and helped Val with carpet and
wall paper and windows. "She must be content," he told
himself. But there was another reason: he found health
in her presence. She knew so clearly what she wanted, and
moved so directly toward it!
There was the wall paper, for instance. How was she
going to fasten it to the logs? Between the logs the chink-
ing was not as thick as the logs themselves. If she pasted
the paper tight against the wall, there would be great bony
ribs of colored pattern alternating with depressions. If
she made the surface even and straight, she would have to
stretch the wall paper from log to log and tack it there with
carpet tacks. But then if anyone leaned against it where
there was a depression underneath, the back of the chair
would go through. And the twins: they could poke a stick
or even a finger through not far above the floor. She solved
the problem by stretching old discarded canvas sacks over
the lower wall and then pasting newspapers over the strong
sacking. Then on top of these came the wall paper. When
THE FIREMAKERS
297
one pressed the wall at the right level, it rustled. But the
wall was straight and even.
The carpet, too, required thought. Despite all the
stretching they could do, there would still be a spot of floor
three or four feet long and a yard wide without covering.
Val sewed the carpet together so that the spot came in the
corner under the bed. Then she turned the bed round so
that its length ran the other way across the room, and the
spot was quite concealed.
"If you don't have things," Luke declared, very proud of
her ingenuity, "what you want to do is to find out that you
don't need them."
There were bright, warm days still; but the leaves were
gone. And before sunset each day, there came into the air
a nipping quality which served notice that Indian summer
was over-for that year. Then sharp frosts came. Early
in the morning when Luke went to the pot-shop, the hollow
was as white as if there had been a light fall of snow. It
took the sun two or three hours to clear the white from the
last protected corners of the hollow. Luke worked with
greater persistence. For Mr. Sturgis had warned him that
when winter itself arrived, the old shop could not be kept
warm enough to prevent the fresh, "green" ware from
freezing.
Then one afternoon the clouds were disturbed and angry-
looking, and the wind was cold. Luke built a dry-fire of
well-seasoned kiln-wood on the dirt floor of the shop in
front of the stove that afternoon before he went home. The
next morning the ground was as hard as rock. He was at
the shop when daylight came. The wood had burned too
quickly. Through the long last hours of the night there had
been no fire at all. He built a roaring fire in the old
cracked cannon-ball stove, and soon had the thick cast-iron
sides of it red hot. As it roared cheerfully, the frostiness
vanished from the air, which smelled more and more of
298
THE FIREMAKERS
wood-smoke as it grew warm again. The "green" ware
looked all right. Perhaps it was not frozen after all. But
later when Luke examined it carefully, the edges crumbled
a little under his fingers. Winter had arrived.
Mr. Sturgis had told him that he need not count on run-
ning the pot-shop in December and January and in the
earlier part of February. In late February certainly in
early March-the log fire in front of the stove would be
equal once more to the incursions of outside temperatures.
Of course, there were winter occupations. Luke milked
the cow, fed the chickens and guineas, and carried in wood
for the kitchen stove and the great fireplace in the front
room. In early autumn he had supposed the wood which
Mr. Sturgis had left nicely ricked up by the back-yard fence
would last all winter. But before the middle of Decem-
ber he could see that it would not. He brought old Fan,
the blind claygrinder, from the shop to the hill stable
when he closed the pottery for the cold weather, and now
he hitched her to a little low sled which Mr. Sturgis had
left, and hauled up numerous cords of wood from the hillside
of oak trees down toward the pot-shop. This he cut into
shorter lengths for the stove and fireplace.
Mr. Sturgis had counseled him to utilize the winter
months in the cutting of kiln-wood. Kiln-wood ought to
stand through at least one full season before it was burned.
So when Luke had nothing else to do, the oak trees beck-
oned him.
He did not like chopping wood in winter. His body had
long been accustomed to working underground where the
temperature was invariable. It was well enough to make
one's ax ring cheerfully when the sun was bright and warm
on the southern hillside, but when the sky was overcast and
leaden, and the cold winds drifted down from the north,
and the bodies of the trees, filling with frost, startled one
with loud, cracking reports sometimes like a rifle shot,
THE FIREMAKERS
299
sometimes like an ax splitting off a great, heavy chip-he
preferred to do something else. The woods on such days
made him too much aware of a mighty and extensive pres-
ence.
One blustery, snowy forenoon Nathan Boyd appeared.
There was no work, and since the next day was Saturday
with little chance of work before Monday, he thought he
had better come over. They reviewed the entire autumn
season. When Luke spoke about the dismal wood-chopping
in wintry weather, Nathan thought there must certainly be
something else useful to do.
"There's nothing else to be done that is, except getting
the clay out. And it seems to me that stripping off the
frozen ground and digging and shoveling around in the wet
would be worse than freezing in the woods."
"Why don't you go under for it-like coal? Seems like
you ought to be able. Then you could work where it's
warm."
"Think the roof would hold?"
"Don't see why not."
"It's nothing but shaly, gravelly dirt."
That afternoon they went exploring for clay. Just
where Mr. Sturgis had been getting his there was no hill to
go under. That was why he dug it there. The stripping
was easy. But across the hollow one might easily drift un-
der the edge of the hill.
Luke was greatly elated. He told Val in full detail about
this means of economizing his sensibilities as well as his
time. Inasmuch as he had knowledge of mining, why not
make use of it?
Val listened skeptically. "The roof will come down on
you," she said, "down there by yourself; and then where
will you be?"
"Why," he said, a little taken aback, "I'll be there. But
300
THE FIREMAKERS
if I build a solid entry of props and cross-caps, how is it
going to come down?"
The next day he and Nathan selected the site for the
mine opening. On Monday he went to work. For a few
days, it was worse than chopping wood. But soon he was
getting under the hill. He was then away from the winds,
and the snows had not yet saturated the earth over the
clay.
He found four low wheels back of the stable, and upon
these he built a little car. This he could push out of the
mine on to the shaky little tipple and empty of its load of
white and blue-gray clay. A dozen feet below on the clean
ground, the pile soon began to take the form of a great
cone, with all the heavy lumps lying round the outer edge
of the base.
Val required frequent reports from him. Now that they
were somewhat "out," she did not want her husband killed
in a clay mine. Luke humored her by going to the house
every day for dinner, although the distance was not slight,
and the trip in the cold. after a half-day in the warm hill,
was not inviting.
One day he had not arrived when dinner was ready. Val
looked down the ridge. She waited. She looked often.
She waited an hour. Then she could wait no longer. She
penned the fireplace off with some chairs, cautioned the
twins not to cry until she returned, and hurried away to
discover what had happened. She wanted to run, but she
was afraid. She was afraid she might arrive before she
was ready.
Breathless with fear she came up to the little tipple. The
car was not outside. He must be in there still.
The mine was only a few rods long, but the entry curved
sharply a time or two in order to keep under the edge of
the hill where the clay was soft. She walked up to the
Not a sound except the

least bit of water dripping. She bent low and crept in a

THE FIREMAKERS

The twins-what had become of the twins? They were
racing back and forth over the new carpet, trying to make
their feet follow the lines the carpet-chain had made in the
pattern.
Many things went to make up the months which reached
from one summer to the next. One day when Luke was ex-
ploring on the hillside to see which direction the entry
should take, he came upon a solitary little evergreen tree
in the edge of the thicket of locust and hickory. It was the
kind which children in that region hunted for usually
without success-just before Christmas. He knew what he
would do with that one.
Clay did not require hurrying. Luke had plenty of
time to get ready for Christmas. The twins listened with
wondering, poetic faces while he explained that Kriss
THE FIREMAKERS
303
Kringle would come down the big chimney while they slept,

Luke thought it marvelous how much could be in a book,
a little thing not half as big as a lump of coal. You could
learn anything if you only knew where it was.
Nor did important events cease coming with the depar-

have spoken to him before. "I believe there's something

Luke looked deep into her eyes.
"Yes."
Luke threw a mitten into the corner of the room; he
danced; he capered about like a puppy; he grabbed up the
twins and buried his face playfully in their bodies as
though he would eat them; he tossed them to the low ceil-
