When he was out of breath, she cautioned him gently.
"It will mean another mouth to feed and more clothes to
buy."
But he would not be suppressed. "One more or less
won't make any difference."
He lost his impatience at the season. He helped Val do
the work of the house. He became more intent in his dis-
cussions with her of the many things which she had studied,
but which he had not. And all through the day, evening
was something to look forward to. No, he was no longer
impatient at the season. The season itself had become a
source of great enlightenment.
304
THE FIREMAKERS
One afternoon in middle January when it had been snow-
ing all day, Luke announced that he contemplated a grand
enterprise for the morrow-if she should be willing. He
had put the sled in order, and if they should hitch the old
flea-bitten claygrinder to it, put in plenty of warm straw
and a jug or two filled with hot water, they could drive to
Green Valley as warm as toast. The snow could not pos-
sibly melt off in one day.
By eight o'clock the next morning they were on their
way. Old Fan, the claygrinder, did not relish the trip. She
preferred a worn track that went safely round and round
in a circle. But she developed a degree of reliance in
Luke's firm, guiding hands.
It was not so exciting to be back in Green Valley as they
had thought it would be. When they arrived, long before
noon, all the men were in the mines, and most of the chil-
dren were in school. Sudie Boyd was in a state of readi-
ness; she always was. But she was feeling "a little dauncy."
Val had to report all the news, including what she called
the important news, while her mother hunted up playthings
for the twins. Luke strolled over to the Valley House to visit
with Mrs. Canaday, but she was busy and he did not stay
long. He strolled up to the Hand-out, too, but the slender
little man in spectacles who operated it for the Company
was the only person by the stove-and the stove was not
very warm. He and Val and the twins and Sudie ate a
generous dinner together and chatted awhile. At train
time he walked to the station to see a train come in. The
steam of the engine escaped with a singing sizzle in the cold
air.
Early in the afternoon, children began to arrive from
school. Pomp had come back from the Last Chance in
pleasant mood that day, and was disposed to grant the chil-
dren early freedom from intellectual tasks. Some of them,
when they heard that Val was in town, stopped to see her
THE FIREMAKERS
305
and tell her something about school. They did not sing
any more, and Pomp had found a new spittoon. The chil-
dren sneezed and blew their troublesome noses and coughed
unrestrainedly. It was something to be thankful for, Sudie
explained, that they were able to be on the go at all, for
there had been lots of sickness.
Luke thought they had better start back toward Wig-
gam's Glory. He wanted to see the men, but it would be
late before they were at home and cleaned up. As it was,
his chickens would probably be at roost before he arrived,
and would have to live through the cold night on empty
craws. Val thought, too, that they ought to get back. She
did not want her geraniums and bleeding-heart to freeze.
Old Fan, cold from standing, and disgusted with Green Val-
ley's sulphurous air, snorted in relief and trotted away home-
ward, scarcely aware that she was carrying with her the
four people tucked in the sled behind.
"This is something like," Luke said when they were once
back in their own well-kept front room before a roaring
fire.
Some days after their visit to Green Valley, one of the
twins whimpered in the night. She was restless, and she
coughed.
The next day she was no better. Luke had to draw upon
his ingenuity in order to keep her from whimpering most of
the time. And then the boy began to exhibit the same symp-
toms.
Soon after nightfall the little girl began to cough a hoarse,
croupy cough. Val and Luke counseled together and tried
to recall croup remedies from remote corners of their mem-
ories. Despite everything they could devise, she grew
worse. By midnight she and the little boy were feverish,
and filled with alarm at something they could not under-
stand. The little girl began to start up suddenly, gasp, and
then choke when some obstruction in her throat kept her
306
THE FIREMAKERS
from breathing. Luke watched her helplessly through one
agonizing convulsion that lasted longer than any of its
predecessors. "I'm going to Culbertson," he announced,
"and find that doctor over there. It's less than three miles,
and I can make it in no time."
Val concurred.
He hurried away. He was nervous and wanted to run,
but he knew that in the end running would be folly. Be-
fore he had gone a half-mile he settled down to a firm,
swinging stride.
The night was the coldest they had had. The snow was
white and deep everywhere, even in the road which he fol-
lowed. The heavens were full of cold-looking stars.
As he walked, he marveled at the unending ritual of the
skies. It was magnificent. But it did not reveal a sugges-
tion of warmth anywhere.
He was alone. Not so much as a raccoon or a stray house
cat followed the top of the rail fence by the roadside. Not
so much as a silent fox, out to prey upon less fleet neigh-
bors, appeared anywhere on the cold white of the hillsides.
Only the stars.
He was glad when he came down the hill into Culbert-
son and saw pale blue smoke mounting from chimneys in
two or three dozen dark houses.
Would the doctor be so good as to go over? Why, yes,
he supposed he might as well. He had just come in from a
case "One more mortal to suffer," he said, in the poetry
of his profession-and he might as well stay up the rest of
the night and sleep the next day. "Five or six miles of a
walk ought to help a man's circulation-on a night like
this."
They were soon back. The trip seemed only half as
long when there were two of them to make it together.
As they entered the house the little girl was coughing
and gasping. Dr. Sutton stopped in his tracks and listened.
THE FIREMAKERS
307
"That's diphtheria," he said. "That's bad. You got that
when you were in Green Valley the other day."
He went carefully over each patient with many com-
forting words, and with a professional intentness that
steadied Luke and Val, as well as the twins.
"Just you don't be afraid. Maybe I can help the cough
so it won't hurt so much," he said with great gentleness to
each frightened, restless child.
But he had little comfort for the two who watched over
them. "It is virulent in form," he said quietly, when they
moved to the fireplace to talk things over. "I'm afraid she
won't last. He may. If you'll make me some good stout
coffee I'll stay awhile. We might know about her by
morning. The bad cases often develop swiftly."
Toward morning her coughing became less violent, but
her suffocation increased. Her breathing began to seem
shallow and over-rapid.
"She won't last," Dr. Sutton said, turning away. "She
can't hold on for another hour-I'm afraid."
When all was over and the red, cold-looking sun was
coming out of the east, Dr. Sutton prepared to take his de-
parture. "If I were in your place," he said with as much
comfort as he could command, "I'd not wait too long to
bury her. I'll be back this afternoon to see how the boy
is coming along. He has a better chance."
He saw how solitary they were. "Who are your neigh-
bors?" he asked. "I'll stop and tell some one for you,
some one where there are no children."
They had not made many acquaintances. Their nearest
neighbor was a third of a mile away. But within an hour,
while Luke was planing some yellow poplar boards that he
had found in the stable loft, the old man who lived down the
way on the Culbertson road walked in.
"You oughtn't to have to do that," he said with mascu-
line compassion. "I'll do it."
308
THE FIREMAKERS
At the same moment, his wife appeared in the house and
took charge there. Later, two or three other women came.
By noon the old man had finished his task. "I had to
walk back home," he explained, "and get some screws.
Don't like the idea of nailin' 'em," he said in brief explana-
tion.
The claims of the dead took away some attention from
the living. The boy did not cough so much. He was bet-
ter, apparently. So they were at his bedside less continu-
ously. They were cheered to see that he was not in great
pain. But when Dr. Sutton arrived in the afternoon, all
thought was again concentrated on the boy. Dr. Sutton
studied him, and then turned away with a subdued little
whistle of despair. "Constitutional infection," he said
briefly. "His heart will never hold out."
The old man had all his work to do once again.
The next morning he and some others scraped away the
heavy snow in the corner of the orchard, and with mattocks
and shovels cut through the frozen crust down to where it
was warmer. Nathan came over from Green Valley. A
young minister at Culbertson, who had heard that misfortune
had visited newcomers in the community, came out to offer
the decencies of Christian burial, if the bereft so desired.
The little group gathered under the apple trees and saw
all that was material in the chapter instituted by Winnie put
in the friendly earth. To Luke the chapter had seemed
without fundamental logic; and now it had ended in this
tiny fashion. What difference, he wondered, could it all
make to those stars he had beheld on the road to Culbert-
son? Yet it had made a difference to him.
L
CHAPTER III
UKE and Val were good philosophers. When they
were together, their psychic kinship brought them
the repose of complete honesty.
One evening while the house still resounded with pa-
thetic hollowness, Val asked, as they sat before the fire:
"Have you ever thought how free you now would be left, if
you hadn't married me?"
"I was wondering if you had thought of that," Luke an-
swered.
They sat for a long time in silence. The wood which
Luke had put on the fire was covered with half-loosened
bark, and the flames which had burned the great sticks
almost in two had revived some large ants and chased them
from their winter quarters between the bark and the wood.
They raced frantically about at the ends of the sticks as
the fire crept nearer.
"You would have nothing to keep you now if you had
known."
"Yes, if I had known,' I might not have done several
things I did."
He pushed the wood over into the grate so that the fire
could get at the unburned ends.
"I thought that all over this afternoon," he said after he
had resumed his place in the high-backed, poplar rocking
chair. "I went over it. And what do you suppose I con-
cluded?"
She smiled. "To run off and leave me here in the hills?"
"I decided," he said with the earnestness which usually
309
310
THE FIREMAKERS
characterized his speech, "that I wanted things just as they
are."
She did not so much as move an eyelash.
"Suppose we hadn't married," he went on. "You'd be
stuck in Green Valley and I'd be stuck here. I could not
go anywhere without a little money. So where'd I be any
better off? If I make enough to get myself out, I can make
enough to get both of us out."
His beneficence stirred his own enthusiasm. "Why, I was
figuring a little on the back of my shovel down at the clay-
bank this afternoon; and look here." He found a little
piece of pencil and a scrap of paper. "See that. There it
is in figures. If I work no faster than I worked last fall-
and of course I was just getting started then-why-" and
he went into great detail to show her what everything would
sell for after one had made all allowance for breakage.
Yes, they were on the way.
"The old mare doesn't work very hard. She's standing
still half the time unless you're behind her with a gad.
This spring I'm going to bring her up of evenings and put
in a garden and a potato patch. We can grow half of our
living, and put that much more money aside. Two seasons
-think of that! -two just fairly good ones, and we're out.
And I'm not wasting my time in other ways either. I'm
learning a lot about practical pottery. I'll know some
things from A to Z."
The snow was lasting. He was out the next morning
soon after daylight with the old mare and sled, hauling clay
from his little mine to the millshed clay pens. The old
mare was nervous when she was hitched to anything other
than the clay mill, and maintained a speed that was almost
a trot. She and Luke constituted a picturesque arrange-
ment on the scene when they came from the mine over the
unblackened snow with a load of clay. She was ewe-
necked, and twisted her head to one side with her nose ex-
THE FIREMAKERS
311
tended when the load was heavy. Luke in his short coat
and cloth cap hurrying to keep up with her and the sled
might well have been taken for a peasant in the Basque
country had not something of the aristocrat in him, some-
thing of an invisible world which he struggled to see, given
his body a free, confident rhythm.
Val, he knew, would be lonely. So he began the prac-
tice of going up to the house at nine-thirty to get a bite of
bread and butter. Val soon reciprocated by coming down
in the afternoon and bringing him a snack of something.
They let the old mare haul them to the mine in the
empty sled. When the load was on, Luke let her hustle
away over the road she now knew well, and he and Val
walked at their own pace until the load got too far ahead
and he shouted an eloquent "Whoa," which the old mare
was always in readiness to hear. When at last they caught
up with her, Luke clucked gently and she was away again
until he shouted to her once more.
Soon after the middle of February the bright sun sent
the remaining snow hurrying away in muddy streams. The
spring was likely to be an early one, Luke thought, and he
returned to the potter's wheel at once.
He worked early. He worked late. He filled the boards
in every foot of the scaffolds. As the ware dried, he passed
it up to the edge of the floor of the great loft by stepping
on an inverted eight-gallon jar from the ware-yard out
front, and when the edge of the loft-hole had been com-
pletely covered, he mounted the ladder on the wall and
carried the ware back from the loft-hole and built it up in
enormous, regular pyramids.
Val came down one day and found him lofting ware in
this slow, single-handed fashion. "Is that the way Mr.
Sturgis did it?" she asked.
"No. I pitched it to him. He taught me how to send
it up to him through the loft-hole without breaking any.
312
THE FIREMAKERS
Then when we had the floor around him covered, I went up
and we carried it back and piled it up."
"Why couldn't I catch it?" Val asked.
He looked at her as if she had proposed something crim-
inal. "I don't think you'd better."
"Yes! I can catch one just as well as any old man could.
I'm going to show you."
Before he could protest, she was climbing to the loft.
He was skeptical. But he prepared to pitch a gallon
fruit jar to her. He stooped with a slow, easy movement
until the piece was well between his spread knees, and then
swung it upward with a firm movement that sent it float-
ing in the air to a spot directly in front of Val over the edge
of the loft-hole. She made a quick movement to catch it,
but fearing that she was going to tumble through the four-
foot opening, she drew as quickly back, and the fruit jar
crashed to bits on the floor at her feet.
Before he could refuse to waste another, she cried, "Oh,
I know how now. It looked so funny coming right up there
without turning over. Try another."
"Wait," he said. In a moment he returned with one in
his hand. "Here's one with the handle knocked off. We'll
practice with it. Here she comes!" And without waiting
for Val to get ready he sent the piece up. He expected to
hear it crash. But he did not.
"This is fun," she said, proud of having caught the sec-
ond one. "I'll drop it down to you and we can try it again."
He pitched it a dozen times. Sometimes he pitched it "a
good one" with the jar very perpendicular in the air; some-
times "a bad one" with the jar rotating as it arrived. She
caught it every time. Then he pitched two or three dozen
undamaged pieces, and she caught them all without drop-
ping them.
"Now, don't you think your wife is useful? I'm coming
THE FIREMAKERS
313
down every day. This is lots more fun than staying up
there alone."
She kept her word. Through the windy raw days of
March, through the acrid sun of early April, through peach
blossoming and apple blossoming on into May she came
daily.
And when the long spring, so full of work and waiting
had passed, and the great loft was piled high from end to
end and side to side, with only a narrow path or two to
make carrying possible, she insisted on coming down and
carrying fruit jars to the kiln while he set up the first stands.
He did not object. It was a great enterprise-a chapter in
the history of the world. He had his own notions from
somewhere or other about the proper occupations for a
woman with child. But this first kiln was so momentous in
its possibilities that he recognized the logic of her presence.
He had to rely upon his memory of those four or five
kilns which he had helped Mr. Sturgis set and burn. Val's
assistance left him free to concentrate uninterruptedly on
his task. He wanted to put down those foundations of
heavy, stiff chuck-clay so that the stands would not topple
when the ware was at white heat. He spent two days, in-
stead of one, setting that first kiln.
He lighted the slow fire. Then that night he stayed up
without daring to snatch a wink of sleep. He kept the fire
pits crowded tight with hard, dry wood. Val came down
at midnight to bring him food and a supply of hot coffee.
She carried a lantern for safety. She did not wish to
step on any silent, rusty copperhead that might be en-
joying the pleasant night. The lantern did not make much
of a circle of illumination. She felt ill at ease as soon as
she was a distance from the house. But when she came
down the ridge above the shop, she was comforted. The
glowing flames poured steadily from the flue-holes and cat-
314
THE FIREMAKERS
holes and center-hole in the dome of the kiln, and restored
her belief in the tensity of life.
She proposed to watch the kiln for a half-hour while one
firing of wood burned. Luke could take a nap. At the end
of the period he did not sleep-he crowded the doors full
of wood again and then relaxed once more while Val watched
to see that he did not rest too long.
At three she went home. On the ridge above the shop
she turned to look once more at the kiln. The light from
its dome reached to the trees that overhung the hollow from
the steep hillsides.
As she approached the house she was enveloped in a
great quietness. She passed the corner of the orchard.
Were there such things as spirits of the departed? Did the
feverish vibrations which animated human bodies continue
active after the bodies were useless? Suppose she should
encounter some subtle vibrations of this kind in the quiet
of the deep night. Would she be afraid of them? No, she
thought not. She thought it would be pleasantly adventur-
ous to meet the twins, for instance, in even their most ethe-
real form.
That morning she overslept. When she awoke and saw
the spectacle of full day about her, she dressed hurriedly,
prepared some bread and butter, some eggs, and some coffee
and hastened away to the shop. Luke-yes, where would
Luke be when she came out on the little bluff above the
shop?
He was on top of the kiln drawing fire-pieces. He
reached down into the kiln with a slender iron hook almost
as long as he was himself, drew out a tag-shaped piece cut
from the side of a discarded fruit jar in the loft, and then
watched it cool and assume its color, in order to see how
much longer he should fire.
She stopped, breathless. He was going to try one from
the center-hole in the top of the kiln dome.
THE FIREMAKERS
315
He stepped up on the side of the dome between two large
flue-holes where the white flames came forth with pushing
steadiness as if they were fanned by some gigantic bellows.
He shielded his face with his forearm and took a step or
two up between the smaller cat-holes where the flames were
just as white.
What if the thin dome should not hold him? Val wanted
to shout to him from the hillside. But he took another
step or two, and then, shielding his eyes as best he could
with his arms at the same time he was using them for his
chief purpose, he thrust the hook down through the center-
hole, no more than eight or ten inches in diameter, and
fished for a fire-piece where he had placed a generous sup-
ply on top of the stands. He brought one out, as white as
a glowing piece of steel, and tossed it with a dexterous swing
of the hook to the dirt path round the top of the kiln. Then
he leaped to the path himself, broke the piece in two with
the hook, and studied the glaze, the middle of the clay, and
the blackening slip.
He was down off the kiln by the time she walked to the
shed. He mopped his red, sweaty face with a great ban-
danna handkerchief. His clothes were as wet as if he had
been dipped in the creek.
"She'll be ready to burn off before noon," he said, smil-
ing and panting as he tried to regain an even breathing after
his experience on top of the kiln.
He ate some breakfast, gulped down some coffee, and
began at once to wall up the lower fire doors. He continued
to fire in the smaller, upper doors, where the draft could
be controlled easily by the heavy, tight iron shutters. Be-
fore noon he fired lightly for the last time, and went to the
house to tell Val about every detail of his night's experi-
ence and to eat some dinner and sleep a little, if possible.
He walked down in the evening and covered the kiln.
He put some large-sized stoneware plates over the flue-
316
THE FIREMAKERS
holes and cat-holes, and then he put a covering of boards
over the entire dome. He rested a long stout pole in two
forks that extended upward from the outer edge of the logs
which enclosed the kiln, so that the pole passed over the
heated dome without touching it. Then he sloped long
boards from this ridge-pole down to similar poles on two
sides of the kiln. These weighted boards, carefully lapped,
would keep out disastrous showers that might otherwise
force some water past the flue-hole coverings, and they
would make the cooling of the tops of the stands of ware
a little more gradual.
He had begun to burn ware none too soon. Two days
later, when he was yet fearful that he might violate Mr.
Sturgis's injunction not to unwall the door till the ware was
cool, a visitor came to the shop. It was Mr. Rigdon, of Cul-
bertson, who kept a store, and bought and shipped ware.
Evidently Mr. Rigdon thought the danger of late killing
frosts was past.
"I could ship six carloads if I had them soon," he said
in explanation of his visit. "I'm trying to hunt up that
many."
Luke explained in return that he could contribute some-
thing to the shipment. "I've got a kiln full and a loft full."
Mr. Rigdon was all business. "Let's see them," he pro-
posed.
They went to the loft.
"Nice cans, these." He called them cans. "They're well
turned, too. You're a born potter, I can see that."
He stood up close against one pile, stretched high, and,
lest he fall into the fragile pieces, balanced himself by put-
ting a finger of each hand lightly on the top of a fruit jar.
"This is nice ware. If you can get these burned, one kiln
right after the other, I'll give you two cents extra a dozen."
Luke heard his feet on the floor. Otherwise he would
have thought he walked on air. He heard his voice, too,
THE FIREMAKERS
317
explaining that since they were up there in the loft, they
might as well peep into the kiln.
Yes, Mr. Rigdon thought it would be quite safe to pull
down enough of the bricks of the door to enable them to
look. By the time two or three bricks were down Luke
peeped in to see if the stands had toppled. They were
straight.
When the opening was larger, he reached in with his
chisel-like iron and pried loose the two top fruit jars in a
low stand close against the door.
They were well burned.
Yes, they were well burned; but they were speckled with
something. He hesitated a little and then passed one of
them to Mr. Rigdon.
"Why, there's ore in your clay," Mr. Rigdon declared
with killing promptitude. "The ware is burned all right-
fine! But they won't buy cans any more with these black
splotches on them.
"Look inside here, too. See there. See how it runs down
and breaks the surface of the slip where the slip is thin."
Luke felt not only the air on which he had walked now
rapidly receding, but the earth as well.
"You mean-" His mouth was dry, and his tongue
like a stick.
Mr. Rigdon looked disappointed. He wanted fruit jars
badly.
"I can't understand it," he said. He peeped into the kiln
to see if they were all alike. "Old Sturgis's cans were al-
ways clear. He never had the slightest trouble with ore.
Aren't you getting your clay in the same place?"
Luke had to confess that he had got it in the hill across
the hollow from Sturgis's pits.
"You'd hardly think it would make that much difference.
But sometimes it does."

THE FIREMAKERS
He read the consternation in Luke's face. "Let's take

Luke tore down the wall and tossed the bricks aside with

"There's a clear one," he said hopefully. "And those

These told Luke everything he needed to know. "I made

before winter. And they're all mixed up with the others.
I changed the piles early this spring to make more room."
He stepped out of the narrow doorway on to the plat-

"Mighty sorry," Rigdon said.
Luke turned away speechless. He walked toward the
loft door and looked in over the high, even piles of ware that
filled the room.
"I'd never bother burning any of that if I were you,"
Rigdon counseled him. "It'd be just a waste of time and
wood. If you could find the ones you made last fall out
of the old man's clay, you could burn them. But if they're
mixed up, you can't pick them out."
He wanted to be helpful. He liked the looks of Luke.
"Let's go down and look at the clay."
They found a clay-pick and walked along the wagon
track of a road toward the pits where the old man had
taken out clay for twenty or thirty years.
"You can tell when it has ore in it," Rigdon explained-
"if you know how."
He wanted to be consoling. "Of course, it will be too
late now to sell many fruit jars this year. Takes time.
You have to have the ware in my hands almost two months
THE FIREMAKERS
319
before the wives expect to use it-fully two months now-
adays with the railroads crowded full of coal cars. But you
might sell a kiln or two for late peaches-if you hurry."
"There's where I got mine out," Luke said with tragic
simplicity.
Rigdon was struck by Luke's ingenuity in going under
the hill for clay. "But why didn't you go under over there
on the other side, where the old man dug his?"
"The ground was too flat. Not enough hill over the clay
to be safe."
"Oh, I see," Rigdon replied, prolonging the last word.
"Look here," he said, picking up a lump of clay. He
turned it over and over to make sure that he himself was
not in error. "There! See that? And that? And that?"
He picked up another good-sized lump. "Now let's go
over and look at some on the other side."
He made the comparison swiftly. "See there. That
brownish speck is ore. Here's a speck in this or a streak-
but it's bluish. See the difference? The sharp edge of your
pick cuts through a speck of ore, and when it weathers a
little you have a streak pretty nearly like this-but not
quite. See? There's not much difference till you burn the
ware and melt the ore."
It was clear enough to Luke, clear enough to anybody-
now.
They walked back to the shop and Rigdon hurried away.
He had left his horse and sulky by the road up on the
ridge. His young sorrel would be restless.
It was all too staggering for Luke to believe. He must
look into the kiln again. He took down parts of a few
other stands and peeped through. Some of the splotches
were larger and left little pock marks with a streak below
where the ore had run down when it melted in the white
heat.
He turned the claygrinder out to pasture and went dis-

As they walked along in silence there was not a ray of
brightness in the universe.
Luke sat at the supper table and nibbled at a little bread.
He was so baffled that his stomach rebelled at the sugges-

detail of what Mr. Rigdon had said and done.
After they had gone through the motions of eating sup-

the grade where the railroad swung sharply northward and
westward near Culbertson and made its way through the
hills only a scant mile from where they stood, and on out

Suddenly Luke straightened up. "I'm going back and
work awhile."
His proposal seemed foolish to Val, but she offered no
protest.

"Guess you can get along without the lantern. You've

Down in the hollow, night was already near. Two or
three rabbits raced out of his way, kicking their heels un-
necessarily high. A thick, rusty copperhead moved in sway-
ing curves across the path, but in the dusk Luke did not try
to overtake it. Night-hawks made the gentlest of whirrings
in the air as they came down from the higher levels and
passed near his head.
He climbed the steps to the kiln platform and walked into
the loft where the great piles of carefully wrought fruit
jars stared grayly at him out of the darkness.
Behind the pot-shop where the little run swept over
against the hillside and left a level area, horseweeds grew
in rank profusion every summer. There was no entrance
to the shop on that side, and the weeds grew and tumbled
down and grew again year after year without molestation.
On that side of the loft the builders of the shop had cut
a two-foot section from one of the logs and cased up the
opening for a single window sash. But the window had
never been put in. Instead, two boards had been nailed
over the opening to keep out snow and rain. Light for all
purposes came in at the east doorway overlooking the
ware-yard out front, and at the north doorway, which led
to the kiln.
Luke remembered this window. It was behind a great
pile of gallon fruit jars. He lighted his lantern. Pewees on
their nests high on the sides of the roof logs, frightened at
the clear eye of light which gleamed so near, darted out
through the doors into the growing night. He hung the
lantern on a nail in the crooked little brick chimney in the
middle of the room, and then tried to climb over the fruit
jars to the window. Some of them gave way under a weight
so suddenly and so unevenly applied, and crunched into
pieces. What did it matter? They were worthless any-
322
THE FIREMAKERS
how. He reached the window, jerked the two boards loose,

Without moving from his position, he began to throw fruit

He seemed to make such feeble headway. A fruit jar
slipped to the floor from his nervous grasp. From where
he stood he hurled the other two at the window. They
missed and crashed into bits against the logs.
An idea! He went down to the millshed and brought up
the big shovel. Then he resumed his hurling. How long
he had worked he could not say, but the gallon jars were
mostly out. The much greater number of half-gallon ones
were light. He hurled them from wherever he stood. He
maintained a steady bombardment of the window. If any of
them went through, very well; if not, very well. When a
huge pile of débris had accumulated under the window, he
stopped his bombardment, seized the shovel, and worked
until the floor was clear again.
By midnight Val had become restless. She was restless
in these days, anyhow, with forewarnings given by the
child within her body. The premonitions that dashed
over her nerves put her in a state of mind which made fear
about Luke easy. When fate chanced along so casually
and slipped away everything that a man was about to grasp,
THE FIREMAKERS
323
it was enough to drive him to any extremity. And there

She walked in darkness toward the shop. When she

Cautiously she climbed the steps to the kiln platform and

She watched him for a time as he kept up his rhythmic

as well to let him work.
She slipped away. When she reached the house, her
heart a-flutter from the climb along the ridge, she went to
bed and to sleep.
When she awoke at six the next morning he had not re-

He was haggard and nervous.
"The loft's cleared, anyway," he declared; "and the scaf-
fold. Got anything to eat?"
He was ready to eat. Val felt heartened. Sudie Boyd
said a man was not to be worried about so long as he asked

He ate breakfast. He drank much coffee. He must
hurry back.
Armed with his drawing-iron he assailed the ware in the

"You mustn't work yourself to death," Val admonished

When he had finished eating, he threw himself on the bed

CHAPTER IV
E can't wait till next year to sell something,"
he said at breakfast the next morning. "The
money's about gone, and there'll be things to
pay for before another summer."
Sleep had restored him somewhat, but he was still des-
perate. Before the early sun was in the depths of the hol-
low, he went down the ridge with long strides which re-
corded his state of mind. Old Fan stood in blind peace
under a spreading elm tree. He got his clay picks and
shovel from the millshed and hurried on to the field of pits,
some of them half full of water and inhabited by frogs and
water snakes, where for years Mr. Sturgis had got his clay.
On a spot near one of the pits he began to strip off the
earth. It was four feet down to the clay. The top earth
was so soft that he did not have to dig at all; he spaded.
But down two feet or more he came into the heavy, resist-
ing clay-cover, and had to use his picks. He kept the area
of the pit small-just large enough to enable him to work.
He must waste no time getting down to the white layer.
Once he had a small supply of clay out of the pit and in the
millshed, he could work regularly at making fruit jars, and
could get clay out at odd times-even at night.
Two days later he invited Val to come down and see his
ware. Work at his craft had already restored him to hope-
fulness.
"Funny thing I never noticed that clay," he said. "I was
so anxious to dig it out like coal that I didn't think about
whether it would be the same grade of clay. You can tell
there's a difference of some sort, just by feeling it."

326
THE FIREMAKERS
Several boards of fruit jars were out in the sun. While
Val regarded them with pride, he picked up two or three
of the pieces and turned them in his hands to make sure
that the sun was not drying them too fast and warping

His days were full. Every morning he was out soon after
the summer sun was up, and hoed in the garden till break-
fast. He ate and went to the shop before six. The old
mare resisted such high-pressure methods. He had to make
much use of the gad by the millshed door. He had to over-
come her resistance, too, when he wanted to haul clay from
the pits late in the evening. Respectable mares at that
time of day should groan in pleasant relaxation in pasture
fields.
Once more life could be thought of pleasantly as sum-
mers, as stoneware seasons. Other things, many other
things, inevitably would come in between. Yet, after all,
they constituted only a loose, casual sequence which bound
one summer to the next.
True, some of the casual incidents were persistent in
claiming attention. Val, for example, could not help won-
dering when her first-born was due. If only her mother.
could come to the country for a time. But Sudie's good-
for-nothingness about which she complained when Val was
leaving home had developed into "daunciness" that kept her
even more in the house than formerly. Val was obliged,
therefore, to fall back on the wisdom of Mrs. Burrell, on
the Culbertson road. When Mrs. Burrell had come over
that morning with her husband to preside over the burial
of the twins, she asked Val, when she had opportunity, if
she were not "on the way." Thereafter she came from time
to time to see that everything was as it should be. She
was expert as midwife, as well as undertaker. "Out in the
country," she said, "where everybody is so far off and
maybe the doctor not at home when you go after him, it's
THE FIREMAKERS
327
just as well to have somebody who can drop work and run
in."
The Burrells could not see Luke's house from where they
lived-save the roof and chimney-but they could see the
out-buildings. It was arranged that in case of need, Val
was to hang a white pillow slip against the end of the corn-
crib. As June stretched away into July, Mrs. Burrell never
let a half-hour go by without looking toward the corncrib,
a third of a mile distant.
One morning when she thought that Luke had not much
more than gone to work, she saw the pillow slip.
That noon when Luke came up from the shop and en-

"Got a nice name for a boy?" she asked, looking mer-

"Why," Luke began-but she read the question in his

"Just fine," she said; "both of them."
Luke was still at a loss. "Why, when did all this hap-

"Why, this forenoon, when do you s'pose? 'Taint no
trick for girls with any spunk. Just you go in and see for

She opened the door to the front room, and when he had

All afternoon Luke kept his wheel spinning at a new

fortably in her deep tracks in the millshed, he swung the
gad with a new fervor. Ware had to be made. Ware had
to be sold.
For the time, their warfare had become defensive. Just
to have something to eat until next season-that would re-
quire ingenuity. Val felt the desperateness of their situ-
ation, even while she was engrossed with the first days of
Giles Dabney's life. At the earliest moment she was up
THE FIREMAKERS
329
and about, busy with devices that would bring winter food
to their cave.
She turned over in her mind every possible expedient.
Food. She thought she began to understand why men take
up chicken thieving and sheep killing. Food. How impor-
tant it seemed when there was prospect of little of it.
They had plenty for the summer. Nobody need be with-
out food in summer. If only she could find something to
put into fruit jars, they would have something for the win-
ter.
One morning she took her tiny, red-faced boy in her
arms and went exploring in the thicket along the edge of
the woods down over the hill. The briars were loaded with
blackberries, many of them ripe.
The next day she put a quilt and a small bucket in a
large market-basket which she swung on her arm, and took
the helpless, jelly-like little body in her other arm and
went forth to add to their winter's reserve.
It was like a different world down over the hill. There
was no evidence of humankind anywhere immediately at
hand. Plants, birds, animals, struggled for their own su-
premacies.
She put the quilt on the ground under a low-hanging
walnut tree where the grass was clean, and placed Giles on
it. Then she took the small bucket and began to pick
berries. How strange that she should be here! How dif-
ferent this was from earning thirty dollars a month in a
schoolhouse with pictures on the wall!
At first she picked only a few feet from Giles. As the
line of berries retreated, she ventured a little farther; then
a little farther still. When she filled her small bucket she
came and emptied it into the basket and took a look at the
tiny sleeper.
Once when she came up, she stopped petrified. A black-
snake five or six feet long, his head lifted high in the air,
330
THE FIREMAKERS
looked with curiosity at the strange bundle under the tree.
He fled like a streak down the hill when he saw Val.
She knew he was not venomous. Moreover, she had
grown up on the strange tradition that blacksnakes were
the mortal enemies of copperheads. But she had also seen
blacksnakes wrapped snugly round woods-pasture bars,
and she had heard stories of how they were cousins of boa-
constrictors and strangled babes and men who slept. She
tied the four corners of the quilt to the forked low-hanging
branch of the walnut tree and made a swinging cradle in
which she placed her son.
At noon when Luke came up from the shop, she showed
him the basket of large berries.
"I'll buy your fruit jars," she said in sympathetic banter.
"I'll need a lot of them."
She told Luke about the snake and about the cradle she
had made on the limb of the tree.
"Better keep an eye out," he cautioned her. "You know
these blacksnakes climb trees and eat young birds out of
the nest."
That afternoon she canned all the berries she had picked.
The next morning she went to pick again. She made the
cradle as she had done the day before. Every time she
came to empty the bucket into the basket, she peeped into
the cradle.
Once she wandered well down to the edge of the woods
where the berries were plentiful and large. While she
picked, the crows set up a terrifying chatter and cawing and
scolding on the hillside above her. She could see some of
them in the top of the walnut tree.
She rushed through the briars like a she-wolf, tearing her
clothes and spilling her bucket of berries in her efforts to
arrive before another moment should pass. She could see
the crows walking sidewise on the upper limbs of the tree
and peering down at something. As she came to the green
THE FIREMAKERS
331
opening the crows flew in wild confusion and with much

Would they have pecked him? Well, Luke thought not.
But the next day she took along some mosquito netting,
purchased to keep flies away from him in the house

She would feel a little more secure now when she went
down into the region of the great briars.
"It's fun," she told Luke. "I go exploring down among
all those big briars in the corner of the field, and find some-
thing interesting every day."
"Better watch out for copperheads. This July sun brings
them all out."
His warning stayed in her ears. She was not going to
be taken by surprise. Nor was she, when she tramped
down some undergrowth to get at specially large, sweet-
looking berries, and saw a rusty-looking reptile on the
brown, soft particles of an old, crumbling stump. He was
already kinked up in a knot ready for warfare, waiting in
absolute silence, his triangular head as bright as a penny,
his eyes as red as tiny coals.
She backed away and looked for a club. It never oc-
curred to her that she might try to avoid the immemorial
conflict.
She tested her club by striking the end of it firmly upon
the ground. It seemed strong.
He had no thought of avoiding the battle, either. When
she returned, he awaited her. He was a yard long, perhaps,
and thick and blunt with a slender, lithe-looking neck.
Could she reach him before he could reach her?
She teased him by pushing the end of the club up close
332
THE FIREMAKERS
in front of him. He struck it so hard that she felt the im-
pact. The resistance to his fangs infuriated him. He
writhed and swayed, watching for another chance. She
was so nervous that she felt involuntary gasps in her throat
every time she moved; yet she was not so frightened but
what she could see two little spots of moisture spread out
where he had tried to sink his fangs into the wood when
he was hoping for flesh.
She baited him again and he could not resist striking.
But this time she jerked the club away, and then just as he
was extended at full length in furious thrust, she brought it
down with desperate force upon his head. He writhed
wildly, but could not move away. She rained blows upon
him, her fear enhancing her courage.
At last he became silent except for tremors of his spotted,
rusty body, and rapid vibrations of his tail in the loose
brown leaves. She looped him over the end of her club
and with a terrific swing tossed him over the fence into
the woods.
She picked the berries which she specially desired, but
she kept her club at hand. Despite herself, she was jumpy
when a twig broke under her foot, or a long, springy briar
swung back against her after it had been held in tension.
"Say," Luke said, not wishing to frighten her, yet wish-
ing her to keep on her guard, "you're marching into dan-
gerous country. One of them big rusty old granddads is
just as poisonous as any rattlesnake. And he's a sneak
besides. Watch out. Where there's one there's likely to
be two."
"I have to get those berries," Val answered, "and I'm
not going to let any snake keep me from them."
She kept Luke fed, and Giles nurtured, and the berries
picked. In August she canned peaches. In September she
canned ripe tomatoes and green ones. In both months she
dried apples.

Luke burned some fruit jars and sold them. Then he
burned some more and sold these. After that he had to
content himself with a few sales to farmers-a few fruit
jars, some milk crocks, some butter pots. He was encour-

kind of clay.
He joined Val in her efforts to assemble winter food. He
dried the earth on the sweet-potatoes with great care in
order to reduce the dangers of rot. With as much care he
harvested the turnips, though they scarcely required care.
He gathered chestnuts and hickory-nuts and walnuts-
bushels of them. "They'll make eating of some sort," he
said whenever Val looked inquiringly at the new bag of
them.
Autumn brought the same brilliance as a year before.
But they did not see so clearly as they had seen then.
Something that induced doubt, something that bespoke the
hovering imminence of something still more benumbing
than doubt, robbed the colors of their overtones of warmth
and left instead only so many millions of dying leaves.
One Sunday Luke rigged out a contraption in which he
could carry Giles easily, and with the boy on his arm he
and Val walked the long, roundabout road to Wiggam's
Glory. The Joan of Arc in the quiet of the fading maples
and hickories and half-naked locusts swept away for a mo-
ment all their anxiety. In the presence of what a man with
a mind had done, Luke became eloquent. He talked about
how people in Washington and Philadelphia and New York
and Boston, and all other sorts of great places, would buy
his tea-sets and his vases when he learned about glazes and
clays. He could design a vase that had just as much style
as the ones on the clock-shelf over at the house-and maybe
more. As for the tea-sets, he had already solved the prob-
lem of color, if only he could find out about clays of fine
texture.
334
THE FIREMAKERS
In the presence of something beautiful, all these thoughts
of what could be done welled up naturally. But on the way
back, the fight for life claimed him once again, so that he
saw unpicked winter apples, and walnuts, and ripening
blackhaws, instead of yellows and browns and clarets.
The fall was warm and long. "Somebody," Luke said,
with a significant accent on the first syllable, "is helping us
all he can. Old Fan hasn't even started on her hay yet.
I'm going to make her hunt her own keep as long as there's
no snow."
The warm weather reached to Thanksgiving. It reached
to the middle of December. When it stretched on to Christ-
mas with only a few crisp, freezing nights, it became the
subject of daily conversation. "We'll have to pay up for
it, sooner or later," Mr. Burrell declared. "We always do.
One year's just about the same as any other when you mix
the hot and cold together from January to December."
Luke was not troubling about the history of the seasons.
He was taking advantage of the warm days they now had.
He made hundreds of fruit jars, pitched them to Val in the
loft, burned them in the kiln, and piled them, all clean and
gray, in the ware-yard, with their mouths sloping downward
the least bit so that the rain would not collect in them and
freeze disastrously.
He and Val ate boiled turnips and cabbage and canned
blackberries and dried apples, and spent their evenings be-
fore the fire reading books or making rough sketches of
vases and teapots which they argued about pleasantly. Was
the bulge of this one too full? Was the lip of that one too
wide? What about the spout of this teapot-was it too
high and too straight?
For a time in January it was too cold to operate the
shop. Luke tied his ears up, put on heavy mittens, and dug
clay. Sometimes when he came up out of the pit after he
had worked to the bottom of the vein, he looked across the
THE FIREMAKERS
335
hollow at the tipple and the great spreading cone of un-
used clay where he had worked the winter before, and at
the sight he lost the power of utterance.
Spring did as much as autumn to make the winter short.
Early in February the last of such snow as they had had
was gone. Before the end of the month, the ground had
dried as it usually did a month later, and the air was full
of a warmth which invited life.
Luke worked at his wheel with furious skill. As matters
had turned out, they had not starved-nor had Fan or the
cow. And now that they had wintered, what difference did
it make if they had missed the sales last year? He had al-
most the output of two ordinary seasons ready for this year.
The money would be all the more precious when it came.
Every day when he washed the slushy clay from his arms
and hurried off to dinner, he stopped on the bluff of the
ridge and surveyed the piles of gray fruit jars. Then he
ran half-way up the ridge.
Spring and warmth and promise filled him and Val with
a strange peace. Not even the report that Sudie Boyd was
less active than she had been could wholly break this feel-
ing of harmony with the external world. It was impossible
to look about and see life springing everywhere from a dead
earth, without being steadied by a great assurance. When
what they craved above all else was within reach, a few
hardships-and most of them now in the past-only
heightened their contemplation of what was to come.
Had it not been for the tempering messages about the
failing health of Sudie Boyd, their state would have been
unbroken ecstasy. She read as usual. But she fell asleep
while she read and let the book tumble from her hands.
She prepared Nathan's meals with the same devoted con-
cern as always, but she ate little herself. When people
dropped in to see her she brightened up for a time, but lan-
guidness soon overtook her and she sat and chatted with
336
THE FIREMAKERS
effort, as if what they talked about were receding from view.
One afternoon in early May Mr. Rigdon walked into
the shop. "Thought I'd just see what you had," he said
pleasantly, in explanation of his visit.
Luke was finishing a half-gallon fruit jar. He lifted it to
the board and went out with Mr. Rigdon to look the ware
over.
"Fine," Mr. Rigdon kept saying. As a matter of fact, he
had looked at it before he came into the shop, but since
he liked it he wanted to say so in Luke's presence.
"Fine! Fine! Couldn't be better."
He liked the look that spread over Luke's face. "You'll
make up for last year, won't you?" he asked, as though the
joke were on last year.
A shadow spread over Luke's face as if a cloud had
floated through the sky. Then he brightened up. "I'm
mighty glad you told me when you did."

"Don't mention it," Rigdon commanded in generosity

could have made some money for both of us."
He sized up the piles, walked round the kiln and exam-

"Got a boy, I notice," he remarked to fill up a silence

"Yes."

If everything goes all right, I'll clean up your yard before

It was not possible for Luke to work to the end of the

"They can't find anything wrong this year," he declared
to her as if she might have a stray doubt in her mind some-

In confirmation of his joy, he pirouetted on the broad

ecstatically, to be sure he persisted in teasings until
she told him that if he didn't behave, he would stab himself
on the huge paring knife.
He turned to Giles: he held him up by the heels; whooped
him to the joists of the whitewashed ceiling; made as if to
eat him bodily-all to Giles's great delight.
The next fortnight passed, but none too quickly. Then
one day, quite before Luke was expecting it, a wagon with
a high extra top on the bed appeared before the shop door.
Here was a man with a team sent over by Rigdon for the
first load of fruit jars.
When the wagon was loaded and the man was ready to
drive away, he said, "I'll be back after some more to-mor-
row morning, if it's not raining."
It was not raining. But before he drove away with the
second load, there were soft, velvety-edged clouds in the
sky.
"See you in the morning," he called casually as he gave
his slender whip a mild snap over the backs of his sleek
bays.
The rain came in the afternoon-lightning, thunder,
wind, downpour. Then came more lightning, heavier
crashes, more wind, more of a cloudburst. It seemed like
some spring storm postponed from mid-April. It seemed
even more like such a storm when it finished off with a per-
338
THE FIREMAKERS
fectly clear sky at nightfall and a breeze from the north-
west so cool that Luke and Val kept a fire going in the
kitchen stove till bedtime.
Luke sniffed the outer air. "Woo-o-h!" he said shiver-
ingly the second time he looked into the west, which was
still the least bit alight with the sun's afterglow. "It feels
cold enough to frost."
He stopped short at the last word. For three weeks
frosts-frosts that might kill fruit-had seemed out of the
question.
"Do you suppose it could?" Val asked, half in skep-
ticism, half in terror.
They looked into each other's eyes as helplessly as if they
were alone on a planet of ice.
Luke turned to the door again. He walked about in the
side yard and got the full effect of the northerly wind.
"I don't see how it could frost-at least very much-on
the twenty-eighth of May," he said. "It doesn't stand to
reason."
"Mother has talked all her life about one on the fourth
of June," Val said. "It was before the War."
Luke's hopes were not so easily dashed. "It has been
warmer since the War," he said humorously.
With this sally of Luke's, they both felt relieved. His
view was reasonable. The fruit was oversize and luxuriant.
How could there be enough frost to kill much of it?
Nothing could be done about it till morning, anyhow. It
would be a cool night for sleep.
Somewhat after midnight, they heard a pounding at the
front door. It was Pewee Dunkle. He had walked across
from Green Valley.
"Don't like to bring you bad news," he said with as much
of an effort to make protocol as possible, while Luke lighted
the lamp, "but Mrs. Boyd had an awful bad sinking-spell
to-night-after supper, that is."
THE FIREMAKERS
339
"You mean," Val asked, springing up in bed in the corner
of the room, "that she didn't get over it?"
"Well-yes, that's what I had in mind saying."
He nodded to Luke and they stepped out into the kitchen.
"There's another lamp out here," Luke said, feeling for

When he had found the match and lighted the lamp, he

Val soon appeared, ready to make the trip. Pewee
walked along the ridge road, got Mr. Burrell out of bed,
and asked him to milk Luke's cow and look after things in
general until Luke should return. Luke himself went down
by the shop to find the old claygrinder. He found her
under her favorite elm tree and brought her up to the house
and hitched her to the fragile spring wagon.
It was two o'clock before they were off, and it was four
and getting light when they descended the hill to Green
Valley.
Everywhere the lowlands were white with frost-very
white. Luke had observed the Green Valley tradition of
not speaking about "worldly" matters when there was a
death in the family, but when he saw the whiteness every-
where, he said to Pewee: "Pewee, if this frost has killed all
the fruit so that nobody wants fruit jars, we'll starve to
death before this time next year."
As soon as the decencies would permit after he had had a
warming breakfast in the kitchen, he stole away from the
house to see an early copy of the Centerville Gazette. The
Gazette expressed the fear that the cold wave following
rains that were general in that part of the state would be
destructive in the peach counties of the entire region. At
four o'clock when the paper went to press, the temperature
340
THE FIREMAKERS
at Centerville had set a new low record, and fears for all

When Sudie Boyd was buried, those who always attended
funerals for the purpose of observing the demeanor of the
bereft remarked that Luke Dabney seemed to take it quite
as hard as Val did. He was not yet thirty, they guessed,
but his face was drawn and his shoulders sagged as if he
were growing old.

CHAPTER V
T proves that next year will be a great one," Nathan
said to Luke and Val a week later as they drove to-
ward Wiggam's Glory. "You never see a frost like
that two years hand running. Things even themselves up
if you don't interfere. The farmers would all starve to
death from frosts and droughts and too much wet weather
if 'twasn't so. It ain't nature that makes trouble in the
end; it's human beings-with an ax to grind."
He had given the five older girls all the belongings in the
hewed-log house except Sudie's books and his own big bed.
The books were for Val, and the bed was for himself-in
Val's upstairs. "Wouldn't know how to sleep in any other
bed," he observed in sufficient explanation.
Luke had brought the books and the bed over in the
spring wagon the day before. It was a strange sight to see
-the great walnut headboard of the bed elevated so high
that the tops of the wabbly, slender-spoked wheels could
not scrape against the wood.
And now he brought Nathan and Val and Giles and a
few small bundles that they could tuck in or tie on. They
were having opportunity to see together what damage the
frost had done.
As they came farther over into the farming region, they
saw men and women and boys hastily replanting corn, and
setting out spindling sweet-potato and tomato plants that,
until the frost, had been forgotten in abandoned hot-beds.
Peaches and cherries had been frozen black. Tiny apples,
creeping out of bunches of blossoms into individual life
were shriveling and dropping from the trees. Not only ber-
341
342
THE FIREMAKERS
ries, but the tenderer leaves of the plants were "scalded"
by the frost and sun.
This general desolation was what proved to Nathan that
there would be an abundance of fruit, and consequently a
great demand for fruit jars, the next year. "I've seen some
mighty hard frosts in my day, but never two hard ones
without a year between when there was none."
Despite all that had happened in the past two years,
Luke and Val were still youthful. They wanted to believe.
Nathan's theory seemed reasonable. But in the immediate
presence of the desolation that had come so casually, so
swiftly, it was difficult even with their combined enthusiasm
to face yet another year with hope deferred.
Nathan saw their hesitancy of spirit. "I can do a lot
myself," he assured them. "We'll get to work and plant
everything that'll grow late: turnips, and cabbage, and more
tomatoes. And why not some buckwheat? You plant it
late. About next March buckwheat cakes would go down
pretty well. And tap some sugar trees and have maple
molasses."
His own spirits were rising now after his first overwhelm-
ing sense of loss when Sudie faded away. He felt cheered
by his own proposals to Luke and Val. His grizzled soul
yet found nourishment in hope.
"You're not so bad off. We can manage to hang on
somehow till next year. Why, I get twelve dollars a month
pension and I hope to get more. That's three dollars a
week. I don't see where I'll have to buy anything much
except tobacco. And twenty cents a week will do me for
that. We shan't starve!"
At this he tickled Giles, who sat in Val's lap and looked
wonderingly at him while he spoke. "You don't think so,
do you?"
Giles evidently thought not, for he smiled. But he made
THE FIREMAKERS
343
no answer other than to bury his face for a moment under

"Things come along so matter-of-course like," Luke said
in reply to Nathan's entire thesis. "You get all ready for
one thing, and something else comes along."
"Yes," Nathan admitted. "It's the same as a battle.
Lots of people think a battle means two armies in a long,
even crash, and that when that's over everybody knows who
has the best of it. But it's not like that. It's just a little
change of front here, and a little bombardment there, and a
change of front somewhere else later in the day, and it's
over. Maybe you can't see who's licked till somebody
comes along and tells you; and like as not he don't know."
As soon as Nathan was established in the dim room up-
stairs, he set out, as he said, to stiffen up the defense. He
planted. He cultivated. He helped Luke at the shop. He
walked to Culbertson, ostensibly to stretch his legs, and
brought back something to eat.
Later he harvested. Everything he had planted seemed
to mature about as early as if it had been planted at the
usual time. "Don't make much difference when you plant,"
he said, "if the days are still getting longer. The hot sun
hurries everything."
He as well as Luke liked to go to the woods to gather
nuts. In the flaming days of early October and in the
bronze days that followed they took empty bags into the
chestnut groves and hazel thickets and came back with them
full. The nuts seemed to be about the only wild food that
had escaped the frost.
While they were on one of these trips, Val had a caller.
It was middle afternoon. She had dressed Giles up in some-
thing bright, had touched up her own appearance, and was
looking at some chrysanthemums that bowed forward with
heavy heads of yellow and brick-red from the rich earth
along the side of the house.

"How do you do?" said a nicely modulated, suave voice.

The winter passed. The prospect of a season of success

knowledge that would enable him to make the beautiful

He must find out. He must ask some one who knew. It

Mr. Rigdon ought to know. He would ask him. "Why,"

In reply he received the following note: "Dear Sir: Beg

Don't take any chances trying to find something else."
Nathan thought the preacher at Culbertson ought to
know about such things. The preacher had been decent,
kind even, when they were in sorrow. But when Luke told
him that he wanted knowledge, he launched into a wild dia-
tribe against ungodly dispensers of material knowledge in
institutions of learning. He had never set his foot in one,
and never meant to. They were all bad enough.
Mr. Wingfield, Luke was sure, would have told him. At

have tried to find out. But Mr. Wingfield had revealed too
much interest in the welfare of his parish. He had been
transferred to a place so remote that Luke could not go to
him to talk things over, even if he knew where he was.
Father McGuire. Did not Father McGuire get him out

Father and talk about glazes without being seduced into

had come from somewhere else. He would know all about

The next morning Luke tramped to St. Rose's Church on

He came up to the parish house. The grounds, the walk,

Luke jumped when he heard it there just on the other side

A young, black-haired priest came in response. "Father
McGuire" Luke began in puzzled query.
"Father McGuire is no longer here," the young priest

northern part of the state somewhere."
Then in professional tone he asked: "Is there something
I can do for you?" He was thinking of a wedding or a
christening.
"No," said Luke, and moved as if to turn away.
And the young priest closed the door.
That evening after darkness had come, Luke stood in the
quiet of the side yard and pondered. Not a star was visible,
not so much of a sound as a dog's barking broke the still-
ness. How far, he wondered, could a man go in a straight
line through the night, in any direction, before he encoun-
tered a light? Aye, how far could he go in a straight line
before he came upon anyone who knew about glazes and
clays?
When he walked into the house Nathan said: "I've got
an idea. Why don't you write to the Governor of the state?
He ought to be able to tell you what you want to know.
Why don't you? I voted for him."
A letter to the Governor. That ought to bring results.
The next day Luke framed a letter and copied it carefully

THE FIREMAKERS
in pencil on blue-lined paper torn from a tablet, and mailed

Almost before he expected a reply he stopped at the Cul-

The letter itself was not from the Governor. It was

The president of the institution would undoubtedly give
the matter immediate attention. The Governor was always
happy to be of service to the citizens of the great state of
Ohio, and wished him abundant success.
Luke read the letter a half-dozen times on the way home.
When he arrived he asked Nathan to read it.
"Well, at least you're on the track of something," Nathan

Every day Luke walked the two and a half miles to the

on to the appropriate dean. Undoubtedly the matter would
receive immediate attention.
Luke continued his trips. Some days after the presi-

"How many times do they have to pass it on?" Luke

Well, he could satisfy them, couldn't he?

the greatest day of my life," Nathan declared when Luke
