it some more. For three hours he worked impetuously.
Then he wiped his slushy hands on his bed-ticking apron,
got out the booklet, and read it from cover to cover once
again. To think that there was a place like that not even
a hundred miles away!
The late winter fled like a pursued wolf. Early spring
came and brought bees humming dully over maple blos-
soms which yielded little. Full spring came swiftly after,
warm and nervous with life. Then, before they knew it, the
season for fruit jars was upon them.
They had plenty-the accumulations of two seasons. If
no late frosts pounced upon them in the night, they would
be safe. Fearfully yet hopefully they watched the weather
from day to day. But no late frost came.
"See what I told you?" Nathan asked. "It never comes
two years hand running. The Centerville paper says there's
to be the greatest crop of all kinds of fruit that this region
ever saw."
But there was no immediate great demand for fruit jars.
Farmers happened along and bought two or three dozen
each, and hauled them away in their spring wagons. They
THE FIREMAKERS
351
expected to put up lots of everything that year. But Mr.
Rigdon did not come.
Luke grew impatient. He went to Culbertson to learn
what had caused the delay. Mr. Rigdon was out of town.
Some thought he did not mean to return. He was a good
business man. He had made money. It was rumored that
he was going to Kansas where he would have a more exten-
sive field of activity.
"I'll have to sell to the man who buys him out," Luke
said over and over to himself as he trudged homeward. A
great hotness encompassed his face, and he felt unsteady in
the knees. It was all foolish, he knew, to have doubts-
most of all now, when everything important was in sight.
The new man could not come and take possession of Rig-
don's business in an hour. He would go over again in a
day or two. Rigdon would tell the new man, if he had not
already done so, that Luke Dabney made good fruit jars.
But he did not have to go. The next forenoon while he
worked in the shop, and Nathan hoed the early potatoes,
and Val cultivated the flowers in the front yard with a
sturdy table fork, news of Rigdon came to the log house.
"Well," said a familiar, suave voice, "you seem to be de-
voted to your flowers. You were looking at them last fall
when I was here."
She stood up, fork in hand, and confronted Eli.
"You don't have some more of that good buttermilk?"
he asked, a little overgraciously.
"Why-yes, if you like it. The cow was fresh in April
and we have plenty of milk and butter and buttermilk."
He drank a glassful. He thought he should like to have
another. While he drank he fell into his customary inti-
mate chat.
"Pottery business pretty good?"
"Oh," Val replied quietly, yet a bit nervously, "not so
352
THE FIREMAKERS
good as it might be. We've got plenty, and they say there's
to be a lot of fruit, but the ware is not selling yet."
He took a swallow of buttermilk and then regarded her
for an instant with superior intentness, as if he possessed
information not entrusted to ordinary mortals.
"Hate to be pessimistic," he said blandly, taking another
swallow to delay the news another second or two, "but I'm
afraid it never will."
Was she hearing aright? She was overcome by a para-
lyzing coldness.
It seemed an hour before she could speak, and then her
own voice seemed far away.
"Why?" she asked.
"Because the tin can age is here."
She stood stupefied.
"You see," he went on, "they can come down to our
stores and buy the tin cans-the same as the factories use
when they ship canned things for forty cents a dozen.
They have to pay seventy-five cents for a dozen of your
fruit jars."
Val was regaining a little mental control.
"Yes," she argued, "but they can use our fruit jars for
a lifetime if they don't break them and they're as good
as ever; all for seventy-five cents. They can use the tin
cans only once."
"That's all right," he answered, and waved his arm to
indicate what her argument amounted to; "chuck 'em into
a gutter and buy some more. Makes business."
Such economics was to Val beyond belief. "You mean
to say that Mr. Rigdon or some one else won't buy what
we've made?"
"I'm sure Rigdon won't," Eli replied, "for we've bought
him out. We're to have a Company store at Culbertson."
"But you don't have mines over this way."
"Soon will have. That's what I'm over this way for.
L
THE FIREMAKERS
353
That's what I was over this way for last fall. Getting
ready, you see. We took over all that oil company's hold-
ings and we'd long ago bought from the farmers. Had to
take top and all in some cases to get the coal. Farmers'
wives said they'd not live here if they had to put up with
dirty coal mines. Ha! They're a damned stuck-up lot."
Val knew Eli's reputation for adding adornment to truth.
While he talked, her sense of logic brought questions to
mind. "But it's more than two miles to Culbertson-nearly
three, isn't it? How are you going to get the coal down
there to the railroad?"
"Don't have to. It's only a mile over north here to
where the railroad swings round the hills. There's a crooked
little hollow all the way up. The railroad company's put-
ting men to work on the new switch this week. We're be-
ginning on the big tipples, too, without waiting for the
switch."
He was warming to his account of what was being done.
"Ah, they do things in a different way these days. We've
started men at work in those entries now, three shifts a day.
They just walk right back through the hill about fifteen feet
every twenty-four hours.
"We're going to be in need of houses, first of all," he said.
"These men object to walking clear from Culbertson and
back every day. Of course we'll build houses there, too.
But just now we've got to build a Row over in the hollow
the other side of Wiggam's Glory. Lumber begins to come
over this afternoon. It's on the switch at Culbertson.
Don't take long to build a house nowadays. And these are
to be good ones, too. We expect to plaster the downstairs."
He was proud of being a part-even an ambiguous part
-of so much progress. "Yes, sir," he said, "they do things
to-day that our fathers never would have thought of doing.
Why, we're going to have a big fan suck the air through
the mine, and cutting machines, and maybe electric motors
354
THE FIREMAKERS
instead of mules. At least they're being tried out. We'd
be about the first in the state to have them. We're putting
lots of money in the finest equipment money can buy. We
just attack a hill and go straight through in no time. Why,
we're likely to have all this coal out of here in ten years-
yes, sir. It's possible, the way they do things nowadays."
Val was more interested in fruit jars. "If you are going
to do all these things, why can't you buy some fruit jars.
Not all the women want tin cans. They're hard to solder,
and they're wasteful."
He shook his head, graciously deliberative in the pres-
ence of a woman who still was beautiful. "No," he said,
"we have to handle the tin cans. You see we sell coal to
the big factories that make such things, and they expect
us to buy what they manufacture. If they scratch your
back for you, why they expect you to scratch their back
for them."
His pride became specific. "Right straight past Wig-
gam's Glory in that deep hollow is where the No. 1 tipple
will be. The switch will come in, as I said, from the north.
No. 2 will be over to the northeast there on the other side
of the Burrell place. We don't have Burrell's coal. They
say he's fussy; so we never tried him. But we've got every-
thing on all sides of him. So I guess he'll be ready to sell
when the time comes at our price, too. No. 3 is farther
on down toward Culbertson. Sooner or later we'll develop
No. 4 and No. 5 off to the south and southeast. But they
call for a longer switch and more time."
He must be going. It was pleasant to see her again. It
always would be.
She walked in a hot daze through the house to the
kitchen. Giles! What had become of him while she talked.
Her heart leaped. She grew faint. But there he was, per-
fectly safe, watching a ridge of earth slowly rise across the
back yard where a mole worked assiduously underneath.
THE FIREMAKERS
355
Mechanically she went through the process of cooking
dinner. Could she ever break the news to Luke? He would
come in all aglow with something very like certitude. He
would sit down and talk until he caught her eye. And
then he would see that something was wrong, and she would
have to tell him news that would break his heart.
She looked on the little clock-shelf by the door in the
front room to see if the booklet was there where he cus-
tomarily kept it. It was there. Tremblingly she took it
down. It was fairly worn out with much handling.
Nothing before in her life had seemed insurmountably
difficult. Finding food when there was little of it; keeping
clothes together when they were falling to pieces; battling
with copperheads, battling with winter seclusion-these
were definite. One could get at them. But when some-
thing too big to grapple with began to roll in on every side
and squeeze the view ahead into a little round passage like
a culvert or a sewer pipe so long that one could not see the
end of it, or whether there was any end-that took one's
breath away.
How could she break the news to Luke?
CHAPTER VI
UKE did not sicken at the news. He had received bad
news before. But he became nervously alert, like
a caged lion that watches a door which ought to
swing open yet remains closed.
What did Patent Leather Eli know about the pottery
business? That was what Luke wanted somebody to tell
him. Just because Eli had agreed to sell tin cans, did it fol-
low that everybody wanted them? Mr. Rigdon was going
into business somewhere else. Wouldn't he want fruit jars,
wherever he was?
Luke left the table and walked out to the paling fence
where he was accustomed to lean his elbows on the rail
and reflect. Nathan at once came out and spread his el-
bows on the rail alongside.
"Begins to look tight," he said. "I don't see just how
we're coming out, but we must come out somehow. There
have been other people in close quarters. You remember
Phil Sheridan."
It gave their situation significance in his own mind thus
to hitch it up with the exploits of one of his heroes.
"You can hunt up Rigdon, as you say. And you ought
to be able to go over to Centerville and sell some ware your-
self. Take a sample along. Let them see how good it is."
Yes, Luke must be active.
That afternoon he put on some better clothes and went
to learn where Mr. Rigdon had reëstablished himself. On
the hill road down into the village he could look across and
see men at work on the slope which faced the south. They
were setting locust posts in the earth and putting slender
sills upon them. In a week or two Culbertson would have a
Company Row.
356
THE FIREMAKERS
357
Mr. Rigdon was there settling up his affairs. Of course
he would help Luke. But that would be later. He had
bought a new business in Kansas. It was a good opening.
He hoped to be a well-heeled man some day. Just how
many fruit jars he would need would depend on circum-
stances. He was not quite sure yet what there was to can
in the West. But of course he would find out. He wished
Luke the best of luck.
In Centerville the next day he went to a dozen stores
and asked if they did not wish to buy some good stone-
ware. They did not. They were trying to get the women
to use tin cans, the same as the big packers had to use
when they put things up to ship. If they could get tin
cans established on the market, they could make new sales
to the same people every year. Then he went to another
place where glassware and dishes and crockery were dis-
played in the front windows. Yes, the proprietor was in-
terested. Stoneware was not so good a buy as it once was;
still he bought.
He looked at Luke's sample. "Are they all as well turned
and as well glazed as this sample? I don't stand for any
shenanigan."
"You don't have to," Luke answered, and looked him
squarely in the eyes. "What you buy will be according to
sample."
The old man was deliberative. "If you can get a hun-
dred dozen 'halves' and fifty dozen 'gallons' to me within
ten days I'll take them. Do you suppose the railroad com-
pany can find a car for you?"
"I'll get them here," Luke assured him, "if I have to
hire some wagons and haul them across the hills."
"That's the way to talk," the old man replied. There
was pleasantness in his voice. "I hope to sell them. Good-
by and good luck."
Luke caught the afternoon train for Culbertson.
Wouldn't he have some news?
358
THE FIREMAKERS
He thought the train would never cover the twenty
miles. It puffed and whistled and rattled and creaked to
such little effect.
Finally it did arrive.
"Yes," the station agent told him, "if you can have your
ware loaded by supper time to-morrow when the local
freight comes through, I'll give you that car. It has to go
back to Centerville anyway."
At supper time the next day the car was loaded. A
freight engine coupled on to it with a crash great enough,
Luke thought, to break half the ware imbedded in the
straw. Then it went puffing away with the fruit jars and
with carloads of sugar and beer and mine powder and oil
and tin cans.
That night after Giles was asleep in his trundle bed, Luke
and Val and Nathan sat outside in the cooling air and talked
of greater sales that were certain to come. When at last
they had wearied their imaginations, they went inside.
Nathan climbed the steep, whitewashed stairs without so
much as a match to light his way-he said he could get
about in that room now without so much as eyes, if he had
to-and Luke and Val sat in the pale circle of warm lamp-
light and read. Val read from a volume of poems which
somebody in 1872 had presented to her mother with good
wishes written on the flyleaf, and Luke, leaning over in his
chair to get the full effect of the light, read once more from
the worn little booklet.
New days brought other excitement. The road along the
horseshoe ridge to Wiggam's Glory was alive with teams
hauling lumber and more lumber and yet more lumber.
There were heavy timbers for the tipple, and light timbers
and thousands of pine boards with knots in them for the
new houses.
Almost before the lumber was off the wagons it was
fitted into something. Locust posts served as stilts on which
THE FIREMAKERS
359
to build the houses. And the knotty pine served as both
weather-boarding and plaster. Was there, then, to be no
plaster, after all? Well, circumstances had to be consid-
ered. Men were needed for all sorts of work in which a
knowledge of pick and shovel was useful, and the Company
decided to forego the distinction of having plastered houses.
It took plaster many days to dry. Anyhow, chairs and
table corners might go through plaster-they had been
known to do so. It would be more difficult for them to
penetrate boards. To be sure, knots might fall out of
boards and leave knot-holes. But if a knot fell out of a
board on the inside of the wall, by all the impartial laws of
chance one was not likely to fall out of a board exactly op-
posite on the outside. It was reasonable, therefore, to as-
sume that cold would have no direct entrance except
through the floor.
Luke thought he would work a day or two at the wheel
and keep some ware on the way to the kiln while he made
further side-trips to sell his product.
In the middle of the morning while he worked in com-
plete absorption, Pewee Dunkle burst in upon him. "Damn
your hide," Pewee exclaimed in the most affectionate cor-
diality, "I'm to be your neighbor again. Our things is on
the way now. We're to have as good a house as there are
in the hollow-because, by God, they're all exactly alike."
He was interested in Luke's great adventure. The pres-
ence of so much ware bespoke prosperity. There was an
abundance of things piled high everywhere. Luke's un-
affected confidence after the sale he had made in Centerville
only deepened in Pewee's mind the impression that Luke's
affluence had long been established.
Pewee wanted to have some connection with such a cred-
itable enterprise. "Say," he said, "we'll all need fruit jars
over in the new Row. There's oodles of stuff to put up this
year. In them fresh houses the women'll be for cannin'
360
THE FIREMAKERS
everything in sight. Eight to ten dozen fruit jars for every
woman is about where I'd put the figure. 'Tain't, much, as
you deal in things, but it would get you some shoes and nut-
megs."
True to Pewee's prediction, the fresh, clean-smelling
houses gave the women a new lease on life. They canned
everything edible. Raspberries, blackberries, elderberries-
these hung in loads on briars and bushes in every hollow
and on every hillside, and were to be had for the picking.
The farmers roundabout had trees breaking with peaches,
and since work was good, the wives bought. Some of them,
in fact, did not trouble themselves with the formality of
buying. In a time of such abundance, why shouldn't a
woman help herself the least bit?
The women did not care for tin cans. They preferred
Luke's product. Luke had been one of them once. He was
a nice boy, the women said. They could have slipped down
into the hollow any night after dark and carried away as
many fruit jars as they required, and Luke would not have
missed them from the great piles in the ware-yard. In-
stead, when work for the day was over and supper was out
of the way, the men in shirtsleeves, and their wives in
mother-hubbards, came up over the hill and down through
the corner of the woods toward the pottery. At a given
point the men whistled with fingers in mouth, or blew in
their hands, or shouted in a long, sustained Hello-0-0-0-0!
so that Luke would know they wanted ware. Then they
trailed on. They philandered in roughshod language, they
made remarks about needing extra fruit for increases in
families certain to result from such sylvan surroundings.
As they made their way down the steepest banks they
pushed each other and laughed and screamed in affected
desperation.
Luke was at the shop by the time they arrived, and
helped them to fill their huge baskets and gunny-sacks, or
THE FIREMAKERS
361
showed the men once more how to string the handles of the
gallon sizes on a strap or a rope so that they might be car-
ried over the shoulders with comfort.
Then the procession started back. The women pro-
fessed great fear of snakes. The men, troubled with fears
of their own ever so slightly, saved themselves by working
on the imaginations of the women. They pictured great
copperheads in every darkening tuft of grass, in every rus-
tling leaf. Luke watched them for a time and smiled, and
then trudged back to the house with more money in his
pocket. These sales were just teasers. But they were
something.
1
Mr. Rigdon eventually wrote. Kansas was a great coun-
try. But it did not grow the things people put in fruit
jars. It specialized in wheat and sunshine. He had bought
a grain elevator.
The man in Centerville thought he could not use more
ware. He again wished Luke good luck.
Luke became jumpy. He wanted more than teasers and
good wishes. At the rate he was traveling he would never
arrive at the point where he had five hundred dollars.
He hurried in every direction to every town big enough
to have any kind of a store. And he found customers, yes.
But there were none of consequence.
Nathan hired a team and went forth to peddle. He al-
ways sold a little. But by the time he had paid for the
horses and wagon and horse-feed, there was little money
left to show for his efforts.
When the wind was from the west one day, Val heard
loud hammering and sawing. It was nearer than the hol-
low beyond Wiggam's Glory. She watched for a time and
at last could see men at work among the trees in the little
park itself. Could it be that there was to be a Company
Row on the ridge?
At supper they discussed the matter. "The first thing we

know," Luke said, "they'll have a gob dump down in the

"Well, I'd rather have that," Val said, "than a Company

A new fear lay hold of them. But that evening when

fully, and who sailed empty whisky bottles at her head.
The music, the calls, the rhythmic shuffle of hard soles
on the rough platform, were not in themselves unpleasant-
especially at a distance. But they were the same sounds
that had vaulted from the Knights of Labor Hall in Green
Valley.
Luke and Val and Nathan sat on the edge of the little

Lycurgus Ott making his speeches over there. He always
finds out where a crowd is."

How Luke lived on and despaired not, it would be diffi-
cult to say. Sales slowly dwindled to nothing. Yes, every-
body liked his ware. It was good ware-fine ware.
nobody was needing much. There were places, he heard,
where people would have nothing else. He tried to learn
where these places were, but he could not find out.
He talked less. He was disturbed when others talked.
He and Val sat silently together and looked away, their
eyes desperately alight for something which threatened to
disappear utterly, yet which they could still sometimes see.

CHAPTER VII
INTER approached. Winter came. Winter be-
gan to relax its grip a little. One day in February
after Val had put the dinner dishes away, all
three stood before the fireplace warming their backs while
they looked at the begonias and geraniums and bleeding-
heart on the triangular flower stand at the south window.
Giles amused himself on the floor with a cat.
Suddenly Nathan turned, half facing the other two. His
manner told them that he was about to say something mo-
mentous.
"Look here!" he said with pent-up earnestness in his
voice. "Do you know you're not getting where you started
out for?"
They did not need to answer.
"I'm not in favor of giving a thing up unless you have to.
But if you can't have a whole loaf with all the butter you
want, why sometimes it hurts nothing to take what you
can get with no butter at all.
"Now this is what I've made up my mind to do. I
don't expect you to agree with me, especially Luke. But
I've gone over it all a hundred times, and I've decided. I'm
going into the mine."
They were too amazed to offer immediate protest.
"Why not?" he asked. "Here it is, right under my nose.
I feel just as well as I ever did in my life. Val won't like
the dirt, but then she don't have to. That smokehouse is
good and tight, and will make a perfect place to clean up in.
I won't have to step inside the house with my diggers on.
In winter a fire in that old stove out there will heat the
water and the air all at the same time.
363
364
THE FIREMAKERS
"I'll pay my board out of my pension and I'll give the
two of you everything I make. When there's enough-five
hundred dollars or whatever it is-Luke can go off and
study and I can stay here with Val and the boy and keep
our heads above water."
"But Val must get out, too," Luke argued. "That has
been a part of my plan all the time."
"That'll come when you're on the way yourself."
While Nathan spoke Val saw a gigantic coal miner as
high as the house stalking up to her kitchen door. She
saw also the artist she had hoped to become, receding like
a wisp of color in the distance.
But she immediately approved her father's plan. "I can
go later just as well as not," she said in support of the
proposal. She feared that Luke's generosity of spirit might
cause him to balk at going away to enjoy enlightenment
alone.
Luke did protest again and again. Still he had to admit
that the plan was reasonable. It was one way out-and
just now there seemed to be no other.
The world became alive again. Val got an early supper,
and they sat long at the table. They talked over all the
details: how Luke could live; what things they could send
to him to save expense; where Nathan could find a suit of
diggers; where he could get a buddy; and whether when he
applied at the mine there would really be a place for him.
There was a place. Charley Ferris, boss at the No. I
mine beyond Wiggam's Glory, declared that such a miner as
Nathan could have the best room in the mine. Eli Raynor,
too, when he heard what was to be done, expressed his
hearty, semi-official sanction.
On the following Monday morning when Nathan went
to work, he knew that Val watched him as long as he was
in sight of the house. He understood how it frightened her
to see a man in diggers leaving in the morning and-if the
THE FIREMAKERS
365
fates should be willing-coming back again in the evening.
He was a little anxious on his own part. He had supposed
that he was through.
The mine did not seem utterly strange, despite the inno-
vations. There were no mules in sight, to be sure, but Jab
Strohl sat on the firm seat of a little motor, ready to push
a crank round and put the train of cars in motion. "There's
only one thing wrong with these God-damned contraptions,"
he told Nathan. "It don't do no good to cuss 'em. I need
a mule once in a while."
Nathan wore a new kind of lamp too-a safety lamp.
Drift mines usually did not have much fire damp, he
thought. But this was a new day that required new de-
vices.
Nathan was to work in a room that was almost worked
out. When it was finished he was to be moved far into the
hill where two men had given up a new room and were not
expected back.
He felt a little lost in the low, black room, although it
was the kind he had worked in for thirty years. Doing
something else for a year and a half or so made a man
see things differently. How long had the room been
idle? Might there be any gas pocketed there? He moved
his safety lamp along the roof, but the flame inside did not
indicate the presence of gas-at least he could not see that
it did. But in the middle of the forenoon when he stopped
to have a smoke, and tossed the burning match away, a
pale, sputtering blue flame ran along the roof. It was noth-
ing much. He had seen that before.
"I'll have enough to pay for my tools by the time I go
to that other room," he told them at home.
Since Val seemed not to suffer such great distress at
thought of the mine, he soon fell to talking about the mar-
vels of the new methods employed. "How they do get
those entries back through the hill!
Three shifts of men
366
THE FIREMAKERS
working. First ones begin at eight in the morning; next
ones at four in the afternoon, and the next ones at midnight.
We used to think we made headway when we worked extra
on Sunday. And that fan, pulling the air through, day
and night! Makes a thousand revolutions a minute, or
something like that, somebody said. At least it's a lot of
them."
The day came when he was to be transferred to the other
room. When he was ready to go to the mine that morning
and Luke was ready to go to the shop, they walked together
until they came to the path down through the woods and up
over the hill past Wiggam's Glory which Nathan was slowly
tramping into existence. In a few days he would have a
buddy. Then it would not sound quite so empty in his
room when he scraped the shovel over the floor.
Luke sold five dollars' worth of ware that morning. A
farmer wanted some milk crocks, and some new jugs for
maple molasses which he had decided to sell, and a big
jar or two for rain water. After that sale, Luke returned
to work with zest.
While he cut the chyme in a gallon fruit jar with a new
chyme-rib he had made himself from the body of an old
apple tree, Pewee Dunkle burst into the shop.
"For God's sake, Luke, the mine's afire and the old
man's in there."
Luke bounded from his wheel and they started, Luke un-
fastening his apron and getting out of it as he ran.
"Explosion, I guess," Pewee explained as they hurried.
He was too much out of breath to say more.
"Only a few in there. No work to-day. But the ones
in there got theirs."
As they slowed down to a dog-trot on the steep hillside,
Pewee made further broken explanation: "Must 'a' been
coal dust. Worse the farther out it got."
They gasped for breath but did not stop. "Poor old
THE FIREMAKERS
367
Jab. He was hauling gob. Was comin' out at the mouth.
Blew him and his damned motor into a switch of empties.
Broke every bone in his body, I guess."
On they ran.
They eventually had to slow down to a hurried walk.
"Who else?" Luke asked fearfully.
"Don't know. Only three or four, I guess. Two or three
fellows just beginnin'. They wanted to clean up their
rooms. Old man wanted to see what his new room was
like."
"Think he's-?" Breathlessness plus fear kept Luke
from finishing the question.
"Don't know. Can't get back to him. Explosion busted
the fan. Air's all movin' the wrong way and feedin' the
fire under the air well. Some bank props piled off there.
Roarin' like forty hells."
When they reached the ridge and were ready to descend
on the other side beyond Wiggam's Glory, they could run
again. The women and children as well as the men had
deserted the new Row utterly.
On the two ran. When they were breathless they walked
a few steps. When they regained their breath they ran
again.
Could it be, Luke thought as they ran, that he was to
experience once again the old suspense, the old pain, the old
dreads? Another cave-in? The thought sickened him. A
disaster to them all, perhaps, in the middle of a bright
forenoon. Nathan's words throbbed in his ears as he ran-
those words about the casual character of triumph and de-
feat.
As they came up to the mine, everything looked normal
at the tipple save the crowd of women who stood talking,
and four or five straight figures under some canvas by the
blacksmith shop.
368
THE FIREMAKERS
They scarcely took notice. They hurried to the mouth
of the mine.
"Don't get your head against that trolley wire," Pewee
cautioned Luke. "There might still be some juice in it."
In the briefest time they were within sound of the fire.
Despite all high-pressure methods of driving entries, men
could not drive through the hills in a year or two.
The first sounds they heard were faint, muffled shouts.
Then they heard the crackling and roar of the flames that
had got into the seasoned, dry bank props.
They came up. Men were shoveling the water-soaked
hard-pan of the floor out to the edges of the crackling
furnace. Others were filling topless powder cans from a
little dam which they had made in the drain along the
track. But there was not enough water. The flames were
taking hold of some slack and were beginning to eat into
the ribs of solid coal the least bit, with much snapping.
They must subdue the fire enough to get past, and learn
about Nathan. They could shut up the mine and smother
the fire, but they did not wish to smother Nathan if he still
lived.
"Look!" some one shouted.
A figure was slowly emerging from the black depths of
the entry beyond the fire. He was feeling his way along.
Some of the men yelled in joyous release at the sight of him.
He hesitated. Then he moved forward again, very, very
slowly.
"He's dazed or something," Luke said. "He's coming
straight into the fire."
"Go back! Go back! Go back!" they yelled to him at
the top of their voices. "Stay there. We'll come."
Whether he could make out their words, or could com-
prehend if he heard, was more than they could say. The
men frantically scooped up every gallon of water that came
down the drain and dashed it on to the edge of the fire that
THE FIREMAKERS
369
reached farthest into the entry. For an instant the red
blackened. Then the glow came again.
A breath of heat caused Nathan to turn back. He felt
a little farther in the other direction along the side of the
entry and then turned again, like a sleepwalker in his
uncertainty.
Luke grabbed somebody's coat, threw it into the little
pool of water, wrapped it round his head and neck, and, with
his face turned from the fire, darted past.
"By God, he's there!" yelled Pewee, hysterical.
The props and sawed timber could not burn forever, and
the edges of firm coal did not burn rapidly. The men
fought on against the hotness in the entry. Luke tested
the heat from time to time and waited. After one testing
he wrapped his wet coat round Nathan's head, pulled his
own shirt up till it protected his nose and mouth a little,
took a firm hold on Nathan's arm and told him to get ready
and not to stop running till somebody said so.
As he stumbled to a stop the men gathered round him.
"Are you all right, Mr. Boyd?"
"Yes, I'm all right. But I can't see. I must have been
knocked senseless. Can't seem to remember. When I got
up from the floor of my room there was something over my
eyes, and I can't get it off. Let's get out where it's light."
S
CHAPTER VIII
IGHTLESS eyes brought to Nathan a timidity which
he had never before experienced. "Seems like I'm
stepping off the edge of something all the time," he
said, as he felt his way cautiously about the house. His
face and hands were burned, but they afforded him little
solicitude. "If I could feel sure there was going to be any-
thing to step on when I step, I'd not mind a burn or two.
Hide will patch itself if you give it time."
His blindness wrought a change in Val and Luke, too.
Something of his timidity lay hold of them. At first they
were too much occupied to make the discovery themselves.
Val had to keep the chairs in their places so that Nathan
would not tumble over them. She had to caution him about
stepping up one step when he went from the kitchen to the
front room. And she had to remind Giles hourly that he
must always say something when he saw grandpoppy com-
ing in his direction.
Luke found a discarded rake handle and shaped it into
a long, stafflike cane with which Nathan could feel his way
about. For a time he contented himself with the inside of
the house, pecking the table legs, the chair legs, and the
bed as he moved the staff back and forth in short, semi-
circular sweeps. After he had become a little less fearful of
stepping off into a chasm wherever he went, he ventured
out of doors and away from the doorstep.
"No, don't lead me," he said to Val. "Just watch me.
If I'm about to fall into anything, holla at me. I've got to
learn walking all over again."
One day he declared that he believed he could follow the

Val directed him along many paths. He was becoming

"I'm learning them; I am, for a fact." On another oсса-

He must be persistent. He wanted to learn the region so
well that he could go about without so much as anyone

one morning. "Let me get my bearings. I want to be able
to tell where they're dumping coal."
He stood listening, a gaunt, angular figure in the morn-
ing sun. After a time the breeze carried to him the distant
rattle and roar of a carload of coal going over the screens
in some tipple. "That's at No. 1, ain't it?" he asked. "So
over here would be Number 2. Listen. Yes, there goes a
car at No. 2. And No. 3 would be over there." He waited
a long time but heard nothing. "Wind's wrong, I reckon."
He heard a roar off in another direction. "Or is that
No. 3 off here?"
"No," Val explained, "that's the new No. 4 that they've
just opened."
"And the hammering and pounding-where is that? Off
to the south, ain't it?"
"That's at the tipple for the new No. 5. They're not
ready to take coal out over there yet."
He turned slowly round, his head in an attitude of lis-
tening. Then he started on his way back toward the house.
"They'll have us completely surrounded pretty soon," he
remarked.
372
THE FIREMAKERS
"Yes," Val answered, and she was glad he could not see
her face when she spoke.
From his former fellow miners when they dropped in to
see him he added to his acquaintance with what was taking
place in the vicinity. Pewee Dunkle sat for two hours with
him and Val and Luke one evening and told them of the
many things he understood were true. He detailed a long
list. After these had been discussed one by one, he said
with a little sarcasm, "And-I understand that Patent
Leather Eli means to bring you a check from Bogart."
"He'd better not!" Luke retorted savagely. "Not while
I'm here. I'll brain him with a fruit jar or something if he
tries any of his fine manners while I'm around."
"Why, whoever heard you talk like that?" Val asked in
consternation.
"Well, do you want to see him yourself with his slick
speech and his little dab of money?"
"Don't know as I want to see him at all," she said, know-
ing the while that she felt just as Luke did about him.
Pewee's eyes were wide as he looked smiling from Luke
to Val and from Val back to Luke. "Well, don't worry;
the chances are he'll never come, anyway." Thus he re-
stored unanimity.
"But anyhow," he went on, "I'd think you ought to get
an increase in your pension. When a man can't work, why
just what the hell is he to do if somebody don't look out
for him?"
Nathan had thought of that, yes. One of his comrades
had gone to the pension agent in Centerville to present the
case. The agent did not know just what could be done.
He would talk it over with their congressman, who was
soon to be out for reëlection and therefore might reasonably
be expected to be generous in his outlook. "But of course,"
he said, "Mr. Boyd will have to remember that it's one
thing to lose your eyesight in a coal mine and another to
THE FIREMAKERS
373
lose it in the service of your country. We have to watch
out. Everybody is finding some reason for wanting an in-
crease. But I'll see what can be done. Of course I'd be
glad, myself, if he could get it."
"Sounds like sorghum," Pewee declared. "I'll bet he
never smelled gunpowder or loaded a car of coal either."
Probably so; Nathan didn't know, of course.
He was glad to hear all that Pewee reported. He was
glad just to sit and chat. He found that he enjoyed having
nothing in particular to do, now that he had to face the ex-
perience. These old friends dropping in from time to time
-he would enjoy nothing better than just greeting them
when they came, and gassing with them as long as they
wanted to stay, if only he had his eyes. Even without eyes,
it was pleasant to feel that he was through, that he could
amuse himself by living over experiences which could now
bring him no pain more poignant than a lingering sadness.
The next morning he sat in the shade of a cherry tree in
the front yard and mused. While he was wishing that the
day might pass swiftly so that some of the men could come
over from the Wiggam's Glory hollow and sit through the
evening with him, an unheralded visitor came up. He was
a brisk young man at least he had a brisk voice and
with the briefest preliminary "How do you do," proceeded
at once to sell Nathan a book. It was called The Great
Wonders of the World.
"But don't you see I can't read?" Nathan asked him.
The young man was taken aback at the discovery. "Oh,
I beg your pardon; I didn't notice that you were-were-"
"No, 'twouldn't do me no good to have it."
But the brisk young man soon recovered his equilibrium.
He needed money for college bills. "Haven't you a wife or
somebody who would like a copy? It's unquestionably one
of the greatest bargains ever offered to the American pub-
lic."
374
THE FIREMAKERS
"Maybe my daughter might. She reads-reads a lot.
But I don't know as she'd care to buy."
"Then I'll be glad to trade. I have to eat, and sometimes

wives to sign up, and if your daughter will give me my din-
ner and supper and let my horse eat some grass, I'll give
her a copy of the book-in full cloth with gilt lettering.
Really this volume is the only complete thing of its kind
published."
For a book of such distinction, Val was ready to cook
two meals. While the young man ate, he regaled them
with stories of what he did in college. At supper, after he
had sold a dozen copies of the book in the Company Row
beyond Wiggam's Glory, he was especially voluble. He
told of how he and some of his fellows had put a bag of
beans high over the desk in chapel, with a silk thread at-
tached, and when the president was in the middle of his
prayer, had pulled the thread and emptied the bag on his
bald head; of how they had found a dozen nice, dry sleighs
in barns in the college town and had hauled them all to the
campus one night before a football game and burned them
in a great bonfire; of how they spent most of two nights
filing the clapper off the college bell so that there remained
no means of calling the students to their work; of how
they kept an agreement not to go back to college after
Christmas until two days later than the time fixed upon by
the faculty.
Luke heard all this with interest. There evidently were
many things in college about which he knew little. Finally
he made inquiry: "Ever studied anything about glazes and
mixing clays?"
No, the young man had not. He had never looked into
anything very closely; he was not certain what he would do
when he got out of college, and he did not wish to waste
his time studying too much of the wrong thing. He did not

There were "lesser wonders." Poppies against Italian
mountain sides; snow on Pike's Peak; dykes in Holland;
the Stonehenge in England; Greek vases; pottery by na-
tives in Central America; a petrified forest; totem poles in
Alaska.
Val read because the book cheered Nathan. But to her
the pages brought infinite pain. So many interesting things
in the world to see, so many beautiful things; yet how much
of a chance was there that she and Luke would ever see
them? Her father in a four-year respite from digging coal
had offered his breast to Minié balls and canister, and inci-
dentally had seen some of these marvelous things. She
THE FIREMAKERS
377
would gladly take the risks he had taken if she were as-

places. But it looked as if she should have to content her-

ing. They were creeping closer and closer on every side.
From the mines they had once escaped, but the mines had
caught up with them. The towering white-oaks in the
woods on the hillsides would be cut for bank props, the
pastures would be abandoned to broomsedge, thickets of
briars and locust trees would come, Company Row would
deteriorate into a lot of old shacks, and once more she and
Luke would be living in Green Valley.
When they had grown a little away from their preoccu-
pation with Nathan's new needs, Luke tramped restlessly
about one evening, trying to forget that he was selling less
stoneware than ever. There would always be some sale,
he could see that. But the tin can age had brought an end
to the part of the business that was most profitable. If only
he could learn more about making fine-looking things, the
kind for which the tin can age could never offer substitutes,
he would be safe. He could then sell to people who had
notions of what they wanted. But every time he turned in
whatsoever direction, every time anyone else moved in his
behalf, something tightened its grip upon him, made his
clear head and sturdy hands a little more impotent.
Val lighted the lamp in the house and he came in out of
the twilight. In the illuminated circle on the stand lay the
new book. Luke picked it up in an attitude of "Well,
what's in this book, anyhow?" Something caught his at-
tention. He dropped into a chair and made the mottled
edges of the leaves crackle sharply as he filliped them with
thumb and finger. Then he began to leaf through the vol-

in desperate concentration. Val saw, and dared not speak.

He turned the piece round and round on the board.

"There are some things," he said, "that you simply can't

of you for a year, I might learn. There must be people

Val was not a tearful person. But tears stood in her eyes

more happily once before in the same quiet, they hid their

After this full acknowledgment that the way was pretty
black, they felt somewhat relieved. They climbed the long
ridge together, hand in hand, like chastened lovers. They
scarcely spoke.
At the house they apprised Nathan and Giles of their re-
turn. Luke walked out to the side yard where he was ac-
customed to meditate, and leaned his elbows on the rail of
the paling fence.
So here he was. And this was not Out. Sometimes he
had transient moments of believing that he felt as others
did who had found the way-the designers, the builders,
the poets, the envisagers of satisfying prospects. Flashes
of a strange confidence assured him that he was living in the
fringe of a world inhabited by vivid personalities, and that
if he could only breathe a freer air for a time, he would be-
come a member of that world himself.
But always something that men had organized for their
own advantage kept him like a lively spiral under relentless
weight. Wherever he turned, Coal stared at him with a
black face. "You thought you were quite a hero, did you?"
Coal asked. "Well, you see how much I bother about
heroes."
Val came out and leaned on the fence beside him.
"Maybe," Luke said, "if we begin early enough, we'll be
able to get the boy out."
"We ought to. But doesn't it sound as if people them-
selves were about done for when they begin to talk about
saving their children?"
380
THE FIREMAKERS
"Maybe so. But do you see anything else to do?"
"No."
They pressed each other's hands on the rail, and stood
trembling and numb before an assailant with whom they
could not grapple to any avail.
A mile to the north the coal trains-double headers-
puffed and snorted in their efforts to make their way up the
steep grade from the river valley and out into the flat coun-
try which stretched away and away to the cities of the
Great Lakes. Occasionally when a fireman on one of the
locomotives threw open the firedoor, the heavy smoke above
the level of the hills was faintly illuminated.
They talked on. The evening faded into the blue dark-
ness of summer night. Directly in front of them, not a
mile away, the gobpile at No. 2 mine filled the sky with a
dull glow. Farther to the east another pile burned so
brightly that the oaks stood in clear outline against the
horizon. To the west the light from another pile com-
bined with the flaring, wavering gas-light at Wiggam's
Glory to make a misplaced, degraded aurora borealis.
Occasionally the breeze brought to them the laughter
and shouts of those who had come up from Company Row
in the hollow beyond Wiggam's Glory to enjoy the fresh air
and relaxation of the ridge. Then they heard fiddles.
Then they heard music and dancing.
In a lull between dances they could hear an oratorical,
exhorting voice. Nathan had prophesied aright; it was
old Lycurgus Ott, in the midst of his disjointed harangue
"the imminence of cosmic springs if ever we lift the
veil and remove the pressure."
...
They turned to go in to Nathan and Giles.
"Are those diggers of your dad's still hanging in the
smokehouse?" Luke asked. "I had better see if they'll
fit me, or if I'll have to get some new ones."
